viii  
INTRODUCTION 
 
 
I, the Chairperson, of the Standing Committee on Finance, having been 
authorised by the Committee, present this Thirty- Sixth Report (Eighteenth Lok Sabha) 
on the Bill ‘The Securities Markets Code, 2025’ of the Ministry of Finance (Department 
of Economic Affairs). 
2. The Securities Markets Code, 2025, introduced in Lok Sabha on 18 December, 
2025 was referred to the Committee on 19 December, 2025 for examination and report 
thereon, by the Speaker, Lok Sabha under Rule 331E of the Rules of Procedure and 
Conduct of Business in Lok Sabha. 
3. The Committee took briefing of the representatives of the Ministry of Finance 
(Department of Economic Affairs) at their sitting held on 08 January, 2026. Thereafter, 
on 20 January, 2026, the Committee took oral evidence of the representatives of 
the National Stock Exchange of India Ltd. (NSE) and the Bombay Stock Exchange 
Ltd. (BSE). Subsequently, on 02 February, 2026, the Committee took oral evidence of 
the representatives of the Securities and Exchange Board of India (SEBI). In their next 
sitting on 09 February, 2026, the Committee took oral evidence of the representatives 
of the Insurance Regulatory and Development Authority of India (IRDAI). Further, on 
11 February, 2026, the Committee took oral evidence of the representatives of the 
Reserve Bank of India (RBI). Thereafter, on 10 April, 2026, the Committee heard views 
of Shri M. Damodaran, Former Chairperson, Securities and Exchange Board of India 
(SEBI). On the same day, they also heard views of the representatives of TRILEGAL. 
Further, on 23 April, 2026, the Committee heard views of Dr. M.S. Sahoo, Former  
Chairperson, Insolvency and Bankruptcy Board of India (IBBI) and Whole-time Member 
of the Securities and Exchange Board of India (SEBI). On the same day, the 
Committee also heard views of the representatives of the Institute of Cost Accountants 
of India (ICMAI), the Institute of Company Secretaries of India (ICSI) and the Institute 
of Chartered Accountants of India (ICAI) on the Bill. The Committee thereafter, on 24 
April, 2026 took the oral evidence of the representatives of the Pension Fund 
Regulatory and Development Authority (PFRDA), International Financial Services 
Centers Authority (IFSCA) and Insolvency and Bankruptcy Board of India (IBBI). On 
the same day, the Committee also heard views of the representatives of Federation of 
Indian Chambers of Commerce & Industry (FICCI) and The Associated Chambers of 
Commerce & Industry of India (ASSOCHAM). 
During the course of their examination of the Bill, the Committee held extensive 
consultations with a wide cross -section of stakeholders, including regulators, market 
infrastructure institutions, industry associations, professional bodies, legal experts, 
market participants, academic institutions and other interested organisations. The 
Committee also undertook a Study Visit to Bengaluru and Mumbai from 23rd to 26th 
February, 2026, during which it interacted with nearly 50 stakeholders representing 
different segments of the securities markets. Overall, the Committee received 1,055 
suggestions from more than 70 stakeholders on various provisions of the Bill. Each 

ix  
suggestion was carefully examined by the Committee and discussed comprehensively 
with the representatives of the Ministry during the clause-by-clause examination of the 
Bill, with a view to assessing its legal, regulatory and practical implications. 
Finally, on 11 June, 2026, the Committee took the concluding evidence of the 
representatives of the Ministry of Law and Justice (Department of Legal Affairs and 
Legislative Department). On the same day, the Committee also took the evidence of 
the representatives of the Ministry of Finance (Department of Economic Affairs) for the 
clause-by-clause examination of the Bill. Thereafter, the Committee held further 
sittings on 12 June, 2026, 18 June, 2026, 25 June, 2026, 9 July, 2026 and 17 July, 
2026, during which it again took the evidence of the representatives of the Ministry of 
Finance (Department of Economic Affairs) to continue the clause -by-clause 
examination of the Bill. 
4. The Committee considered and adopted this Report at their Sitting held on 
21 July, 2026. 
5. The Committee wish to express their thanks to all the representatives of the 
above-mentioned Ministries and organizations for appearing before the Committee 
and furnishing the requisite material and information desired by the Committee in 
connection with the examination of the subject. 
6. The Committee would also like to place on record their deep sense of 
appreciation for the invaluable assistance rendered to them by the officials of Lok 
Sabha Secretariat attached to the Committee. 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
New Delhi; 
21 July, 2026 
30 Ashadha, 1948 (Saka) 
BHARTRUHARI MAHTAB 
Chairperson, 
Standing Committee on Finance

1 
 
CHAPTER I  
 
I. INTRODUCTORY 
 
1.1  The Securities Markets Code Bill, 2025 seeks to introduce the Securities Markets Code, 2025 
with a view to consolidating, rationalising and replacing the following three principal legislations 
governing the securities market, namely:— 
• the Securities Contracts (Regulation) Act, 1956 (SCRA); 
• the Securities and Exchange Board of India Act, 1992 (SEBI Act); and 
• the Depositories Act, 1996. 
 
1.2  Background 
 
The existing legal framework governing the securities market comprises three separate enactments, 
each operating within its own legislative domain, namely:— 
 
• the Securities Contracts (Regulation) Act, 1956, enacted to prevent undesirable 
transactions in securities by regulating the business of dealing therein; 
• the Securities and Exchange Board of India Act, 1992, which provides for the 
establishment of the Securities and Exchange Board of India (SEBI) as the principal 
regulator of the securities market for protecting the interests of investors, promoting 
the development of the securities market and regulating the securities market; and 
• the Depositories Act, 1996, which provides for the regulation of depositories in 
securities. 
 
These enactments were passed at different points in time over several decades. Over the years, 
certain provisions have become overlapping, fragmented or obsolete. Consequently, the existing 
legislative architecture warranted review and adaptation in light of evolving regulatory practices and 
rapid technological advancements. 
 
Recognising this need, the Union Budget for 2021 –22 proposed the consolidation of the provisions 
contained in the aforesaid enactments, together with the Government Securities Act, 2006, into a 
comprehensive and rationalised Securities Markets Code. 
 
Pursuant to this announcement, the Department of Economic Affairs constituted a Committee 
comprising representatives from the Department of Economic Affairs, the Legislative Department, the 

2 
 
Department of Legal Affairs, the Ministry of Corporate Affairs and the Department of Financial 
Services to prepare the draft Securities Markets Code. 
 
 
1.3 The Committee constituted for drafting the Securities Markets Code was guided by the 
following Terms of Reference:— 
 
• improving regulatory governance; 
 
• incorporating prior publication of proposed regulations, except in cases of emergency, as 
part of the regulation-making process; 
 
• ensuring an arm’s -length separation between  fact finding functions and adjudicatory 
functions, as well as the corresponding functionaries within the regulator; and 
 
• strengthening the responsibility of regulators to build internal institutional capacity for 
effective market regulation, including measures to minimise systemic risks and market 
failures, and to facilitate the timely identification of aberrant conduct by regulated entities. 
 
1.4 Since Government securities are intrinsically linked with public debt management and the 
conduct of monetary policy, it was decided to keep the Government Securities Act, 2006 outside the 
ambit of the proposed Securities Markets Code. 
 
1.5 The draft Securities Markets Code was subsequently revised on the basis of the Department’s 
internal examination and consultations with the Securities and Exchange Board of India (SEBI). 
 
Inter-Ministerial consultations were held with the Department of Financial Services, the Department 
of Revenue, the Department of Expenditure, the Department of Investment and Public Asset 
Management, the Department of Public Enterprises, the Department of Legal Affairs, the Legislative 
Department, the Department for Promotion of Industry and Internal Trade and the Ministry of 
Corporate Affairs. Consultations were also held with the Securities and Exchange Board of India, the 
International Financial Services Centres Authority (IFSCA) and the Reserve Bank of India (RBI). The 
comments received during the Inter -Ministerial consultation process were examined by the 
Department of Economic Affairs and suitably addressed in the draft Bill. 
 
Following legal drafting and vetting by the Ministry of Law and Justice, the Department of Economic 
Affairs circulated the advance copy of the Draft Cabinet Note to the Cabinet Secretariat on 22 March 
2025. 

3 
 
Subsequently, the Cabinet Secretariat, vide Office Memorandum dated 4 August 
2025, constituted a Group of Ministers (GoM) to examine the proposed Bill and make 
recommendations for consideration by the Ministry of Finance. The recommendations of the  
Group of Ministers were duly examined, following which the revised Note for the Cabinet was 
forwarded to the Cabinet Secretariat. 
 
After obtaining the approval of the Cabinet, the Bill was introduced in Lok Sabha on 18 December 
2025 and was thereafter referred to the Department -related Parliamentary Standing Committee on 
Finance for examination. 
 
1.6  Salient Features of the Securities Markets Code Bill, 2025  
The salient features of the proposed Securities Markets Code, 2025 are as follows:— 
 
• The Code seeks to establish a principles -based legislative framework with the objective 
of reducing the compliance burden and strengthening regulatory governance, thereby 
promoting ease of doing business. 
• The language of the Code has been simplified by eliminating obsolete concepts, removing 
duplication of provisions and incorporating consistent regulatory procedures for standard 
processes, thereby establishing a uniform and streamlined framework for securities laws. 
• The maximum strength of the SEBI Board has been increased from the existing nine 
Members under the SEBI Act to fifteen Members. (Clause 4). 
• A new ground has been introduced for the removal of a Member where such Member has 
acquired any financial or other interest likely to prejudice the discharge of his functions. 
(Clause 6). 
• Every Member is required to disclose any direct or indirect interest, including the interests 
of family members, in matters placed before the Board and to abstain from participating in 
deliberations where such interests exist. (Clause 8). 
• SEBI has been entrusted with additional statutory responsibilities, including periodic 
review of its own performance and functioning, assessment of the proportionality and 
effectiveness of its regulations, capacity -building of its employees, and promotion of 
research and periodic studies. It is also required to lay down guiding principles for the 
implementation of the Code. (Clause 11). 
• The Code institutionalises a transparent and consultative regulation-making framework by 
mandating public consultation before the framing of regulations and bye-laws by SEBI and 
Market Infrastructure Institutions. It also requires prior publication of draf t rules by the 
Central Government, except in cases where such publication is dispensed with in 
accordance with the provisions of the Code. (Clauses 39(4), 145, 146 and 147). 
• The Code authorises the issuance of subsidiary instructions, including circulars, for 
clarifying regulations or prescribing procedural requirements ancillary thereto, after 
consultation with the appropriate Advisory Committee wherever necessary. (Clause 149). 
• A statutory time limit of one hundred and eighty days has been prescribed for completion 
of investigations, subject to extension on a case-to-case basis. (Clause 13(2).) 

4 
 
• The proposed Code bars the initiation of inspection or investigation after the expiry of eight 
years from the date of the alleged contravention , except under certain circumstances  
(Clause 16). 
• The Code codifies the principle of maintaining an arm’s -length separation between fact-
finding functions, such as inspection and investigation, and enforcement functions, 
including issuance of show -cause notices and adjudication. Accordingly, an official w ho 
has conducted an inspection or investigation, dealt with a settlement application, or issued 
an interim order in a matter shall not be designated as the adjudicating officer in the same 
matter. (Clause 17(2).) 
• The Board is empowered to issue interim orders during investigation or adjudication after 
providing the affected person an opportunity of being heard. However, where the purpose 
of the interim order is likely to be defeated by delay, such opportunity may b e afforded 
after the order is issued. Interim orders shall ordinarily remain in force for one hundred 
and eighty days and may be extended by a Bench comprising up to three Members of the 
Board, including the Chairperson or Whole-time Members, for a period not exceeding two 
years. (Clause 27). 
• Under the existing securities laws, contravention of any provision of the principal Acts may 
attract imprisonment for a term extending up to ten years. The proposed Code represents 
a significant shift in legislative policy by substantially decriminalising such contraventions 
in order to promote ease of doing business and ease of compliance. Criminal liability has 
been confined to the following categories: 
• non-compliance with adjudication orders; (Clause 94); 
• failure to cooperate with or comply with the directions of the Investigating Officer; (Clause 
95); and 
• serious offences involving market abuse, which may attract imprisonment, fine, or both, 
having regard to the adverse impact of such conduct on market integrity. (Clause 96). 
• Fraudulent and unfair trade practices have been decriminalised and made subject to a 
civil penalty regime. The penalty provisions are linked to unlawful gains or losses, 
wherever quantifiable, and prescribe corresponding ranges within which penalties may be 
imposed. (Clauses 92 and 97–109). 
• The Code codifies and standardises the procedure governing settlement of administrative 
and civil proceedings before the Board and compounding of offences by the Securities 
Appellate Tribunal or the competent Court. (Clauses 26 and 117). 
• The Code introduces a statutory definition of “Market Infrastructure Institution” comprising 
stock exchanges, clearing corporations and depositories. It also enables the Central 
Government to notify additional categories of Market Infrastructure Institutio ns. (Clauses 
2(v) and 37). 
• Market Infrastructure Institutions are empowered to frame bye -laws consistent with the 
principles contained in the Code to ensure non -discriminatory access, interoperability, 
transparency and prevention of market abuse. (Clause 39). 
• To strengthen the regulatory framework, SEBI is empowered to delegate specified 
registration functions to Market Infrastructure Institutions and Self -Regulatory 
Organisations. Self -Regulatory Organisations are required to obtain registration from 
SEBI and operate in accordance with norms approved by the Board. (Clauses 28, 31 and 
36). 

5 
 
• SEBI is required to specify an Investor Charter for protecting investors and facilitating their 
participation in the securities market. (Clause 71). 
• The Code requires SEBI to establish an Investor Grievance Redressal Mechanism and 
empowers it to direct Securities Market Service Providers and issuers to establish similar 
mechanisms. (Clause 72). 
• SEBI may designate one or more officers as Ombudspersons for receiving and redressing 
investor grievances. Orders of the Ombudsperson shall be appealable before the 
Securities Appellate Tribunal. (Clauses 73 and 85). 
• The Code provides an enabling framework for inter -regulatory coordination by 
empowering SEBI, in consultation with the concerned regulatory authority, to frame 
regulations facilitating the listing of other regulated instruments, their holding through 
depositories, and interoperability among Market Infrastructure Institutions. (Clause 118  
and clause 119). 
• The Board is empowered to establish a Regulatory Sandbox to promote innovation in 
financial products, contracts and services. (Clause 128). 
 
 
  

6 
 
CHAPTER II 
 
II. AMALGAMATION 
 
2.1 As noted earlier, the Securities Contracts (Regulation) Act, 1956, the Securities and Exchange 
Board of India Act, 1992 and the Depositories Act, 1996 were enacted at different points in time to 
address distinct aspects of the securities market. Over the years, however, the three enactments 
have developed overlapping provisions and contain certain provisions that have become outdated in 
view of evolving market practices, technological developments and changes in the regulatory 
landscape. 
 
Recognising the need for a modern, coherent and integrated legislative framework, the Union Budget 
for 2021–22 announced the proposal to consolidate the provisions of the aforesaid enactments, 
together with the Government Securities Act, 2006, into a single, rationalised Securities Markets 
Code. It was recognised to keep the Government Securities Act, 2006 outside the ambit of the 
proposed Securities Market Code. 
 
The proposed Securities Markets Code is intended to provide a comprehensive legislative framework 
governing the securities market by harmonising the provisions presently contained in the three 
principal enactments, reducing duplication, improving regulator y clarity and establishing a more 
coherent legal architecture for the regulation and development of the securities market. 
  

7 
 
CHAPTER III 
 
III. PREAMBLE AND APPLICATION 
 
3.1 The Preamble to the proposed Securities Markets Code provides that the Code seeks “to 
consolidate and amend the laws relating to the securities markets and for matters connected 
therewith or incidental thereto.” 
 
3.2 During the course of deliberations, several stakeholders suggested that the Preambles of the 
three existing enactments —the Securities and Exchange Board of India Act, 1992, the Securities 
Contracts (Regulation) Act, 1956 and the Depositories Act, 1996 —should be harmonised and 
consolidated to formulate a comprehensive Preamble for the proposed Code. 
 
The stakeholders submitted that the Preamble constitutes an important aid to statutory interpretation 
and assists the Courts in ascertaining the legislative intent where the meaning of a statutory provision 
is ambiguous or susceptible to more than one inte rpretation. In this regard, reference was made to 
the judgment of the Supreme Court in Securities and Exchange Board of India v. Rakhi Trading 
Private Limited (2018 INSC 119), as well as to the Insolvency and Bankruptcy Code, 2016, another 
codifying legislation whose Preamble clearly sets out the objectives underlying its enactment.  
 
Certain stakeholders further suggested that the Preamble should expressly state that the proposed 
legislation seeks to consolidate and amend the laws relating to the regulation and development of the 
securities market in India, prevent undesirable transact ions in securities, protect the interests of 
investors, and promote the orderly development and regulation of the securities market. 
 
It was further submitted that, in the Re Berubari Union case (1960), the Supreme Court observed that 
the Preamble is the key to the minds of those who enact the legislation. According to the stakeholders, 
the existing draft Preamble merely records the cons olidation of the three enactments, which 
represents the legislative mechanism rather than the underlying legislative objective. They, therefore, 
suggested that the Preamble should instead emphasise the larger objectives sought to be achieved 
through the proposed Code.  
 
Some stakeholders also recommended that the Preamble should explicitly provide that the proposed 
legislation seeks to protect the interests of investors and promote the development of the securities 
market so as to effectively serve the financing needs of the economy through efficient and effective 
regulation of the securities market, together with matters connected therewith or incidental thereto.  
 

8 
 
3.3 When queried regarding the rationale underlying the proposed Preamble and the concerns 
expressed by stakeholders about its limited scope, the Ministry acknowledged the merit of the 
suggestions received. The Ministry informed the Committee that the Preambl e would be suitably 
revised so as to more adequately reflect the objectives and legislative intent of the proposed 
Securities Markets Code, including investor protection and the development and regulation of the 
securities market. 
 
3.4 The Committee note that the proposed Securities Markets Code seeks to consolidate 
and amend the provisions of the Securities and Exchange Board of India Act, 1992, the 
Securities Contracts (Regulation) Act, 1956 and the Depositories Act, 1996 into a 
comprehensive legislative framework governing the securities market. 
 
The Committee are of the view that, in a codifying legislation of this nature, the Preamble 
assumes considerable significance, as it serves both as an aid to statutory interpretation and 
as an authoritative statement of the legislative intent underlying the enactment. 
 
The Committee further observe that the existing draft Preamble principally emphasises the 
consolidation and amendment of the three enactments. While such consolidation constitutes 
an important feature of the proposed legislation, it is essentially the legislative mechanism 
adopted to achieve the broader policy objectives of the Code rather than an objective in itself. 
The Committee, therefore, consider that the Preamble should extend beyond merely recording 
the consolidation of the existing enactments and should clearly articulate the substa ntive 
objectives sought to be achieved through the proposed legislative framework. 
 
Taking note of the observations of the Supreme Court in the Re Berubari Union case that the 
Preamble serves as the key to the minds of the legislature and constitutes an important aid to 
statutory interpretation in cases of ambiguity, the Committee are of the considered view that 
the Preamble to the proposed Code should more comprehensively reflect the legislative 
objectives sought to be achieved by the proposed enactment. 
 
3.5 The Committee, therefore, recommend that the Ministry undertake a comprehensive 
exercise to appropriately harmonise and integrate the objectives contained in the Preambles 
of the Securities and Exchange Board of India Act, 1992, the Securities Contracts (Regulation) 
Act, 1956 and the Depositories Act, 1996 while finalising the Preamble to the proposed 
Securities Markets Code. 
 
The Committee further recommend that the revised Preamble expressly incorporate the 
objectives of protecting the interests of investors, promoting the development of the 
securities market, ensuring effective regulation of market activities, maintaining fai r, 
transparent and efficient markets, and preventing undesirable transactions in securities. 

9 
 
 
The Committee also desire that the revised Preamble appropriately recognise the role of the 
securities market in serving the financing needs of the economy, facilitating capital formation 
and fostering confidence in the financial system. 
 
 
3.6 Clause 1(1) of the proposed Code provides that the legislation shall be called the “Securities 
Markets Code, 2025.” 
 
3.7 Clause 1(2) provides that the Code shall come into force on such date as the Central 
Government may, by notification published in the Official Gazette, appoint. It further provides that 
different dates may be appointed for bringing different provisions of  the Code into force, and any 
reference to the commencement of the Code in any such provision shall be construed as a reference 
to the commencement of that particular provision. 
 
3.8 Certain stakeholders suggested that Clause 1 should expressly provide that the proposed 
Code extends to the whole of India. 
 
In response, the Ministry clarified that, following the Constitution (Application to Jammu and Kashmir) 
Order, 2019 and the subsequent Presidential Order issued under Article 370(3) of the Constitution, 
the Constitution of India applies in its entirety to the Union Territories of Jammu and Kashmir and 
Ladakh. Consequently, Articles 1 and 245 of the Constitution empower Parliament to legislate for the 
whole of India without the necessity of incorporating an express provision regarding territorial extent 
in individual enactments. 
 
The Ministry, therefore, submitted that such a provision is no longer required, as the territorial 
application of Parliamentary legislation follows directly from the Constitution. 
 
3.9 The Committee have carefully examined the concern regarding the absence of an 
express provision stating that the proposed Securities Markets Code extends to the whole of 
India. 
 
The Committee further note the clarification furnished by the Ministry that, following the 
constitutional changes relating to the erstwhile State of Jammu and Kashmir, the Constitution 
of India applies in its entirety throughout the territory of India and that Parliament possesses 
the legislative competence under Articles 1 and 245 of the Constitution to enact laws 
applicable to the whole of India. 

10 
 
 
The Committee find merit in the clarification furnished by the Ministry and agree that, in view 
of the prevailing constitutional position, the inclusion of a separate provision regarding 
territorial extent is unnecessary. 
  

11 
 
 
CHAPTER IV 
 
DEFINITIONS (CLAUSE 2) 
 
(i) RBI-related Exclusions in Definitions 
 
4.1 During the course of deliberations, several stakeholders raised concerns regarding the 
manner in which the exclusions relating to activities regulated by the Reserve Bank of India (RBI) 
have been incorporated in the definitions contained in Clauses 2(1)(g), 2(1)(k), 2(1)(q), 2(1)(v) and 
2(1)(zn). Th e stakeholders submitted that the exclusions should appropriately relate to activities 
regulated by the RBI, rather than to the persons or entities undertaking such activities.  
It was further submitted that several entities operating in the financial sector perform activities falling 
within the regulatory jurisdiction of more than one regulator. For instance, credit rating agencies 
undertake ratings relating both to securities ma rkets regulated by SEBI and to banking products 
regulated by the RBI. Consequently, regulatory jurisdiction is generally determined with reference to 
the nature of the activity performed rather than the identity of the entity itself.  
The stakeholders also pointed out that entities such as the Clearing Corporation of India Limited are 
authorised by the RBI to operate as payment system providers under the Payment and Settlement 
Systems Act, 2007. They submitted that a literal reading of the proposed definitions may exclude 
entire entities —such as stock exchanges, clearing corporations, depositories and certain 
intermediaries—from the ambit of the Code merely because they undertake specified RBI-regulated 
activities. According to the stakeholders, such an interpretation could inadvertently create regulatory 
gaps and blur the intended demarcation between the respective jurisdictions of SEBI and the RBI.  
4.2 When the matter was taken up with the Ministry, the Ministry acknowledged the merit of the 
concerns raised by the stakeholders and informed the Committee that appropriate drafting changes 
would be made to the relevant provisions.  
 
Recommendation 
 
4.3 The Committee note that Clauses 2(1)(g), 2(1)(k), 2(1)(q), 2(1)(v) and 2(1)(zn) define key 
market participants and institutions under the proposed Securities Markets Code. These 
provisions also prescribe exclusions in respect of certain activities regulate d under the 
Reserve Bank of India Act, 1934, the Government Securities Act, 2006 and the Payment and 
Settlement Systems Act, 2007.  
 
 

12 
 
 
4.4 The Committee further observe that the Indian financial system comprises several 
entities that perform functions falling within the regulatory jurisdiction of more than one 
financial sector regulator. The Committee are of the view that regulatory jurisdict ion should 
ordinarily be determined with reference to the nature of the activity being undertaken rather 
than the identity of the entity performing such activity. Accordingly, an entity may legitimately 
remain subject to the jurisdiction of SEBI in res pect of securities market activities while 
simultaneously being regulated by the RBI in relation to other activities falling within its 
statutory mandate.  
 
4.5 The Committee are, therefore, of the considered view that an activity -based approach 
would provide greater legislative clarity, minimise jurisdictional uncertainty and ensure that 
securities market activities continue to remain within the regulatory framew ork of the Code 
even where such activities are undertaken by entities that are also regulated by the RBI for 
other purposes. 
 
The Committee, therefore, recommend that the relevant definitions contained in Clauses 
2(1)(g), 2(1)(k), 2(1)(q), 2(1)(v) and 2(1)(zn), together with any allied provisions, be suitably 
amended to clarify that the proposed exclusions apply only to activities regulated by the RBI 
and not to persons or entities merely because they are subject to regulation by the RBI. 
 
The Committee further recommend that the revised provisions clearly delineate the respective 
regulatory jurisdictions of SEBI and the RBI so as to avoid overlap, eliminate potential 
regulatory gaps and provide greater certainty to market participants.  
 
IV. DEFINITIONS (CLAUSE 2) 
 
(ii) Depository Services – Clause 2(1)(l) 
 
4.6 Clause 2(1)(l) defines the expression “depository services” as follows:— 
 
(The statutory extract should remain exactly as reproduced in the Bill and should not be edited, except 
for formatting, punctuation and typographical corrections.)  
 
4.7 During the course of deliberations, certain stakeholders suggested that the expression 
“securities-based benefits associated with any securities or other regulated instruments” occurring in 
Clause 2(1)(l)(ii) be replaced with the broader expression “other benefits”. 

13 
 
 
The stakeholders submitted that the existing expression may give rise to ambiguity. While the 
provision seeks to cover benefits associated with both securities and other regulated instruments, the 
phrase “securities -based benefits” could be interpreted as restricting its application primarily to 
securities, thereby creating uncertainty regarding benefits arising from regulated instruments that are 
not themselves securities. The stakeholders, therefore, recommended the adoption of a broader and 
technology-neutral expression.  
 
4.8 When the matter was taken up with the Ministry, the Ministry acknowledged the merit of the 
concern raised by the stakeholders and informed the Committee that appropriate drafting changes 
would be carried out in the relevant provision.  
 
Recommendation 
 
4.9 The Committee have examined Clause 2(1)(l), which defines the expression 
“depository services” and, inter alia, includes within its ambit the distribution of monetary or 
securities-based benefits associated with securities or other regulated instruments. 
 
The Committee observe that the expression “securities-based benefits”, as presently drafted, 
may not adequately convey the intended scope of the provision. Although the clause seeks 
to encompass benefits associated with both securities and other regulated instruments, the 
existing terminology may lend itself to an unintended restrictive interpretation, particularly in 
relation to benefits arising from regulated instruments that do not fall within the definition of 
securities.  
 
4.10 The Committee are of the considered view that statutory definitions should be drafted 
with precision and clarity so as to minimise interpretational uncertainty. In the present case, 
a broader and more neutral expression would more accurately reflect the legislative intent and 
ensure that the provision extends uniformly to benefits associated with both securities and 
other regulated instruments covered by the Code. 
 
The Committee further observe that the adoption of such terminology would enhance 
consistency across the Code and reduce the possibility of avoidable interpretational disputes.  
 
4.11 The Committee, therefore, recommend that Clause 2(1)(l)(ii) be suitably amended by 
replacing the expression “securities -based benefits associated with any securities or other 
regulated instruments” with a broader and more inclusive formulation, such as “o ther 

14 
 
benefits”, or with any other expression that clearly encompasses benefits arising from both 
securities and other regulated instruments. 
 
The Committee believe that such a formulation would better reflect the intended legislative 
scope of the provision, eliminate potential ambiguity and promote consistency with the overall 
framework and objectives of the proposed Securities Markets Code. 
(iii) Intermediary – Clause 2(1)(q) 
 
4.12 Certain stakeholders suggested that intermediaries operating in the International Financial 
Services Centre (IFSC), such as ESG Rating and Data Products Providers, Distributors and Clearing 
Members, be expressly included within the definition of “intermediary”. It was submitted that these 
are recognised categories of securities market intermediaries under the existing regulatory framework 
but do not find mention in the proposed Securities Markets Code. The stakeholders further pointe d 
out that SEBI already recognises additional categories of intermediaries, including ESG Rating 
Agencies, under its regulatory framework and, therefore, such entities may appropriately be included 
within the definition. 
 
The stakeholders further suggested that Self -certified Syndicate Banks (SCSBs), which perform 
important functions in ASBA, UPI and debt and equity issuance processes, should also be expressly 
included within the definition. It was submitted that these are recognised categories of intermediaries 
required to obtain registration with SEBI, either directly or through Market Infrastructure Institutions. 
Their explicit recognition would also facilitate the formation of distinct industry associations or Self -
Regulatory Organisations. 
 
4.13 On the issue of including certain intermediaries operating in the IFSC, such as ESG Rating 
and Data Products Providers, Distributors and Clearing Members, within the definition of 
“intermediary”, the Ministry informed the Committee that SEBI already recog nises certain additional 
categories of intermediaries, including ESG Rating Agencies, under the existing regulatory framework 
and that such entities may appropriately be included within the definition. 
 
However, the Ministry did not agree with the suggestion to specifically include PARRVA, clarifying 
that PARRVA is a risk assessment tool and not an intermediary and, therefore, does not warrant 
separate inclusion in the definition. 
 
4.14 Certain stakeholders also suggested that the words “in relation to indices traded in the 
securities markets” occurring in Clause 2(1)(q)(vii) be deleted. 
 

15 
 
It was submitted that indices are not themselves traded in the securities markets. While derivative 
products are available on a limited number of benchmark indices, such as the BSE Sensex, NIFTY 
50, NIFTY Bank and NIFTY Financial Services, the majority of indices are used by Mutual Funds and 
Exchange Traded Funds as benchmarks for measuring or tracking the performance of their assets 
under management. The stakeholders, therefore, submitted that the existing expression may not 
accurately reflect the role of indices within the securities market framework. 
 
The Ministry, while responding to the suggestion, acknowledged that the concern had merit and 
informed the Committee that appropriate drafting changes would be made. 
 
4.15 The stakeholders further proposed that Foreign Portfolio Investors (FPIs) be expressly 
included within the definition of “intermediary” in order to align the proposed Code with the existing 
SEBI regulatory framework and to avoid potential regulatory gaps.  It was submitted that FPIs are 
registered with and regulated by SEBI and that their exclusion from the statutory definition may give 
rise to avoidable interpretational issues. 
 
The stakeholders also suggested that the words “or securities” be inserted after the word 
“instruments” in the proviso to Clause 2(1)(q), so that the exclusion appropriately extends to both 
instruments and securities referred to in Chapter IIID of the Reserve Bank of India Act, 1934. 
 
4.16 In response, the Ministry informed the Committee that FPIs are recognised as intermediaries 
under the existing regulatory framework and that, in continuation of the existing position, they may 
appropriately be included within the definition of “intermediary”. 
 
The Ministry further clarified that Clause 2(1)(q), read with Clause 34, enables the Board to notify 
additional classes of intermediaries as the securities market evolves. It also pointed out that Clause 
152 preserves the continuity of the existing regulat ory framework by continuing the existing 
regulations to the extent that they are not inconsistent with the provisions of the proposed Code. 
 
4.17 The Committee have carefully examined Clause 2(1)(q) and observe that the definition 
of “intermediary” is a foundational provision of the proposed Code, as it determines the 
categories of persons and entities that fall within the regulatory, supervisory and enforcement 
jurisdiction of the Board. 
 
4.18 The Committee further note that, while the proposed definition substantially reflects 
the existing regulatory framework, certain categories of market participants presently 
recognised within the securities market ecosystem have either not been expressly in cluded 
or require greater legislative clarity. The Committee are of the considered view that the 
definition should comprehensively capture all established categories of intermediaries while 

16 
 
retaining sufficient flexibility to accommodate emerging categories through the notification 
mechanism provided under Clause 34. 
 
4.19 Accordingly, the Committee recommend the following: 
(i) Inclusion of Clearing Members: Clearing Members perform an integral role in the securities 
market and are recognised under the existing regulatory framework. They should, therefore, 
be expressly included within the definition of “intermediary”. 
 
(ii) Inclusion of Foreign Portfolio Investors (FPIs): FPIs are registered and regulated under the 
existing securities market framework. Their omission from the statutory definition may give 
rise to avoidable interpretational uncertainty. The Committee, therefore, recommend that FPIs 
be specifically included within the definition of “intermediary”. 
 
(iii) Index Providers: The expression “in relation to indices traded in the securities markets” 
in Clause 2(1)(q)(vii) does not accurately reflect the role of indices within the securities market. 
The Committee recommend that the provision be suitably redr afted to provide greater 
precision and legislative clarity. 
 
(iv) Emerging Categories of Intermediaries: The Committee note that Clause 34 empowers the 
Board to notify additional classes of intermediaries from time to time. While established 
categories of intermediaries should be expressly recognised in the Code, future categories 
may continue  to be brought within the regulatory framework through the notification 
mechanism provided under Clause 34. 
 
4.20 The Committee, therefore, recommend that Clause 2(1)(q) be suitably redrafted in the 
light of the above observations so as to ensure that the definition of “intermediary” is 
comprehensive, clear and capable of accommodating future developments in the secur ities 
market. 
 
(iv) Trading Member – Clause 2(zp) 
 
4.21 Clause 2(zp) defines “trading member” as under: 
“trading member” means a person having trading rights in any stock exchange and includes 
a stock broker; 
 

17 
 
4.22 Certain stakeholders suggested that, instead of defining “trading member” under Clause 2(zp), 
the Code should define the expression “stock broker” as follows: 
“stock broker” means a person having trading rights in any stock exchange and includes a 
trading member. 
The stakeholders submitted that Clause 2(q), read with clauses (xvii) and (xviii), refers separately to 
“stock broker” and “trading member”. It was further pointed out that the penalty provision under Clause 
99 refers only to a “stock broker”. According to the stakeholders, defining “stock broker” in the above 
manner would ensure that the penal provisions apply uniformly to both stock brokers and trading 
members. 
 
When the matter was taken up with the Ministry, the Ministry, in its written reply, stated as under: 
 
“SMC defines the broader expression ‘trading member’, which includes the concept of a stock 
broker. The penalty under Clause 99 is based on Section 15F of the SEBI Act and is 
specifically in the context of activities performed by a stock broker, since cert ain positive 
obligations have been imposed upon them under Clause 34(2). However, the suggestion may 
be considered so as to make the penalty provisions applicable to all trading members, the 
definition of which includes stock brokers.” 
 
4.23  The Committee have examined the definition of “trading member” under Clause 2(zp). 
The Committee note that, while the term has been defined to mean a person having trading 
rights in a stock exchange and includes a stock broker, various provisions of the C ode, 
including Clause 2(q) and clauses (xvii) and (xviii), continue to refer separately to “stock 
broker” and “trading member”. 
 
The Committee further note that the penalty provision contained in Clause 99 specifically 
refers only to a “stock broker”. In such a situation, ambiguity may arise regarding the 
applicability of the penal provisions to all categories of trading members possessing trading 
rights in a stock exchange. 
 
4.24  The Committee also take note of the Ministry’s submission that “trading member” is 
the broader expression and includes the concept of a stock broker. The Committee are, 
however, of the considered view that the legislative intent should be reflected with g reater 
clarity and consistency throughout the Code. 
 
The Committee, therefore, recommend that the Ministry suitably examine the relevant 
definitions and consequential provisions, including Clause 99, so as to ensure that the rights, 
obligations and penalties intended to apply to trading members extend clearl y and 
unambiguously to every entity falling within the scope of that definition. 

18 
 
 
4.25  The Committee further recommend that the Ministry undertake a comprehensive review 
of the usage of the expressions “stock broker” and “trading member” throughout the Code to 
ensure consistency in terminology and to avoid any ambiguity in interpretation, 
implementation or enforcement. 
 
(v) Investment Scheme – Clause 2(1)(r) read with Clause 32 
 
4.26 Clause 2(1)(r) of the Code defines “investment scheme” as follows: 
 
“investment scheme” means the scheme referred to in section 32; 
 
 
4.27 Certain stakeholders submitted that contributions towards mandatory “skin in the game” or 
employee contributions, as specified by the regulator, should be excluded from the ambit of the 
definition. They further suggested that appropriate carve -outs may be  provided for investment 
schemes operating in the International Financial Services Centre (IFSC) that do not have an 
independent fund manager and are managed directly or indirectly by the investor. 
 
The stakeholders pointed out that the proposed definition characterises an “investment scheme” as 
one in which the persons participating in the scheme or arrangement do not exercise day -to-day 
control over the management of the assets. However, in line with global practices, several investment 
structures are established for a single investor or a defined investor group, such as Sovereign Wealth 
Funds, family offices and large corporate investment funds. In such structures, there may not be an 
independent fu nd manager, and the fund may instead be managed directly or indirectly by the 
investor. The stakeholders, therefore, submitted that such schemes may require appropriate 
regulatory carve-outs. 
 
4.28 The Committee sought the views of the Ministry on the suggestion to provide appropriate 
carve-outs for specified investment schemes, including employee contribution schemes and certain 
IFSC-based schemes without an independent fund manager, while aligning the proposed framework 
with international best practices. 
 
The Ministry acknowledged the concern and informed the Committee that appropriate drafting 
changes would be made. 
 

19 
 
4.29 The Committee have examined the scope and implications of the definition of 
“investment scheme” under Clause 2(1)(r), read with Clause 32. The Committee note that the 
provision seeks to identify schemes and arrangements under which investors pool their funds 
for investment purposes without exercising day -to-day control over the management of the 
underlying assets. 
 
The Committee further note the concerns expressed by stakeholders that the proposed 
framework may not adequately accommodate certain investment structures operating in the 
IFSC, particularly those established for a single investor or a defined investor group, such as 
Sovereign Wealth Funds, family offices and corporate-sponsored investment funds. 
 
4.30 The Committee observe that such investment structures are frequently managed 
directly or indirectly by the investors themselves and may not involve an independent fund 
manager. The Committee are of the view that applying to such structures the same regulatory 
requirements as are applicable to widely pooled investment vehicles may not adequately 
reflect their distinct ownership, governance and operational characteristics. 
 
The Committee also note the concerns regarding the applicability of regulatory requirements 
relating to mandatory “skin in the game” or employee contributions in circumstances where 
the interests of the investors and those responsible for managing the fund  are substantially 
aligned. 
 
4.31 The Committee, therefore, recommend that the Ministry suitably review Clause 32 and 
the allied provisions governing investment schemes so as to provide appropriate carve-outs 
or clarifications for eligible IFSC-based investment structures, particularly those established 
for a single investor or a defined investor group and managed directly or indirectly by such 
investors. 
 
The Committee further recommend that, while finalising the provision, the Ministry ensure that 
the regulatory framework remains proportionate to the nature, ownership structure and risk 
profile of the investment scheme, while remaining aligned with evolvin g international best 
practices and facilitating the continued growth and competitiveness of the IFSC ecosystem. 
 
(vi) Market Infrastructure Institution – Clause 2(1)(v) 
 
4.32 Clause 2(1)(v) of the Code defines “market infrastructure institution” as under:— 
 

20 
 
“market infrastructure institution” means a stock exchange, a clearing corporation or a depository and 
includes such other class or classes of market infrastructure institutions, as may be notified by the 
Central Government, except those authorised or requ ired to be authorised by other regulatory 
authorities; 
 
4.33 Certain stakeholders suggested that the expression “other regulatory authorities” be used in 
place of “other regulatory bodies”. 
 
It was submitted that the expression “other regulatory authority” has been specifically defined under 
the Code and is used consistently throughout the Code to refer to financial sector regulators other 
than SEBI, as defined in Clause 2(za). The stakeholder s, therefore, suggested that the same 
expression be adopted in Clause 2(1)(v) to maintain consistency in terminology. 
 
4.34 When the Committee sought the views of the Ministry on the suggestion to replace the 
expression “other regulatory bodies” with “other regulatory authorities”, so as to ensure consistency 
with the terminology used elsewhere in the Code, the Ministry acknow ledged the merit of the 
suggestion and informed the Committee that appropriate drafting changes would be carried out. 
 
The Committee have examined Clause 2(1)(v), which defines the expression “market 
infrastructure institution”. The Committee note that Market Infrastructure Institutions, 
comprising stock exchanges, clearing corporations and depositories, constitute the cor e 
institutional infrastructure of the securities market and are subject to a distinct regulatory 
framework under the proposed Code. 
 
The Committee further note the concern that, while the Code employs the defined expression 
“other regulatory authorities” in various provisions to refer to financial sector regulators other 
than SEBI, the definition of “market infrastructure institution” u ses the expression “other 
regulatory bodies”, which has not been defined under the Code. 
 
The Committee are of the view that consistency in legislative terminology is essential to avoid 
ambiguity and promote certainty in interpretation. Since the expression “other regulatory 
authority” is already a defined term under the Code and is consistentl y used elsewhere, the 
Committee recommend that the expression “other regulatory bodies” appearing in Clause 
2(1)(v) be substituted with “other regulatory authorities” to ensure uniformity in drafting and 
consistency across the Code. 
 
(vii) Securities – Clause 2(1)(zi)(xi) 
 

21 
 
4.35  Clause 2(1)(zi) defines “securities” as under:— 
 
“securities” include- 
 
(i) shares, scrips, stocks, bonds, secured or unsecured debentures, warrants, or debenture 
stock or other marketable securities of a like nature in or of any incorporated company or a 
pooled investment vehicle or other body corporate; 
 
(ii) hybrid instruments; 
 
(iii) convertible instruments; 
 
(iv) derivatives; 
 
(v) units or any other instruments issued in respect of any investment scheme by a pooled 
investment vehicle to the investors in such schemes; 
 
(vi) security receipts managed and regulated in accordance with the provisions of the 
Securitisation and Reconstruction of Financial Assets and Enforcement of Security Interest 
Act, 2002; 
 
(vii) a certificate or an instrument, by whatever name called, issued to an investor by any 
issuer as a special purpose distinct entity which possesses any debt or receivable, including 
mortgage debt, assigned to such entity, and acknowledging the benefici al interest of such 
investor in such debt or receivable, including mortgage debt, as the case may be; 
 
(viii) global depository receipts and Indian depository receipts; 
 
(ix) notes or papers issued for the purpose of raising capital, which are listed or proposed to 
be listed; 
 
(x) Electronic Gold Receipts; 
 

22 
 
(xi) Government securities managed and regulated under the provisions of the Reserve Bank 
of India Act, 1934 and the Government Securities Act, 2006; 
 
(xii) zero coupon zero principal instruments; 
 
(xiii) bullion spot delivery contracts and bullion depository receipts with underlying bullion in 
respect of issuance and trading in an International Financial Services Centre under the 
provisions of the International Financial Services Centres Authority Act, 2019; 
 
(xiv) onshore rupee bonds issued by multilateral institutions such as the Asian Development 
Bank and the International Finance Corporation; 
 
(xv) other regulated instruments, to the extent permitted for listing under section 119; 
 
(xvi) such other instruments as may be notified by the Central Government; and 
 
(xvii) rights or interests in securities, but shall not include any unit -linked insurance policy or 
scrips or any such instrument or unit, by whatever name called, which provides a combined 
benefit of risk on the life of a person and investment by such per son and is issued by an 
insurer as defined in clause (9) of section 2 of the Insurance Act, 1938. 
 
4.36  Certain stakeholders suggested that the following proviso be inserted in the definition of 
“securities”: 
 
“Notwithstanding anything contained in this Code, the management of Government securities and the 
regulation of transactions in such securities shall be undertaken by the Central Government or a State 
Government, and the Reserve Bank of India, as applicable, in accordance with the provisions of the 
Government Securities Act, 2006 and Chapter IIID of the Reserve Bank of India Act, 1934.” 
 
Elaborating on the rationale for the suggestion, the stakeholders submitted that Government 
securities are listed and traded on recognised stock exchanges. In recognition of this position, the 
earlier (2024) version of the Code contained an explanation und er the corresponding provision 
clarifying that, notwithstanding anything contained in the Code, the management and regulation of 
Government securities would continue to vest in the Central Government or the State Governments 
and the Reserve Bank of India, as applicable, under the Government Securities Act, 2006. 
 

23 
 
The stakeholders further submitted that the Reserve Bank of India had suggested that the explanation 
should instead be incorporated as a substantive proviso so as to place the existing legal position 
beyond doubt. Although the 2025 draft of the Code includ es Government securities within the 
definition of “securities”, they contended that such inclusion by itself may not adequately preserve the 
existing regulatory framework. In the absence of an express statutory provision, there could be scope 
for interpreting the Code as conferring concurrent regulatory jurisdiction upon both the Securities and 
Exchange Board of India and the Reserve Bank of India. The stakeholders, therefore, requested that 
the proposed proviso be incorporated to provide greater legal clar ity and preserve the existing 
regulatory arrangement. 
 
4.37  When the Committee sought the views of the Ministry on the above suggestion, the Ministry 
acknowledged the merit of the concern and informed the Committee that appropriate drafting changes 
would be carried out. 
 
4.38  The Committee have examined Clause 2(1)(zi), particularly sub -clause (xi), which 
includes Government securities within the definition of “securities”. The Committee note that, 
while the inclusion of Government securities within the definition is appropriate, the provision 
does not expressly preserve the existing statutory framework governing their management 
and regulation. 
 
The Committee further note that Government securities, though listed and traded on 
recognised platforms, continue to be governed by a distinct statutory and regulatory 
framework under the Government Securities Act, 2006 and the relevant provisions of the 
Reserve Bank of India Act, 1934. The Committee are of the view that the inclusion of 
Government securities within the definition of “securities” should not be construed as altering 
or diluting the existing regulatory responsibilities of the Central Governme nt, the State 
Governments or the Reserve Bank of India. 
 
4.39  The Committee observe that the absence of an express statutory clarification may give 
rise to uncertainty regarding the respective jurisdiction of the Securities and Exchange Board 
of India and the Reserve Bank of India in relation to Government securitie s. The Committee 
are of the considered view that legislative clarity on this aspect would eliminate the possibility 
of jurisdictional overlap and ensure that the existing regulatory framework continues to 
operate without ambiguity. 
 
4.40  The Committee, therefore, recommend that a suitable proviso or other substantive 
provision be incorporated in the Code to expressly clarify that, notwithstanding anything 
contained therein, the management of Government securities and the regulation of 
transactions in such securities shall continue to be governed by the Government Securities 
Act, 2006 and the relevant provisions of the Reserve Bank of India Act, 1934, and shall remain 

24 
 
within the regulatory domain of the Central Government, the State Governments and the 
Reserve Bank of India, as applicable. 
 
(viii) Underlying – Clause 2(1)(zr) 
 
4.41 Clause 2(1)(zr) defines “underlying” as under:— 
 
“underlying”, in respect of a derivative, means a security or a commodity or an actionable 
claim or money or currency or indices on any instrument or prices or yields or volatility or 
liquidity or credit score or ratings or events or activity or progress o r a situation or financial 
measures or other instruments from which such derivative derives its value. 
 
4.42 Certain stakeholders suggested that the term “situation” be omitted from the definition on the 
ground that it is excessively broad and vague and is not capable of objective quantification for pricing 
purposes. They submitted that the expression lacks a se ttled legal or commercial meaning in the 
context of derivative contracts and may consequently give rise to uncertainty regarding the scope of 
permissible underlying references. 
 
4.43 When the Committee sought the views of the Ministry on the use of the expression “situation” 
in Clause 2(1)(zr), particularly in view of the concern that the term is excessively broad and incapable 
of objective valuation, the Ministry acknowledged the mer it of the concern and informed the 
Committee that appropriate drafting changes would be carried out. 
 
4.44 The Committee have examined Clause 2(1)(zr), which defines the expression 
“underlying”, constituting the asset, rate, index, event or other reference from which a 
derivative contract derives its value. The Committee note that the definition is of fundamental 
importance to the regulation and functioning of derivative markets. The Committee are, 
therefore, of the view that the provision should be drafted with a high degree of precision, 
certainty and legislative clarity. 
 
4.45 The Committee further observe that the inclusion of the expression “situation” renders 
the definition unnecessarily broad and susceptible to varying interpretations. The Committee 
note that the term does not possess a well -established meaning within the f ramework of 
securities markets and may create uncertainty regarding the categories of permissible 
underlying references. Such ambiguity may also give rise to practical difficulties in the 
objective determination, valuation and pricing of derivative contracts. 
 

25 
 
4.46 The Committee, therefore, recommend that the definition of “underlying” under Clause 
2(1)(zr) be suitably revised so as to remove ambiguity and enhance legal certainty. The 
Committee further recommend that the categories of permissible underlying referenc es be 
clearly delineated to facilitate consistent interpretation, minimise the scope for disputes, and 
promote the effective implementation of the provisions relating to derivative contracts under 
the Code. 
 
 
(ix) Unlawful Gain – Clause 2(1)(zt) 
 
4.47 Clause 2(1)(zt) defines “unlawful gain” as under:— 
 
“unlawful gain” means an unlawful gain including the profit made and loss averted by a person. 
 
4.48 Certain stakeholders suggested that the definition be redrafted for greater clarity. It was 
submitted that the existing definition is circular in nature and does not adequately explain the meaning 
of the expression “unlawful gain”. The stakeholders proposed that the definition may instead read: 
 
“Unlawful gain” shall include the wrongful gain made or loss averted. 
 
It was further submitted that, as presently drafted, the definition conflates the concepts of “profit made” 
and “loss averted”, although the latter is conceptually distinct from a gain. The stakeholders 
accordingly suggested that: 
 
(i) “unlawful gain” should be defined so as to capture only positive enrichment; 
 
(ii) “loss averted” should be defined separately; and 
 
(iii) the Code should specify the circumstances in which each expression is to be applied, 
such as for disgorgement or the determination of monetary penalties. 
 
According to the stakeholders, such an approach would reduce the possibility of double counting, 
improve computational certainty and promote greater consistency in enforcement outcomes. 
 

26 
 
4.49 When the Committee sought the views of the Ministry on the above suggestions, the Ministry 
acknowledged the validity of the concerns and informed the Committee that appropriate drafting 
changes would be carried out. 
 
4.50 The Committee have examined Clause 2(1)(zt), which defines the expressions 
“unlawful gain”, “wrongful gain” and “loss averted”. The Committee note that these 
expressions constitute important concepts for determining the consequences of 
contraventions under the Code, including the quantification of disgorgement and monetary 
penalties. The Committee are, therefore, of the view that these expressions should be defined 
with clarity and precision, as ambiguity in their scope may result in uncertainty during  
enforcement and adjudication. 
 
4.51 The Committee further observe that the existing definition of “unlawful gain” does not 
clearly distinguish between the concepts of “profit made” and “loss averted”. While profit 
made represents a positive economic benefit, loss averted is conceptually distinct and may 
warrant separate treatment for the purposes of computation and enforcement. The Committee 
are of the view that the present formulation may give rise to interpretational difficulties and 
could potentially result in inconsistencies in determining liabilities under the Code. 
 
4.52 The Committee, therefore, recommend that the definition of “unlawful gain” be suitably 
redrafted to provide greater clarity and legal certainty. The Committee further recommend that 
the concepts of “unlawful gain”, “wrongful gain” and “loss averted” be clearly distinguished 
and appropriately defined, and that the Code specify the circumstances in which each 
expression is to be applied. Such an approach would facilitate objective computation, 
minimise the possibility of double counting, promote consistency in enforcement actions and 
contribute to more predictable and rational regulatory outcomes. 

27 
 
CHAPTER V 
 
V Composition of Board 
 
Clause 4 
 
5.1 Clause 4 provides for the composition of the Board, as under:— 
 
(1) The Board shall consist of the following Members to be appointed by the Central 
Government, namely:— 
 
(a) Chairperson of the Board; 
 
(b) two Members from amongst the officers of the Ministry of the Central Government 
dealing with Finance and administration of the Companies Act, 2013, ex officio; 
 
(c) one Member from amongst the officers of the Reserve Bank, ex officio; and 
 
(d) eleven other Members, of whom at least five shall be whole-time Members. 
 
(2) Without prejudice to anything contained in section 6, on the date of commencement of this 
Code, the Chairman and Members appointed under section 4 of the Securities and Exchange 
Board of India Act, 1992, shall be deemed to be the Chairperson and Member s appointed 
under clauses (a) and (d) of sub-section (1) for the remainder period. 
 
(3) The Chairperson and Members referred to in clauses (a) and (d) of sub -section (1) shall 
be appointed by the Central Government in such manner as may be prescribed, and the 
Members referred to in clauses (b) and (c) of that sub-section shall be nominated by the 
Central Government and the Reserve Bank, respectively. 
 
(4) The Chairperson and Members referred to in clauses (a) and (d) of sub -section (1) shall 
be persons of ability, integrity and standing in dealing with problems relating to securities 
markets or have special knowledge or experience in law, finance, econo mics, technology, 

28 
 
commodity markets, accountancy, administration or in any other discipline which, in the 
opinion of the Central Government, is useful to the Board: 
 
Provided that, while appointing Members referred to in clause (d) of sub -section (1), the 
Central Government shall endeavour to appoint at least three persons with expertise in the 
securities markets.” 
 
(i) Search-cum-Selection Committee – Clause 4(3) 
 
5.2  Domain experts submitted that the Bill provides that the Chairperson and Members of the 
Board (other than ex officio Members) shall be appointed by the Central Government in such manner 
as may be prescribed. It specifies broad eligibility criteria, requiring appointees to be persons of ability 
and integrity, possessing special knowledge or experience in fields such as law, finance, economics, 
technology, commodity markets, accountancy or administration. Beyond this, the Bill does not 
prescribe the procedure or mechanism for the selection of Members of the Board. 
 
The stakeholders further submitted that the Securities and Exchange Board of India Act, 1992 
contains a similar provision regarding appointments. However, the relevant rules provide that the 
Chairperson and Whole -time Members are appointed by the Central G overnment on the 
recommendation of the Financial Sector Regulatory Appointments Search Committee (FSRASC). 
The Committee is chaired by the Cabinet Secretary and comprises the Additional Principal Secretary 
to the Prime Minister, the Secretary, Department o f Economic Affairs, the Chairperson of SEBI (for 
the selection of Whole -time Members), and three external experts nominated by the Central 
Government. 
 
The stakeholders further submitted that, in contrast, the statutes governing regulators such as the 
Central Electricity Regulatory Commission (CERC) and the Competition Commission of India (CCI) 
expressly provide for the constitution of a Selection Committ ee and specify its composition in the 
parent legislation itself. 
 
Referring to the recommendations of the Financial Sector Legislative Reforms Commission (FSLRC), 
the stakeholders submitted that the Commission had emphasised the need for a statutory framework 
to ensure a fair and transparent process for the appointment of Members of the Boards of regulators. 
The Commission had recommended that the Central Government should be assisted by a Search -
cum-Selection Committee comprising a Government representative as Chairperson, the Chairperson 
of the concerned regulator (or another Government representative where the appointment relates to 
the Chairperson of the regulator), and three experts drawn from a panel maintained by the 
Government. To enhance institutional independence, the Commission had further recommended that 
a majority of the members of such Committee should be persons unconnected with the Government. 

29 
 
It had also recommended disclosure of relevant documents, recording of reasons, and preparation of 
a report outlining the selection process and the criteria adopted. 
 
 
 
5.3  In response thereto, the Ministry stated that, under Clause 145(2)(a), the manner of 
appointment of the Chairperson and Members may be prescribed by the Central Government through 
rules. The Ministry was of the view that the concern need not be addressed in the Code itself and 
could, if considered necessary, be dealt with at the implementation stage through the rules. The 
Ministry further stated that such provisions are presently contained in the existing rules. 
 
5.4  When asked whether a Search -cum-Selection Committee has been incorporated in the Act 
and whether any regulatory body has such a Committee, the Secretary, Ministry of Finance, stated 
that no such provision exists in the Act itself. The Secretary further stated that the rules may provide 
for such a mechanism and that the existing rules already provide for a Search -cum-Selection 
Committee. In the case of a regulatory body, incorporation of such a provision in the parent legislation 
may not be necessary, as the mechanism is already embedded in the rules. 
 
Further, when asked how the Boards of other regulatory bodies are constituted, the Secretary, 
Ministry of Finance, informed the Committee that the requisite information would be furnished. The 
Ministry subsequently submitted the following written reply:— 
 
Current Position 
 
● As per the Securities and Exchange Board of India (Terms and Conditions of Service of Chairman 
and Members) Rules, 1992, the Chairman and Whole -time Members are appointed on the 
recommendation of the Financial Sector Regulatory Appointments Search Committee comprising the 
Cabinet Secretary (Chairman) and other members, including the Additional Principal Secretary to the 
Prime Minister, the Secretary, Department of Economic Affairs, the Chairman of SEBI (for selection 
of Whole -time Members), and three outs ide experts of repute to be nominated by the Central 
Government from a panel of experts in the fields of finance, economics, law, public administration, 
financial markets and related subjects maintained by it. 
 
● Prior to the Securities and Exchange Board of India (Terms and Conditions of Service of Chairman 
and Members) Amendment Rules, 2015, appointments were made on the recommendation of a 
Search-cum-Selection Committee comprising the Cabinet Secretary (Chairman) and other members, 
including the Secretary, Department of Economic Affairs, the Chairman of SEBI (for selection of 
Whole-time Members), two experts of eminence from relevant fields to be nominated by the Central 

30 
 
Government, and such other person, if any, not exceeding two, as the Minister of Finance might 
nominate. 
 
FSRASC is a search committee which recommends candidates for appointment; however, its 
recommendations are not binding on the Central Government. It was constituted with the objective of 
adopting a uniform selection process for all financial sector regulators. 
 
SMC Position 
 
● Under Clause 4(3), the Central Government shall appoint the Chairperson and Members in the 
manner prescribed in the rules. By virtue of Clause 152, the existing rules will continue under the 
SMC. 
 
● Accordingly, as per the current practice, the Chairman and Whole -time Members of SEBI would 
continue to be appointed by the Central Government on the recommendation of the Financial Sector 
Regulatory Appointments Search Committee and not a Search -cum-Selection Committee. Hence, 
reference to a Search-cum-Selection Committee may not be appropriate. 
 
Contemporary Laws 
 
(a) Pension Fund Regulatory and Development Authority (PFRDA) 
 
As per the Pension Fund Regulatory and Development Authority (Salary and Allowances Payable to, 
and Other Terms and Conditions of Service of, Chairperson and Whole-time Members) Rules, 2014, 
since 2023, the Chairperson and Whole -time Members of the PFRDA a re appointed by the Central 
Government on the recommendation of the Financial Sector Regulatory Appointments Search 
Committee. Prior to 2023, the Chairperson and Whole -time Members were appointed on the 
recommendation of a Selection Committee. 
 
(b) Insurance Regulatory and Development Authority of India (IRDAI) 
 
As per section 4 of the Insurance Regulatory and Development Authority Act, 1999, the Chairperson, 
Whole-time Members and Part-time Members are appointed by the Central Government. The Act and 
the rules and regulations made thereunder do not lay down the p rocess for selection of candidates 
for these offices. However, the vacancy circulars inviting applications for the office of the Chairperson 

31 
 
and Whole-time Members specify that appointments shall be made by the Central Government on 
the recommendation of the Financial Sector Regulatory Appointments Search Committee. 
 
(c) Reserve Bank of India (RBI) 
 
As per section 8 of the Reserve Bank of India Act, 1934, the Central Board of the Reserve Bank of 
India consists of— 
 
(a) a Governor and not more than four Deputy Governors appointed by the Central 
Government; 
 
(b) four Directors nominated by the Central Government, one from each of the four Local 
Boards; 
 
(c) ten Directors nominated by the Central Government; and 
 
(d) one Government official nominated by the Central Government. 
 
The Act and the rules and regulations made thereunder do not prescribe the process for selection of 
candidates for these offices. However, as per the existing administrative practice, the Governor and 
Deputy Governors of the Reserve Bank of India are appoi nted on the recommendation of the 
Financial Sector Regulatory Appointments Search Committee.” 
 
 
(ii) Regarding the wording of “useful” in the provision – Clause 4(4) 
 
5.5  On the question of whether the expression “useful” employed in Clause 4(4) should be 
replaced with the expression “relevant”, in the interest of greater legislative precision, consistency 
and certainty, considering that “relevant” is a formulation more co mmonly adopted in comparable 
regulatory statutes and investigative frameworks, the Committee sought the views of the Ministry. 
 
In response, the Secretary, Ministry of Finance, while deposing before the Committee, stated that the 
suggestion had merit and informed the Committee that the proposed change would be incorporated 
as a drafting modification in Clause 4(4). 

32 
 
 
5.6  The Committee have examined Clause 4(3), which empowers the Central Government 
to prescribe, by rules, the manner of appointment of the Chairperson and Members of the 
Board. The Committee note that the existing framework provides for appointments to be made 
on the recommendation of the Financial Sector Regulatory Appointments Search Committee 
(FSRASC) and that the Ministry has indicated that the existing rules would continue under the 
Code. 
 
The Committee are of the view that a transparent, objective and institutionalised appointment 
process is essential to preserve the independence, credibility and effectiveness of the 
regulator. The Committee, therefore, desire that, at the stage of implemen tation of the Code, 
the rules governing appointments be framed or continued in a manner that expressly provides 
for appointments to be made on the recommendation of the Financial Sector Regulatory 
Appointments Search Committee, or an equivalent institutional mechanism, thereby ensuring 
a transparent, merit-based and consistent process for the selection of the Chairperson and 
Members of the Board. 
 
5.7  The Committee have examined Clause 4(4) and note that it prescribes the 
qualifications and experience required for appointment as the Chairperson and Members of 
the Board. The Committee further note that the provision requires such persons to possess 
ability, integrity and standing in dealing with securities markets or special knowledge or 
experience in specified disciplines, including “any other discipline which, in the opinion of 
the Central Government, is useful to the Board.” 
 
The Committee are of the view that the expression “useful” is broad and subjective and may 
admit varying interpretations. In statutory provisions governing appointments to regulatory 
bodies, the use of precise and objective terminology assumes significance in ensuring clarity, 
consistency and transparency in the selection process. The Committee, therefore, consider 
that the expression “relevant” would more appropriately convey the legislative intent and 
would also be consistent with the terminology adopted in comparable regulatory and 
investigative statutes. 
 
The Committee, therefore, recommend that Clause 4(4) be suitably amended by replacing the 
expression “useful” with “relevant”, or such other appropriate expression conveying the 
same intent, so as to enhance legislative precision, reduce interpretational a mbiguity and 
ensure consistency with established drafting practices. 
 
  

33 
 
CHAPTER VI 
 
VI Terms and Conditions of Service of Chairperson and Members of the Board 
 
Clause 5 
 
6.1 Clause 5 provides for the terms and conditions of service of the Chairperson and Members of the 
Board, as under:— 
 
5 (1) The Chairperson and every whole -time Member of the Board shall hold office for a term not 
exceeding five years: 
 
Provided that no person shall hold office as the Chairperson or a whole-time Member on attaining the 
age of sixty-five years. 
 
(2) Every other Member referred to in clause (d) of sub-section (1) of section 4 shall hold office for a 
term not exceeding three years: 
 
Provided that no person shall hold office as such Member on attaining the age of seventy years. 
 
(3) The other terms and conditions of service of the Chairperson and the Members referred to in 
clauses (a) and (d) of sub-section (1) of section 4 shall be such as may be prescribed. 
 
(4) The term of office of the ex officio Members shall continue as long as they hold the office by virtue 
of which they are such Members. 
 
(5) Notwithstanding anything contained in sub-section (1) or sub-section (2), the Central Government 
shall have the right to terminate the services of the Chairperson or a Member appointed under clauses 
(a) and (d) of sub-section (1) of section 4, at any time before the expiry of the period specified under 
sub-section (1) or sub -section (2), by giving not less than three months’ notice in writing or three 
months’ salary and allowances in lieu thereof. The Chairperson or such Member, as the case may 
be, shall have the right to relinquish office, at any time before the expiry of the period specified under 
sub-section (1) or sub-section (2), by giving not less than three months’ notice in writing to the Central 
Government. 
 

34 
 
(6) The Chairperson and the whole-time Members shall not accept any employment under the Central 
Government or any State Government, or any appointment with a securities markets service provider, 
a market participant or any other person associated with the  securities markets for a period of one 
year from the date on which they cease to hold office, except with the prior approval of the Central 
Government.” 
 
(i) Cooling-off period – Clause 5(6) 
 
6.2  Certain stakeholders expressed concern regarding Clause 5(6) on the ground that the Bill 
prohibits the Chairperson and Whole -time Members from accepting employment under the Central 
Government or any State Government, or any appointment with a securities markets service provider, 
market participant or any other person associated with the securities markets for a period of one year 
after demitting office, unless prior approval of the Central Government has been obtained. It was 
submitted that such post-tenure restrictions are intended to address potential conflicts of interest. The 
stakeholders further pointed out that comparable cooling -off provisions exist under the statutes 
governing other regulators and that, in most cases, such laws prescribe a cool ing-off period of two 
years. They also noted that the Reserve Bank of India Act, 1934 does not contain a corresponding
