provision relating to post-tenure employment. 
 
6.3  When the views of the Ministry were sought on the suggestion to provide for a cooling -off 
period of two years, the Ministry, in its written reply, stated as under:— 
 
“Presently, Rule 3(4) of the SEBI (Terms and Conditions of Service of Chairman and Members) Rules, 
1992 provides that the Chairman or Whole-time Member shall not accept any employment before the 
expiry of a period of one year from the date of demitting off ice in SEBI, except with the previous 
sanction of the Central Government. A similar one -year requirement has been retained under the 
SMC. The provision has been accorded statutory status with suitable modifications. The scope has 
also been expanded to incl ude appointments with a securities markets service provider, market 
participant or any other person associated with the securities markets.” 
 
Further supplementing the above, the Secretary, Ministry of Finance, while deposing before the 
Committee, stated that the cooling -off period had been retained at one year to balance two 
considerations, namely, avoiding conflicts of interest and ensuring th at persons possessing high 
levels of expertise and experience remain willing to join the institution. The Secretary stated:— 
 
“This position emerged from extensive consultations. In order to attract talent to positions such as 
membership of the SEBI Board, we want people from the market and people with substantial 
experience to be willing to join. Originally, the proposed period was two years, but after consultations, 
it was reduced to one year in order to preserve the attractiveness of serving on the SEBI Board.” 

35 
 
 
6.4  When asked whether the cooling -off period provided under Clause 5(6) warrants 
enhancement to two years and to furnish details of international practices and the corresponding 
provisions applicable to other regulatory bodies, the Ministry, in its written reply, stated as under:— 
 
“This provision is based on Rule 3(4) of the Securities and Exchange Board of India (Terms and 
Conditions of Service of Chairman and Members) Rules, 1992, which restricts post -retirement 
employment for one year, subject to the approval of the Central Government. Clause 5(6) of the SMC 
codifies this rule. 
 
Contemporary Regulators 
 
• Statutes governing contemporary regulators such as the International Financial Services Centres 
Authority (IFSCA) and the Pension Fund Regulatory and Development Authority (PFRDA) expressly 
provide for such a requirement, with a cooling-off period of two years. Other regulators, such as the 
Insurance Regulatory and Development Authority of India (IRDAI) and the Reserve Bank of India 
(RBI), do not prescribe similar restrictions. 
 
Global Practices 
 
• In the United States, while there is no employment -related prohibition, former employees of the 
Securities and Exchange Commission are prohibited from making any communication or appearing 
on behalf of any other person before the Commission in matters in which they participated personally 
or which were pending under their official supervision. Further, former senior officers of the 
Commission are prohibited, for a period of one year after cessation of service, from representing a 
foreign government entity before any department or agency of the United States Government or from 
aiding or advising a foreign entity. 
 
• In other jurisdictions, such as the United Kingdom and Singapore, there is no formal cooling -off 
period. Instead, conflicts of interest are addressed through internal safeguards, including mandatory 
disclosure of future employment, notice period controls , restricted access to information during the 
notice period, and continuing confidentiality obligations.”** 
 
 
6.5  The Committee have examined Clause 5(6), which provides that the Chairperson and 
Whole-time Members of the Board shall not accept employment under the Central Government 
or any State Government, or any appointment with a securities markets service provide r, 
market participant or any other person associated with the securities markets for a period of 

36 
 
one year after demitting office, except with the prior approval of the Central Government. The 
Committee note that the provision seeks to safeguard the independence of the regulator by 
addressing the possibility of conflicts of interest and regulatory capt ure arising from post -
tenure engagements. 
 
6.6  The Committee further note that, while the existing provision has been carried forward 
from the SEBI (Terms and Conditions of Service of Chairman and Members) Rules, 1992, the 
proposed Code accords the restriction statutory status and expands its scope to cover 
appointments with securities markets service providers, market participants and other 
persons associated with the securities markets. The Committee also note that certain 
contemporary financial sector regulators in India, including the Internati onal Financial 
Services Centres Authority (IFSCA) and the Pension Fund Regulatory and Development 
Authority (PFRDA), prescribe a cooling-off period of two years, whereas some other domestic 
regulators and certain foreign jurisdictions adopt different appro aches through conflict -of-
interest safeguards and post-employment restrictions. 
 
6.7  The Committee are, however, of the considered view that the Securities and Exchange 
Board of India occupies a unique position as the principal regulator of the securities market, 
exercising wide-ranging regulatory, supervisory, investigative and enforcement powers over 
market participants. Given the nature of these functions and the sensitivity of the information 
available to the Chairperson and Whole -time Members during their tenure, a longer cooling -
off period would provide a stronger institutional s afeguard against actual or perceived 
conflicts of interest, enhance public confidence in the independence and impartiality of the 
regulator, and reinforce the credibility of the regulatory framework. 
 
6.8  The Committee, therefore, recommend that Clause 5(6) be suitably amended to provide 
for a cooling-off period of two years, in place of one year, before the Chairperson and Whole-
time Members may accept employment or any appointment with a securities markets service 
provider, market participant or any other person associated with the securities markets. 
  

37 
 
CHAPTER VII 
 
VII Powers and Functions of the Board 
 
Clause 11 
 
7.1 Clause 11 provides for the powers and functions of the Board, as under:— 
 
11 (1) Subject to the provisions of this Code, the Board shall protect the interests of investors in 
securities and promote the development of, and regulate, the securities markets, by such measures 
as it may deem fit. 
 
(2) Without prejudice to the generality of the foregoing provisions, the Board shall exercise the powers 
and perform the following functions, namely:— 
 
(a) register and regulate the market infrastructure institutions; 
 
(b) register and regulate the intermediaries; 
 
(c) register and regulate the self-regulatory organisations; 
 
(d) regulate the business in securities markets; 
 
(e) regulate the issue, listing, delisting, transfer of securities, dealings in securities and other 
related matters by market participants, subject to the provisions of Chapter XV; 
 
(f) regulate advertisement soliciting money by way of issue of securities; 
 
(g) regulate or prohibit the solicitation of money from investors in respect of any investment 
scheme; 
 
(h) regulate substantial acquisition of shares and take-over of companies; 

38 
 
 
(i) regulate the working of any class or classes of market participants, either directly or 
indirectly through a market infrastructure institution or a self-regulatory organisation; 
 
(j) regulate the qualifications and certification of the securities markets service providers; 
 
(k) protect the interests of investors, promote investor education and awareness, and ensure 
effective and time-bound redressal of investor grievances; 
 
(l) prohibit fraudulent and unfair practices relating to the securities markets and market abuse; 
 
(m) promote training of intermediaries and market participants; 
 
(n) promote transparency and best practices in its governance; 
 
(o) ensure fair and timely disclosure of information by a securities markets service provider; 
 
(p) ensure adequate training and capacity-building of its officers and employees for facilitating 
the efficient discharge of its functions; 
 
(q) lay down principles for the implementation of this Code; 
 
(r) call for information from a securities markets service provider, a market participant or any 
other person associated with the securities markets for the implementation of this Code; 
 
(s) call for information and records from any person, including any bank or financial institution 
or any other authority, Board or corporation established or constituted by or under any Central 
Act or State Act, which may be relevant to any inspection, investigation or adjudication in  
respect of any transaction in securities; 
 
(t) call for information from, or furnish information to, other authorities, whether in India or 
outside India, in matters relating to the prevention or detection of contraventions in respect of 
securities laws, subject to the provisions of any other law for the time being in force: 

39 
 
 
Provided that the Board may, for the purpose of furnishing any information to any authority 
outside India, enter into an arrangement, agreement or understanding with such authority with 
the prior approval of the Central Government; 
 
(u) undertake inspection, investigation and adjudication; 
 
(v) share any information or other documents obtained during inspection, investigation or 
adjudication, as the case may be, regarding the contravention of any other law for the time 
being in force with the authorities concerned, if such information or documents are relevant or 
useful to such authority; 
 
(w) levy fees or other charges for carrying out the purposes of this Code, which shall be 
reviewed periodically; 
 
(x) conduct periodic research, regulatory impact assessment studies and audits of the 
functioning and performance of the securities markets, including securities markets service 
providers, and publish such information and data on its website; 
 
(y) maintain its website or any other universally accessible electronic database relating to the 
securities markets, and publish on such website or database all regulations, subsidiary 
instructions, orders of the Adjudicating Officer and the Ombudsperson, and such other 
information as it may deem fit; and 
 
(z) perform such other functions as may be prescribed. 
 
(3) The Board shall review its performance and functioning, including the proportionality and 
effectiveness of the regulations made in this behalf. 
 
(4) Notwithstanding anything contained in any other law for the time being in force, while exercising 
the powers under clauses (r), (s) or (u) of sub-section (2), the Board shall have the same powers as 
are vested in a civil court under the Code of Civil P rocedure, 1908, while trying a suit, in respect of 
the following matters, namely:— 
 
(a) discovery and production of books of account and other documents, at such place and 
time as may be specified by the Board; 

40 
 
 
(b) summoning and enforcing the attendance of persons and examining them on oath; 
 
(c) inspection of any books, registers and other documents of a securities markets service 
provider or an issuer, at any place; or 
 
(d) issuing commissions for the examination of witnesses or documents.” 
 
(i) Sharing of information with other authorities – Clause 11(2)(v) 
 
7.2  Certain stakeholders submitted that Clause 11(2)(v) presently provides that information or 
documents may be furnished to the concerned authorities in relation to a possible contravention of 
laws other than securities laws where such information is conside red relevant or useful. The 
stakeholders suggested that it would be appropriate to clarify that any sharing of information or 
documents should be undertaken strictly in accordance with the provisions of the applicable law 
governing such disclosure, as contemplated under Clause 11(2)(t), rather than solely on the basis of 
relevance or usefulness. 
 
The stakeholders further submitted that, until a contravention is conclusively determined by a 
competent court or authority, the matter remains at the stage of an alleged violation. Keeping in view 
the principle of presumption of innocence, they suggested that the furnishing of information or 
documents should be undertaken only in accordance with due process of law and where the 
concerned authority is legally empowered to seek, request and receive such information. According 
to the stakeholders, this would ensure that any disclosure is authorised by law and follows established 
legal processes, rather than being made unilaterally. 
 
7.3  On the issue of whether the provision permitting the Board to furnish information or documents 
to other authorities on the ground that such information is “relevant or useful” in relation to a possible 
contravention of laws other than securities laws shou ld be suitably qualified to ensure that such 
disclosure is made only in accordance with the applicable legal framework and due process of law, 
the Committee sought the views of the Ministry. Concern was also expressed that, until a 
contravention is established by a competent court or authority, the matter remains at the stage of an 
alleged violation and, therefore, having regard to the principle of presumption of innocence, 
information should be shared only where the concerned authority is legally empow ered to seek and 
receive such information. 
 
In its written reply, the Ministry acknowledged the concern and stated as under:— 
 

41 
 
“The appropriate drafting changes will be made.” 
 
7.4  The Committee have examined Clause 11(2)(v), which empowers the Board to share 
information or documents obtained during inspection, investigation or adjudication with other 
authorities where such information or documents are considered relevant or useful in relation 
to the contravention of any other law. The Committee further note the concerns expressed by 
stakeholders that such sharing of information should be undertaken only in accordance with 
the applicable legal framework and established principles of due process, particularly where 
the alleged contravention has not yet been determined by a competent court or authority. 
 
7.5  The Committee are of the view that the exchange of information between regulatory 
and enforcement authorities is essential for the effective enforcement of law. However, such 
disclosure should be subject to appropriate statutory safeguards so as to ensur e that it is 
made only where authorised under law and only to an authority legally empowered to seek 
and receive such information. The Committee, therefore, recommend that Clause 11(2)(v) be 
suitably amended to provide that information or documents may be furnished only in 
accordance with the provisions of the applicable law and due process, and only to authorities 
legally empowered to seek and receive such information. This would ensure consistency with 
Clause 11(2)(t), uphold the principles of fairness  and legal certainty, and prevent any 
unintended or unauthorised disclosure of information. 
  

42 
 
CHAPTER VIII 
 
VIII Inspection, Investigation, Adjudication and Interim Order 
 
Clause 13 — Investigation 
 
8.1 Clause 13 of the Code provides for investigation, as under:— 
 
13 (1) If the Board, on receipt of any information or otherwise, has reasonable grounds to 
believe that— 
 
(a) transactions in securities are being undertaken in a manner detrimental to investors 
or the securities markets; 
 
(b) any securities markets service provider, market participant or any other person 
associated with the securities markets has contravened any of the provisions of this 
Code or the rules or regulations made thereunder, or any order issued by the Board; 
or 
(c) any person has aided or abetted such contraventions, it may, by an order in writing, 
direct one or more persons from amongst its Chairperson, whole -time Members or 
officers to act as the Investigating Officer to investigate such transactions or 
contraventions, or the affairs of such securities markets service provider, market 
participant or any other person associated with the securities markets, and submit a 
report to the Board: 
 
Provided that the Board may seek the assistance of a securities markets service provider or an expert 
to assist the Investigating Officer under this Chapter, in such manner as may be specified by 
regulations. 
 
(2) The order referred to in sub-section (1) shall be in such form as may be specified by regulations, 
and the investigation shall be completed within a period of one hundred and eighty days: 
 
Provided that where the investigation report is not submitted within the aforesaid period, the 
Investigating Officer shall provide the status of the investigation to the Board, record the reasons for 
the delay and request the whole-time Member concerned for an extension of time: 

43 
 
 
Provided further that, on receipt of a request for extension of time, the whole -time Member may, if 
necessary, grant such extension, subject to such conditions, if any, as he may deem fit, or reject the 
request. 
 
(3) The Investigating Officer shall, where quantifiable, quantify the unlawful gains made or likely to be 
made, or the loss caused or likely to be caused to an investor or group of investors, as a result of any 
default or contravention, in accordance with such criteria and in such manner as may be specified by 
regulations. 
 
(4) If the Board, upon consideration of the investigation report, is of the opinion that further 
investigation is called for, it may, by an order, direct the Investigating Officer to undertake further 
investigation and submit a supplementary report within such period as may be specified therein. 
 
(5) The procedure for conducting an investigation under this Chapter shall be such as may be 
specified by regulations.” 
 
(i) Inclusion of “aid or abetment” in Clause 13(1) 
8.2 Certain stakeholders suggested a drafting amendment to Clause 13(1) by inserting the words 
“or aid or abetment” after the word “contraventions” in the opening portion of the clause. It was 
submitted that aiding and abetment are expressly referred to in Clause 13(1)(c), but are not included 
in the opening portion of the provis ion. While addressing the suggested drafting amendment, the 
Ministry stated that the concern had been accepted and that appropriate drafting changes would be 
made. 
(ii) Timeline for completion of investigation — Clause 13(2) 
8.3 Certain stakeholders expressed concerns regarding the computation of the period of 
investigation under Clause 13(2). It was submitted that where an investigation or any related 
proceeding has been stayed by a court or tribunal, the period during which suc h stay remains in 
operation should be excluded while computing the prescribed period under the Code. Consequently, 
the period of stay should also be excluded while calculating the validity of an interim order under 
Clause 27(3). 
 
The stakeholders also furnished a summary of the time taken to complete cases, based on the nature 
of the violation, during the preceding three financial years. In cases relating to fraudulent and unfair 
trade practices, out of a total of 221 cases completed during the three-year period, 33 per cent were 
completed within one year, 49 per cent were completed within a period ranging from one year to two 
years, and 18 per cent took more than two years. It was stated that the time required for completion 
of an investigation also depends upon the period under investigation, liquidity in the scrip, volume of 

44 
 
trading, number of entities examined, evidence collected and witnesses involved, among other 
factors. 
 
It was further suggested that the period of one hundred and eighty days prescribed for completion of 
an investigation should be reconsidered. According to the stakeholders, the proposed timeline may 
compromise the quality of investigations by prioritising speed over thoroughness. Comprehensive 
investigations require adequate time for the identification of irregularities, corroboration of evidence 
involving various entities, coordination with and examination of multiple parties, including through the 
issuance of summons, and verification of the information received before arriving at appropriate 
findings. 
 
It was pointed out that such investigations may also involve scheduling constraints and, in some 
cases, cross-border procedural requirements. For instance, in matters involving front -running, the 
investigation process may require tracing trading patterns a cross multiple accounts and examining 
causal links between the possession of information and the trades executed. Such inquiries demand 
substantial time and resources. Further, in complex cases involving misstatements in financial 
statements, misappropriation, siphoning or diversion of funds, SEBI may appoint forensic auditors, 
following which it conducts its own investigation. 
 
The stakeholders submitted that the imposition of a rigid timeline may result in incomplete or imperfect 
investigations that fail to capture the full scope of the misconduct or adequately identify all the persons 
involved. Accordingly, the requirement to c omplete an investigation within the proposed period, 
though extendable, was stated to significantly underestimate the inherent complexities of 
investigations conducted by SEBI. 
 
It was also suggested that the period of one hundred and eighty days should exclude the time taken 
by the Investigating Officer to secure information relevant to the case from the persons concerned. 
According to the stakeholders, the prescribed period is i ntended to ensure efficiency and diligence 
on the part of the Investigating Officer and should not penalise the investigation for delays attributable 
to a noticee, market infrastructure institution or any other person relevant to the investigation. 
Otherwise, the timeline could be used to frustrate an investigation by delaying the furnishing of replies 
or other information to the Investigating Officer. 
 
8.4 When the views of the Ministry were sought on the suggestion that the period of one hundred 
and eighty days prescribed for completion of an investigation should be reconsidered, having regard 
to the complexity and duration of securities market investigations, the Ministry submitted as under:— 
 
“Based on the data provided, the period of one hundred and eighty days may be extended to one 
year. Appropriate drafting changes will be made.” 

45 
 
 
8.5 When asked about the rationale for prescribing a timeline for completion of investigations by 
the Investigating Officer and whether separate timelines should be prescribed for different categories 
of contraventions and offences, the Ministry submitted as under:— 
 
“In this regard, we think that we should accept the suggestion of prescribing a period of 365 days. 
We received further data from SEBI and found that, even at present, most investigations are being 
completed within a period of one year to one -and-a-half ye ars. We, therefore, considered the 
suggestion of prescribing a period of one year to be appropriate. It would maintain a strict timeline 
while also providing adequate time to the Investigating Officer to complete the investigation. 
 
However, prescribing different timelines for different offences would defeat the purpose of having a 
definitive timeline for Investigating Officers, particularly as the procedure for seeking an extension 
has also been laid down. Such an approach may, therefore, not be helpful.” 
 
(iii) Public disclosure of ongoing investigations 
8.6 During the course of deliberations, the Committee sought to know why an order extending the 
period of investigation could not be made public by placing it on the website of the regulator. It was 
observed that where an entity is under investigation by a re gulator, investors and the market may 
have a legitimate interest in being informed, particularly where the investigation is continued beyond 
the prescribed period. 
 
In response thereto, the Secretary, Ministry of Finance, stated as under:— 
 
“There is nothing confidential about that, but the issue is that the investigation concerns the person 
who may be described as the alleged violator. At present, an investigation may concern a highly 
reputed investor or a highly reputed company and remains within the confines of the investigation 
process. I am not sure whether details of ongoing investigations are presently placed on the portal. I 
am considering the issue as I respond, as it is relevant and important. 
 
Ongoing investigations are not presently placed on the portal. Publication of an order extending the 
timeline would disclose that an investigation is underway against a particular company, although the 
eventual outcome of the investigation may be entirely different.” 
 
8.7 On the question of whether a period of six months is sufficient to determine the existence of 
a prima facie case and whether investors should be informed where an investigation continues 
beyond such period, the Committee desired to know whether an appropr iate mechanism for 
disclosure could be incorporated. The Committee further desired to know whether the absence of 

46 
 
such disclosure could result in investors continuing to trade in the securities of an entity despite the 
existence of serious regulatory concerns. 
 
In response thereto, the Ministry submitted as under:— 
 
“My humble submission is that this is an ongoing enforcement procedure. We have already accepted 
the suggestion to extend the period to one year. We have also examined data indicating that, even 
at present, most cases are completed within approximately one -and-a-half years. By prescribing a 
definite period of one year, we expect a number of such cases to be completed within that period. 
However, whether informing the market and the public through publication of an order granting an 
extension would be premature is an issue that requires consideration.” 
 
The Ministry further submitted that a balance needs to be maintained between investor protection 
and avoiding premature disclosure of investigations that may ultimately result in no contravention 
being established. In this regard, the Secretary, Ministry of Finance, stated as under:— 
 
“What I am trying to humbly submit is that a complaint may be received and, on the face of it, may 
also be supported by some data. Since it is a serious complaint, an inspection or investigation may 
be ordered. I am, therefore, returning to the question of  whether such information should be placed 
on the website at all. 
 
The investigation may ultimately conclude that nothing has been established. In the meantime, market 
prices may fall, unless disclosure of ongoing investigations is already an established practice. That is 
why I am unable to provide a concrete answer at this stage. Ultimately, fluctuations in market prices 
may result in erosion of public money.” 
 
8.8 On the question of whether disclosure of ongoing investigations should be subject to 
appropriate safeguards, particularly in cases involving systemic risk or where such disclosure may 
adversely affect market stability, the Committee sought the views of th e Ministry. In this regard, the 
Ministry submitted as under:— 
 
“Sir, let us revert on this issue. I will also ascertain whether SEBI routinely publishes details of cases 
under investigation.” 
 
The Ministry further stated that, since such investigations involve companies and investors, disclosure 
is highly sensitive to market movements. Subsequently, in its written reply, the Ministry submitted as 
under:— 

47 
 
 
Current and SMC Position 
 
Investigation Orders 
 
An investigation order merely reflects SEBI’s decision to examine whether any contravention of 
securities laws has occurred and does not constitute a finding of wrongdoing. Public disclosure at this 
stage may cause significant reputational harm to the person being investigated, even where the 
investigation ultimately results in no enforcement action. 
 
In the case of listed entities, such disclosure may also lead to unwarranted market volatility and 
influence investor behaviour on the basis of unverified allegations rather than established facts. 
Investigations frequently involve sensitive commercial information and trading data, the confidentiality 
of which may be important both for preserving the integrity of the process and protecting the interests 
of the affected parties. 
 
Extension Orders 
 
Orders extending the period of investigation are procedural in nature and do not contain any finding 
regarding a contravention. Public disclosure of such orders may create an impression that SEBI has 
uncovered additional concerns or that the likelihood of a contravention has increased, whereas the 
extension may merely be required owing to the complexity of the matter, the volume of records 
involved or delays in obtaining information. 
 
At present, such orders are not made public by SEBI. 
 
Contemporary Laws 
 
Investigation orders issued by PFRDA and IRDAI are also not made public. 
 
8.9 The Committee have examined Clause 13, which lays down the statutory framework 
governing investigations by the Board, including the circumstances in which an investigation 
may be initiated, the appointment and powers of the Investigating Officer, the time line for 
completion of investigations, the quantification of unlawful gains or losses, and the 
submission of investigation reports. The Committee note that the objective of prescribing a 

48 
 
statutory timeline is to promote the timely and efficient completion of investigations while 
ensuring effective enforcement of the provisions of the Code. 
8.10 The Committee further note the concerns raised regarding the proposed investigation 
period of one hundred and eighty days, particularly in cases involving complex market 
misconduct, forensic audits, examination of multiple entities, cross-border transactions, and 
extensive collection and verification of evidence. The Committee are of the view that, while 
investigations should ordinarily be completed within a definite timeframe, the prescribed 
period must be realistic and commensurate with the nature an d complexity of securities 
market investigations. 
 
The Committee also note the suggestion that the opening portion of Clause 13(1) should 
expressly include the words “aid or abetment” so as to maintain drafting consistency with 
clause (c) of the same sub-section. 
 
8.11 The Committee, therefore, recommend that Clause 13 be suitably amended to provide 
a period of one year for completion of investigations by the Investigating Officer. The 
Committee further recommend that Clause 13(1) be suitably redrafted to expressly include the 
words “aid or abetment” in the opening portion of the provision so as to ensure internal 
consistency. 
 
The Committee also recommend that the Code continue to prescribe a uniform timeline for 
completion of investigations, rather than separate timelines for different categories of 
contraventions, while retaining the existing mechanism for grant of extensions in appropriate 
cases. This would balance the need for timely completion of investigations with the practical 
complexities associated with securities market enforcement. 
 
(iv) Investor protection and disclosure of ongoing investigations 
 
8.12 The Committee have carefully examined the issue of public disclosure of ongoing 
investigations, particularly where the period of investigation is extended beyond the 
prescribed timeline. The Committee note that an investigation order or an order extending the 
period of investigation is only a procedural step and does not constitute a finding of 
contravention. At the same time, the Committee are of the view that prolonged non-disclosure 
of regulatory investigations may have implications for investor awareness, particularly where 
the investigation concerns entities whose securities continue to be actively traded in the 
market. 
8.13 The Committee recognise that premature disclosure of investigations may adversely 
affect the reputation of regulated entities, influence investor behaviour on the basis of 
unverified allegations and, in appropriate cases, have implications for market stab ility. The 
Committee are, therefore, of the view that any disclosure framework should carefully balance 

49 
 
the objectives of investor protection, market integrity, confidentiality of investigations and the 
principles of natural justice. 
8.14 The Committee, therefore, recommend that the Ministry, in consultation with SEBI, 
examine the feasibility of developing an appropriate framework governing the disclosure of 
ongoing investigations, particularly where investigations continue beyond the prescribed 
period. Such a framework may, inter alia, provide for suitable safeguards, objective criteria 
and appropriate exceptions in cases involving systemic risk , market stability or the 
confidentiality of commercially sensitive information, or where disclosure may prejudice the 
investigation. 
 
The Committee further recommend that the framework be designed in a manner that balances 
investors’ interest in material regulatory information with the need to avoid unwarranted 
reputational harm arising from investigations that may ultimately result in n o finding of 
contravention. 
  

50 
 
CHAPTER IX 
 
IX General Powers and Obligations of Investigating Officers 
 
Clause 14 
 
9.1 Clause 14 provides for the general powers and obligations of Investigating Officers, as 
under:— 
 
14 (1) Notwithstanding anything contained in any other law for the time being in force, where an 
investigation has been directed under section 13,— 
 
(a) every key managerial personnel, director, manager, secretary or other officer or employee of a 
securities markets service provider or a market participant or a person being a body corporate; 
 
(b) every trustee or manager or employee of a securities markets service provider or a market 
participant or a person being a trust; 
 
(c) every partner, manager, secretary or key managerial personnel or other officer of the firm or 
society of a securities markets service provider or a market participant or a person being a firm or 
society; and 
 
(d) every other person or body of persons who has or had dealings in the course of business with any 
of the persons mentioned in clauses (a), (b) and (c), whether directly or indirectly, shall— 
 
(i) preserve and produce before the Investigating Officer or any person authorised by him in this 
behalf all such books of account, registers, records, information and other documents in his custody 
or power relating to, or having a bearing on, the subject matter of such investigation, as the case may 
be; 
 
(ii) furnish any such statement, undertaking or certification relating thereto, as may be required, within 
such period as may be specified by the Investigating Officer or any person authorised by him in this 
behalf; 
 

51 
 
(iii) provide reasonable assistance to the Investigating Officer or any person authorised by him in this 
behalf in relation to the investigation; and 
 
(iv) appear personally and take oath before the Investigating Officer, when required to do so, and 
answer any question put to him during such appearance in relation to the affairs of his business. 
 
(2) The Investigating Officer shall record the examination referred to in sub -section (1) in writing, 
which shall be read over and explained to, or by, and signed by the person examined, and may 
thereafter be used in evidence. 
 
(3) The Investigating Officer may keep in his custody any books, registers, other documents and 
records produced under sub -section (1) for a period of six months and shall thereafter return the 
same to the person concerned. 
 
(4) The Investigating Officer may, if he deems it necessary, again call for any book, register, other 
document or record. 
 
(5) If the person who produced the books, registers, other documents and records under sub-section 
(1) requires certified copies thereof, the Investigating Officer shall provide such copies. 
 
(6) The Investigating Officer shall, before exercising his powers under this section against any person, 
serve a notice upon him in such form and manner as may be specified by regulations: 
 
Provided that where the Investigating Officer is satisfied that the issuance of a notice would cause 
undue delay in the investigation or there is an apprehension that the records, books of account, 
registers, information and other documents referred to in sub-section (1) may be destroyed, mutilated, 
concealed, altered, falsified or secreted after the notice is served, he may, for reasons to be recorded 
in writing, dispense with the issuance of such notice. 
 
(7) Notwithstanding anything contained in any other law for the time being in force, the Investigating 
Officer shall have the same powers as are vested in a civil court under the Code of Civil Procedure, 
1908, while trying a suit, in respect of the following matters, namely:— 
 
(a) discovery and production of books of account and other documents, at such place and time as 
may be specified by the Board; 
 

52 
 
(b) summoning and enforcing the attendance of persons and examining them on oath; 
 
(c) inspection of any books, registers and other documents of a securities markets service provider 
or an issuer, at any place; and 
 
(d) issuing commissions for the examination of witnesses or documents.” 
 
9.2 Certain stakeholders suggested that Clause 14(6) be deleted. Alternatively, if the provision is 
retained in the Code, it should be clarified that notice may be served only upon the person from whom 
information or assistance is sought and not upon the person against whom the investigation is being 
conducted, unless information or assistance is also sought from such person. 
 
It was submitted that, under the existing framework, the Investigating Officer issues a notice in the 
form of a summons calling for information or requiring the personal appearance of persons suspected 
of contraventions, although summons may not be issued in every case. Even where summons are 
not issued, information such as bank statements and the rationale for particular trades may be called 
for through electronic mail, which may provide sufficient notice to such persons that the matter is 
under investigation. 
 
The stakeholders further submitted that the time required for an investigation may be affected by the 
following considerations:— 
 
(a) in certain cases, information may not be called for from suspected persons owing to an 
apprehension that records may be mutilated or tampered with; 
 
(b) in matters relating to the prohibition of fraudulent and unfair trade practices, there may be 
numerous suspected persons and, therefore, issuing notices under Clause 14(6) to every such 
person may not be practically feasible. Further, depending upon th e identified modus operandi, 
additional suspected persons may emerge during the later stages of the investigation; 
 
(c) the issuance of notices at different points of time to different suspected persons may alert them 
and provide further opportunity to destroy relevant records or digital evidence; and 
 
(d) the issuance of such notices may cause unnecessary delay in the investigation process. 
 

53 
 
When the views of the Ministry were sought on the suggestion that Clause 14(6) be omitted or, 
alternatively, clarified to provide that notice be served only upon the person from whom information 
or assistance is sought, having regard to the practical diffi culties that the provision may pose to 
investigations, including the risk of alerting suspected persons, destruction of evidence and delay in 
the investigation process, the Ministry submitted as under:— 
 
“The provision may be clarified to specify that the notice requirement applies only to the person 
against whom investigative powers are to be exercised, and drafting changes will be carried out in 
this regard.” 
 
9.3 Certain stakeholders suggested that provisions available under the existing framework should 
be appropriately incorporated in Clause 14 of the Code. They submitted that the existing clauses (ia) 
and (ib) of sub -section (2) of section 11 of the SEBI Act, 1 992 should be retained. They further 
suggested that, in line with the existing section 11C, clause (a) should be expanded to include every 
intermediary or person associated with the securities market. 
 
Drawing upon rule 4(6) of the SEBI (Procedure for Holding Inquiry and Imposing Penalties by 
Adjudicating Officer) Rules, the stakeholders suggested that an express provision be incorporated to 
enable any person acquainted with the facts and circumstances o f the subject matter under 
investigation to be required to furnish information, documents or evidence relevant to the 
investigation. 
 
The stakeholders further pointed out that the provision contained several drafting errors and 
omissions. In particular, it did not expressly refer to the “person being investigated”, a “person 
associated with the securities market” or a “person acquainted with the facts” as persons who may 
be called upon to provide information or assistance. Instead, the provision directly referred to persons 
in charge of corporate or other entities. It was questioned how the provision would operate where the 
person under investigation was an individual or an investor. Concerns were also raised regarding the 
meaning of the expression “dealings in the course of business” and whether it would cover a situation 
where records belonging to the person under investigation were kept with another person with whom 
he had no business dealings. 
 
On the question of incorporating relevant provisions of the existing framework in Clause 14, including 
expanding its scope to cover every intermediary, person associated with the securities market and 
any person acquainted with the facts of the investigation, and addressing the drafting omissions and 
ambiguities identified by the stakeholders, the Committee sought the views of the Ministry. In 
response, the Ministry stated that, for the sake of clarity, it may be provided that any person may be 
required to assist in the investigation and that appropriate drafting changes would be made. 
 

54 
 
9.4 On the question whether the culmination of an investigation under the proposed legislation 
would entail only civil consequences or could also lead to criminal proceedings, it was pointed out 
that the answer would have a direct bearing on the interpretation of Clause 14(2), which provides that 
the statement recorded by the Investigating Officer may thereafter be used in evidence. In response 
thereto, the Ministry stated as under:— 
 
“It could, because the offence is only under Clause 92, which is civil action, and Clause 93, which is 
market abuse. The finding of the investigation and adjudication could be that the offence is market 
abuse.” 
 
9.5 Certain stakeholders expressed concern that where an investigation could ultimately result in 
criminal consequences, including prosecution before a Special Court and conviction, permitting 
statements made before an Investigating Officer to be used in evidence would be contrary to settled 
principles of criminal jurisprudence. 
 
It was pointed out that, under criminal law, statements made before a police officer under the relevant 
provisions of criminal procedure law are generally not admissible in evidence against the accused 
and that statements intended to be used as evidence are ordinarily required to be recorded before a 
judicial authority. 
 
The Committee further observed that a provision permitting statements recorded before an 
Investigating Officer to be used in evidence may raise concerns relating to the constitutional 
protection against self-incrimination guaranteed under Article 20(3) of the Constitution of India. One 
of the Members specifically observed that a person cannot be compelled to be a witness against 
himself and that the provision, in its present form, may be inconsistent with this constitutional 
safeguard. 
 
In response thereto, the Ministry submitted as under:— 
 
“In the previous SEBI Act, as well as in continuation in the SMC, what has been said is correct that, 
apart from documents, you have the power to furnish statements, books of account, and also give a 
statement. All of that put together will be evidence.” 
 
The Ministry further clarified that the formulation was not novel and that similar provisions existed 
under other economic legislations. In this regard, the Secretary, Ministry of Finance, while deposing 
before the Committee, stated as under:— 
 

55 
 
“Sir, not just the PMLA, we will just recheck, it is also there in the Companies Act, insofar as the SFIO 
is concerned. The distinction between the police and the SFIO, for example, because I have worked 
in that organisation, has been recognised in High Co urt judgments. The distinction remains, which 
means that courts have upheld the non -police nature of investigations undertaken under another 
economic offences law, namely, the Companies Act. A similar position exists here also. 
 
The intent is, of course, that economic offences, particularly those handled by SEBI in relation to 
listed companies, are of a very serious nature and, therefore, this needs to be taken forward together 
with all the other provisions relating to documentary  evidence, etc., which courts would ordinarily 
appreciate.” 
 
9.6 When asked whether statements or evidence recorded by the Investigating Officer under 
Clause 14(2) would be admissible, or could be relied upon as evidence in subsequent proceedings, 
whether appropriate safeguards should be incorporated in Clause 14(2), a nd what the position was 
under other enactments governing similar investigative powers, the Ministry, in its written submission, 
stated as under:— 
 
Current Position 
 
Clause 14(2) is based on section 11C(7) of the SEBI Act, 1992, which provides that notes of any 
examination shall be taken down in writing, read over to, or by, and signed by the person examined, 
and may thereafter be used in evidence against him. 
 
SMC Position 
 
Clause 14(2) is consistent with the existing position under the SEBI Act. Under sections 11C(3), 
11C(5) and 11C(7) of the SEBI Act, 1992, the Investigating Authority may examine persons on oath, 
record their statements, and the notes of such examinations are admissible in evidence. Clause 14(2) 
substantially reflects this existing framework. 
 
The provision is also necessary for effective SEBI investigations, as investigations often involve 
complex facts and information available only from persons examined during the investigation. 
Permitting such statements to be used in evidence is essential t o enable SEBI to establish 
contraventions and take appropriate enforcement action. 
 
Contemporary Laws 
 

56 
 
Section 217(7) of the Companies Act, 2013 provides that statements made to an inspector during an 
investigation may be used in evidence. 
 
Further, section 16(6) of the PFRDA Act, 2013 also contains a similar requirement that information 
provided by the person examined may be used as evidence against such person.” 
 
9.7 The Committee have examined Clause 14, which sets out the general powers and 
obligations of the Investigating Officer during the course of an investigation. The Committee 
note that the provision seeks to empower the Investigating Officer to call for infor mation, 
examine persons on oath, secure documents and records, and exercise powers analogous to 
those of a civil court for the effective conduct of investigations. 
9.8 The Committee further note the concerns raised regarding the scope and drafting of 
the provision. In particular, the Committee find merit in the suggestion that the persons from 
whom information, documents or assistance may be sought should be described m ore 
comprehensively so as to include not only officers and functionaries of regulated entities but 
also intermediaries, persons associated with the securities market, the person under 
investigation and any other person acquainted with the facts and circumstances of the matter. 
9.9 The Committee are also of the considered view that the notice requirement under 
Clause 14(6) should not impede the effectiveness of investigations or result in the destruction, 
concealment or alteration of evidence. At the same time, the provision should clearly specify 
the persons to whom such notice is required to be issued so as to avoid ambiguity and 
unnecessary procedural complications. 
9.10 The Committee, therefore, recommend that Clause 14 be suitably amended to 
expressly empower the Investigating Officer to require any intermediary, person associated 
with the securities market, the person under investigation or any other person acquainted with 
the facts and circumstances of the subject matter to furnish information, documents, records 
or evidence relevant to the investigation. 
 
The Committee further recommend that the existing investigative powers available under 
section 11C of the SEBI Act, 1992 and the relevant provisions of the SEBI (Procedure for 
Holding Inquiry and Imposing Penalties by Adjudicating Officer) Rules be appropr iately 
reflected in the Code so as to avoid any dilution of the existing enforcement framework. 
 
9.11 The Committee further recommend that Clause 14(6) be suitably clarified to provide 
that the requirement of prior notice shall apply only to the person against whom the 
Investigating Officer proposes to exercise the powers under the provision, without prejudicing 
the authority of the Investigating Officer to dispense with such notice in exceptional 
circumstances where there exists a reasonable apprehension that the issuance of notice may 
result in the destruction, concealment, alteration or tampering of  evidence or otherwise 
prejudice the investigation. 

57 
 
 
This would strike an appropriate balance between procedural fairness and the effective 
conduct of investigations. 
 
Clause 14(2) — Admissibility of Statements Recorded During Investigation 
 
9.12 The Committee have carefully examined Clause 14(2), which provides that the 
statement recorded by the Investigating Officer, after being read over to and signed by the 
person examined, may thereafter be used in evidence. The Committee note that the provision 
substantially reflects the existing framework under section 11C(7) of the SEBI Act, 1992 and 
that similar provisions are also contained in certain other economic legislations governing 
regulatory investigations. 
9.13 The Committee are, however, of the considered view that the evidentiary use of 
statements recorded during an investigation assumes greater significance in view of the 
enforcement framework under the Code, where the outcome of an investigation may not be 
confined to civil or regulatory consequences but may also lead to prosecution for offences 
such as market abuse before the Special Court. 
 
In such circumstances, the Committee are of the opinion that the provision must be carefully 
calibrated so as to preserve the constitutional guarantee against self -incrimination under 
Article 20(3) of the Constitution and ensure adherence to the principles of natural justice and 
fair trial. 
 
9.14 The Committee, therefore, recommend that appropriate regulatory safeguards be 
incorporated to ensure that the evidentiary use of such statements does not prejudice the 
rights of the person under investigation or dilute the constitutional protection agains t self-
incrimination. 
  

58 
 
CHAPTER X 
 
X  Bar on Initiation of Inspection and Investigation 
 
Clause 16 
 
10.1 Clause 16 provides for a bar on the initiation of inspection and investigation, as under:— 
 
“16. (1) The Board shall not direct— 
 
(a) an inspection under section 12; or 
 
(b) an investigation under section 13, 
 
for any default or contravention of the provisions of this Code or the rules or regulations made 
thereunder after the expiry of a period of eight years from the date of such default or contravention: 
 
Provided that the Board may direct an inspection or investigation after the period specified above in 
the event of a matter being referred to it by an investigating agency or if, in the opinion of the Board, 
the default or contravention had, or may have, a systemic impact on the securities markets. 
 
(2) In the case of a continuing default or contravention, a fresh period under sub -section (1) shall 
begin to run at every moment of the time during which the default or contravention continues. 
 
(3) If more than one default or contravention is committed in a series of connected acts forming part 
of the same transaction and such defaults or contraventions are committed at different times, the 
period under sub-section (1) in respect of such defaults  or contraventions shall be deemed to have 
commenced from the date of the last default or contravention.” 
 
10.2 Certain stakeholders suggested the insertion of a sub -section prescribing a statutory period 
for the retention of documents and maintenance of records aligned with the eight -year limitation 
period under Clause 16. They suggested that records should be retained for a minimum period of ten 
years, with provision for extension where systemic impact is suspected or notified. 

59 
 
 
It was submitted that, in the absence of a statutory retention requirement, relevant evidence may not 
be available in systemic or complex cases, thereby undermining both effective enforcement and the 
ability of affected persons to defend themselves. Accord ing to the stakeholders, a record -retention 
obligation would operationalise the limitation framework under Clause 16 and contribute to the 
preservation of market integrity. 
 
Acknowledging the merit of the concern raised, the Ministry, in its written reply, stated as under:— 
 
“The concern is valid and appropriate drafting changes will be made to provide an enabling provision 
for the retention of such data for such period as may be specified, in addition to the requirements 
under other laws and in line with global best practices.” 
 
10.3 When asked to clarify the meaning, scope and parameters of the expression “systemic impact 
on the securities markets” used in Clause 16, which permits inspection or investigation beyond the 
period of eight years in cases involving such systemic impact, the Committee further enquired 
whether events such as a significant fall in market indices, the trigg ering of circuit breakers, 
heightened market volatility or other market -wide disruptions arising from activities such as short -
selling would constitute systemic impact, and how such impact would be assessed and quantified for 
the purpose of invoking the provision. 
 
In response thereto, the Ministry stated as under:— 
 
“It is on a case-by-case basis at present.” 
 
10.4 On the question of whether the determination of “systemic impact on the securities markets” 
should be guided by objective criteria rather than being left entirely to the discretion of the Board, so 
as to ensure uniformity, consistency and certainty in the  application of the provision and avoid 
divergent interpretations in similar circumstances, the Committee sought the views of the Ministry. 
 
Explaining the rationale underlying the provision, the Secretary, Ministry of Finance, stated as 
under:— 
 
“This is intended to be a collective decision of the Board. As presently framed, the provision states 
that the Board may direct an inspection or investigation in the event of a matter being referred to it by 
an investigating agency or if, in the opinion of  the Board, the default or contravention has already 
had, or may have, a systemic impact on the securities markets. 

60 
 
 
The provision is also preventive in nature, inasmuch as the Board may anticipate that the matter is 
sufficiently serious to have an impact, or may determine that it has already had an impact which could 
have wider consequences. The effect of the clause is to permit an investigation to be initiated beyond 
the period of eight years.” 
 
10.5 When specifically asked whether any definition of “systemic impact” existed, the Secretary, 
Ministry of Finance, submitted as under:— 
 
“No, Sir. That is the point which the hon. Member has also raised. We are leaving it to the Board to 
determine whether an investigation may go beyond the period of eight years so as to examine events 
that occurred more than eight years earlier. That is the  limited scope of this discussion and of the 
clause. 
 
The Board would permit such action only in cases where, collectively, it has material, evidence and 
other inputs to determine that the matter is sufficiently serious and has the potential to cause, or has 
caused, a systemic impact. The limitation is that, ordinarily, the Board cannot go back beyond eight 
years. In order to investigate a matter beyond that period, it must determine that there is a systemic 
impact. This would permit the Board to examine matters dating back ten or twelve years. The 
relevance of systemic impact is limited to that extent.” 
 
10.6 On the question of whether the exercise of the Board’s power to investigate matters beyond 
the limitation period of eight years on the ground of “systemic impact on the securities markets” should 
be guided by objective criteria, and whether prolonged and undetected contraventions extending over 
several years may also indicate regulatory shortcomings, the Committee sought the views of the 
Ministry. 
 
In response, the Ministry stated that there may be cases warranting investigation beyond eight years 
and referred to instances where proceedings relating to older market transactions had continued for 
extended periods. The Committee, however, emphasised th at the exercise of such extraordinary 
powers should be appropriately regulated and based on established rules and procedures. 
 
In this regard, the Ministry stated as under:— 
 
“That is precisely what was suggested by the Chairperson —that certain qualifications should be 
introduced and objective criteria should be laid down through regulations.” 
 

61 
 
10.7 When the views of the Ministry were sought on the scope and meaning of the expression 
“systemic impact” used in Clause 16, the Ministry, in its written reply, stated as under:— 
 
Current Position 
 
The existing SEBI framework does not prescribe any limitation period for the initiation of an inspection 
or investigation. 
 
SMC Position 
 
Clause 16 of the SMC prescribes a limitation period of eight years. Additionally, the proviso to Clause 
16(1) provides an exception to the prescribed period of limitation in order to enable the initiation of 
enforcement action in cases that have had, or may have, a systemic impact on the securities markets 
or where an investigating agency refers a matter to the Board. 
 
These powers may be exercised in exceptional circumstances to address cases posing systemic risk, 
as determined on a case -to-case basis. Specific parameters or illustrations may not be required to 
be incorporated in the Code.” 
 
10.8 On the question of whether the approach adopted under the Right to Fair Compensation and 
Transparency in Land Acquisition, Rehabilitation and Resettlement Act, 2013 and the proposal put 
forth by stakeholders regarding the concept of “systemic failure” could be examined while framing the 
provision relating to “systemic impact” under Clause 16, the Ministry, in its written reply, stated as 
under:— 
 
“The suggestion received from Trilegal proposes the introduction of objective indicators for 
determining systemic impact under the Code. The suggestion is as follows:— 
 
In Clause 16, after sub-clause (3), the following Explanation shall be inserted, namely:— 
 
‘Explanation.—For the purposes of this clause, the expression “systemic impact” means material 
adverse consequences affecting the integrity, stability or orderly functioning of the securities market, 
and shall be deemed to exist where at least two of the following circumstances are present, namely:— 
 

62 
 
(a) a quantifiable loss has been caused to investors, such loss has been determined, and the Board, 
for reasons to be recorded in writing, considers such loss to be substantial having regard to the 
liquidity or trading volume of the relevant security; 
 
(b) any loss or adverse impact has been caused to not less than ten investors who are not connected 
with each other in any manner; 
 
(c) damage has been caused to market infrastructure, including corruption of securities market -
related data, disruption of market connectivity or damage to the technological or physical 
infrastructure of the Board or a market infrastructure institution, and the value of such damage at the 
relevant time exceeds the cumulative depreciation of such asset or infrastructure for a period of eight 
years; or 
 
(d) the alleged unlawful activity causes such price movement or volatility in a security that it, by itself, 
triggers a circuit breaker in respect of trading in that security or at the index level, and such non -
compliance relates to raising money from the public, insider trading, or fraudulent and unfair trade 
practices.’ 
 
However, the indicators proposed by Trilegal may not adequately define the scope of systemic impact 
in the securities market. 
 
According to SEBI’s submission, having regard to the broad spectrum of the securities market, the 
four proposed tests may not adequately cover all possible aspects of the market. The proposed 
Explanation largely covers trading -related matters and does not include other aspects such as the 
primary market, corporate governance issues, intermediaries having no direct role in trading, 
including KRA agencies, and pooled investment vehicles. 
 
In view of the myriad factors that may potentially trigger systemic impact, there appears to be no 
objective manner in which the expression may be comprehensively defined, and the incorporation of 
such a definition may not be appropriate. 
 
Contemporary Laws 
 
An analysis of systemic impact is generally not provided under other laws. 
 

63 
 
The Right to Fair Compensation and Transparency in Land Acquisition, Rehabilitation and 
Resettlement Act, 2013, which regulates land acquisition for public purposes, mandates an 
assessment of the economic and social impact of an acquisition proposal upon p ersons displaced 
and affected by it. For determining such social impact, the Government takes into consideration 
factors such as the public purpose served by the proposed acquisition, the estimated number of 
affected and displaced families, alternative sites for acquisition, and the nature and cost of addressing 
the social impact. 
 
The systemic impact of a default or contravention upon the securities markets envisaged under the 
Code is conceptually distinct from the social impact of acquisition projects under the Land Acquisition 
Act, and the same indicators cannot be imported into the Code.” 
 
10.9 On the question of whether the first exception under Clause 16 was unqualified and 
insufficiently circumscribed, inasmuch as it permitted a matter to be reopened by SEBI after eight 
years solely on the basis of a reference from a State police agency, with out any additional statutory 
safeguards or threshold requirements, the Secretary, Ministry of Finance, submitted as under:— 
 
“The understanding was that other agencies may have discovered material upon which they were 
unable to proceed further. They may have closed their report and submitted it under the respective 
statute. However, the matter may nevertheless involve a securities market dimension.” 
 
10.10 When asked whether the conjunction “or” in the proviso should be substituted with “and”, so 
that the power could be exercised only where both prescribed conditions were satisfied, whether the 
eight-year limitation period was susceptible to misuse, and whe ther SEBI should be empowered to 
initiate action suo motu in cases involving systemic impact, the Committee sought the views of the 
Ministry. 
 
In response, the Secretary, Ministry of Finance, stated as under:— 
 
“The current drafting leaves the matter to the Board. It provides that the Board may direct an 
investigation in either of the two situations. Therefore, it is not the case that whenever a reference is 
made, the Board must necessarily order an inspection or investigation. 
 
The proviso states that the Board may direct an inspection or investigation in the event of a matter 
being referred to it by an investigating agency or if, in the opinion of the Board, the default or 
contravention has had a systemic impact.” 
 

64 
 
10.11 Concerns were raised regarding the scope of the proposed proviso permitting an inspection 
or investigation after the expiry of eight years and the possibility of misuse of the provision. In 
particular, it was questioned whether a mere reference by an investigating agency should be sufficient 
to enable the Board to reopen a matter beyond the prescribed limitation period. 
 
It was submitted that the two exceptions contained in the proviso, namely, a reference by an 
investigating agency and systemic impact on the securities markets, operated independently of each 
other, thereby conferring wide discretion upon the Board without objective safeguards or qualifying 
criteria. 
 
10.12  Concerns were also raised regarding the use of the expression “or” in the proviso to Clause 
16. It was pointed out that the present drafting enabled the two exceptions —a reference by an 
investigating agency and systemic impact on the securities markets—to operate independently. This 
could permit an inspection or investigation to be initiated after eight years solely on the basis of a 
reference by any investigating agency, without objective criteria or any nexus with systemic market 
concerns, thereby creating scope for misuse. 
 
In its written submission, the Ministry stated as under:— 
 
“The existing SEBI framework, comprising the three securities laws, namely, the Securities and 
Exchange Board of India Act, 1992, the Securities Contracts (Regulation) Act, 1956 and the 
Depositories Act, 1996, does not prescribe a limitation period for the  initiation of an inspection or 
investigation. Consequently, SEBI may initiate an inspection or investigation into any alleged 
contravention, irrespective of the number of years that have elapsed. 
 
Further, these three enactments contain omnibus criminal provisions which categorise any 
contravention of the securities laws, rules, regulations and relevant bye -laws as an offence under 
section 23M of the Securities Contracts (Regulation) Act, 1956, section 24 of the SEBI Act, 1992 and 
section 20(1) of the Depositories Act, 1996. 
 
In furtherance of the policy of decriminalisation, the omnibus criminal provisions have been omitted 
from the SMC and three specific categories of criminal offences have been incorporated in Clauses 
94, 95 and 96 of the SMC. 
 
Further, in order to provide certainty to the market and promote ease of doing business, Clause 16 
was incorporated to provide that an inspection or investigation shall not ordinarily be initiated after 
the expiry of eight years from the date of the contra vention, except in the case of a continuing 
contravention. 

65 
 
 
As an exception to this general rule, and in consultation with the Ministry of Law and Justice, the 
proviso to Clause 16(1) was incorporated to exempt the application of the eight-year limitation period 
in the following two cases:— 
 
(a) where a matter is referred to the Board by an investigating agency; or 
 
(b) where, in the opinion of the Board, the default or contravention had, or may have, a systemic 
impact on the securities markets. 
 
The view of the Hon’ble Committee is that the two exceptions are not comparable. Under the first 
exception, an investigation may be initiated merely upon a reference by another agency. Under the 
second exception, the existence of systemic risk circumscribes SEBI’s power. The first exception is, 
however, unqualified and uncircumscribed. 
 
Further, concerns have been raised regarding the wide ambit of the expression ‘systemic impact’, 
which has neither been explained nor defined in the Code, thereby giving rise to subjectivity. 
 
The current formulation does not prevent SEBI from initiating an inspection or investigation suo motu 
where the alleged default or contravention had, or may have, a systemic impact on the securities 
markets. Such powers may be exercised in exceptional circ umstances to address cases posing 
systemic risk, as determined on a case-to-case basis. 
 
In order to address concerns regarding the possibility of misuse of the eight -year exception and the 
subjective ambit of the expression ‘systemic impact’, the proviso may be reformulated as follows:— 
 
‘Provided that the Board may direct an inspection or investigation after the period specified above if 
the Board, on its own motion or upon a matter being referred to it by an investigating agency, for 
reasons to be recorded in writing, has reasonable grounds to believe that the default or contravention 
had, or may have, a systemic impact on the securities markets.’ 
 
This formulation retains SEBI’s power to initiate an inspection or investigation after eight years in 
cases involving systemic impact.” 
 
10.13 The Committee note that Clause 16 seeks to introduce, for the first time, a limitation 
period of eight years for directing an inspection or investigation into any default or 

66 
 
contravention under the proposed Code. The Committee appreciate that the proposed 
limitation seeks to bring certainty and finality to regulatory proceedings while preserving the 
Board’s power to act in exceptional circumstances. 
10.14 The Committee, however, observe that the proviso to the clause permits an inspection 
or investigation beyond the prescribed period in two situations, namely, where a matter is 
referred by an investigating agency or where, in the opinion of the Board, the default or 
contravention had, or may have, a systemic impact on the securities markets. 
The Committee further note that concerns were specifically raised during the course of 
evidence that the present drafting enables the two exceptions to operate independently. This 
may permit an inspection or investigation after the expiry of eight years solely on the basis of 
a reference by an investigating agency, without any objective criteria or demonstrable nexus 
with systemic concerns affecting the securities markets. 
 
The Committee are of the considered view that such a formulation is unduly broad and may 
result in uncertainty for regulated entities, create scope for arbitrary or unintended exercise 
of powers, and increase the possibility of misuse. 
 
10.15 The Committee further observe that the expression “systemic impact” has not been 
defined in the Code and, in the absence of adequate legislative guidance, may be susceptible 
to varying interpretations. At the same time, the Committee recognise that the Bo ard should 
retain the ability to act beyond the prescribed period in exceptional cases involving serious 
and continuing threats to the integrity, stability or orderly functioning of the securities 
markets. 
 
The Committee, therefore, recommend that the proviso to Clause 16 be comprehensively 
redrafted so that the power to direct an inspection or investigation beyond eight years is 
exercised only in narrowly defined and exceptional circumstances. A mere referen ce by an 
investigating agency should not, by itself, constitute a sufficient ground for reopening a 
matter after the expiry of eight years. 
 
The Board should independently satisfy itself, for reasons to be recorded in writing, that the 
matter involves a serious case of market abuse or a continuing systemic impact on the 
securities markets warranting such action. A reference by an investigating agency should 
operate only as a trigger for the Board to independently examine the matter and should not, 
by itself, dispense with the requirement of establishing a nexus with systemic impact on the 
securities markets. 
 
The Committee also recommend that the Ministry prescribe appropriate statutory parameters 
or objective guiding principles governing the exercise of this extraordinary power so as to 
minimise the possibility of misuse while preserving the Board’s ability to  protect market 
integrity effectively in exceptional cases. 

67 
 
 
The Committee further recommend that, in order to address concerns regarding possible 
misuse of the exception to the eight -year limitation period and the subjective ambit of the 
expression “systemic impact”, the proviso to Clause 16(1) be modified as under:— 
 
“Provided that the Board may direct an inspection or investigation after the period specified 
above if the Board, on its own motion or upon a matter being referred to it by an investigating 
agency, for reasons to be recorded in writing, has reasonable grou nds to believe that the 
default or contravention had, or may have, a systemic impact on the securities markets.” 
 
 
CHAPTER XI 
 
XI Power of the Board to Designate an Adjudicating Officer 
 
Clause 17 
11.1 Clause 17 provides for the power of the Board to designate an Adjudicating Officer, as under:- 
(1) Subject to sub-section (2), where the Board, after consideration of—  
(a) the findings of the inspection made under section 12;  
(b) the investigation report, together with any supplementary report, received under section 13, and 
any other information, if any, is of the opinion that there are sufficient grounds to pass an order under 
section 20, it may designate one or more persons from amongst its Chairperson, whole-time Members 
or any other officer not below such rank of a Division Chief, as may be specified by regulations, to 
act as an adjudicating officer for the purposes of this Chapter. 
 (2) No person shall be designated as an adjudicating officer, if he has–– 
 (a) authorised the initiation of the inspection or investigation, which is the basis for issuance of a 
show cause notice to a person in that matter; or 
 (b) been involved in the conduct of the inspection or investigation, which is the basis for issuance of 
a show cause notice to a person or assisted in the conduct of such processes in that matter; or 5 10 
15 20 25 30 35 40 45 46 of 2023. 15 5 of 1908. 5 10 15 20 25 30 35 40 45  
(c) considered the findings of the inspection or investigation in that matter and decided to appoint the 
adjudicating officer for adjudication under this Chapter; or 
 (d) considered any settlement application filed by a person to whom a show cause notice is to be 
issued in that matter; or 
 (e) passed an interim order under sub-section (2) of section 27. (3) Where a person is designated as 
an adjudicating officer under sub-section (1), he shall not––  

68 
 
(a) conduct the inspection or investigation in respect of the person to whom the adjudicating officer 
has issued a show cause notice until the final disposal of the matter relating to such show cause 
notice; and 
 (b) consider or take a decision on any settlement application filed by a person to whom the 
adjudicating officer has issued a show cause notice.  
(4) The adjudication proceedings shall be conducted in such manner as may be specified by 
regulations.  
 
11.2 Certain stakeholders suggested that consideration may be given to prescribing that a person 
designated as an Adjudicating Officer should either possess qualifications in law or be provided with 
adequate legal training. 
 
It was submitted that the responsibilities proposed to be vested in the Adjudicating Officer involve the 
discharge of quasi-judicial functions and are substantially wider than those under the existing legal 
framework. These responsibilities include, inter alia, the passing of a common order, imposition of 
enhanced penalties and issuance of various directions, including directions relating to disgorgement. 
In view of the enhanced nature of the role, the stakeholders suggested that it may be appropriate to 
require persons designated as Adjudicating Officers to possess a legal background or undergo 
suitable legal training. 
 
11.3 When the matter was taken up with the Ministry, it stated in its written reply as under:— 
 
“An Adjudicating Officer can be designated only from amongst the Chairperson, whole-time Members 
or any other officer not below such rank of a Division Chief as may be specified by regulations. Any 
further requirement relating to legal training may be prescribed in such regulations. 
 
Further, the Code already provides adequate safeguards to ensure informed adjudication. Clauses 
17(2) and 17(3) provide for separation of functions. The requirement of providing an opportunity of 
being heard is embedded in the Code so as to ensure adherence to the principles of natural justice. 
Independent oversight by the Board has also been retained by virtue of Clauses 21 and 22, which 
empower it to call for records and rectification of AO order.” 
 
11.4 On the question of the rationale for providing under Clause 17 that adjudication shall ordinarily 
be preceded by an inspection or investigation, thereby departing from the existing framework under 
which adjudication may be initiated with or without a prior inspection or investigation, the Committee 
sought the views of the Ministry. 
 
In response, the Ministry stated as under:— 

69 
 
 
“Clause 17 concerns the power of the Board to designate an Adjudicating Officer. It sets out the 
manner in which the Board may designate an Adjudicating Officer after considering the findings of an 
inspection or an investigation report under Clause 12. 
 
We are streamlining the process so that adjudication will necessarily follow a fact -finding inspection 
or investigation, which is not the position at present. Presently, these processes may proceed in 
parallel and adjudication may take place with or without a prior inspection. We are now streamlining 
the framework into a structured system.” 
 
11.5 During the course of deliberations, the Committee sought to know whether the Adjudicating 
Officer would be of the same rank as, or senior to, the Investigating Officer. 
 
It was observed that while the Code provides that an Adjudicating Officer shall not be below the rank 
of a Division Chief, no corresponding provision has been made regarding the rank of the Investigating 
Officer. 
 
In response thereto, the Secretary, Ministry of Finance, while deposing before the Committee, stated 
as under:— 
 
“Yes, Sir, probably, because we work in a hierarchical system. Therefore, while framing the 
procedure, we can incorporate such a requirement.” 
 
11.6 The Committee also considered the possibility of Investigating Officers and Adjudicating 
Officers being drawn from a common pool of officers, which may affect the perceived independence 
of the adjudicatory process.
