In this regard, it was suggested that the Board should designate Investigating Officers and 
Adjudicating Officers for a specified period, such as a calendar year or a fixed tenure, in a manner 
analogous to the roster system followed by courts. 
 
In response thereto, the Secretary, Ministry of Finance, while deposing before the Committee, stated 
as under:— 
 

70 
 
“These two clauses are intended to hardwire the separation. The further details regarding how the 
arrangement would operate during a period of twelve months may appropriately be prescribed 
through regulations. Clauses 17(2)(b) and 17(3) already incorporate safeguards at both ends.” 
 
11.7 The Committee have examined Clause 17, which seeks to institutionalise a clear 
separation between the investigative and adjudicatory functions by providing that 
adjudication shall ordinarily follow a fact -finding inspection or investigation and by 
prescribing safeguards to avoid conflicts of interest in the designation of the Adjudicating 
Officer. 
11.8 The Committee appreciate the effort to introduce a structured and sequential 
adjudicatory framework, which is expected to strengthen procedural fairness and reinforce 
the independence of the adjudication process. The Committee are, however, of the view th at 
certain additional safeguards would further enhance the credibility and transparency of the 
proposed framework. 
11.9 The Committee, therefore, recommend that the regulations to be framed under the 
Code should, inter alia, prescribe an appropriate framework governing the designation, tenure 
and allocation of functions of Investigating Officers and Adjudicating Officers so as to ensure 
institutional independence and transparency in the adjudicatory process. 
 
The Committee further recommend that the hierarchical relationship between the 
Investigating Officer and the Adjudicating Officer be appropriately specified to ensure that the 
Adjudicating Officer is not subordinate to the Investigating Officer in the same matter. 
 
The Committee also recommend that suitable provisions be made, at the implementation 
stage, to ensure that persons designated as Adjudicating Officers possess adequate legal 
expertise or undergo appropriate legal training, having regard to the quasi -judicial functions 
and enhanced powers proposed to be exercised by them under the Code.  

71 
 
CHAPTER XII 
 
XII Factors for Adjudication 
 
Clause 19 
 
12.1 Clause 19 provides for the factors to be considered for adjudication, as under:— 
 
19  The Adjudicating Officer shall have due regard to the following factors before making any 
order under section 20, namely:— 
 
(a) the order shall be proportionate to the default or contravention committed by the noticee and act 
as a deterrent against the commission of such default or contravention by the noticee or any other 
person in future; 
 
(b) the nature, seriousness and impact of the default or contravention, including— 
 
(i) whether the contravention was deliberate, reckless or negligent; 
 
(ii) the duration, frequency or repetitive nature of the default or contravention; 
 
(iii) the unlawful gains made or likely to be made as a result of the default or contravention; 
 
(iv) the loss caused or likely to be caused to an investor or group of investors as a result of the default 
or contravention; or 
 
(v) the impact of the default or contravention on the integrity of the securities markets; 
 
(c) the conduct of the noticee after the default or contravention, including— 
 
(i) bringing the default or contravention to the notice of the Board within a reasonable time; 

72 
 
 
(ii) the remedial or preventive measures taken in respect of the default or contravention; 
 
(iii) the extent of cooperation with the Board during the inspection, investigation or adjudication; 
 
(d) the past conduct of the noticee or whether the noticee fulfils the fit and proper criteria to render its 
services in the securities markets; 
 
(e) action taken in previous similar cases by any Adjudicating Officer; and 
 
(f) any other factor which the Adjudicating Officer may consider necessary.” 
 
 
12.2 While considering the suggestions received from certain stakeholders, the Committee sought 
the views of the Ministry on the proposal that sanctions should be based strictly on the quantifiable 
extent of harm caused to specific persons and that this method ology should be codified. In the 
alternative, it was suggested that binding guidelines be issued prescribing specific aggravating and 
mitigating factors, drawing upon the approach adopted under the SEBI (Settlement Proceedings) 
Regulations, 2018. 
 
In response thereto, the Ministry stated as under:— 
 
“The concern is valid and appropriate drafting changes will be made to Clause 19 to provide an 
enabling power to the Board to specify the methodology for quantification of unlawful gains by the 
Adjudicating Officer (to be specified through regulations). 
 
It is further noted that the Adjudicating Officer is already required to consider the unlawful gains made 
or losses caused before taking any enforcement action. Clause 13 already empowers SEBI to make 
regulations prescribing the criteria and manner for quantification of such unlawful gains or losses by 
IO. 
 
A further enabling provision may be incorporated under Clause 19 in the manner proposed above.” 
 

73 
 
12.3 The Committee have examined Clause 19, which lays down the factors to be considered 
by the Adjudicating Officer while determining the appropriate enforcement action under the 
Code. The Committee note that the provision seeks to promote proportionality, co nsistency 
and fairness in the adjudicatory process by requiring due regard to factors such as the nature 
and gravity of the contravention, unlawful gains, investor losses, market impact and the 
conduct of the noticee. 
12.4 The Committee are of the view that greater certainty and uniformity in adjudication 
would be achieved if the methodology for quantification of unlawful gains and losses is 
supported by a clear statutory framework. The Committee further consider that objec tive 
guidance on aggravating and mitigating factors would facilitate greater consistency and 
transparency in the exercise of adjudicatory discretion. 
12.5 The Committee, therefore, recommend that Clause 19 be suitably amended to 
expressly empower the Board to prescribe, through regulations, the methodology and criteria 
for quantification of unlawful gains and losses for the purposes of adjudication. 
 
The Committee further recommend that the Board frame comprehensive guidelines setting 
out the relevant aggravating and mitigating factors to be considered by the Adjudicating 
Officer while determining the appropriate enforcement action so as to ensure proportionality, 
consistency and predictability in adjudication. 
  

74 
 
CHAPTER XIII 
 
XIII Final Order by the Adjudicating Officer 
 
Clause 20 
 
13.1 Clause 20 provides for the final order to be passed by the Adjudicating Officer, as under:— 
 
“20. The Adjudicating Officer shall, on receipt of the reply to the show cause notice and after 
considering all the relevant material on record, by an order, either dispose of the show cause notice 
if no cause exists or, if sufficient cause exists, take one or more of the following actions, namely:— 
 
(a) suspend or cancel the certificate of registration granted under this Code; 
 
(b) issue appropriate directions or warnings under section 23; 
 
(c) pass an order under section 24; 
 
(d) direct disgorgement under section 25; or 
 
(e) impose a penalty under sections 97 to 109: 
 
Provided that no order shall be passed unless the person concerned has been given an opportunity 
of being heard in the matter.” 
 
13.2 Certain stakeholders suggested that the expression “dismiss the show cause notice” be 
replaced with the expression “dispose of the show cause notice”. It was submitted that the latter 
expression is more appropriate from the perspective of legislative drafting, as a show cause notice is 
ordinarily disposed of after consideration of the reply and the material on record. 
13.3 On the issue raised regarding the proposed drafting change in Clause 20 to replace the 
expression “dismiss the show cause notice” with “dispose of the show cause notice”, the Ministry, in 
its written reply, acknowledged the concern and stated as under:— 
 

75 
 
“The concern is valid and appropriate drafting changes will be made.” 
 
13.4 The Committee have examined Clause 20 relating to the final order to be passed by the 
Adjudicating Officer. The Committee are of the view that legislative drafting should employ 
precise and legally appropriate terminology. 
 
The Committee note that the expression “dismiss the show cause notice” is not the 
conventional formulation employed in adjudicatory proceedings, as a show cause notice is 
ordinarily disposed of after consideration of the reply and the material on record. 
 
The Committee, therefore, recommend that Clause 20 be suitably amended to replace the 
expression “dismiss the show cause notice” with “dispose of the show cause notice” so as 
to ensure greater drafting precision, consistency and legal clarity. 
  

76 
 
CHAPTER XIV 
 
XIV Board to Call for Records 
 
Clause 21 
 
14.1 Clause 21 provides for the power of the Board to call for records, as under:— 
 
21 (1) The Board may call for and examine the record of any proceedings under this section within a 
period of thirty days from the date of the order passed by the Adjudicating Officer under section 20 
and, if it considers that such order is not in the interest of the securities markets, it may, after making 
or causing to be made such inquiry as it deems necessary, pass an order enhancing the quantum of 
penalty or the severity of any direction, if the circumstances of the case so justify: 
 
Provided that no such order shall be passed unless the person concerned has been given an 
opportunity of being heard in the matter: 
 
Provided further that the Board shall not call for the records and pass an order where an appeal has 
been filed and disposed of under section 85. 
 
(2) The Board shall pass an order within a period of four months from the date of calling for the record 
of any proceedings under this section. 
 
(3) The provisions of sub -sections (2) and (3) of section 17 shall, mutatis mutandis, apply to sub -
sections (1) and (2).” 
 
14.2 On the question of the rationale for conferring upon the Board the power under Clause 21 to 
review and enhance the penalty or directions imposed by the Adjudicating Officer, particularly where 
such order is considered to be inadequate or otherwise not in the interest of the securities markets, 
the Secretary, Ministry of Finance, stated as under:— 
 
“Just like the previous provision, we are introducing that eight -year condition for the first time. 
Therefore, we had provided a carve-out. We are saying that if somebody is aggrieved by an order of 
an Adjudicating Officer, he may file an appeal before the  Securities Appellate Tribunal. At the same 
time, we are conferring a specific power upon the Board that, within a predefined period, it may call 

77 
 
for the records from the date of the order passed by the Adjudicating Officer if it considers that such 
order is not in the interest of the securities markets and, after making or causing to be made such 
inquiry as it deems fit, pass an order enhancing the quantum of penalty or the severity of the direction.” 
 
The Ministry further submitted as under:— 
 
“Currently, if an Adjudicating Officer passes an order imposing a very high penalty or severe 
directions, it can reasonably be assumed that the aggrieved person would file an appeal if he 
considers the penalty excessive. However, if the penalty imposed is very low, the person proceeded 
against would ordinarily have no reason to file an appeal because the order operates in his favour. 
 
At present, SEBI has no power to appeal such an order passed by its own Adjudicating Officer. Clause 
21, therefore, enables the Board, within a period of thirty days, to call for the records only in those 
cases where the order of the Adjudicating Officer is considered not to be in the interest of the 
securities markets. The Board may call for the records, conduct such inquiry as may be necessary, 
and its power is confined to enhancing the penalty or the severity of the directions. Such a power is 
presently absent under the existing framework.” 
 
14.3 Certain stakeholders expressed concern regarding the one -sided nature of the proposed 
revisionary power, which permits the Board only to enhance the quantum of penalty or the severity 
of directions and does not empower it to reduce or otherwise modify an order where the penalty 
imposed may be excessive or unjustified. 
 
It was further submitted that such a provision may not withstand constitutional scrutiny and that the 
Board should have the power to interfere on the merits of the case in either direction. 
 
The Committee also examined the second proviso to Clause 21(1), which provides that the Board 
shall not call for the records and pass an order where an appeal has been filed and disposed of under 
Clause 85. Concern was expressed that the use of the words “filed and disposed of” may imply that 
the Board could still exercise its revisionary powers where an appeal has already been filed but is 
pending before the appellate forum. 
 
It was also suggested that the expression “enhancing” occurring in Clause 21(1) should be replaced 
by the words “on merits”. 
 
14.4 On being asked regarding the suggestion that the Board should also have the power to reduce 
a penalty and that the word “enhancing” in Clause 21(1) be replaced by the words “on merits”, the 
Ministry, in its written reply, stated as under:— 

78 
 
 
Current Position 
 
This provision is based on section 15 -I(3) of the SEBI Act, 1992, section 23 -I(3) of the Securities 
Contracts (Regulation) Act, 1956 and section 19H(3) of the Depositories Act, 1996. Under these 
enactments, the provision empowers the Board (or its delegate ) to revise the orders of the 
Adjudicating Officer and enhance the quantum of penalty where such order is considered not to be 
in the interest of the securities markets. 
 
SMC Position 
 
Relevance of Clause 21 
 
The existing provisions have been continued under the SMC. However, since the SMC enables 
directions to be issued through a common order under Clause 20, the provision has been modified 
to enable the Board (or its delegate) to enhance the severity of the d irections issued by the 
Adjudicating Officer. 
 
These powers are revisionary in nature. If the Board were also empowered to reduce the penalty, it 
would effectively confer appellate jurisdiction upon SEBI. This would create parallel appellate 
proceedings before the Board and the Securities Appellate Tribunal, which may not be appropriate. 
 
SEBI has also submitted that, if the Board is empowered to review the order of the Adjudicating 
Officer or reduce the penalty imposed by the Adjudicating Officer, appeals which would otherwise lie 
before the Securities Appellate Tribunal may instead be required to be considered by the Board. This 
may create practical and administrative difficulties and may effectively render the role of the Securities 
Appellate Tribunal redundant. 
 
The existing provisions have therefore been continued under the SMC, although their ambit has been 
broadened. Under the SEBI Act, 1992, the Board may issue directions under section 11B, whereas 
the Adjudicating Officer is empowered only to impose monetary penalties. Accordingly, section 15-
I(3) empowers the Board to revise the orders of the Adjudicating Officer and enhance the quantum 
of penalty where such orders are not in the interest of the securities markets. 
 
Under the SMC, the adjudicatory framework has been unified to enable the Adjudicating Officer to 
pass a consolidated order comprising directions, cease and desist orders, disgorgement orders and 

79 
 
monetary penalties. Consequently, Clause 21 has been modified to enable the Board to enhance the 
severity of the directions issued by the Adjudicating Officer. 
 
If the provision is amended to enable the Board also to reduce the penalty, it would effectively confer 
appellate jurisdiction upon SEBI, resulting in parallel appellate proceedings before the Board and the 
Securities Appellate Tribunal. Such an arrangement may also give rise to forum shopping. 
 
Further, a two -tier appellate mechanism may enable parties to prolong litigation and abuse the 
process, thereby defeating the objectives of the Code. 
 
Global Jurisprudence 
 
Comparable international practice cannot readily be adopted because of structural differences. For 
instance, in the United States, the initial orders are passed by Administrative Law Judges, who are 
functionally independent of the Commission. Their decisions are recommendatory in nature and attain 
finality only upon the issuance of an order by the Securities and Exchange Commission declaring the 
decision to have become final. Accordingly, the Commission has been conferred broader powers to 
affirm, reverse, modify, set aside or remand such proceedings. Further, there is no direct appeal 
against the orders of the Administrative Law Judge before any court, whereas the orders of the 
Commission are appealable before the Court of Appeals. 
 
Under the SMC, however, a different structure has been envisaged. The Adjudicating Officer is an 
officer or Member of SEBI and the order passed by the Adjudicating Officer is final without requiring 
confirmation by the Board. Such orders are directly appea lable before the Securities Appellate 
Tribunal, which may independently adjudicate upon the issues and grant appropriate relief. 
Accordingly, the Board’s powers under Clause 21 have been confined to enhancing the penalty or 
directions imposed by the Adjudi cating Officer, while the adjudicatory function itself is exercised 
exclusively by the Adjudicating Officer.” 
 
14.5 On the question of whether permitting the Securities Appellate Tribunal to review the decisions 
of the Adjudicating Officer reduces litigation, or whether permitting SEBI also to review such decisions 
would instead increase litigation, the Committee sough t information from SEBI regarding the extent 
to which the analogous provisions under the existing securities laws had been invoked. 
 
In its written reply, the Ministry stated as under:— 
 

80 
 
“The power to revise the orders of the Adjudicating Officer is based upon the existing provisions 
contained in section 15 -I(3) of the SEBI Act, 1992, section 23 -I(3) of the Securities Contracts 
(Regulation) Act, 1956 and section 19H(3) of the Depositories Act, 1996. 
 
Under these enactments, the Board is empowered to revise the orders of the Adjudicating Officer and 
enhance the quantum of penalty where such order is considered not to be in the interest of the 
securities markets. The scope of the Board’s power under Clause 21 has been broadened to enable 
it also to enhance the severity of the directions issued by the Adjudicating Officer, since the SMC 
unifies the adjudication process and enables the Adjudicating Officer to pass a consolidated order 
comprising directions, cease and desist orders, disgorgement orders and monetary penalties. 
 
Moreover, the existing provision bars the calling for records only where the appeal has been disposed 
of and not where it is merely pending. The same position has been retained under the SMC. 
 
Further, under the existing framework, the Securities Appellate Tribunal already possesses appellate 
jurisdiction over both the orders of the Adjudicating Officer and the Board. Consequently, it cannot be 
conclusively stated whether the proposed provisions would either reduce or increase litigation. 
 
The number of cases pending before the Securities Appellate Tribunal as on 9 July 2026 is enclosed 
in the Annexure. 
 
SEBI has further submitted that the existing revisionary power has been exercised only in seven 
scrips.” 
 
14.6 The Committee have examined Clause 21, which substantially reproduces the existing 
provisions contained in the SEBI Act, 1992, the Securities Contracts (Regulation) Act, 1956 
and the Depositories Act, 1996 empowering the Board to call for and examine the records of 
proceedings before an Adjudicating Officer and enhance the penalty where the order is 
considered not to be in the interest of the securities markets. 
 
The Committee further note that the proposed Code introduces procedural safeguards by 
prescribing specific timelines within which such revisionary powers are to be exercised. 
 
14.7 The Committee are, however, of the considered view that the proposed revisionary 
power is one-sided in as much as it enables the Board only to enhance the penalty imposed 
by the Adjudicating Officer without conferring a corresponding power to reduce the penalty 
in appropriate cases. 

81 
 
 
The Committee feel that such an asymmetrical revisionary mechanism may not be fully 
consistent with the principles of fairness and natural justice. The Committee also observe that 
requiring every aggrieved person to approach the Securities Appellate Tribunal for reduction 
of penalty may result in avoidable litigation, increased costs and delays, particularly in view 
of the pendency of cases before the Tribunal. 
 
14.8 The Committee, therefore, recommend that Clause 21 be suitably amended to reflect 
the revisionary nature of the power conferred upon the Board. 
 
The Committee further recommend that the Board be empowered, on merits and after 
recording reasons in writing, not only to enhance but also to reduce or otherwise modify the 
penalty or directions passed by the Adjudicating Officer wherever the facts and circumstances 
of the case so justify. 
 
The Committee also recommend that the second proviso to Clause 21(1) be suitably clarified 
to provide that the Board shall not call for or examine the records once an appeal has been 
filed before the Securities Appellate Tribunal, irrespective of whether s uch appeal has been 
disposed of. 
 
This would ensure procedural fairness, avoid any overlap between the revisionary jurisdiction 
of the Board and the appellate jurisdiction of the Securities Appellate Tribunal, and maintain 
institutional clarity in the appellate framework. 
  

82 
 
CHAPTER XV 
 
XV Power of the Adjudicating Officer or the Board to Rectify an Order 
 
Clause 22 
 
15.1 Clause 22 provides for the power of the Adjudicating Officer or the Board to rectify an order, 
as under:— 
 
22  The Adjudicating Officer or the Board may, on his or its own motion or on a request made by 
any aggrieved party, modify an order passed under section 20 or section 21, as the case may be, to 
rectify any error apparent on the face of the record, within a period of thirty days from the date of such 
order: 
 
Provided that, while rectifying the error, the Adjudicating Officer or the Board shall not modify the 
substantive part of the order.” 
 
15.2 The Committee sought to understand the rationale for distinguishing between the substantive 
and non-substantive parts of an order, particularly when an error apparent on the face of the record 
may itself be substantive in nature. 
 
In response thereto, the Ministry stated as under:— 
 
“The intention was only to limit the provision to apparent errors because, if there is any substantive 
difficulty with the order, it should ideally be tested before the appellate tribunal. That was the 
underlying thought process. 
 
The idea was that the provision should not become a means of reopening the entire case. 
 
It is only rectification. Perhaps a name has been incorrectly recorded, or an amount contains an error 
in decimal placement. The provision is intended to address such situations. 
 

83 
 
The intention is only rectification and not review. That is why the power has been given to the 
Adjudicating Officer or the Board to correct an order without, in effect, rehearing the matter. If a party 
seeks reconsideration of the substantive issues, the appropriate course would be to file an appeal. 
 
They have confined the provision only to that aspect, Sir. 
 
That is what is intended. Only such errors can be rectified. For the remaining issues, there will have 
to be a fresh hearing before a higher forum. 
 
This provision is not titled as a review. It is not intended to confer a power of review. Such a power 
does not exist with the Adjudicating Officer.” 
 
15.3 The Committee also deliberated upon the distinction between rectification and review and 
observed that the clause incorporates only the ground of “error apparent on the face of the record” 
and does not include the ground of discovery of a new fact, as rec ognised under the Code of Civil 
Procedure. 
 
The Committee noted that the present provision is intended only to enable rectification of apparent 
errors and not to permit a rehearing or de novo consideration of the matter. 
 
15.4 The Committee, however, reiterated that once the power to rectify an error apparent on the 
face of the record is conferred, it may not be appropriate to restrict such rectification merely to clerical 
or non-substantive errors, particularly where the error itself is substantive in nature. 
 
Accordingly, it was suggested that the proviso prohibiting modification of the substantive part of the 
order be reconsidered. 
 
15.5 The Committee have examined Clause 22, which empowers the Adjudicating Officer or 
the Board to rectify any error apparent on the face of the record within a period of thirty days 
while prohibiting modification of the substantive part of the order. The Committe e note that 
the provision is intended to confer only a limited power of rectification and not a power of 
review or rehearing. 
15.6 The Committee are, however, of the view that while the rectification jurisdiction should 
remain confined to errors apparent on the face of the record, the nature of the error, rather 
than whether it affects a substantive or non -substantive part of the ord er, should determine 
whether rectification is permissible. 

84 
 
 
The Committee are of the considered view that an error apparent on the face of the record 
may, in appropriate cases, affect the substantive part of an order, and that the existing proviso 
may unnecessarily restrict the effective exercise of the power of rectification. 
 
15.7 The Committee, therefore, recommend that Clause 22 be suitably amended to clarify 
that the power of rectification extends to correcting any error apparent on the face of the 
record, including an error affecting the substantive part of the order, provided that such 
rectification does not amount to a review, rehearing or reconsideration of the merits of the 
case. 
 
The Committee further recommend that the proviso restricting modification of the substantive 
part of the order be suitably reconsidered so as to avoid defeating the very purpose of the 
rectification jurisdiction. 
  

85 
 
CHAPTER XVI 
 
XVI Power of the Adjudicating Officer to Issue Directions or Warnings 
 
Clause 23 
 
16.1 Clause 23 provides for the power of the Adjudicating Officer to issue directions or warnings, 
as under:— 
 
23 (1) Where the Adjudicating Officer is satisfied that it is necessary— 
 
(a) in the interest of investors or the orderly development of the securities markets; 
 
(b) to prevent the affairs of any securities markets service provider or market participant from being 
conducted in a manner detrimental to the interests of investors or the securities markets; or 
 
(c) to secure the proper management of the persons referred to in clause (b), 
 
he may issue such directions or warnings to any securities markets service provider, market 
participant or any other person associated with the securities markets, as he may deem fit. 
 
(2) The directions or warnings referred to in sub-section (1) may include one or more of the following 
actions, namely:— 
 
(a) suspension of trading of any securities on a stock exchange, subject to the orderly close-out; 
 
(b) suspension of clearing of any contracts by any clearing corporation; 
 
(c) cancelling or annulling any trades, or execution of any contract on any stock exchange, in whole 
or in part, and reversing any payments made in respect thereof; 
 

86 
 
(d) restraining persons from accessing the securities markets and prohibiting any person associated 
with the securities markets from buying, selling or otherwise dealing in securities for such period as 
may be specified in the order; 
 
(e) suspending any office-bearer of any market infrastructure institution, self-regulatory organisation 
or securities markets service provider from holding such position for such period and in such entities 
or class of entities as may be specified in the order; 
 
(f) directing a person holding securities or rights over any securities, or voting or management rights 
in an issuer, to divest such holdings or rights in such manner as may be specified in the order; 
 
(g) directing any securities markets service provider, market infrastructure institution or self-regulatory 
organisation to divest securities or rights held by it in other entities in contravention of the provisions 
of this Code or the rules or regulations  made thereunder, and specifying the manner of such 
divestment; 
 
(h) directing the transfer of any proceeds or securities to a designated account; 
 
(i) impounding and retaining the proceeds or securities in respect of any transaction under 
adjudication; 
 
(j) pending adjudication, attaching the bank accounts or other property of any person involved in a 
contravention of any provision of this Code or the rules or regulations made thereunder for a period 
not exceeding ninety days: 
 
Provided that the Board shall, within the said period of attachment, obtain confirmation from the 
Special Court having jurisdiction and, upon such confirmation, the attachment shall continue, and 
upon conclusion of the proceedings, the provisions of section 136 shall apply: 
 
Provided further that only such property, bank account or accounts, or transactions entered therein, 
as relate to the proceeds actually involved in the contravention of the provisions of this Code or the 
rules or regulations made thereunder, shall be liable to attachment; or 
 
(k) pending adjudication, directing any person not to dispose of or alienate any asset forming part of 
a transaction which is under adjudication: 
 

87 
 
Provided that the Adjudicating Officer may take any of the actions specified in clauses (i), (j) or (k) in 
respect of any issuer, not being an intermediary, where he has reasonable grounds to believe that 
such issuer has indulged in fraudulent and unfair practices or market abuse referred to in section 92 
or section 93, as the case may be.” 
 
16.2 During the course of examination of the clause, certain stakeholders suggested a drafting 
change by substituting the word “namely” with the word “include” in sub -section (2), so that the 
provision is not construed as limiting the nature of the directions that may be issued by the 
Adjudicating Officer. 
16.3 On being asked to explain the proposed change, the Ministry stated as under:— 
 
“The change was only from ‘namely’ to ‘include’, because the word ‘namely’ may limit the nature of 
directions that can be issued. We have, therefore, accepted that drafting change. At present, the 
provision reads, ‘the directions may include one or more of the following actions, namely…’.” 
 
16.4 Certain stakeholders also pointed out that clauses 23(2)(a) and 23(2)(b) empower the 
Adjudicating Officer to direct suspension of trading in any security on a stock exchange or suspension 
of clearing of any contract by a clearing corporation. However, the  provision does not require the 
order to specify the period for which such suspension would remain in force. 
 
It was submitted that the absence of such a requirement may result in an indeterminate restriction on 
trading or clearing, which could be disproportionate to the nature of the violation. Requiring the 
Adjudicating Officer to specify the duration of such di rections would ensure that they remain 
proportionate, time-bound and do not operate as a de facto permanent suspension. It was further 
pointed out that a similar requirement already exists in clauses 23(2)(d) and 23(2)(e). 
 
16.5 Regarding the suggestion that the Adjudicating Officer should be required to specify the 
duration of such directions, the Ministry, in its written reply, stated as under:— 
 
“The concern is valid. In order to align the provision with clauses 23(2)(d) and 23(2)(e), appropriate 
drafting changes will be made.” 
 
16.6 On the question of whether a statutory requirement to record reasons in writing should be 
incorporated while issuing directions under Clause 23, particularly under clauses 23(2)(i), (j) and (k), 
the Ministry stated as under:— 
 

88 
 
“This flows from Clause 20, which sets out the powers of the Adjudicating Officer. Clause 20 provides 
that the Adjudicating Officer shall, upon receipt of the reply to the show cause notice and after 
considering all relevant material, pass an order taking one or more of the specified actions, including 
issuing directions under Clause 23. 
 
Accordingly, any direction issued under Clause 23 forms part of the adjudication order itself. Such an 
order is necessarily expected to be a reasoned order and, therefore, it will already be in writing.” 
 
16.7 On the question of whether Clause 23 contains both interim and final directions and whether 
the requirement of recording reasons in writing should be expressly incorporated, the Ministry further 
explained the scheme of Clauses 20 and 23 as under:— 
 
“If we read Clause 20(b), which relates to the final order, it provides that the Adjudicating Officer shall, 
by an order, either dispose of the show cause notice if no cause exists or, if sufficient cause exists, 
take one or more of the specified actions. One such action is the issuance of appropriate directions 
or warnings under Clause 23. 
 
Thus, Clause 23 merely sets out the nature of the directions or warnings that may be included in the 
final adjudication order. The Adjudicating Officer may issue one or more such directions together with 
any other action, such as cancellation of registration or imposition of penalty. 
 
The change introduced under the Code is that, whereas at present Adjudicating Officers may pass 
separate orders at different stages, the proposed framework consolidates all consequential actions 
into one common adjudication order. If there is reason to believe that a contravention has occurred, 
the Adjudicating Officer will issue one consolidated order containing one or more of the available 
powers, supported by reasons.” 
 
16.8 On the question of whether an express requirement to record reasons in writing should be 
incorporated in Clause 23, particularly in respect of powers such as attaching bank accounts or other 
property pending adjudication or directing a person not to dispose of or alienate assets, having regard 
to the interim nature of such directions and the need for adequate procedural safeguards, the 
Committee sought the views of the Ministry. 
 
In response thereto, the Ministry stated:— 
 
“We can do that, Sir.” 
 

89 
 
16.9 The Committee have examined Clause 23, which empowers the Adjudicating Officer to 
issue directions or warnings in the interests of investors and the orderly development of the 
securities markets. The Committee note that the provision confers wide -ranging powers, 
including the power to suspend trading or clearing, restrain access to the securities markets, 
impound proceeds, attach bank accounts or property and issue other consequential 
directions. 
16.10 The Committee note the concerns expressed regarding the drafting of the provision, 
particularly that the use of the word “namely” in Clause 23(2) may be construed as limiting 
the scope of the directions that may be issued by the Adjudicating Officer. The Committee 
also note the absence of an express requirement to specify the duration of directions relating 
to suspension of trading or clearing under clauses 23(2)(a) and 23(2)(b), which may result in 
uncertainty and disproportionate restrictions. 
16.11 The Committee further note the Ministry’s clarification that directions under Clause 23 
are intended to form part of the final adjudication order under Clause 20 and are, therefore, 
expected to be reasoned orders. The Committee are, however, of the view t hat where the 
provision specifically empowers the Adjudicating Officer to issue directions having immediate 
and significant consequences, particularly interim measures such as attachment of bank 
accounts or property, impounding of proceeds or securities and restraint on the alienation of 
assets pending adjudication, the requirement to record reasons in writing should be expressly 
incorporated in the Code itself. 
16.12 The Committee, therefore, recommend that Clause 23 be suitably amended to 
expressly require the Adjudicating Officer to record reasons in writing while issuing directions 
under the clause, particularly directions of an interim nature. 
 
The Committee further recommend that Clause 23(2) be suitably amended by replacing the 
word “namely” with the word “include” so as to clarify that the list of directions is illustrative 
and not exhaustive. 
 
The Committee also recommend that clauses 23(2)(a) and 23(2)(b) be amended to expressly 
require the Adjudicating Officer to specify the period for which any direction suspending 
trading or clearing shall remain in force, thereby ensuring that such directio ns are 
proportionate, time-bound and consistent with the scheme of the Code. 
  

90 
 
CHAPTER XVII 
 
XVII Settlement of Administrative and Civil Proceedings 
 
Clause 26 
 
17.1 Clause 26 provides for the settlement of administrative and civil proceedings, as under:— 
 
26 (1) Notwithstanding anything contained in any other law for the time being in force, any person— 
 
(a) who seeks to voluntarily disclose any true and vital information which may amount to a 
contravention of the provisions of this Code or the rules or regulations made thereunder; or 
 
(b) against whom any proceedings may be initiated or have been initiated and are pending before the 
Board or any court or Tribunal, may file an application in writing to the Board proposing settlement of 
the proceedings initiated or to be initiated, subject to such conditions and payment of such fee, within 
such time and in such manner, as may be specified by regulations. 
 
(2) The Board may constitute a committee, comprising such members and on such terms and 
conditions, for advising it on the settlement of proceedings in such manner as may be specified by 
regulations. 
 
(3) The Board may, after taking into consideration the nature, gravity and impact of the default or 
contravention, the advice of the committee constituted for the purpose and such other factors as may 
be specified by regulations, by an order, consider the proposal for settlement, on payment of such 
amount by the applicant and on such terms and conditions as may be determined by the Board: 
 
Provided that where the proposal for settlement is considered by the Board during the pendency of 
proceedings before a court or Tribunal, it shall place such proposal before such court or Tribunal for 
appropriate orders. 
 
(4) Where an application under sub-section (1) is filed after the designation of the Adjudicating Officer, 
the adjudication proceedings shall continue, but no order under section 20 shall be passed until the 
settlement application is decided by the Board, and where the proposal for settlement is— 
 

91 
 
(a) accepted by the Board, the Adjudicating Officer shall, by an order, dismiss the show cause notice 
in respect of the applicant; or 
 
(b) rejected by the Board, the Adjudicating Officer shall proceed to complete the adjudication and 
pass an order under section 20. 
 
(5) Where the applicant fails to comply with the terms and conditions referred to in sub -section (3), 
the proceedings shall be initiated or continued before the Adjudicating Officer, the Tribunal or the 
court, as the case may be. 
 
(6) The settlement proceedings shall be conducted in such manner as may be specified by 
regulations. 
 
(7) No appeal shall lie to the Tribunal under section 85 against any order passed under this section. 
 
(8) All settlement amounts, excluding the disgorgement amount and legal costs, realised under this 
Code shall be credited to the Consolidated Fund of India in such manner as may be prescribed.” 
 
17.2 On the question of the scope and intent of Clause 26, the Ministry explained that the provision 
is intended to facilitate the settlement of administrative and civil proceedings at any stage of the 
enforcement process. In this regard, the Ministry stated as under:— 
 
“The title of Clause 26 itself refers to the ‘settlement of administrative and civil proceedings’. This has 
been incorporated following stakeholder consultations. The settlement mechanism has been 
expanded because it may operate at any stage of the enforcement process. It provides an additional 
option to participants in the securities market who discover that they have committed a contravention 
or become aware of a violation to make a voluntary disclosure and seek settlement. Such a 
mechanism does not presently exist. It is, therefore, intended as an additional facility in furtherance 
of an investor-friendly regulatory framework. 
 
I would also like to mention that, just as the existing settlement framework does not require an 
applicant to admit guilt, the proposed provision also does not require any admission, since it forms 
part of the settlement mechanism itself. The intention is that it should apply only to administrative and 
civil proceedings.” 
 

92 
 
With regard to the suggestion that the substantive provision should expressly reflect the phraseology 
of the heading, the Ministry, in its written reply, clarified as under:— 
 
“Clause 26(1)(b) refers to proceedings that may be initiated or have already been initiated and are 
pending against a person. The proceedings contemplated under this provision are administrative and 
civil proceedings. However, for abundant clarity, an appropriate drafting change may be carried out.” 
 
17.3 On being asked whether the timelines and procedures governing settlement of administrative 
and civil proceedings should be expressly incorporated in the Code instead of being left to regulations, 
and whether the expression “appropriate orders” in relation  to proceedings before a court or the 
Securities Appellate Tribunal required reconsideration, the Ministry stated as under:— 
 
“In relation to timelines and procedure, we examined the SEBI (Settlement Proceedings) Regulations, 
2018 in detail. We found that those Regulations already prescribe a comprehensive procedure, which 
is well understood in the market. In practice, the settlement process requires consideration by multiple 
committees. There is a High Powered Advisory Committee headed by a former Judge and there is 
also an internal committee, which may seek additional information or require the personal appearance 
of the applicant. A settlement is arrived at only after such consideration. 
 
The existing Regulations also prescribe timelines at different stages, including that an applicant may 
apply for settlement within sixty days from the service of the show cause notice; a revised application 
may be submitted within fifteen days; revised set tlement terms may also be submitted within fifteen 
days; and a notice of demand is issued within seven days of acceptance by the High Powered 
Advisory Committee. 
 
The applicable timelines may vary depending upon the facts and circumstances of each case. 
Accordingly, our submission is that hard-coding the procedure and timelines in the statute may neither 
be feasible nor desirable and may, in fact, prove restrictive.  Leaving these matters to regulations, 
particularly when regulations are preceded by public consultation, would provide the necessary 
flexibility and enable SEBI to take into account stakeholder feedback while refining the settlement 
process. Settlement ma y occur at any stage of the enforcement cycle and, therefore, it would be 
preferable to retain the existing legislative approach.” 
 
17.4 The Committee have examined Clause 26, which provides for the settlement of 
proceedings. The Committee note that the objective of the provision is to facilitate the early 
resolution of administrative and civil proceedings, including through voluntary disc losure, 
thereby promoting regulatory efficiency while preserving investor confidence. 
17.5 The Committee further note that the heading of the clause expressly refers to the 
settlement of administrative and civil proceedings, whereas the text of Clause 26(1)(b) refers 

93 
 
broadly to “any proceedings” without expressly reflecting this limitation. The Committee are 
of the view that the substantive provision should clearly correspond with the heading so as 
to remove any ambiguity regarding its scope and to avoid any interpreta tion that criminal 
proceedings are also capable of being settled under the provision. 
 
17.6 The Committee, therefore, recommend that Clause 26(1)(b) be suitably amended to 
expressly provide that the settlement mechanism shall apply only to administrative and civil 
proceedings, consistent with the heading of the clause and the legislative intent underlying 
the provision. This would provide greater clarity, avoid interpretational uncertainty and ensure 
that the scope of the settlement mechanism is unambiguous. 

94 
 
CHAPTER XVIII 
 
XVIII Power of the Board and the Adjudicating Officer to Pass Interim Orders 
 
Clause 27 
 
18.1 Clause 27 provides for the power of the Board and the Adjudicating Officer to pass interim 
orders, as under:— 
 
27 (1) The Board may, for the purpose of passing an interim order, authorise the Chairperson, a 
whole-time Member or an officer not below the rank of Division Chief, if— 
 
(a) pending an inspection under section 12 or an investigation under section 13; or 
 
(b) upon completion of an inspection under section 12 or an investigation under section 13, but before 
an Adjudicating Officer has been designated under section 17, 
 
it has reasonable grounds to believe that the person being inspected or investigated is taking, or is 
about to take, any action which— 
 
(i) contravenes the provisions of this Code or the rules or regulations made thereunder; 
 
(ii) may prevent the conduct of the inspection or investigation; or 
 
(iii) may adversely affect the conduct of adjudication: 
 
Provided that no person shall be authorised to pass an interim order in any matter if such person is 
conducting or has conducted the inspection or investigation in that matter. 
 
(2) The person authorised under sub-section (1) or, where adjudication proceedings are pending, the 
Adjudicating Officer, as the case may be, may pass an interim order, for reasons to be recorded in 
writing, if he has reasonable grounds to believe that, in the absence of such an order— 

95 
 
 
(i) the interests of investors or the securities markets are likely to be adversely affected; 
 
(ii) the person conducting the inspection or the Investigating Officer, as the case may be, will not be 
able to carry out the pending inspection or investigation; or 
 
(iii) the final order to be passed by the Adjudicating Officer under section 20 may not be capable of 
enforcement owing to a change in circumstances: 
 
Provided that no order shall be passed unless the person concerned has been given an opportunity 
of being heard: 
 
Provided further that, in cases of urgency where the object of granting the interim order is likely to be 
defeated by delay, the requirement of providing an opportunity of being heard before passing the 
order may be dispensed with for reasons to be recorde d in writing, and the person concerned shall 
be given an opportunity of being heard as soon as practicable thereafter. 
 
(3) The interim order shall remain valid for a period not exceeding one hundred and eighty days. 
 
(4) On the expiry of the period specified in sub-section (3), where the inspection or investigation has 
not been completed or, although completed, an Adjudicating Officer has not been designated, or 
where the adjudication proceedings are pending, as the case may be, and an extension of the interim 
order is sought, the Board may designate not more than three persons from amongst its Chairperson 
and whole-time Members who may, if satisfied, extend the interim order in writing. 
 
(5) The provisions of sections 23 and 24 shall, mutatis mutandis, apply to all proceedings relating to 
the passing of an interim order.” 
 
18.2 Certain stakeholders pointed out that while the initial validity of an interim order, including an 
ex parte interim order, is limited to one hundred and eighty days, Clause 27(4), as drafted, permits 
extension of such order for a period of up to two years . It was submitted that such a prolonged 
extension may be excessive and onerous. 
 
The stakeholders, therefore, suggested that extensions should be granted only in blocks of one 
hundred and eighty days, with reasons to be recorded in writing, and should remain co -terminous 
with the period permitted for inspection or investigation. 

96 
 
 
18.3 On being asked whether the maximum permissible extension of an interim order for a period 
of up to two years, as against an initial validity of one hundred and eighty days, was appropriate, the 
Ministry, in its written reply, stated as under:— 
 
“The concern has merit. It may be clarified that every extension shall be valid for a period of one 
hundred and eighty days at a time, subject to an overall maximum period of two years. Appropriate 
drafting changes will be considered.” 
 
The Ministry further proposed the following drafting changes:— 
 
“(4) On the expiry of the period specified in sub-section (3), if the inspection or investigation has not 
been completed or the inspection or investigation is completed but an Adjudicating Officer has not 
been designated or the adjudication is pending, as the case may be, and an extension of the interim 
order is sought, the Board may designate not more than three persons from amongst its Chairperson 
and whole-time Members who may, if satisfied, extend such interim order, for reasons to be recorded 
in writing: 
 
Provided that every extension under this sub-section shall be for a period not exceeding one hundred 
and eighty days at a time. 
 
Provided further that no extension shall be ordered after the expiry of two years from the date on 
which the interim order was first issued under this section.” 
 
18.4 The Committee have examined Clause 27. The Committee note that Clause 27(3) 
provides that an interim order shall remain valid for a period not exceeding one hundred and 
eighty days, whereas Clause 27(4), as originally drafted, permits extension of such in terim 
order for a further period of up to two years. 
 
The Committee observe that permitting an interim order to remain in force for such an 
extended period without requiring periodic reconsideration may result in an interim measure 
continuing for an unduly long duration and may impose an onerous burden upon the person 
concerned. 
 
18.5 The Committee, therefore, recommend that every extension of an interim order under 
Clause 27(4) be limited to a period not exceeding one hundred and eighty days at a time, with 

97 
 
reasons for granting each extension being recorded in writing, subject to an overall maximum 
period of two years from the date on which the interim order was first issued. 
 
The Committee further recommend that the necessary drafting changes be carried out in 
Clause 27(4) to clearly provide for both the maximum duration of each extension and the 
overall outer limit for the continuation of an interim order. 
  

98 
 
CHAPTER XIX 
 
XIX Board to Delegate the Power of Registration 
 
Clause 31 
 
19.1 Clause 31 provides for the delegation of the power of registration by the Board, as under:— 
 
31 (1) The Board may delegate the power of registration of any class or classes of intermediaries or 
investors to a market infrastructure institution or a self-regulatory organisation, by an order in writing, 
subject to such conditions, if any, as may be specified therein. 
 
(2) The provisions of sections 28 and 29 shall, mutatis mutandis, apply to such registration under 
sub-section (1). 
 
(3) The market infrastructure institution or self-regulatory organisation to whom the power has been 
delegated under sub-section (1) shall— 
 
(a) maintain standards of fairness and confidentiality while exercising such delegated powers; and 
 
(b) not refuse to grant a certificate of registration to any person except by an order passed after giving 
such person an opportunity of being heard. 
 
(4) Where the market infrastructure institution or self -regulatory organisation refuses to grant a 
certificate of registration to any person, such person may apprise the Board of such refusal in writing, 
and the Board may issue such directions in the matter as it may deem appropriate.” 
 
19.2 Certain stakeholders suggested that, similar to the immunity afforded to the Board and its 
officers under Clause 139 of the Code, a corresponding safe harbour provision should also be 
extended to Market Infrastructure Institutions while exercising delegat ed regulatory functions under 
Clause 31. 
 
It was submitted that, while the clause delegates statutory functions relating to registration to Market 
Infrastructure Institutions and Self-Regulatory Organisations, it does not extend corresponding legal 

99 
 
protection to such entities. Consequently, they may be exposed to liability for bona fide actions taken 
in the discharge of delegated functions. 
 
The stakeholders pointed out that, for instance, where an intermediary obtains registration through a 
Market Infrastructure Institution by misrepresenting material facts, the Market Infrastructure Institution 
could subsequently be exposed to allegations of failure to exercise due diligence. In contrast, a similar 
consequence would not ordinarily arise in respect of the Board or its officers acting in good faith. It 
was submitted that the absence of an appropriate statutory safeguard may have a chilling effe ct on 
the personnel of Market Infrastructure Institutions while discharging delegated regulatory 
responsibilities. 
 
19.3 Regarding the suggestion that similar protection should be extended to stock exchanges, 
clearing corporations and depositories while exercising delegated powers under the clause, the 
Ministry, in its written reply, stated as under:— 
 
“The concern is accepted and appropriate protection will be extended to stock exchanges, clearing 
corporations and depositories, subject to the necessary safeguards. Appropriate drafting changes will 
be made.” 
 
19.4 On the question of whether Clause 31 should be clarified to expressly provide the manner in 
which the Board may delegate the power of registration, including delegation to a member in 
consultation with the Central Government, the Ministry proposed the following drafting formulation:— 
 
31 (1) The power of registration of any class or classes of intermediaries or investors may be 
delegated— 
 
(a) by the Board to a market infrastructure institution or a self-regulatory organisation; or 
 
(b) by the Board, in consultation with the Central Government, to a member, 
 
by an order in writing, subject to such conditions, if any, as may be specified therein.” 
 
 
19.5 The Committee have examined Clause 31, which empowers the Board to delegate the 
power of registration of any class or classes of intermediaries or investors to a Market 
Infrastructure Institution or a Self -Regulatory Organisation. The Committee further no te that 

100 
 
entities exercising such delegated statutory and regulatory functions are required to take 
decisions in the discharge of powers entrusted to them under the Code. 
19.6 The Committee observe that while Clause 139 provides protection to the Board and its 
officers in respect of acts done or purported to be done in good faith in the discharge of their 
functions under the Code, no corresponding protection is presently availa ble to Market 
Infrastructure Institutions, including stock exchanges, clearing corporations and 
depositories, and their officers, while exercising powers delegated under Clause 31. 
 
The Committee are of the view that the absence of such protection may expose these entities 
and their officers to undue liability for bona fide actions taken in the discharge of delegated 
statutory functions and may have a chilling effect on the effective exercise of such powers. 
 
19.7 The Committee, therefore, recommend that appropriate provisions be incorporated in 
the Code to extend necessary protection to Market Infrastructure Institutions, including stock 
exchanges, clearing corporations and depositories, and their officers, in respect of acts done 
or purported to be done in good faith while exercising powers or discharging functions 
delegated under Clause 31. 
 
The Committee further recommend that such protection should be appropriately 
circumscribed and should not extend to acts or omissions involving mala fide conduct, fraud, 
gross negligence or wilful misconduct. 
 
19.8 The Committee further observe that Clause 31, as presently drafted, does not expressly 
clarify the manner in which the power of registration may be delegated, including the 
circumstances in which the Board may delegate such power to a Market Infrastructur e 
Institution or a Self-Regulatory Organisation and those in which such power may be delegated 
to a member in consultation with the Central Government. 
 
The Committee note that, in response to the concerns raised, the Ministry has proposed to 
amend Clause 31 to provide that the power of registration of any class or classes of 
intermediaries or investors may be delegated by the Board to a Market Infrastruct ure 
Institution or a Self-Regulatory Organisation or, by the Board in consultation with the Central 
Government, to a member, by an order in writing and subject to such conditions as may be 
specified therein. 
 
19.9 The Committee are of the view that the proposed formulation would provide greater 
clarity regarding the manner in which the power of registration may be delegated and the 
authority competent to exercise such delegated powers. 
 

101 
 
The Committee, therefore, recommend that the proposed amendment to Clause 31 be 
incorporated in the Code, together with appropriate safeguards and accountability 
mechanisms governing the exercise of the delegated powers. 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 

102 
 
CHAPTER XX 
 
XX Investment Schemes 
 
Clause 32 
 
20.1 Clause 32 provides for investment schemes, as under:— 
 
32 (1) Any scheme or arrangement, the purpose or effect of which is to enable persons taking part 
therein, whether by becoming owners of any asset or any part thereof or otherwise, to participate in 
or receive a beneficial interest in the form of profits, income, dividend, produce or any other benefit 
arising from the acquisition, holding, management or disposal of any asset, or sums paid out of such 
profits, income, dividend, produce or benefit, and where— 
 
(a) the persons participating in such scheme or arrangement do not have day-to-day control over the 
management of such assets, irrespective of whether or not they have the right to be consulted or to 
give directions; and 
 
(b) the scheme or arrangement has the following characteristics— 
 
(i) the contributions of the persons participating in such scheme or arrangement, and the profits, 
income, dividend, produce or benefit out of which payments are to be made to such persons, are 
pooled; 
 
(ii) the contributions of the persons participating in such scheme or arrangement, or the assets 
acquired using such contributions, are managed as a whole by any person on behalf of the persons 
participating in such scheme or arrangement; and 
 
(iii) the arrangement is not regulated by any other regulatory authority, shall be an investment 
scheme. 
 
(2) The following schemes and arrangements shall not be treated as investment schemes— 
 

103 
 
(a) any scheme or arrangement made or offered by a co -operative society registered under the Co-
operative Societies Act, 1912, or a society registered or deemed to be registered under any law 
relating to co-operative societies for the time being in force in any State; or 
 
(b) deposits accepted by a non-banking financial company as defined in clause (f) of section 45-I of 
the Reserve Bank of India Act, 1934; or 
 
(c) a contract of insurance to which the Insurance Act, 1938 applies; or 
 
(d) any Scheme, Pension Scheme or Insurance Scheme made under the Employees’ Provident 
Funds and Miscellaneous Provisions Act, 1952; or 
 
(e) deposits as defined in clause (31) of section 2 of the Companies Act, 2013; or 
 
(f) deposits accepted by a company declared as a Nidhi or a Mutual Benefit Society under section 
406 of the Companies Act, 2013; or 
 
(g) a chit business as defined in clause (e) of section 2 of the Chit Funds Act, 1982; or 
 
(h) deposits accepted for the purposes of a real estate project as defined in clause (zn) of section 2 
of the Real Estate (Regulation and Development) Act, 2016; or 
 
(i) a unit-linked insurance policy, scrip or any such instrument or unit, by whatever name called, which 
provides a component of investment in addition to a component of insurance and is issued by an 
insurer as defined in clause (9) of section 2 of the Insurance Act, 1938; or 
 
(j) such other schemes or arrangements as the Central Government may, in consultation with the 
Board, notify. 
 
(3) Investment schemes shall be conducted, operated or promoted, including in relation to the 
contribution or subscription made by an investor, in such manner as may be specified by regulations.” 
 
20.2 Certain stakeholders suggested that contributions made pursuant to mandatory “skin in the 
game” requirements or employee contribution requirements prescribed by the regulator should be 

104 
 
exempted. It was also suggested that appropriate carve -outs may be considered for schemes 
established in the International Financial Services Centre, where there may not be an independent 
fund manager and the fund may be managed indirectly by the investor. 
 
The stakeholders pointed out that the definition of an “investment scheme” requires that persons 
participating in the scheme or arrangement should not have day-to-day control over the management 
of the assets. It was further submitted that, in line with global practices, schemes may be established 
for a single investor or investor group, including sovereign wealth funds, family offices and large 
corporate groups. In such schemes, there may not be an independent fund manager, and the fund 
may instead be managed indirectly by the investor. 
 
20.3 On the issue of providing appropriate carve -outs from the definition of “investment scheme” 
for mandatory “skin in the game” or employee contributions prescribed by the regulator, and for 
schemes established in the International Financial Services Centre where there may not be an 
independent fund manager and the fund is managed indirectly by a single investor or investor group, 
the Ministry, in its written submission, stated as under:— 
 
“The concern is valid and appropriate drafting changes will be made.” 
 
20.4 During the deliberations, the Committee sought to understand the rationale for exempting 
mandatory “skin in the game” or employee contributions prescribed by the regulator. The Committee 
desired to know whether, if such contributions were treated as ordinary investor contributions under 
Clause 32(3), the manager or employee might inadvertently be treated as an investor for all regulatory 
purposes, thereby creating compliance and structuring difficulties. 
 
The Committee also sought details of the existing framework prescribed by the Securities and 
Exchange Board of India, including the percentage and quantum of such contributions, the categories 
of funds to which such requirements apply, and the threshold requirements followed in other pooled 
investment vehicles, both in India and internationally. 
 
In response thereto, the Ministry stated as under:— 
 
“Sir, we will examine the regulations, since there are three categories of Alternative Investment Funds 
under the applicable regulations, and revert with the relevant information.” 
 
The Ministry further elaborated upon the matter as under:— 
 

105 
 
Current Position 
 
At present, section 11AA of the Securities and Exchange Board of India Act, 1992 provides the 
statutory framework for collective investment schemes. However, details concerning such schemes, 
including matters relating to contributions or subscriptions made by investors, are prescribed through 
regulations. For instance, the Securities and Exchange Board of India (Alternative Investment Funds) 
Regulations, 2012 and the Securities and Exchange Board of India (Mutual Funds) Regulations, 2026 
govern the manner in which investors may subscribe to such schemes. 
 
SMC Position 
 
Clause 32 of the Securities Markets Code defines investment schemes and has been streamlined to 
bring schemes with specified characteristics within the regulatory jurisdiction of the Securities and 
Exchange Board of India, based on existing regulatory prac tice. Clause 32(3) further provides that 
investment schemes shall be conducted, operated or promoted, including in relation to contributions 
or subscriptions made by investors, in such manner as may be specified by regulations. 
 
Current Position 
 
Section 11AA of the Securities and Exchange Board of India Act, 1992 pertains to collective 
investment schemes. It specifies the characteristics of a collective investment scheme and provides 
a list of exclusions. 
 
SMC Position 
 
The definition of an investment scheme under Clause 32 of the Securities Markets Code broadens 
the concept of a collective investment scheme under section 11AA. The principal characteristics are— 
 
(i) the arrangement denotes an interest or participation in profit-sharing; 
 
(ii) management is undertaken by a separate entity; 
 
(iii) investors do not exercise day-to-day control; and 
 

106 
 
(iv) contributions by investors are pooled. 
 
The absence of day-to-day control by investors is an essential feature of an investment scheme and 
is also recognised in other jurisdictions, including the United States of America. 
 
However, under the Securities and Exchange Board of India (Mutual Funds) (Amendment) 
Regulations, 2025, read with the circular dated 21 March 2025 concerning the framework for 
alignment of the interests of designated employees of Asset Management Companies  with the 
interests of unitholders, the Securities and Exchange Board of India has prescribed that a minimum 
slab-wise percentage of the salary, perquisites, bonus or non-cash compensation, forming part of the 
gross annual cost to company and calculated ne t of income tax and statutory contributions, of 
designated employees of Asset Management Companies shall be mandatorily invested in units of 
Mutual Fund schemes in which they have a role or oversight. 
 
Similarly, under the Securities and Exchange Board of India (Alternative Investment Funds) 
Regulations, 2012, sponsors and investment managers are required to maintain ‘skin in the game’. 
 
The rationale for such requirements is to align the economic interests of managers, sponsors and key 
employees with those of investors. Accordingly, ‘skin in the game’ contributions and employee 
contributions may be included as an exception to Clause 32(1)(a). 
 
Preliminary Proposed Formulation 
 
After Clause 32(1)(b), the following proviso may be inserted:— 
 
‘Provided that nothing in this clause (a) of this sub-section shall apply to any day-to-day control over 
the management of assets obtained pursuant to a requirement laid down under any law for the time 
being in force.’” 
 
20.5 On being asked whether the removal of the monetary threshold and the adoption of a 
principle-based definition might render the provision overly broad, the Secretary, Ministry of Finance, 
stated during the deliberations that the proposed framework was not intended to create an 
overarching law, since only schemes required to be registered with the Securities and Exchange 
Board of India and not otherwise regulated by any other regulatory authority would fall within its ambit. 
 

107 
 
The Secretary further explained that the proposed proviso was intended to clarify that schemes 
involving mandatory “skin in the game” requirements under existing laws would continue to be treated 
as investment schemes, notwithstanding such participation by fund managers or employees. 
 
The Ministry, in its written reply, further stated as under:— 
 
“Section 2(da) of the Securities Contracts (Regulation) Act, 1956 defines the term ‘pooled investment 
vehicle’ to mean a fund established in India in the form of a trust or otherwise, such as a mutual fund, 
alternative investment fund, collective investment scheme or business trust as defined in sub-section 
(13A) of section 2 of the Income -tax Act, 1961, and registered with the Securities and Exchange 
Board of India, or such other fund which raises or collects monies from investors and invests such 
funds in accordance with regulations made by the Securities and Exchange Board of India. 
 
Investment schemes established by pooled investment vehicles are governed under the respective 
Securities and Exchange Board of India regulations applicable to such schemes. Mutual funds are 
regulated under the Securities and Exchange Board of India (Mutual Funds) Regulations, 2026; real 
estate investment trusts under the Securities and Exchange Board of India (Real Estate Investment 
Trusts) Regulations, 2014; infrastructure investment trusts under the Securities and Exchange Board 
of India (Infrastructure Investment Trusts) Regulations, 2014; alternative investment funds under the 
Securities and Exchange Board of India (Alternative Investment Funds) Regulations, 2012; and 
collective investment schemes under the Securities and Exchange Board of India (Collective 
Investment Schemes) Regulations, 1999. 
 
Each of these regulations makes suitable distinctions between different categories of investment 
schemes and prescribes requirements specific to the nature and proportionate to the size of such 
schemes. 
 
Clause 32(3) of the Securities Markets Code provides that investment schemes shall be conducted, 
operated or promoted, including in relation to contributions or subscriptions made by investors, in 
such manner as may be specified by regulations. Accordingly , the Code adequately empowers the 
Board to specify all matters relating to investment schemes, including differential treatment based on 
their nature, size and purpose. Therefore, no amendment to the Securities Markets Code may be 
required in this regard. 
 
By way of illustration, the Securities and Exchange Board of India (Alternative Investment Funds) 
Regulations, 2012 prescribe the following differential monetary thresholds or corpus requirements for 
various schemes or funds— 
 

108 
 
(a) General requirement for Category I, Category II and Category III Alternative Investment Funds: 
each scheme must have a corpus of at least twenty crore rupees; 
 
(b) Angel Funds, categorised as Category I Alternative Investment Funds: no minimum corpus 
requirement, as they are permitted to onboard only accredited investors; 
 
(c) Corporate Debt Market Development Funds, categorised as Category I Alternative Investment 
Funds: no minimum corpus requirement; 
 
(d) Social Impact Funds, categorised as Category I Alternative Investment Funds: each scheme must 
have a corpus of at least five crore rupees; and 
 
(e) Special Situation Funds: each scheme must have a corpus of at least one hundred crore rupees. 
 
Similar provisions exist for other investment schemes under the respective regulations governing 
them.” 
 
20.6 On being asked whether, in the absence of statutory thresholds, exemptions or differentiated 
treatment based on the size and nature of investment arrangements, the proposed provision might 
cast an unduly wide regulatory net, the Ministry stated in its wri tten reply that a detailed response 
would be submitted. 
20.7 Certain stakeholders suggested that the Code should clarify that the digital representation of 
an investment arrangement does not alter its legal character. It was submitted that any arrangement 
involving the pooling of funds, profit-sharing and absence of operational control by investors should 
be treated as a pooled investment scheme, even where it is implemented through digital tokens or 
distributed ledger technology, so as to prevent regulatory arbitrage in token -based investment 
structures. 
 
 
It was further suggested that the possible application of the definition of “investment scheme”, and of 
Clauses 32 and 33, to tokens, Virtual Digital Assets or crypto-assets should be expressly clarified by 
either their inclusion or exclusion, so as to avoid ambiguity and ensure regulatory certainty. 
 
20.8 On being asked whether the Code should clarify that the digital form of an investment 
arrangement does not alter its legal character and that tokenised or distributed ledger -based 

109 
 
arrangements involving pooling of funds should continue to be treated as pooled investment 
schemes, the Ministry, in its written response, stated as under:— 
 
“Clause 32, read with Clause 33, is broadly worded and covers all forms of entities, including trusts 
and arrangements ‘or otherwise’. Clause 32(1) is particularly wide in scope, since it contemplates 
participation in pooled arrangements through full or partial ownership of assets, or otherwise. 
 
Although India does not yet have a dedicated legal regime for Virtual Digital Assets, the sector is 
subject to regulation for the purposes of anti-money laundering and taxation. 
 
The application of Clauses 32 and 33 would depend upon whether the relevant arrangement satisfies 
the elements of an ‘investment scheme’, rather than upon the nature of the underlying asset alone. 
 
The extent of the jurisdiction of the Securities and Exchange Board of India in this area would depend 
upon the exercise of its regulation -making powers under Clause 32(3), which relate to the conduct, 
operation and promotion of investment schemes.” 
 
20.9 On being asked whether the exclusion of Virtual Digital Assets from the scope of Clause 32 
would leave an unregulated grey area, the Ministry, in its written response, stated as under:— 
 
“Under the Securities Markets Code, the application of Clauses 32 and 33 would depend upon 
whether the relevant arrangement satisfies the elements of an ‘investment scheme’, rather than upon 
the nature of the underlying asset alone. The extent of the jurisdiction of the Securities and Exchange 
Board of India in this area would depend upon the exercise of its regulation -making powers under 
Clause 32(3), which relate to the conduct, operation and promotion of investment schemes. 
 
According to the Securities and Exchange Board of India, the definition of ‘securities’ under section 
2(h) of the Securities Contracts (Regulation) Act, 1956, as incorporated in Clause 2(1)(zi) of the 
Securities Markets Code, is technology-neutral. Any tokenised representation of an existing security, 
such as a unit of an investment scheme, bond or share, would continue to fall within the scope of 
‘securities’ under the Securities Contracts (Regulation) Act, 1956 and the Securities Markets Code, 
even where the form of record-keeping shifts to distributed ledger technology or a tokenised system. 
 
In contrast, Virtual Digital Assets such as cryptocurrencies that do not exhibit the defining 
characteristics of a ‘security’ or ‘derivative’ under the Securities Contracts (Regulation) Act, 1956 or 
the Securities Markets Code may remain outside the scope of the definition of ‘securities’, 
notwithstanding their digital nature or the terminology used to describe them.” 

110 
 
 
20.10 On being asked whether certain Virtual Digital Asset arrangements involving pooling of 
investor funds, passive participation by investors and management of assets by a third party possess 
characteristics similar to investment schemes under Clause 32 and, if so, whether such arrangements 
should expressly be brought within the regulatory framework of the Code, the Committee also sought 
the views of the Ministry on whether crypto-based investment schemes and crypto exchanges dealing 
in securities-like Virtual Digital Assets might otherwise remain outside the scope of the proposed 
Code and whether a suitable enabling provision should be incorporated. 
 
In response thereto, the Ministry, in its written reply, stated as under:— 
 
“The Securities Markets Code has been designed as a technology -neutral law. The definition of 
‘securities’ under section 2(h) of the Securities Contracts (Regulation) Act, 1956, as incorporated in 
Clause 2(1)(zi) of the Securities Markets Code, is also tec hnology-neutral. Any tokenised 
representation of an existing security, such as a unit of an investment scheme, bond or share, would 
continue to fall within the scope of ‘securities’ under the Securities Contracts (Regulation) Act, 1956 
and the Securities Markets Code, even where the form of record-keeping shifts to distributed ledger 
technology or a tokenised system. 
 
In contrast, Virtual Digital Assets such as cryptocurrencies that do not fall within the definitions of 
‘security’ or ‘derivative’ under the Securities Contracts (Regulation) Act, 1956 or the Securities 
Markets Code may remain outside the purview of the de finition of ‘securities’, notwithstanding their 
digital nature or the terminology used to describe them. 
 
The position of the Central Government regarding crypto -assets, including Virtual Digital Assets, is 
that such assets are presently unregulated in India, except for the limited purposes of taxation, 
prevention of money laundering and reporting. Any regulat ory framework for such assets would 
require significant international and domestic coordination. Accordingly, the inclusion of Virtual Digital 
Assets under the Securities Markets Code would require broader policy consideration by the 
Government and wider consultation among regulators and Departments. In view thereof, it may not 
be appropriate to include such assets within the Securities Markets Code at this stage. 
 
The treatment of Virtual Digital Assets across jurisdictions is guided by their respective policy 
objectives. A brief comparison of the definitions of securities and the legal frameworks governing 
Virtual Digital Assets is set out below.” 
 
United States 
 

111 
 
“The definition of ‘securities’ under section 3(a)(10) of the Securities Exchange Act of 1934 is as 
follows:— 
 
‘any note, stock, treasury stock, security future, security -based swap, bond, debenture, evidence of
