indebtedness, certificate of interest or participation in any profit -sharing agreement, collateral -trust 
certificate, preorganisation certificate or subscription, transferable share, investment contract, voting-
trust certificate, certificate of  deposit for a security, fractional undivided interest in oil, gas or other 
mineral rights, any put, call, straddle, option or privilege on any security, certificate of deposit, or 
group or index of securities, including any interest therein or based on the value thereof, or any put, 
call, straddle, option or privilege entered into on a national securities exchange relating to foreign 
currency, or, in general, any interest or instrument commonly known as a security, or any certificate 
of interest or participation in, temporary or interim certificate for, receipt for, guarantee of, or warrant 
or right to subscribe to or purchase, any of the foregoing.’ 
 
The definition of securities in the United States is technology-neutral. 
 
The United States does not have a dedicated legal framework for crypto-assets. However, the Howey 
Test is used to determine whether an instrument constitutes an investment contract. Since the 
definition of securities is technology -neutral, the Securities and Exchange Commission has applied 
this test to classify several Virtual Digital Assets as securities and subject them to securities 
regulation.” 
 
Singapore 
 
“The definition of ‘securities’ under section 2(1) of the Securities and Futures Act, 2001 is as follows:— 
 
‘securities’ means— 
 
(a) shares, units in a business trust or any instrument conferring or representing a legal or beneficial 
ownership interest in a corporation, partnership or limited liability partnership; 
 
(b) debentures; or 
 
(c) any other product or class of products as may be prescribed, 
 

112 
 
but does not include— 
 
(d) any unit of a collective investment scheme; 
 
(e) any bill of exchange; 
 
(f) any certificate of deposit issued by a bank or finance company, whether situated in Singapore or 
elsewhere; or 
 
(g) such other product or class of products as may be prescribed.’ 
 
The definition of securities in Singapore is technology-neutral. 
 
In Singapore, the Monetary Authority of Singapore regulates crypto -related businesses under the 
Payment Services Act, 2019. Crypto-assets exhibiting the characteristics of securities may, however, 
be regulated under the Securities and Futures Act, 2001.” 
 
United Kingdom 
 
“The legal framework in the United Kingdom applies distinct definitions of securities depending upon 
the context. For the regulation of activities relating to securities, section 22 of the Financial Services 
and Markets Act, 2000, read with Part III of the Financial Services and Markets Act 2000 (Regulated 
Activities) Order, 2001, treats the following kinds of investments as securities— 
 
(a) shares in any body corporate; 
 
(b) instruments creating or acknowledging indebtedness, including debentures, bonds, loan stocks 
and certificates of deposit; 
 
(c) alternative finance investment bonds; 
 
(d) government and public securities; 

113 
 
 
(e) warrants or instruments giving entitlement to investments; 
 
(f) certificates representing certain securities; 
 
(g) units in a collective investment scheme; 
 
(h) rights under a pension scheme; 
 
(i) emission allowances; and 
 
(j) rights to or interests in the securities referred to above. 
 
For the purposes of listing, the Financial Services and Markets Act, 2000, read with the United 
Kingdom Listing Rules, defines ‘securities’ as anything which has been, or may be, admitted to the 
official list and permits specified categories of securities to be listed, including equity shares, closed-
ended investment funds, open -ended investme nt companies, certificates representing certain 
securities, non -equity shares, non -voting equity shares, debt and debt -like securities, securitised 
derivatives, warrants, options and other miscellaneous securities. 
 
The definition of securities in the United Kingdom is technology-neutral. 
 
Crypto-assets are regulated by the Financial Conduct Authority under the Financial Services and 
Markets Act, 2000. Crypto -assets possessing the characteristics of traditionally regulated activities 
are governed under the existing regulatory framework, whil e other qualifying crypto -assets and 
stablecoins are subject to a separate regulatory regime introduced under the Financial Services and 
Markets Act 2000 (Cryptoassets) Regulations, 2026.” 
 
European Union 
 
“In the European Union, Virtual Digital Assets are regulated under the Markets in Crypto -Assets 
Regulation unless they are already governed under an existing regulatory framework. The Markets 
in Crypto-Assets Regulation operates as a residual framework gov erning digital assets that do not 
fall within any existing regime. Certain Virtual Digital Assets that qualify as financial instruments are 
regulated under the Markets in Financial Instruments Directive II. 

114 
 
 
Virtual Digital Assets possessing the characteristics of securities are generally regulated under the 
existing securities law framework in most jurisdictions, even where a specialised regulatory 
framework for Virtual Digital Assets exists. Since the defini tion of securities in such jurisdictions is 
technology-neutral, the existing securities law framework is applied to maintain regulatory 
coherence.” 
 
20.11 The Committee have examined Clause 32, which seeks to provide a statutory 
framework for investment schemes by broadening the scope of the existing concept of 
collective investment schemes. The Committee note that the clause provides, inter alia, that 
persons participating in such schemes should not have day -to-day control over the 
management of the assets. The Committee further note that detailed operational requirements 
relating to investment schemes, including contributions by investors, would conti nue to be 
governed through regulations. 
20.12 The Committee take note of the concerns raised by various stakeholders that 
mandatory “skin in the game” or employee contributions prescribed under the existing 
regulatory framework for sponsors, investment managers and designated employees of 
investment vehicles, such as Alternative Investment Funds and Mutual Funds, may create 
ambiguity in the application of Clause 32. 
 
The Committee also note the concerns that certain investment structures operating in the 
International Financial Services Centre, including sovereign wealth funds, family offices and 
other single-investor fund structures, may not have an independent fund m anager and may 
instead be managed indirectly by the investor, and may therefore not fall squarely within the 
proposed statutory definition of an investment scheme. 
 
20.13 The Committee, therefore, recommend that Clause 32 be suitably amended by 
incorporating an express proviso to clarify that mandatory regulatory requirements relating 
to day-to-day management shall not affect the character of an investment scheme. 
 
The Committee accordingly recommend that the following proviso be incorporated after 
Clause 32(1)(b):— 
 
“Provided that nothing in this clause shall apply to any day-to-day control over management 
of assets obtained pursuant to a requirement laid down under any law for the time being in 
force.” 
 
20.14 The Committee further recommend that, while finalising the provision, the Ministry may 
ensure that appropriate carve -outs are provided, wherever necessary, for recognised 

115 
 
investment structures operating in the International Financial Services Centre, so that 
legitimate and globally accepted fund structures are not subjected to unintended regulatory 
uncertainty under the proposed Code. 
 
Virtual Digital Assets 
 
 
 
20.15 The Committee note that the proposed Code adopts a technology -neutral 
definition of securities; however, it excludes Virtual Digital Assets (VDAs) that do not 
satisfy the legal characteristics of securities or derivatives under the proposed 
framework. The Committee further note that, although many categories of VDAs are 
increasingly traded and invested in as financial assets and exhibit  characteristics 
commonly associated with securities and derivatives such as investment for financial 
returns, tradability on organised platforms, price discovery through market forces, 
speculative trading, leverage, and, in certain cases, exposure to the value or 
performance of an underlying asset or index they are not expressly recognised or 
regulated under the proposed Code unless they independently satisfy the statutory 
definition of a security or derivative. In view of the rapid growth of the VDA ecosystem 
and increasing retail participation, the Committee are of the view that this exclusion 
creates a regulatory grey area, resulting in regulatory uncertainty, exposing investors 
to heightened risks of fraud, market manipulation, misrepresentation and ina dequate 
grievance redressal, while also creating opportunities for regulatory arbitrage. Such 
uncertainty may adversely affect investor confidence and undermine the integrity, 
transparency and orderly development of the securities market. 
 
20.16 The Committee, therefore, recommend that the Government comprehensively 
examine the need for an appropriate statutory and regulatory framework for Virtual 
Digital Assets. Pending the establishment of a comprehensive legislative framework, 
the Government may consider introducing an interim mechanism through recognised 
Self-Regulatory Organisations (SROs) operating under the oversight of the designated 
regulator. Such a framework should prescribe minimum standards of governance, 
transparency, disclosure, investor protection, grievance redressal, compliance with 
prescribed codes of conduct and appropriate regulatory oversight, so as to mitigate 
risks arising from the existing regulatory vacuum while promoting market discipline 
and safeguarding investor interests. 
  

116 
 
CHAPTER XXI 
 
XXI Pooled Investment Vehicles 
 
Clause 33 
 
21.1 Clause 33 provides for pooled investment vehicles, as under:— 
 
33 (1) A pooled investment vehicle means a fund established in respect of an investment scheme, in 
the form of a trust or otherwise, such as a mutual fund, an alternative investment fund, a real estate 
investment trust, an infrastructure investment trust or such other fund, which raises or collects money 
from investors and invests such funds in such manner as may be specified by regulations. 
 
(2) Notwithstanding anything contained in the Indian Trusts Act, 1882, or in any other law for the time 
being in force, or in any judgment, decree or order of any court, Tribunal or any other authority, a 
pooled investment vehicle, whether constituted as a trust or otherwise, and registered with the Board, 
shall be eligible to borrow and issue debt securities in such manner and to such extent as may be 
specified by regulations. 
 
(3) Every pooled investment vehicle referred to in sub -section (2) shall, subject to the provisions of 
the trust deed, be permitted to provide security interest to lenders in terms of the facility documents 
entered into by such pooled investment vehicle. 
 
(4) Where any pooled investment vehicle referred to in sub -section (2) defaults in repayment of the 
principal amount or payment of interest or any such amount due to the lender, the lender shall recover 
the defaulted amount and enforce the security interes t, if any, against the trust assets by initiating 
proceedings against the trustee acting on behalf of such pooled investment vehicle in accordance 
with the terms and conditions specified in the facility documents: 
 
Provided that, upon initiation of proceedings against the trust assets, the trustee shall not be 
personally liable and his assets shall not be utilised towards recovery of such debt. 
 
(5) The trust assets which remain after recovery of the defaulted amount shall be remitted to the unit 
holders on a proportionate basis.” 
 

117 
 
21.2 Certain stakeholders suggested that the expression “trust deed” may be replaced with 
“constitutional documents”. It was submitted that, since pooled investment vehicles may not 
necessarily be constituted as trusts, a generic reference to constitutional do cuments would have 
broader applicability. 
 
The stakeholders further pointed out that, while Clauses 33(1) and 33(2) refer to pooled investment 
vehicles constituted as trusts “or otherwise”, Clauses 33(3), 33(4) and 33(5) are confined to trust -
specific terminology and should not be limited to pooled investment vehicles constituted as trusts. 
 
21.3 On being asked for its views on the suggestion that the expression “trust deed” be replaced 
with “constitutional documents”, the Ministry, in its written reply, stated that the concern was valid and 
that appropriate drafting changes would be made. 
21.4 On being asked about the proposed formulation of the provision, the Ministry, in its written 
reply, furnished the following draft formulation:— 
 
“Clauses 33(3), 33(4) and 33(5) may be redrafted in the following manner to accommodate other 
corporate structures within the definition of pooled investment vehicles: 
 
‘33. (1) …(3) Every pooled investment vehicle referred to in sub -section (2) shall, subject to the 
provisions of the trust deed or any other arrangement, be permitted to provide security interest to 
lenders in terms of the facility documents entered into by such pooled investment vehicle. 
 
(4) Where any pooled investment vehicle referred to in sub -section (2) defaults in repayment of the 
principal amount or payment of interest or any such amount due to the lender, the lender shall recover 
the defaulted amount and enforce the security interes t, if any, against the assets of a pooled 
investment vehicle, by initiating proceedings against the trustee or any other person acting on behalf 
of such pooled investment vehicle in accordance with the terms and conditions specified in the facility 
documents: 
 
Provided that, upon initiation of proceedings against the trust assets or any other assets or otherwise, 
the trustee or such other person shall not be personally liable and his assets shall not be utilised 
towards recovery of such debt. 
 
(5) The assets of the pooled investment vehicle, which remain after recovery of the defaulted amount 
shall be remitted to the unit holders on a proportionate basis.’” 
 
 

118 
 
21.5 The Committee have examined Clause 33, which provides the framework for pooled 
investment vehicles, including those constituted in the form of a trust or otherwise. 
 
The Committee note the concerns raised by stakeholders that, while Clauses 33(1) and 33(2) 
recognise that a pooled investment vehicle may be constituted as a trust “or otherwise”, 
Clauses 33(3), 33(4) and 33(5) continue to use trust-specific expressions such as “trust deed”, 
“trust assets” and “trustee”, thereby creating an inconsistency within the provision. 
 
The Committee are of the view that retaining trust -specific terminology in the operative 
provisions may inadvertently exclude, or create ambiguity in relation to, pooled investment 
vehicles constituted in other legal forms, notwithstanding the broader scope of sub-sections 
(1) and (2). 
 
The Committee, therefore, recommend that Clause 33 be modified as under:— 
 
“(3) …(3) Every pooled investment vehicle referred to in sub-section (2) shall, subject to the 
provisions of the trust deed or any other arrangement, be permitted to provide security 
interest to lenders in terms of the facility documents entered into by such pooled investment 
vehicle. 
 
(4) Where any pooled investment vehicle referred to in sub -section (2) defaults in repayment 
of the principal amount or payment of interest or any such amount due to the lender, the lender 
shall recover the defaulted amount and enforce the security interest, if any, against the assets 
of a pooled investment vehicle, by initiating proceedings against the trustee or any other 
person acting on behalf of such pooled investment vehicle in accordance with the terms and 
conditions specified in the facility documents: 
 
Provided that, upon initiation of proceedings against the trust assets or any other assets or 
otherwise , the trustee or such other person shall not be personally liable and his assets shall 
not be utilised towards recovery of such debt. 
 
(5) The assets of the pooled investment vehicle which remain after recovery of the defaulted 
amount shall be remitted to the unit holders on a proportionate basis.’” 
.” 

119 
 
CHAPTER XXII 
 
XXII Intermediary 
 
Clause 34 
 
22.1 Clause 34 provides for intermediaries, as under:— 
 
34 (1) An intermediary shall facilitate or provide services related to the issue, sale, purchase or any 
dealings in securities in such manner as may be specified by regulations. 
 
(2) A stock broker shall charge brokerage, deliver securities and make payment to the investor in 
such manner and within such period as may be specified by regulations. 
 
(3) The Board may, if required, notify a new category of intermediary for the development of securities 
markets.” 
 
22.2 During the deliberations, the Committee examined the suggestions relating to Clause 34, 
particularly the proposal to delete sub -clauses (2) and (3). The Committee sought the views of the 
Ministry on the necessity of retaining a separate provision prescribing obligations specifically for stock 
brokers, especially when the expression “intermediary” is broadly defined under Clause 2(1)(q). 
 
The Committee also desired to know the rationale for retaining the enabling provision empowering 
the Board to notify new categories of intermediaries under Clause 34(3), when the definition of 
“intermediary” already contains an inclusive clause. 
 
Explaining the rationale, the Secretary, Department of Economic Affairs, Ministry of Finance, stated 
as under:— 
 
“Sir, Clause 34 is about the functions of intermediaries. The suggestion was that Clause 34(2) may 
be deleted. However, we did not find any merit in deleting this provision because it casts a positive 
obligation on what a stock broker is required to do. Clause 34, as a whole, deals with the functions of 
intermediaries and the services they are expected to facilitate. Clause 34(2) is specific to stock 
brokers because they are one of the most important intermediaries in the securities market 
ecosystem. The obligations placed on stock brokers have been carried forward from the existing legal 
framework and have, therefore, been retained. The whole tenor of this provision is to cast positive 
obligations on intermediaries, particularly on stock brokers. 
 

120 
 
Similarly, with regard to Clause 34(3), the suggestion was that it may be deleted since the definition 
of ‘intermediary’ already covers the subject. However, we considered it necessary to retain this 
provision because intermediaries are important functionaries of the securities market ecosystem. The 
provision merely reiterates that, as the market evolves, new categories of intermediaries may emerge 
and the Board should have the power to notify such intermediaries so that they can function within 
the regulatory framework. 
 
Clause 34(1) provides the overall framework by explaining that an intermediary is a person who 
facilitates or provides services relating to the issue, sale, purchase or dealing in securities. If, in future, 
any new category of intermediary emerges within t his framework, the Board would have the power 
under Clause 34(3) to notify such category. 
 
Further, the definition of ‘intermediary’ under Clause 2(1)(q) itself refers to persons notified by the 
Board under Clause 34(3). Thus, the definition derives its enabling power from Clause 34(3). 
Therefore, we considered it appropriate to retain the provision.” 
 
22.3 On the question of whether the expression “brokerage” in Clause 34(2) should be expanded 
to include “fee”, in view of the prevailing market practice whereby stock brokers levy not only 
brokerage but also fees for various services rendered, so as to ensure  that the provision accurately 
reflects the existing securities market framework, the Secretary, Department of Economic Affairs, 
Ministry of Finance, stated that the Ministry had taken note of the suggestion and would examine the 
matter while finalising the drafting of the provision. 
22.4 The Committee have examined Clause 34 relating to intermediaries and note that the 
provision lays down the functions of intermediaries, prescribes specific obligations for stock 
brokers and empowers the Board to notify new categories of intermediaries in response to 
the evolving needs of the securities market. 
22.5 The Committee note the Ministry’s justification for retaining Clauses 34(2) and 34(3), 
particularly that stock brokers occupy a central role in the securities market ecosystem and 
that the power to notify new categories of intermediaries is necessary to a ccommodate 
emerging market participants within the regulatory framework. 
22.6 The Committee are of the view that the existing framework under Clause 34 broadly 
strikes an appropriate balance between prescribing the core obligations of intermediaries and 
providing the flexibility necessary to respond to market developments. The Comm ittee also 
note that the enabling power under Clause 34(3) forms the statutory basis for the inclusive 
definition of “intermediary” under the Code and, therefore, merits retention. 
22.7 The Committee, however, recommend that Clause 34(2) be suitably amended to replace 
the expression “brokerage” with “brokerage or such other fees or charges, as may be 
applicable”, or an equivalent formulation, so that the provision appropriately reflects 
prevailing market practices where stock brokers may levy charges for services in addition to 
brokerage. This would ensure greater clarity, avoid interpretational ambiguity and align the 
provision with the operational realities of the securities market.  

121 
 
CHAPTER XXIII 
 
XXIII Obligations of Securities Markets Service Providers, Market Participants, etc. 
 
Clause 35 
 
23.1 Clause 35 provides for the obligations of securities markets service providers, market 
participants, etc., as under:— 
 
35 (1) The securities markets service provider shall make fair disclosure of such information and at 
such frequency as may be specified by regulations. 
 
(2) The securities markets service provider, market participant or a person associated with the 
securities markets shall furnish such information to the Board in such manner as may be specified by 
regulations. 
 
(3) The securities markets service provider shall invest money collected by it in such manner and 
within such period as may be specified by regulations. 
 
23.2 Certain stakeholders suggested that the provision should expressly clarify that the obligations 
contained therein are in addition to, and intended to give further effect to, the requirements prescribed 
under the Code and the rules and regulations made thereunder. 
23.3 On being asked whether the provision should expressly clarify that the obligations contained 
therein are in furtherance of, and are to be read alongside, the requirements prescribed under the 
Code and the rules and regulations made thereunder, the Ministry stated as under:— 
 
“The concern is acknowledged and appropriate drafting changes will be made.” 
 
The Ministry further proposed the following drafting amendments:— 
 
35. Obligations of securities markets service providers, market participants, etc. 
 

122 
 
(1) The securities markets service provider shall make fair disclosure of any information, in 
furtherance of the provisions of this Code and the rules and regulations made thereunder. 
 
(2) The securities markets service provider, market participant or a person associated with the 
securities markets shall furnish information to the Board in furtherance of the provisions of this Code 
and the rules and regulations made thereunder. 
 
23.4 The Committee have examined Clause 35, which prescribes certain obligations for 
securities markets service providers, market participants and persons associated with the 
securities markets, including obligations relating to fair disclosure of information, furnishing 
of information to the Board and investment of money collected by securities markets service 
providers. 
23.5 The Committee observe that the provision, as presently drafted, does not sufficiently 
clarify the relationship between the obligations expressly set out in the clause and the 
additional requirements prescribed under the Code and the rules and regulations made 
thereunder. The Committee are of the view that, for the avoidance of doubt and to ensure that 
the obligations specified in Clause 35 are not construed as exhaustive, the provision should 
expressly clarify that such obligations are in addition to, and are to be read in conjunction 
with, the requirements prescribed under the Code and the rules and regulations made 
thereunder. 
23.6 The Committee, therefore, recommend that Clause 35 be suitably amended to 
expressly clarify that the obligations specified therein are in addition to, and are intended to 
give further effect to, the requirements prescribed under the Code and the rules and  
regulations made thereunder. 
  

123 
 
CHAPTER XXIV 
 
XXIV Registration of Market Infrastructure Institutions 
 
Clause 37 
 
24.1  Clause 37 provides for the registration of Market Infrastructure Institutions, as under:— 
 
37 (1) No person shall carry on business as a market infrastructure institution unless it is registered 
with the Board under this Code. 
 
(2) Any person desirous of being registered as a market infrastructure institution shall make an 
application to the Board in such form and manner, and accompanied by such fee, as may be specified 
by regulations, including a copy of the bye-laws referred to in section 39 for the regulation and control 
of contracts, and a copy of documents relating generally to the constitution of the market infrastructure 
institution and, in particular, to— 
 
(a) the governing body of such market infrastructure institution, its constitution and powers of 
management, and the manner in which its business is to be transacted; 
 
(b) the powers and duties of the office bearers of the market infrastructure institution; 
 
(c) the admission into the market infrastructure institution of various classes of members, the 
qualifications for membership, and the exclusion, suspension, expulsion and re -admission of 
members therefrom or thereto; 
 
(d) the procedure for the registration of partnerships as members of the market infrastructure 
institution in cases where the rules provide for such membership; and 
 
(e) the nomination and appointment of authorised representatives and clerks. 
 
(3) If the Board is satisfied, after making such inquiry as may be necessary in this behalf and after 
obtaining such other information, if any, as it may require, that— 

124 
 
 
(a) the bye-laws of the market infrastructure institution are in conformity with such conditions as may 
be specified by regulations, with a view to ensuring fair dealing and protecting the interests of 
investors; 
 
(b) the applicant is willing to comply with such other conditions, including conditions regarding its 
members, as the Board may impose, after consultation with the governing board of the applicant and 
having regard to its standing and the nature of the sec urities dealt with by it, for carrying out the 
objects of this Code; and 
 
(c) it is in the interest of trade and also in the public interest to grant registration, it may grant 
registration to the applicant in such form and subject to such conditions as may be specified by 
regulations: 
 
Provided that where the application is found to be defective, the Board may return the application for 
rectification, if rectifiable, within such period as may be specified by regulations. 
 
(4) Every grant of registration to a market infrastructure institution under this section shall be notified 
by the Board, and such registration shall take effect from the date of such notification. 
 
(5) No application for registration under this section shall be refused unless the applicant has been 
given a reasonable opportunity of being heard, and the reasons for such refusal shall be 
communicated to the applicant in writing. 
 
24.2 Certain stakeholders suggested that clause 37(2)(e) should be omitted, as the reference to the 
nomination and appointment of “authorised representatives and clerks” is no longer relevant. It was 
submitted that clause 37(2)(e), which requires the bye -laws a nd other documents of a Market 
Infrastructure Institution to contain provisions relating to the nomination and appointment of 
authorised representatives and clerks, has been carried forward from the Securities Contracts 
(Regulation) Act, 1956 and has since become redundant. 
 
24.3  On the above suggestion, the Ministry, in its written reply, stated that the concern was 
acknowledged and that appropriate drafting changes would be made. 
 
24.4  The Committee have examined Clause 37, which provides for the registration of Market 
Infrastructure Institutions and specifies the documents and particulars required to 
accompany an application for registration. 

125 
 
 
The Committee further note that clause 37(2)(e), relating to the nomination and appointment 
of “authorised representatives and clerks”, has been carried forward from section 3(2)(e) of 
the Securities Contracts (Regulation) Act, 1956. 
 
The Committee observe that, while the provision may have been relevant at the time of 
enactment of the Securities Contracts (Regulation) Act, 1956, the concept of “clerks” no 
longer reflects the present -day governance and operational framework of Market 
Infrastructure Institutions and has become obsolete. 
 
The Committee, therefore, recommend the deletion of clause 37(2)(e), or, alternatively, the 
deletion of the reference to “authorised representatives and clerks”, as the provision has 
become redundant and its removal would improve the clarity and contemporary relevance of 
the Code. 
  

126 
 
CHAPTER XXV 
 
XXV Cancellation of Registration or Suspension of Business 
 
Clause 38 
 
25.1  Clause 38 provides for the cancellation of registration or suspension of business, as under:— 
 
38 (1) Without prejudice to clause (a) of section 20, where the Board is of the opinion that the 
registration granted to a market infrastructure institution under sub -section (4) of section 37 should, 
in the interest of trade or in the public interest, be cancelled, it may, by order, cancel such registration 
in such manner as may be specified by regulations, and shall notify such cancellation: 
 
Provided that no such order shall be made unless the applicant has been given an opportunity of 
being heard. 
 
(2) No cancellation under sub-section (1) shall affect the validity of any contract entered into before 
the date of such notification, and the Board may, after consultation with the market infrastructure 
institution, make such provisions as it deems fit in the notification of cancellation or in any subsequent 
notification for the due performance of any contracts outstanding on that date. 
 
(3) Without prejudice to sub-section (1), where, in the opinion of the Board, an emergency has arisen 
and it considers it expedient to do so for the purpose of meeting such emergency, it may, by 
notification and for reasons to be specified therein, direct a market infrastructure institution to suspend 
such part of its business, for such period not exceeding ninety days, and subject to such conditions 
as may be specified therein. Where, in the opinion of the Board, the interest of trade or public interest 
so requires, it may, by a similar notification, extend the said period for a further period of ninety days: 
 
Provided that where the period of suspension is proposed to be extended beyond the first period, no 
notification extending such period shall be issued unless the governing board of the market 
infrastructure institution has been given an opportunity of being heard. 
 
25.2  During the deliberations, the Committee sought to ascertain the procedural safeguards 
available under the Act, the rules or the regulations governing the exercise of the aforesaid powers 
by the Securities and Exchange Board of India. The Committee furthe r enquired whether similar 
powers are available with other contemporary regulators in India and in comparable international 

127 
 
jurisdictions; the adjudicatory process followed in cases involving cancellation of registration, 
including illustrative instances from past practice; and the number of occasions on which the power 
of suspension has been exercised, together with the circumstances warranting such action. 
 
In its written reply, the Ministry stated as under:— 
 
Current Position 
 
• At present, section 5 of the Securities Contracts (Regulation) Act, 1956 empowers the Central 
Government to withdraw the recognition granted to a stock exchange in the interest of trade or in the 
public interest. Before doing so, the Central Government is required to issue a written notice stating 
the reasons for the proposed withdrawal and provide an opportunity of hearing. 
 
• Section 12 of the Securities Contracts (Regulation) Act, 1956 empowers the Central Government to 
suspend the business of a stock exchange where, in its opinion, an emergency has arisen. The 
Central Government may, by notification and for reasons recorded  therein, direct a stock exchange 
to suspend its business for a period not exceeding seven days, subject to such conditions as may be 
specified in the notification. 
 
• At present, there are no rules or regulations prescribing procedural safeguards in relation to the 
cancellation of registration or suspension of Market Infrastructure Institutions. 
 
• There also does not appear to be a streamlined adjudicatory process governing such cancellation. 
 
• The Central Government has formally exercised its power under section 5 of the Securities 
Contracts (Regulation) Act, 1956 only once, when it withdrew the recognition granted to the 
Hyderabad Stock Exchange. 
 
SMC Position 
 
• Under Clause 38(1), the Securities and Exchange Board of India may cancel the registration of a 
Market Infrastructure Institution where it is of the opinion that such cancellation is necessary in the 
interest of trade or in the public interest, after providing an opportunity of hearing. The manner of 
cancellation may be prescribed by regulations. 
 

128 
 
• Clause 38(2) provides that the Securities and Exchange Board of India may consult the concerned 
Market Infrastructure Institution and make such provisions as it deems fit in the notification of 
cancellation for the due performance of contracts entered in to before the date of cancellation. The 
Board is also required to notify such cancellation. 
 
• Under Clause 38(3), the Securities and Exchange Board of India is required to specify the reasons 
for suspension in the notification together with the applicable conditions. Such suspension cannot, in 
the first instance, exceed ninety days. 
 
• However, to address concerns regarding the exercise of these powers, appropriate safeguards may 
be prescribed through regulations. While Clause 38(1) already enables the Board to prescribe the 
manner of cancellation through regulations, no corresponding regulation-making power has presently 
been provided in relation to suspension. 
 
Contemporary Laws 
 
(a) Banking Regulation Act, 1949 — Under section 22(4), the Reserve Bank of India may cancel the 
licence of a banking company where it ceases to carry on banking business in India or fails to comply 
with its statutory obligations. 
 
(b) Insurance Act, 1938 — Under section 3(4), the Insurance Regulatory and Development Authority 
of India may suspend or cancel the registration of an insurer on specified grounds, including failure 
to comply with statutory obligations, contravention of the Act or non-compliance with directions issued 
by the Authority. 
 
(c) Insolvency and Bankruptcy Code, 2016 — Under sections 201(5) and 210(5), the Insolvency and 
Bankruptcy Board of India is empowered to suspend or cancel the registration of an insolvency 
professional agency and an information utility, respectively. 
 
(d) Payment and Settlement Systems Act, 2007 — Section 8 empowers the Reserve Bank of India 
to revoke the authorisation of a payment system provider for contravention of the Act, the regulations, 
the conditions of authorisation or any directions issued thereunder. 
 
International Practice 
 

129 
 
(a) IOSCO Principles — Principles 33 and 34 provide that exchanges and trading systems should be 
subject to ongoing regulatory supervision and that authorisation should be reviewed or withdrawn 
where the exchange or trading system is unable to comply with the conditions of authorisation or with 
securities laws and regulations. 
 
(b) United Kingdom — Under section 297 of the Financial Services and Markets Act, 2000, the 
Financial Conduct Authority may revoke the recognition of a recognised investment exchange where 
it fails to satisfy the recognition requirements or comply with statutory obligations. 
 
(c) United States — Under section 19(h) of the Securities Exchange Act of 1934, the Securities and 
Exchange Commission may suspend, for a period not exceeding twelve months, or revoke the 
registration of a self -regulatory organisation where such action is necessary or appropriate in the 
public interest, for the protection of investors or otherwise in furtherance of the purposes of the Act, 
after providing notice and an opportunity of hearing. 
 
(d) Singapore — Under section 14 of the Securities and Futures Act, 2001, the Monetary Authority of 
Singapore may revoke the approval or recognition of a stock exchange on specified grounds, 
including contravention of the Act or where it is operating in a manner contrary to the public interest. 
 
In light of the above practices, the Ministry proposed that— 
 
1. an enabling provision may be incorporated in Clause 38(3) empowering the Board to make 
regulations in this regard; and 
2. appropriate procedural safeguards may be incorporated by specifying the relevant grounds for the 
exercise of such powers. 
 
25.3 On being asked whether, in view of the systemic importance of Market Infrastructure Institutions, 
the exercise of the power to cancel their registration should be subject to consultation with the Central 
Government, the Committee also raised concerns regarding the need to incorporate more specific 
procedural safeguards to ensure that the exercise of such powers is transparent, balanced and not 
perceived as arbitrary. 
 
Responding thereto, the Secretary, Ministry of Finance, stated as under:— 
 
“For the suspension part, we had actually come up with exactly this proposal. We can again review 
these three Acts in which the power is vested with the regulator and, as you have specified, in certain 
cases, some consultation can be provided for. 

130 
 
 
Section 3(4) of the Insurance Act provides that the IRDAI may suspend or cancel the registration of 
an insurer on specified grounds, including failure to comply with statutory obligations, contravention 
of the provisions of the Act and non-compliance with directions issued by the Authority. 
 
The Insurance Act also contains a provision under which, where the Authority has reason to believe 
that an insurer carrying on life insurance business is acting in a manner likely to prejudice the interests 
of policyholders, it may, after giving an opportu nity to the insurer, submit a report to the Central 
Government. If the Central Government is satisfied, it may appoint an Administrator for that insurer. 
 
We also examined the international position, as directed by the Committee. We found that the IOSCO 
Principles provide for ongoing regulatory supervision of exchanges and trading systems, including 
the re -examination or withdrawal of authorisation where the  system is unable to comply with the 
conditions of authorisation. 
 
Similarly, in the United Kingdom, section 297 of the Financial Services and Markets Act, 2000 
empowers the Financial Conduct Authority to revoke the recognition of a recognised investment 
exchange where it fails to satisfy the prescribed recognition requirements. 
 
Since the registration is granted by SEBI and the conditions governing such registration are also 
prescribed by SEBI, the Securities Law Committee has proposed that the power of revocation should 
likewise vest with SEBI. We will provide an appropriate formulation.” 
 
25.4 The Committee have examined Clause 38, which empowers the Board to cancel the 
registration of a Market Infrastructure Institution in the interest of trade or the public interest 
and, in cases of emergency, to direct the suspension of its business. The Committee note that, 
while the clause substantially carries forward the existing framework under the Securities 
Contracts (Regulation) Act, 1956, it entrusts these significant regulatory powers to the Board 
under the proposed Code. 
 
25.5 The Committee note that the Ministry has acknowledged the absence of a comprehensive 
statutory or regulatory framework governing the exercise of these powers, particularly in 
relation to the suspension of business, and has indicated that appropriate safeguards may be 
prescribed through regulations. The Committee further note that comparable domestic 
regulatory statutes and international jurisdictions generally provide for clear procedural 
safeguards, specified grounds, notice, opportunity of hearing and reasoned decision-making 
in the exercise of such powers. 
 

131 
 
25.6 The Committee are of the view that, considering the systemic importance of Market 
Infrastructure Institutions, the exercise of the power to cancel registration should be 
accompanied by adequate statutory and procedural safeguards so as to ensure transparency, 
accountability and fairness while preserving the operational independence of the regulator. 
 
Accordingly, the Committee recommend that Clause 38(1) be suitably modified to provide for 
consultation with the Central Government before cancellation of the registration of a Market 
Infrastructure Institution. 
 
The Committee further recommend that the Board be empowered to frame detailed regulations 
prescribing the grounds, objective criteria and procedure for cancellation and suspension, 
including the recording of reasons, opportunity of hearing, protection of existing contractual 
obligations and other requirements of due process. 
 
However, the Committee are of the view that the emergency power of suspension under 
Clause 38(3) should continue to be capable of being exercised expeditiously by the Board in 
appropriate cases, subject to the safeguards prescribed under the Code and the r egulations 
made thereunder. 
  

132 
 
CHAPTER XXVI 
 
XXVI Power of Market Infrastructure Institutions to Make Bye-laws 
 
Clause 39 
 
26.1 Clause 39 provides for the power of Market Infrastructure Institutions to make bye -laws, as 
under:— 
 
39 (1) A market infrastructure institution may, subject to the previous approval of the Board, make 
bye-laws consistent with the provisions of this Code and any rules or regulations made thereunder, 
for the regulation and control of contracts and conduct of its members and market participants. 
 
(2) In particular, and without prejudice to the generality of the foregoing powers, such bye-laws may 
provide for the following matters, namely:— 
 
(a) the appropriate mechanisms for safeguarding the interests of any investors in securities and 
beneficial owners thereof; 
 
(b) the manner of holding, custody, pledging, hypothecation, lending, borrowing, issue, transfer, 
trading, transmission, conversion, transformation, consolidation, switching, clearing, delivery, 
settlement and other dealings in securities; 
 
(c) the risk management and abuse prevention measures such as imposition of margins, position and 
price limits, circuit breakers, collateral management, auction of short positions, order restrictions, 
account suspensions, server placement and other technical requirements; 
 
(d) the particular bye-laws, the contravention of which shall make a contract entered into otherwise 
than in accordance with the bye-laws void under section 47; 
 
(e) the terms and conditions for accessing any services provided by the market infrastructure 
institution; 
 

133 
 
(f) the listing of securities, the inclusion of any security for the purpose of dealings and the suspension 
or withdrawal of any such securities, and the suspension or prohibition of trading in securities; 
 
(g) the procedure for disbursing interest, dividend, bonus and other monetary benefits arising from 
the holding, transfer or any dealings in securities; 
 
(h) the norms of fair and time-bound disclosure of material information by issuers; 
 
(i) the internal control standards such as audits, review and monitoring; 
 
(j) the inter se rights and obligations of members and market participants; 
 
(k) the terms, conditions and incidents of contracts, including the prescription of margin requirements, 
if any, and the conditions relating thereto, and the forms of contracts in writing; 
 
(l) the content, form and manner of executing member-client agreements; 
 
(m) the measures relating to regulation of fees charged by members, dealings by members for their 
own account, and maintenance of separation of client funds and securities; 
 
(n) the process of accrediting or empanelling persons providing services in connection with any 
dealings in securities; 
 
(o) the procedure for conveying information to members and market participants; 
 
(p) the levy and recovery of fees and penalties; 
 
(q) the conduct of inspections, filing of information and such other matters in relation to regulation of 
its members; 
 
(r) the obligations of members to supply such information or explanation and to produce such 
documents relating to their business as may be sought by the market infrastructure institution; 

134 
 
 
(s) the procedure for the settlement of claims or disputes or grievances, including settlement by 
mediation or arbitration, including through electronic means; 
 
(t) the manner of conducting proceedings against the members and market participants for committing 
breach of the bye -laws and provisions for suspension or expulsion of members and market 
participants and cancellation of agreements with the market infrastructure institution; 
 
(u) such other matters as may be specified by regulations. 
 
(3) The bye-laws of a market infrastructure institution shall endeavour to— 
 
(a) promote the objects of this Code; 
 
(b) ensure non-discriminatory access to similarly placed persons using its services; 
 
(c) minimise market abuse, fraudulent and unfair practices related to the securities markets; 
 
(d) foster transparency; and 
 
(e) ensure interoperability with other market infrastructure institutions. 
 
Explanation.—For the purposes of this sub-section, the expressions— 
 
(i) ‘non-discriminatory access’ means not creating differential obligations for similarly placed persons 
or not preventing similarly placed persons from availing a service provided by the market 
infrastructure institution; and 
 
(ii) ‘transparency’ means that adequate information about the functions and transactions carried out 
on the market infrastructure institution is available to all the persons availing the services provided by 
it to enable them to make informed decisions about their transactions. 
 

135 
 
(4) Any bye -laws made under this section shall be subject to public consultation and be approved 
and notified by the Board: 
 
Provided that the Board may, in the interest of trade or in the public interest, by an order and for 
reasons to be recorded in writing, dispense with the condition of public consultation. 
 
(5) The Board may, either on a request in writing received by it from the governing board of a market 
infrastructure institution or on its own motion, after consultation with the governing board, make or 
amend bye-laws for all or any of the matters specified in sub-section (2), after recording the reasons 
thereof.” 
 
26.2 On the suggestion that the existing position under section 9 of the Securities Contracts 
(Regulation) Act, 1956, be retained, whereby bye -laws are framed and notified by Market 
Infrastructure Institutions after obtaining the approval of the Board, ins tead of requiring the Board 
itself to notify such bye -laws under the Code, and that the expression “notified by the Board” be 
omitted so as to preserve the existing regulatory framework, the Committee sought the views of the 
Ministry. 
 
The Committee also desired that the process relating to public consultation and notification of rules 
and bye-laws be standardised and made uniform. In response, the Ministry stated that the concern 
had merit and informed the Committee that appropriate dra fting changes would be made in the 
context of Clauses 39(5) and 40 to clarify the requirement relating to public consultation. 
 
26.3 It was further suggested that the process of making or amending bye-laws should include initial 
consultation with, and approval of, the governing board of the Market Infrastructure Institution, 
followed by pre-publication for inviting comments from th e public through publication in the Official 
Gazette at the Central and State Government levels. Thereafter, the governing board of the Market 
Infrastructure Institution should consider the comments received and submit the proposed bye -laws 
to the Board for approval, followed by final publication. 
 
It was also submitted that, in certain cases, there may be a direction to amend the bye-laws urgently. 
In such cases, an exemption from pre-publication or public consultation may be considered on lines 
similar to the exemption available in respect of regulations under Clause 147(2). 
 
The stakeholders further pointed out that, under Clause 39(4), the bye-laws of a Market Infrastructure 
Institution are subject to public consultation unless such consultation is dispensed with by the Board. 
Amendments to such bye -laws should be subject to the same requirement, irrespective of whether 
the amendment is initiated by the Board or by the Market Infrastructure Institution. Where public 
consultation is dispensed with, the reasons for doing so should be recorded in writing by the Board. 

136 
 
 
It was submitted that public consultation is particularly important in view of the infrastructural 
significance and interconnectedness of Market Infrastructure Institutions with their members, 
intermediaries, market participants and investors. 
 
26.4 On the suggestion that the existing position under section 9 of the Securities Contracts 
(Regulation) Act, 1956, be retained, whereby bye -laws are framed and notified by Market 
Infrastructure Institutions after obtaining the approval of the Board, ins tead of requiring the Board 
itself to notify such bye -laws under the Code, and that the expression “notified by the Board” be 
omitted so as to preserve the existing regulatory framework, the Committee sought the views of the 
Ministry. 
 
The Committee also desired that the process relating to public consultation and notification of rules 
and bye-laws be standardised and made uniform. In response, the Ministry stated that the concern 
had merit and informed the Committee that appropriate dra fting changes would be made in the 
context of Clauses 39(5) and 40 to clarify the requirement relating to public consultation. 
 
26.5 The Committee also examined Clause 39(3)(c), which requires the bye -laws of Market 
Infrastructure Institutions to, inter alia, “minimise market abuse, fraudulent and unfair 
practices”. A concern was expressed that the use of the expression “minimise” may give rise 
to an unintended interpretation that a certain degree of market abuse is permissible or capable 
of being tolerated. 
 
The Committee, therefore, sought to know whether the language of the provision could be 
reviewed and a more appropriate formulation, consistent with the objective of preventing or 
prohibiting market abuse, could be considered. 
 
Responding to the concern, the Secretary, Department of Economic Affairs, Ministry of 
Finance, clarified as under:— 
 
“Clause 39 merely specifies the matters that should be covered in the bye -laws of a Market 
Infrastructure Institution, including promoting the objectives of the Code, ensuring non -
discriminatory access to similarly placed persons and addressing market abus e and 
fraudulent practices.” 
 
Acknowledging the concern raised by the Committee regarding the expression “minimise”, 
the Secretary stated that the point was well taken and informed the Committee that the 

137 
 
language of the provision would be revisited in consultation with the Department of Legal 
Affairs. 
 
26.6 On being asked whether it was necessary for every bye-law framed by a Market Infrastructure 
Institution to be notified by the Board after approval, or whether the concerned Market Infrastructure 
Institution itself could notify the approved bye -laws in the Official Gazette, and whether statutory 
timelines should be prescribed for according approval to such bye-laws in view of the delays pointed 
out by stakeholders, the Secretary, Ministry of Finance, stated during the oral evidence as under:— 
 
“Sir, Clauses 39 and 40 relate to the power of the Market Infrastructure Institutions to make bye-laws 
and the power of the Board to issue directions. The discussion was about the manner in which the 
provision is presently worded, namely, that the Market I nfrastructure Institution shall make the bye -
laws, which will be approved and notified by SEBI. 
 
The question arose as to whether it is necessary for SEBI to notify each of the bye -laws and, if the 
matter is left to the Market Infrastructure Institutions, which authority would get them notified in the 
Official Gazette. The stakeholders had also made t his suggestion. The Market Infrastructure 
Institutions may get the bye-laws approved but may notify them themselves and, therefore, get them 
published in the Official Gazette.” 
 
26.7 When it was suggested that approval by the Board itself should suffice for the purpose of 
notification, the Ministry submitted as under:— 
 
“It could be done, Sir. That is why the current formulation provides for approval and notification by the 
Board. Once the suggestion was received, we examined the current practice and found that the 
Market Infrastructure Institutions are already doing it.” 
 
 
26.8 The Committee observe that Clause 39 empowers Market Infrastructure Institutions to 
make bye-laws, subject to the previous approval of the Board, and further provides that such 
bye-laws shall be subject to public consultation and be approved and notified by the Board. 
 
The Committee note that, under the existing framework, bye -laws are framed and notified by 
the concerned Market Infrastructure Institution after obtaining the approval of the Board. The 
Committee are of the view that the existing practice appropriately recognises the operational 
autonomy of Market Infrastructure Institutions while preserving the regulatory oversight of 
the Board. 
 

138 
 
26.9 The Committee further observe that, given the significant impact of bye-laws on market 
participants, intermediaries and investors, the process of making or amending bye -laws 
should ordinarily be preceded by public consultation so as to promote transpa rency and 
stakeholder participation. 
 
At the same time, the Committee recognise that exceptional situations may arise where urgent 
regulatory intervention is warranted in the interest of trade or in the public interest. In such 
cases, dispensing with public consultation may be justified, provi ded that the reasons 
therefore are recorded in writing. 
 
26.10 The Committee recommend that the requirement of public consultation should apply 
uniformly to the making as well as the amendment of bye -laws, irrespective of whether such 
amendment is initiated by the Market Infrastructure Institution or by the Board. 
 
The Committee also recommend that, where public consultation is dispensed with on grounds 
of urgency or public interest, the reasons for such dispensation should invariably be recorded 
in writing so as to ensure transparency, consistency and accountability in the exercise of the 
regulatory powers of the Board. 
 
The Committee further recommend that a statutory timeline be prescribed for the Board to 
dispose of proposals seeking approval of bye -laws so as to avoid undue delay and provide 
regulatory certainty. 
 
The Committee also recommend that the notification process under Clause 39 be aligned with 
Clause 148 so that every bye-law, after being notified, continues to be laid before both Houses 
of Parliament in accordance with the procedure prescribed under the Code. This would ensure 
transparency, legislative oversight and consistency in the subordinate legislative framework. 
  

139 
 
CHAPTER XXVII 
 
XXVII Power of the Board to Direct Bye-laws to Be Made or to Make Bye-laws 
 
Clause 40 
 
27.1  Clause 40 provides for the power of the Board to direct bye-laws to be made or to make bye-
laws, as under:— 
 
40 (1) Where, after consultation with the governing bodies of market infrastructure institutions 
generally or with the governing body of any market infrastructure institution in particular, the Board is 
of the opinion that it is necessary or expedient so t o do, it may, by order in writing together with a 
statement of the reasons therefor, direct market infrastructure institutions generally or any market 
infrastructure institution in particular, as the case may be, to make any bye -laws or to amend any 
bye-laws already made in respect of all or any of the matters specified in sub -section (2) of section 
39 within a period of two months from the date of the order. 
 
(2) If any market infrastructure institution fails or neglects to comply with any order made under sub-
section (1) within the period specified therein, the Board may make the bye-laws or amend the bye-
laws made by the market infrastructure institution either in the form proposed in the order or with such 
modifications thereof as may be agreed to between the market infrastructure institution and the Board. 
 
(3) Where, in pursuance of this section, any bye-laws have been made or amended, the bye-laws so 
made or amended shall be published in the Official Gazette and, upon such publication, shall, 
notwithstanding anything to the contrary contained in the Compani es Act, 2013, or in any other law 
for the time being in force, have effect as if they had been made or amended by the market 
infrastructure institution. 
 
27.2  Suggestions were received from various stakeholders that amendments to bye -laws 
envisaged under the said clause should be subject to the same requirements of public consultation, 
irrespective of whether such amendments are initiated by the Board or by the  Market Infrastructure 
Institution (MII). It was further suggested that, where public consultation is dispensed with, the 
reasons therefor should be recorded in writing by the Board. 
 
It was submitted that public consultation is necessary in view of the importance, infrastructural nature 
and interconnectedness of Market Infrastructure Institutions with their members, intermediaries, 
market participants and investors. 

140 
 
 
 
27.3  When the Committee sought clarification regarding the requirement of public consultation 
under the said clause, the Ministry stated that the concern was accepted and that appropriate drafting 
changes would be made in the context of Clauses 39(5) and 40 to clarify the requirement relating to 
public consultation. 
 
27.4  It was further suggested that the provision should permit a reasoned extension of up to sixty 
days in cases involving complex technological or process -related changes, having regard to their 
impact and implementation timelines. It was submitted that such changes would require 
multidisciplinary involvement. It was also pointed out that the process of amending bye-laws, including 
publication in the Official Gazette and obtaining the approval of the Board, is itself time-consuming. 
 
27.5 When the Committee took up the suggestion regarding a reasoned extension for complex 
changes, the Ministry, in its written reply, stated that the concern was valid and that appropriate 
drafting changes would be made. 
 
27.6  On the further suggestion that the period prescribed for making or amending bye-laws should 
be increased from two months to four months from the date of the order, the Ministry agreed with the 
suggestion and stated that the necessary drafting changes would be carried out. 
 
27.7  The Committee have examined Clause 40, which empowers the Board to direct Market 
Infrastructure Institutions (MIIs) to make or amend bye -laws and, in the event of non -
compliance, to itself make or amend such bye-laws. 
 
The Committee observe that bye -laws governing Market Infrastructure Institutions have 
significant implications for market participants, intermediaries and investors and, therefore, 
any amendment thereto should ordinarily be preceded by an appropriate proce ss of public 
consultation. The Committee are of the view that, where public consultation is dispensed with, 
the reasons for doing so should be recorded in writing so as to ensure transparency and 
accountability in the bye-law-making process. 
 
27.8  The Committee further observe that the period of two months prescribed under Clause 
40(1) for making or amending bye -laws may not be adequate in cases involving complex 
technological, operational or process -related changes requiring extensive consultation , 
systems development, testing and regulatory approvals. The Committee are of the view that a 
more realistic timeline would facilitate effective implementation without compromising the 
regulatory objectives. 

141 
 
 
27.9  The Committee, therefore, recommend that Clause 40 be suitably amended to provide 
that the making or amendment of bye-laws shall ordinarily be preceded by public consultation 
and that, where such consultation is dispensed with, the reasons therefor shall be recorded 
in writing by the Board. 
 
The Committee further recommend that the period prescribed for making or amending bye -
laws be increased from two months to four months, with an enabling provision permitting the 
Board to grant a reasoned extension in appropriate cases involving complex technological or 
operational changes. 
 
This would ensure greater transparency, facilitate effective implementation, and strengthen 
the quality of the regulatory framework governing Market Infrastructure Institutions. 
  

142 
 
CHAPTER XXVIII 
 
XXVIII Action for Contravention of Bye-laws 
 
Clause 41 
 
28.1 Clause 41 provides for action for contravention of bye-laws, as under:— 
 
41 (1) Where a member contravenes any provision of the bye -laws, the market infrastructure 
institution may, by an order, take one or more of the following actions against such member, 
namely:— 
 
(a) impose penalty; 
 
(b) expel from membership; 
 
(c) suspend from membership for a period specified therein; 
 
(d) annul any transactions or contracts; 
 
(e) direct payment of compensation to the affected party; 
 
(f) suspend or freeze any account, collaterals or security deposits under the management of the 
market infrastructure institution; and 
 
(g) take any other similar action not involving the payment of money: 
 
Provided that before passing an order under clauses (b) to (g), an opportunity of being heard shall be 
given to such member. 
 
(2) The market infrastructure institution shall have mechanisms to— 

143 
 
 
(a) monitor and identify contraventions of the provisions of this Code and the rules, regulations or 
bye-laws made thereunder; 
 
(b) inform the Board of any such contraventions; 
 
(c) take measures, including the imposition of penalties, against any member or market participant 
who contravenes the bye-laws; and 
 
(d) undertake such other matters as may be specified by regulations. 
 
28.2  Several stakeholders suggested that Clause 41(1)(f) should be modified to specify “demat 
account or bank account, as the case may be”, since the expression “account” is too broad and may 
give rise to varying interpretations. 
 
28.3  It was further suggested that the proviso to Clause 41(1) should provide an exception enabling 
interim action to be taken without affording a prior opportunity of hearing in cases where such action 
is necessary to protect market integrity or safeguard inv estors’ interests. It was also suggested that 
a provision be incorporated for a post-decisional hearing in cases involving interim orders passed in 
emergent situations, since providing a prior opportunity of hearing may not always be feasible in such 
circumstances. 
 
28.4  In response to the above suggestions, the Ministry stated that the suggestions had merit and 
that the necessary drafting changes would be made in the Code. 
 
28.5  On being asked whether an opportunity of being heard should also be provided under Clause 
41(1)(a), in keeping with the principles of natural justice, the Ministry stated as under:— 
 
Current Position 
 
Clause 41 is based on section 9(3)(b) of the Securities Contracts (Regulation) Act, 1956 and extends 
the consequences of contravention provided therein to all Market Infrastructure Institutions. 
 
 

144 
 
SMC Position 
 
Clause 41(1) provides an inclusive list of actions that a Market Infrastructure Institution may take 
against a person for contravention of its bye -laws. While the other actions under the list require an 
opportunity of hearing to be provided, no such opportunity is presently required for the imposition of 
penalties. This distinction is based on the existing practice and procedure followed by stock 
exchanges for the imposition of penalties. 
 
At present, stock exchanges impose penalties for violations of their bye -laws without providing an 
opportunity of hearing. Such penalties are based on objective criteria, including requirements relating 
to funds, securities, margins and security deposits, for which predetermined penalties have already 
been prescribed. Accordingly, the imposition of such penalties does not ordinarily require 
adjudication. 
 
Clause 41(1)(a) is distinct from the penalties imposed by the Adjudicating Officer under Clause 20 
through a final order, as Market Infrastructure Institutions impose penalties for contravention of their 
bye-laws in the ordinary course of discharging their regulatory functions, in accordance with the 
criteria prescribed by them. 
 
In any event, an order passed or decision taken by a Market Infrastructure Institution is appealable 
before the Securities Appellate Tribunal under Clause 85(1)(g). 
 
However, the concern may be accepted in order to make the requirement of providing an opportunity 
of hearing consistent across Clause 41(1). Further, as suggested by the Securities and Exchange 
Board of India, a provision for a post-decisional hearing may be incorporated in Clause 41 for urgent 
cases where Market Infrastructure Institutions are required to issue interim orders without providing 
a prior opportunity of hearing, so as to prevent delay. 
 
The Secretary, Ministry of Finance, further supplemented the above during the oral evidence as 
under:— 
 
“Some of the penalties are automated, and we found that certain standard penalties are already 
published, so the concerned persons are aware of them. Even then, there are two categories of 
penalties under clause (1)(a). Therefore, we felt that an appropria te approach would be to replace 
the existing proviso. Instead of limiting it to clauses (b) to (g), we propose to apply it uniformly by 
providing that ‘an opportunity of being heard shall be given to such member in such manner as may 
be specified by regula tions’. The regulations can then provide appropriate fine -tuning for cases 
involving automated actions. Accordingly, we are in a position to accept this suggestion.” 

145 
 
 
28.6 The Committee have examined Clause 41, which empowers Market Infrastructure 
Institutions to take disciplinary action against members for contravention of their bye-laws. 
 
The Committee further observe that the expression “account” used in Clause 41(1)(f) is broad 
and may be susceptible to varying interpretations, thereby creating uncertainty regarding the 
nature and extent of the power to suspend or freeze accounts. The Committee are of the view 
that greater precision in the language of the provision would promote legal certainty and avoid 
unintended consequences. 
 
28.7 The Committee further observe that, while the proviso to Clause 41(1) requires an 
opportunity of being heard before specified actions are taken against a member, exceptional 
situations may arise where immediate interim measures are necessary to safegu ard market 
integrity, protect investors’ interests or prevent further regulatory harm. In such 
circumstances, insisting upon a prior hearing may not always be practicable. 
 
The Committee, therefore, recommend that Clause 41 be suitably amended to clarify that the 
expression “account” shall mean the relevant demat account or bank account, as the case 
may be. 
 
The Committee further recommend that the provision expressly empower Market 
Infrastructure Institutions to take interim action in exceptional and emergent circumstances 
without a prior hearing, subject to recording the reasons in writing and providing the affected 
person with a post -decisional opportunity of being heard within a reasonable period, in 
accordance with the principles of natural justice. 
  

146 
 
CHAPTER XXIX 
 
XXIX Ownership Norms of Market Infrastructure Institutions 
 
Clause 42 
 
29.1 Clause 42 provides for the ownership norms of Market Infrastructure Institutions, as under:— 
 
42 (1) A market infrastructure institution shall have diversified ownership by way of shareholding, 
subject to the following conditions, namely:— 
 
(a) the members of a market infrastructure institution shall not collectively own more than such 
percentage of the equity shareholding in the market infrastructure institution; 
 
(b) such maximum ownership interest is held by a class or classes of persons in a market 
infrastructure institution; 
 
(c) such percentage of the total equity shareholding in a market infrastructure institution is held by a 
person; 
 
(d) such ownership interest of different class or classes of persons is calculated and the manner of 
such calculation; 
 
(e) such levels of cross -holding of a market infrastructure institution in other market infrastructure 
institutions and the acquisition, takeover, merger or consolidation of business amongst market 
infrastructure institutions; 
 
(f) such period within which compliance with the ownership norms may be achieved; and 
 
(g) such other diversified ownership norms as may be specified by regulations. 
 
(2) A market infrastructure institution shall be managed in such manner that— 

147 
 
 
(a) the members of the governing board are eligible and qualified, and satisfy the fit and proper 
criteria; 
 
(b) independent members are appointed to the governing board; 
 
(c) any class or classes of market participants or users are appointed to the governing board; and 
 
(d) such other appropriate measures are undertaken to ensure good governance and avoid any 
conflict of interest as may be specified by regulations. 
 
(3) A market infrastructure institution shall be a company limited by shares and shall be demutualised 
in such manner as may be specified by regulations. 
 
Explanation.—For the purposes of this sub-section,— 
 
(i) “company limited by shares” shall have the same meaning as assigned to it in clause (22) of section 
2 of the Companies Act, 2013; 
 
(ii) “demutualised” means segregation of ownership and management from the trading rights, clearing 
rights or such other operational rights of the members of a market infrastructure institution, as the 
case may be. 
 
(4) The provisions of this section shall apply notwithstanding anything to the contrary contained in the 
Competition Act, 2002 or the Companies Act, 2013. 
 
29.2  Some stakeholders suggested that the provision contained in Clause 42(2)(c) should be 
deleted on the ground that the presence of market participants or investors on the governing board 
of a Market Infrastructure Institution may give rise to conflicts of interest and ought to be avoided. 
 
It was further submitted that the appointment of market participants and users to the governing board 
of Market Infrastructure Institutions would adversely affect the governance framework of these critical 
market infrastructure institutions and would be contrary to the principle of demutualisation. It was also 
pointed out that the existing regulatory framework does not provide for the representation of market 

148 
 
participants or users on the governing board of Market Infrastructure Institutions. Further, there is 
presently no concept of “users” either under the existing legal framework or under the proposed Code. 
 
29.3  On the issue relating to the deletion of Clause 42(2)(c) and the use of the expression “users”, 
which is neither defined in the Code nor explained elsewhere, the Ministry, in its written reply, stated 
that both the concerns were valid and clarified that s uch representation was intended only as an 
enabling provision and not as a mandatory requirement. 
 
The Ministry further stated that the governing boards of Market Infrastructure Institutions may 
endeavour to include market participants as representatives, where considered appropriate, and that 
suitable drafting changes would be made accordingly. 
 
29.4  On the question whether prescribing the ownership and governance norms through 
regulations carries the risk of their gradual dilution and confers excessive discretion on the Securities 
and Exchange Board of India, the Committee sought the views of the Ministry. 
 
The Secretary, Ministry of Finance, during the oral evidence, stated as under:— 
 
“The regulation-making power is intended to provide flexibility to SEBI. We had been advised to study 
the Bimal Jalan Committee Report, and we found that the Bimal Jalan Committee on Review of 
Ownership and Governance of Market Infrastructure Institutions recommended that, in order to 
reduce the influence of trading members on the governance of MIIs and to increase public 
shareholding, a diversified and dispersed ownership model should be adopted. 
 
The Committee also acknowledged the importance of strengthening the regulation-making powers of 
SEBI, not to leave these matters to the MIIs, but to augment SEBI’s powers since it has the 
responsibility of regulating MIIs through direct supervision and monitoring. 
 
Further, with respect to the procedure and criteria for recognition of MIIs, the Bimal Jalan Committee 
recommended that SEBI should prescribe the detailed requirements through its regulations so that it 
can regulate effectively. 
 
Accordingly, the formulation in the Code provides that diversified ownership norms shall be prescribed 
through SEBI regulations. We have also examined the position in the United States, Canada and 
Hong Kong, where similar requirements are prescribed through subordinate legislation.” 
 

149 
 
 
29.5  On the question whether the ownership and governance norms under Clause 42 should be 
prescribed through regulations and whether this approach is consistent with the recommendations of 
the Bimal Jalan Committee, the Ministry, in its written submission, stated as under:— 
 
Current Position 
 
Clause 42 provides the ownership norms for Market Infrastructure Institutions, including the norms 
relating to diversified ownership and the constitution of the governing board. 
 
Under the existing framework, sections 4A and 4B of the Securities Contracts (Regulation) Act, 1956 
prescribe certain public shareholding requirements for stock exchanges and lay down the procedure 
for their corporatisation and demutualisation. 
 
The detailed ownership requirements relating to stock exchanges, clearing corporations and 
depositories are contained in the Securities Contracts (Regulation) (Stock Exchanges and Clearing 
Corporations) Regulations, 2018 and the Securities and Exchange Board of India (Depositories and 
Participants) Regulations, 2018, which recognise the principle of diversified ownership. 
 
These provisions contain detailed norms relating to the maximum shareholding permitted for various 
classes of investors and market participants, the fit and proper criteria for holding securities of Market 
Infrastructure Institutions or occupying positions  as key managerial personnel or directors, and 
procedural safeguards governing the appointment of directors, key managerial personnel and other 
office-bearers. 
 
SMC Position 
 
Continuing the existing regulatory framework, Clause 42 lays down the key principles relating to 
ownership and governance and enables SEBI to prescribe the detailed requirements relating to 
Market Infrastructure Institutions and their members through regulations. 
 
This provides flexibility to SEBI to prescribe appropriate shareholding thresholds through regulations 
while maintaining the overarching requirement of diversified ownership. According to SEBI, this also 
enables the ownership norms to be revised in accorda nce with the evolving needs of the securities 
market. 
 

150 
 
The provision is also consistent with the recommendations of the Kania Committee (2002) on the 
Corporatisation and Demutualisation of Stock Exchanges, which recommended that the three 
principal stakeholders of stock exchanges —shareholders, brokers and the investing public through 
the regulator—should be represented on the governing board of a demutualised stock exchange. 
 
The Bimal Jalan Committee on Review of Ownership and Governance of Market Infrastructure 
Institutions (2010) similarly recommended the adoption of a diversified and dispersed ownership 
model to reduce the influence of trading members on the governance of M arket Infrastructure 
Institutions while increasing public shareholding. The Committee also recognised that certain eligible 
institutions could be permitted to hold a higher ownership interest in appropriate cases. 
 
More importantly, the Bimal Jalan Committee recognised the need to strengthen the regulation -
making powers of SEBI, having regard to its responsibility for direct supervision and regulation of 
Market Infrastructure Institutions. It further recommended that  the detailed procedure and eligibility 
criteria relating to recognition should be prescribed through SEBI regulations rather than through the 
Securities Contracts (Regulation) Rules. 
 
The Committee also recommended replacing the Securities Contracts (Regulation) (Manner of 
Increasing and Maintaining Public Shareholding) Regulations, 2006 with a comprehensive framework 
governing the ownership and governance of stock exchanges. 
 
Accordingly, the Securities Markets Code is consistent with the recommendations of the Bimal Jalan
