Committee. The necessary safeguards have already been incorporated under the Code for the 
making of regulations. Regulations framed by SEBI are subject to public consultation and 
parliamentary scrutiny, thereby ensuring transparency and accountability. 
 
29.6  On being asked whether the expression “managed” should be substituted or omitted, the 
Ministry stated as under:— 
 
“We could substitute the word ‘managed’ with ‘governed’.” 
 
29.7  The Committee have examined Clause 42 and observe that it seeks to prescribe the 
ownership and governance norms for Market Infrastructure Institutions. 
 
The Committee further observe that Clause 42(2)(c) enables the appointment of market 
participants or users to the governing board of a Market Infrastructure Institution. The 
Committee are of the view that the inclusion of market participants on the governi ng board 
may give rise to potential conflicts of interest and may not be consistent with the principle of 

151 
 
demutualisation, which seeks to maintain a clear separation between ownership, management 
and trading rights. 
 
The Committee also observe that the expression “users” has neither been defined under the 
Code nor explained elsewhere, which may give rise to ambiguity in its interpretation and 
implementation. 
 
The Committee, therefore, recommend that Clause 42(2)(c) be suitably modified to clarify that 
the inclusion of market participants, where considered appropriate, shall be only an enabling 
provision and not a mandatory requirement. 
 
The Committee further recommend that the undefined expression “users” be omitted or 
suitably defined so as to avoid ambiguity. 
 
The Committee also recommend that, in Clause 42(2), the expression “managed” be replaced 
with “governed”, as agreed to by the Ministry, since the provision relates to the governance 
framework of Market Infrastructure Institutions. 
  

152 
 
CHAPTER XXX 
 
XXX Power of the Central Government to Supersede 
 
Clause 44 
 
30.1  Clause 44 provides for the power of the Central Government to supersede, as under:— 
 
44 (1) Without prejudice to any other powers vested in the Central Government under this Code, 
where the Central Government, on the recommendation of the Board, is of the opinion that the 
governing board of a market infrastructure institution or a self -regulatory organisation should be 
superseded, then, notwithstanding anything contained in any other law for the time being in force, it 
may serve on the governing board a written notice that it is considering the supersession of the 
governing board for the reasons specified in the notice and, after giving an opportunity of being heard, 
it may, by notification, declare the governing board of such market infrastructure institution or self -
regulatory organisation to be superseded, and may appoint any person or pe rsons to exercise and 
perform all the powers and duties of the governing board, and, where more persons than one person 
are appointed, may appoint one of such persons to be the chairperson and another to be the vice -
chairperson thereof. 
 
(2) On the publication of a notification under sub-section (1), the following consequences shall ensue, 
namely:— 
 
(a) the members of the governing board which has been superseded shall, from the date of 
supersession, cease to hold office as such members; 
 
(b) the person or persons appointed under sub-section (1) may exercise and perform all the powers 
and duties of the governing board which has been superseded; and 
 
(c) the property of the market infrastructure institution or self-regulatory organisation shall vest in such 
person or persons appointed under sub-section (1), as may be necessary for the purpose of enabling 
him or them to carry on the business of the mark et infrastructure institution or self -regulatory 
organisation. 
 
(3) On the supersession of the board of a market infrastructure institution or self -regulatory 
organisation under sub-section (1), the person or persons appointed shall hold office for such period 

153 
 
as may be specified in the notification and the Central Government may, from time to time, by a like 
notification, vary such period. 
 
(4) The Central Government may, at any time before the determination of the period of office of any 
person or persons appointed under this section, call upon the market infrastructure institution or the 
self-regulatory organisation to reconstitute the governing board in accordance with its bye-laws and, 
on such reconstitution, all the property of the market infrastructure institution or self -regulatory 
organisation which has vested in, or was in the possession of, the person or persons appointed under 
sub-section (1), shall revest or vest, as the case may be, in the governing board so reconstituted: 
 
Provided that until a governing board is so reconstituted, the person or persons appointed under sub-
section (1) shall continue to exercise the powers and perform the duties. 
 
30.2  Several stakeholders pointed out that Market Infrastructure Institutions include stock 
exchanges, depositories and clearing corporations. While the proposed Code provides procedural 
safeguards, such as the recording of reasons and the grant of an opportun ity of hearing, it does not 
prescribe any time limit for the duration of supersession. 
 
It was submitted that, in comparison, the Reserve Bank of India Act, 1934 and the Sabka Beema 
Sabki Raksha (Amendment of Insurance Laws) Act, 2025 prescribe a duration for the supersession 
of the governing boards of regulated entities. It was further submi tted that, under laws providing for 
the supersession of regulated entities, such power is ordinarily exercised by the sectoral regulator 
rather than by the Central Government. 
 
It was also clarified that, under the Securities Contracts (Regulation) Act, 1956, the power to 
supersede the governing board of a Market Infrastructure Institution or a Self-Regulatory Organisation 
is vested in the Central Government, although such power is also exercised by SEBI. It was submitted 
that SEBI, being the primary regulator responsible for granting and cancelling certificates of 
registration of Market Infrastructure Institutions and Self -Regulatory Organisations, would be the 
appropriate authority to determine whether the governing board of such an institution or organisation 
should be superseded. 
 
It was further submitted that such power should be invoked only in the rarest of rare and urgent 
circumstances and that vesting the power in the Central Government may delay the process and 
create operational difficulties. Instances cited of the exercise of this power included the supersession 
of the governing bodies of the Pune Stock Exchange, Magadh Stock Exchange, Bhubaneswar Stock 
Exchange, Ahmedabad Stock Exchange and Uttar Pradesh Stock Exchange. 
 

154 
 
30.3  On the issue whether the power to supersede the governing board of a Market Infrastructure 
Institution or a Self-Regulatory Organisation should vest in SEBI, in view of the suggestion received 
from a stakeholder, the Committee sought the views of the Ministry. 
 
The Secretary, Ministry of Finance, while deposing before the Committee, stated as under:— 
 
“We had said that we would accept it. However, the Hon. Committee had suggested that the current 
provision of retaining it with the Central Government may continue. 
 
One of the other discussion points was that, if it is on the recommendation of SEBI, how much 
flexibility can be exercised by the Central Government. Keeping in view both the concerns and the 
suggestions, what we would like to submit is that the power to supersede the Board of an MII may be 
retained with the Central Government, and it may be exercised in consultation with SEBI, rather than 
only on the recommendation of SEBI. 
 
As regards the Board of an SRO, which is a self -regulatory organisation, basically a group such as 
AMFI comprising intermediaries or investors, we would request that this power be left to SEBI itself. 
Since Market Infrastructure Institutions are central to  the functioning of the market, the Central 
Government may retain the power in their case.” 
 
30.4  When asked to examine whether appropriate checks and safeguards, including objective 
grounds, should be prescribed for the exercise of this power and whether the Central Government 
should also be enabled to exercise the power suo motu, the Ministry, in its written submission, stated 
as under:— 
 
Under Clause 44, to supersede the board of the market infrastructure institution, the Central 
Government must act on the recommendation of the Board. Thus, the Central Government cannot 
exercise this power suo motu. To enable the Central Government to exer cise this power suo motu, 
the clause may be suitably amended. 
 
The Hon. Committee has suggested that the exercise of the power to supersede should be— 
 
1. in consultation with the Board; and 
2. in such manner as may be prescribed by the rules, with the safeguards specified therein. 
 
These suggestions are accepted and the proposed formulation is redrafted in the following manner:— 

155 
 
 
44. (1) Without prejudice to any other powers vested in the Central Government under this Code, 
where the Central Government, on its own motion or on the recommendation of the Board, is of the 
opinion that the governing board of a market infrastructure institution or a self-regulatory organisation 
should be superseded, then, notwithstanding anything contained in any other law for the time being 
in force, it may serve on the governing board a written notice that it is considering the supersession 
of the governing board for the reasons specified in the notice and after giving an opportunity of being 
heard, it may, by notification, declare the governing board of such market infrastructure institution or 
self-regulatory organisation to be superseded, and may app oint any person or persons to exercise 
and perform all the powers and duties of the governing board, and, where more persons than one 
person are appointed, may appoint one of such persons to be the chairperson and another to be the 
vice-chairperson thereof, in such manner as may be prescribed, which shall, inter alia, provide— 
 
(a) the grounds, procedure and duration of such supersession; 
 
(b) the members of the governing board which has been superseded shall, from the date of 
supersession, cease to hold office as such members and any other person or persons may be 
appointed to exercise and perform all powers and duties of the governing boar d which has been 
superseded; 
 
(c) the period for which the person or persons appointed under clause (b) shall hold office and any 
extensions required to such period; 
 
(d) the manner of vesting of property of the market infrastructure institution or the self -regulatory 
organisation in such person or persons under clause (b) as being necessary for the purpose of 
enabling him or them to carry on the business of the market infrastructure institution or self-regulatory 
organisation; and 
 
(e) the manner of reconstitution of the governing board after determination of the period of 
appointment of the person or persons appointed under clause (b). 
 
30.5  The Committee have examined Clause 44 and observe that the provision empowers the 
Central Government, on the recommendation of the Board, to supersede the governing board 
of a Market Infrastructure Institution or a Self-Regulatory Organisation. 
 
The Committee further observe that Market Infrastructure Institutions, including stock 
exchanges, depositories and clearing corporations, perform critical functions in the securities 
market and operate under the direct regulatory and supervisory oversight of SEBI. 

156 
 
 
The Committee also note the concern that the provision does not prescribe any maximum 
period for supersession, unlike comparable financial sector legislation, and that stakeholders 
have suggested that the power be vested entirely in SEBI. 
 
30.6  The Committee are, however, of the view that, having regard to the systemic 
importance of Market Infrastructure Institutions, the power to supersede their governing 
boards should continue to vest in the Central Government. 
 
At the same time, the Committee consider that the existing formulation, requiring action only 
“on the recommendation of the Board”, may unduly constrain the Central Government. The 
Committee, therefore, recommend that Clause 44 be modified to provide that the Central 
Government may exercise such power in consultation with the Board, thereby ensuring that 
due weight is accorded to SEBI’s regulatory expertise while preserving the oversight of the 
Central Government in matters of systemic significance. 
 
30.7  The Committee further recommend that, in the case of Self-Regulatory Organisations, 
the power of supersession should vest in SEBI, being the sectoral regulator directly 
responsible for their recognition, supervision and regulation. 
 
The Committee also recommend that the Code prescribe an appropriate maximum period for 
supersession, with provision for extension only for reasons to be recorded in writing, so as 
to ensure that supersession remains a temporary and exceptional measure cons istent with 
the principles of good governance and regulatory accountability. 
  

157 
 
CHAPTER XXXI 
 
XXXI Contracts in Contravention of Bye-laws to Be Void 
 
Clause 47 
 
31.1  Clause 47 provides for contracts in contravention of bye-laws to be void, as under:— 
 
47 Any contract which is in contravention of any bye-laws of a stock exchange specified in that behalf 
shall be void under clause (d) of sub-section (2) of section 39 with respect to— 
 
(a) the rights of any member of the stock exchange who has entered into such contract in 
contravention of such bye-laws; and 
 
(b) the rights of any other person who has knowingly participated in the transaction entailing such 
contravention: 
 
Provided that nothing in this section shall be construed to affect the rights of a person other than a 
member of the stock exchange to enforce any such contract or to recover any sum under or in respect 
of such contract, if such person had no knowledge that the transaction was in contravention of any of 
the bye-laws specified in clause (d) of sub-section (2) of section 39. 
 
31.2  Some stakeholders suggested that the reference to bye -laws “specified in that behalf” be 
replaced with a specific reference to the provision under which such bye -laws are made, so as to 
remove ambiguity and provide greater clarity regarding the bye -laws whose contravention would 
render a contract void. 
 
31.3  When the issue was taken up with the Ministry, the Ministry, in its written reply, stated as 
under:— 
 
“The concern is valid and appropriate drafting changes will be made.” 
 
The Ministry further provided the following formulation for the proposed drafting change:— 

158 
 
 
“47. Contracts in contravention of bye-laws to be void. Any contract which is in contravention of any 
bye-laws of a stock exchange specified under clause (d) of sub-section (2) of section 39 shall be void 
with respect to—" 
31.4  The Committee note that Clause 47 provides that a contract entered into in 
contravention of the bye-laws of a stock exchange specified under clause (d) of sub -section 
(2) of Clause 39 shall be void in respect of the rights of a member of the stock exchange who 
has entered into such contract and the rights of any other person who has knowingly 
participated in the transaction entailing such contravention. 
 
31.5  The Committee observe that the expression “specified in that behalf” in the provision, 
as originally drafted, is ambiguous and does not clearly identify the statutory provision under 
which the relevant bye-laws are made. The Committee are of the view that a specific reference 
to the enabling provision under which such bye -laws are specified would provide greater 
clarity and legal certainty regarding the contracts to which the provision applies. 
 
31.6  The Committee, therefore, recommend that Clause 47 be suitably amended to replace 
the expression “specified in that behalf” with a specific reference to the bye -laws specified 
under clause (d) of sub-section (2) of Clause 39, as proposed by the Ministry. 
  

159 
 
CHAPTER XXXII 
 
XXXII Exemptions for Certain Contracts 
 
Clause 48 
 
32.1  Clause 48 provides for exemptions for certain contracts, as under:— 
 
48 Notwithstanding anything contained in this Code or any other law for the time being in force, a 
contract for the sale or purchase of, or other dealings in, securities falling under any one or more of 
the clauses mentioned hereunder shall not be deemed to be illegal and nothing in this Code shall be 
construed as requiring any person or persons desirous of entering into such contracts to obtain the 
prior permission of the Board, namely:— 
 
(a) a spot delivery contract; 
 
(b) a non-transferable specific delivery contract; 
 
(c) contracts for pre-emption, including right of first refusal, tag-along rights, drag-along rights or such 
other similar rights contained in shareholders’ agreements, investor agreements or the articles of 
association of companies or other body corporate; 
 
(d) contracts in shareholders’ agreements or the articles of association of companies or other body 
corporate for the purchase or sale of securities pursuant to the exercise of an option contained therein 
to buy or sell the securities, where— 
 
(i) the title to and ownership of the underlying securities is held continuously by the selling party to 
such contract for a minimum period of one year from the date of entering into the contract; 
 
(ii) the price or consideration payable for the sale or purchase of the underlying securities pursuant 
to the exercise of any option contained therein is in compliance with the applicable laws for the time 
being in force; and 
 
(iii) the contract is settled by way of actual delivery of the underlying securities; 

160 
 
 
(e) standing agreements or arrangements for the delivery or supply of commodities; 
 
(f) contracts, including derivatives under the provisions of Chapter IIID of the Reserve Bank of India 
Act, 1934; or 
 
(g) contracts between such parties relating to such matters, on such terms and conditions as the 
Central Government may, by notification, specify. 
 
32.2  Some stakeholders suggested that Clause 48(f) be amended by inserting a comma after the 
word “derivatives”, so that the provision reads: “contracts, including derivatives, under the provisions 
of Chapter IIID of the Reserve Bank of India Act, 1934; or” 
 
32.3  On the issue raised regarding the proposed drafting change in Clause 48(f) to include a 
comma after the word “derivatives”, the Ministry, in its written reply, acknowledged the concern and 
stated as under:— 
 
“The concern is valid and appropriate drafting changes will be made.” 
 
32.4  The Committee note that Clause 48 provides for exemptions in respect of certain 
contracts, including contracts and derivatives under the provisions of Chapter IIID of the 
Reserve Bank of India Act, 1934. 
 
32.5  The Committee observe that the absence of a comma after the word “derivatives” in 
Clause 48(f) may create ambiguity in the grammatical construction and interpretation of the 
provision. The Committee are of the view that the provision should be appropriately 
punctuated to make it clear that the exemption applies to “contracts, including derivatives, 
under the provisions of Chapter IIID of the Reserve Bank of India Act, 1934”. 
 
32.6  The Committee, therefore, recommend that Clause 48(f) be suitably amended by 
inserting a comma after the word “derivatives” to improve the clarity and grammatical 
precision of the provision. 
  

161 
 
CHAPTER XXXIII 
 
XXXIII Derivatives in Respect of Commodity 
 
Clause 49 
 
33.1  Clause 49 provides for derivatives in respect of commodity, as under:— 
 
49. The Central Government may, in consultation with the Board, notify such commodities in respect 
of which a derivative may be entered into or made by any person. 
 
33.2  Some stakeholders suggested that the following proviso be inserted:— 
 
“Provided that where such contract is— 
 
(i) regulated by the International Financial Services Centres Authority established under section 4 of 
the International Financial Services Centres Authority Act, 2019; and 
 
(ii) issued in an International Financial Services Centre by, or involves at least one of the parties 
thereto being, a financial institution established in an International Financial Services Centre, the 
goods covered under such contract shall be such as a re otherwise eligible for commodity trading 
under the regulatory framework of the International Financial Services Centres Authority.” 
 
It was submitted that, at present, the list consists of only 104 commodities in respect of which 
derivatives may be issued. Since commodity trading is expected to be enabled in the International 
Financial Services Centre (IFSC) shortly, it was suggested th at the scope of commodities on which 
derivatives may be issued in the IFSC should be expanded. Accordingly, the power to notify such 
commodities, insofar as commodity derivatives in the IFSC are concerned, may be vested in the 
International Financial Services Centres Authority (IFSCA). 
 
33.3  When asked whether the power to notify the list of commodities, insofar as commodity 
derivatives in the International Financial Services Centre are concerned, may be vested in the 
International Financial Services Centres Authority, the Ministry, during th e deliberations, stated as 
under:— 
 

162 
 
“Although everywhere we are saying ‘subject to the current law and the Code’, in Clause 49 we have 
stated that we will accept this language and make the appropriate modifications because this 
provision does not presently contain such language.” 
 
33.4  The Committee have examined Clause 49 and observe that it empowers the Central 
Government, in consultation with the Board, to notify the commodities in respect of which 
derivative contracts may be entered into or made. 
 
The Committee, however, observe that the provision does not expressly recognise the distinct 
regulatory framework applicable to commodity derivatives traded in the International 
Financial Services Centre (IFSC), which are regulated by the International Fin ancial Services 
Centres Authority (IFSCA). The Committee are of the view that, in the absence of an enabling 
provision, the development of commodity derivatives in the IFSC may remain constrained by 
the list of commodities notified for the domestic market,  notwithstanding the separate 
regulatory architecture established under the International Financial Services Centres 
Authority Act, 2019. 
 
The Committee, therefore, recommend that Clause 49 be suitably amended to provide that, in 
respect of commodity derivatives regulated by the International Financial Services Centres 
Authority and traded in an International Financial Services Centre, the po wer to notify the 
eligible commodities may be exercised by the International Financial Services Centres 
Authority in accordance with the applicable legal and regulatory framework. 
  

163 
 
CHAPTER XXXIV 
 
XXXIV Non-transferable Specific Delivery Contract, Spot Delivery Contract and Transferable 
Specific Delivery Contract 
 
Clause 50 
 
34.1  Clause 50 provides for non -transferable specific delivery contracts, spot delivery contracts 
and transferable specific delivery contracts, as under:— 
 
50. (1) If the Central Government is of the opinion that, in the interest of trade or in the public interest, 
it is expedient to regulate and control non -transferable specific delivery contracts in commodities or 
securities or the business of dealing in spot delivery contracts, it may, by notification, declare that all 
or any of the provisions of this Code shall apply to— 
 
(a) such class or classes of non -transferable specific delivery contracts in respect of such 
commodities or securities or class or classes of commodities or securities; and 
 
(b) such class or classes of spot delivery contracts for the sale or purchase of such securities as may 
be specified therein. 
 
Explanation.—For the purposes of this Chapter, the expressions— 
 
(i) “specific delivery contract” means a derivative which provides for the actual delivery of specific 
qualities or types of underlying during a stipulated future period at a price fixed thereby or to be fixed 
in the manner thereby agreed and in which the names of both the buyer and the seller are mentioned; 
 
(ii) “non -transferable specific delivery contract” means a specific delivery contract, the rights or 
liabilities under which, or under any delivery order, railway receipt, bill of lading, warehouse receipt 
or any other document of title relating thereto, are not transferable; 
 
(iii) “spot delivery contract” means a contract which is not executed on a stock exchange and provides 
for— 
 

164 
 
(a) actual delivery of securities and payment of the price, either on the same day as the date of the 
contract or on the next day, the actual period taken for the despatch of the securities or the remittance 
of money through post being excluded from the computation of the aforesaid period, if the parties to 
the contract do not reside in the same town or locality; or 
 
(b) actual delivery of commodities and payment of the price therefor, either immediately or within such 
period, not exceeding eleven days after the date of the contract, inclusive of the actual period taken 
for the despatch of the commodities through post, road or any other physical means, and subject to 
such conditions as the Central Government may notify in respect of any commodity, where the period 
under such contract is not capable of extension by the mutual consent of the parties or otherwise, 
 
but shall not include such contracts performed, either wholly or in part— 
 
(i) by realisation of any sum of money being the difference between the contract rate and the 
settlement rate or clearing rate or the rate of any offsetting contract; or 
 
(ii) by any other means whatsoever, as a result of which the actual tendering of the goods covered 
by the contract or payment of the full price therefor is dispensed with. 
 
(2) A transferable specific delivery contract shall be subject to the provisions of this Code and shall 
be entered into or made on a stock exchange, except for such contracts as are specified under 
Chapter IIID of the Reserve Bank of India Act, 1934. 
 
Explanation.—For the purposes of this Chapter, “transferable specific delivery contract” means a 
specific delivery contract which is not a non -transferable specific delivery contract and is subject to 
such conditions relating to its transferability as may be notified by the Central Government. 
 
34.2  Some stakeholders suggested that clause (b) of section 2(i) of the Securities Contracts 
(Regulation) Act, 1956 should be retained in the Securities Markets Code in its existing form. 
 
It was further suggested that, since the definitions presently contained in the Explanations are cross-
referred to in other provisions of the Code, including the definition clause, they should appropriately 
be incorporated in Clause 2 itself. 
 
In support of the suggestion, it was submitted that the definition of “spot delivery contract” in the 
proposed Code does not include the transfer of securities by a depository from the account of one 

165 
 
beneficial owner to the account of another beneficial owner where such securities are dealt with by a 
depository, as presently provided under section 2(i)(b) of the Securities Contracts (Regulation) Act, 
1956. Consequently, the proposed definition does not  encompass transfers effected through the 
depository system. 
 
34.3  When the Committee took up the matter, the Ministry, in its written reply, stated as under:— 
 
“Appropriate drafting changes will be made. The following language of clause 2(i)(b) of the Securities 
Contracts (Regulation) Act, 1956 may be added:— 
 
‘(b) transfer of the securities by the depository from the account of a beneficial owner to the account 
of another beneficial owner when such securities are dealt with by a depository;’” 
 
34.4  The Committee have carefully examined Clause 50 and observe that the existing 
formulation of the definition of “spot delivery contract” does not expressly recognise 
transfers of securities effected through the depository system from the account of one 
beneficial owner to the account of another beneficial owner, as presently provided under 
section 2(i)(b) of the Securities Contracts (Regulation) Act, 1956. 
 
The Committee further observe that this omission may create ambiguity regarding the legal 
recognition of transfers undertaken through the depository mechanism. 
 
The Committee also note that certain definitions presently incorporated as Explanations to 
Clause 50 are referred to in other provisions of the Code and, therefore, may more 
appropriately be placed in the definition clause to ensure greater consistency and  ease of 
interpretation. 
 
The Committee, therefore, recommend that Clause 50 be suitably amended to incorporate the 
existing language contained in section 2(i)(b) of the Securities Contracts (Regulation) Act, 
1956 relating to transfers of securities through the depository system. The Committee further 
recommend that the relevant definitions presently contained in the Explanations to Clause 50 
be appropriately relocated to Clause 2 of the Code to ensure uniformity and facilitate 
interpretation. 
  

166 
 
CHAPTER XXXV 
 
XXXV Prohibition of Performance of Contracts without Registration 
 
Clause 51 
 
35.1  Clause 51 provides for the prohibition of performance of contracts without registration, as 
under:— 
 
51  No person shall organise or assist in organising or become a member of any association or entity 
in any area which provides facilities for the performance of any contracts in securities, without being 
registered with the Board or permitted under Chapter IIID of the Reserve Bank of India Act, 1934 or 
specifically exempted by the Central Government by notification. 
 
35.2  Some experts expressed the view that the existing provision of the Securities Contracts 
(Regulation) Act, 1956 should be reinstated by inserting the words “assisting in, entering into or” after 
the words “which provides facilities for”. 
 
It was submitted that Clause 51 of the Securities Markets Code proposes the deletion of the 
expressions “assisting in” and “entering into”, which may enable unregistered platforms to adopt 
alternative business models by facilitating transactions as marketp laces without obtaining the 
requisite permission or undertaking activities otherwise not statutorily permitted. 
 
 
35.3  On the specific suggestion of the stakeholders for reinstating the existing provision of the 
Securities Contracts (Regulation) Act, 1956, the Ministry concurred with the suggestion and stated 
that appropriate drafting changes would be carried out. 
 
35.4  The Committee have examined Clause 51 and observe that it seeks to prohibit any 
person from organising or becoming a member of any association or entity which provides 
facilities for the performance of contracts in securities, unless such association or e ntity is 
registered with the Board, permitted under Chapter IIID of the Reserve Bank of India Act, 1934 
or specifically exempted by the Central Government. 
 

167 
 
The Committee, however, observe that the proposed provision omits the expressions 
“assisting in” and “entering into”, which are contained in the corresponding provision of the 
Securities Contracts (Regulation) Act, 1956. 
 
The Committee are of the view that the omission of these expressions may inadvertently 
narrow the scope of the prohibition and create potential regulatory gaps. In particular, entities 
operating unregistered electronic platforms, digital marketplaces or ot her intermediary 
arrangements may seek to structure their activities in a manner that facilitates or assists the 
performance of contracts in securities without directly organising such transactions, thereby 
circumventing the regulatory framework envisaged under the Code. 
 
The Committee further observe that such an interpretation may give rise to regulatory 
arbitrage and undermine the objective of ensuring that all activities relating to the 
performance of contracts in securities are carried out only through duly regulated entities. 
 
35.5  The Committee, therefore, recommend that Clause 51 be suitably amended by restoring 
the expressions “assisting in” and “entering into” after the words “which provides facilities 
for”. 
 
The Committee are of the view that the proposed amendment would preserve the existing 
legislative intent under the Securities Contracts (Regulation) Act, 1956, comprehensively 
capture all activities relating to the performance of contracts in securities, p revent the 
emergence of unregulated business models, and ensure effective regulatory oversight of 
securities market activities. 
  

168 
 
CHAPTER XXXVI 
 
XXXVI Rights of Holder of Securities to Dividends and Income in Respect of Securities 
 
Clause 53 
 
36.1  Clause 53 provides for the rights of holders of securities to dividends and income in respect 
of securities, as under:— 
 
53 (1) It shall be lawful for the holder of any security whose name appears in the books of the issuer 
issuing the said security to receive and retain any dividend or profit or income or return of any kind 
declared by the issuer in respect thereof for any year, irrespective of the fact that the said security 
has already been transferred by it for consideration, unless the transferee, who claims such dividend 
or profit or income or return from the transferor, has lodged the security and all other documents 
relating to the transfer, as may be required by the issuer, with the issuer for being registered in its 
name within a period of fifteen days from the date on which the dividend or profit or income or return 
became due. 
 
(2) The period specified in sub-section (1) shall be extended— 
 
(a) in the case of the death of the transferee, by the actual period taken by the legal representative 
to establish the claim to the dividend or profit or income or return; 
 
(b) in the case of loss of the transfer deed by theft or any other cause beyond the control of the 
transferee, by the actual period taken for its replacement; and 
 
(c) in the case of delay in the lodging of any security and other documents relating to the transfer due 
to reasons connected with the post, by the actual period of such delay. 
 
(3) Nothing contained in sub-section (1) shall affect— 
 
(a) the right of an issuer to pay any dividend or profit or income or return which has become due to 
any person whose name is, for the time being, registered in the books of the issuer as the holder of 
the security in respect of which such dividend or profit or income or return has become due; or 
 

169 
 
(b) the right of the transferee of any security to enforce against the transferor or any other person any 
rights in relation to the transfer, where the issuer has refused to register the transfer of the security in 
the name of the transferee. 
 
36.2  Certain experts expressed concern that the provision does not adequately reflect the near -
complete dematerialisation of securities traded or held in the Indian securities market, as it continues 
to employ concepts relating to physical transfers, lodgement of securities and registration of transfers. 
 
It was submitted that the provision should be suitably updated, as physical holdings of securities have 
become exceedingly rare and exceptional, and the regulatory framework governing the transfer and 
transmission of securities has evolved to facilitate holdings in dematerialised form. 
 
36.3  On being asked whether the provision may be aligned with the framework governing transfers 
of securities in dematerialised form, the Ministry agreed with the suggestion and stated that 
appropriate drafting changes would be made to the provision in terms o f its applicability to 
dematerialised shares. 
 
36.4  The Committee have examined Clause 53 and note that it seeks to provide for the rights 
of holders of securities to receive dividends, profits, income or other returns in respect of 
securities transferred by them. 
 
The Committee, however, observe that the provision continues to employ concepts such as 
lodgement of securities, transfer deeds and registration of transfers, which are primarily 
associated with physical securities. Given the near -complete dematerialisation of securities 
in the Indian securities market and the evolution of the regulatory framework governing the 
transfer and transmission of securities through the depository system, the Committee are of 
the view that the provision does not adequately reflect the prevailing market practice. 
 
36.5  The Committee, therefore, recommend that Clause 53 be suitably revised to align its 
provisions with the framework governing securities held and transferred in dematerialised 
form. The Committee further recommend that references to physical transfer proced ures, 
wherever no longer relevant, be appropriately modified so as to ensure consistency with the 
dematerialised securities regime and provide greater clarity and certainty in the 
implementation of the Code. 
  

170 
 
CHAPTER XXXVII 
 
XXXVII Holding of Securities 
 
Power of Board in Respect of Depository Services 
 
Clause 55 
 
37.1  Clause 55 provides for the power of the Board in respect of depository services, as under:— 
 
55 (1) All securities or other regulated instruments held by a depository shall be dematerialised and 
in a fungible form. 
 
(2) Every issuer offering securities for subscription to any person shall allot such securities in 
dematerialised form and every person subscribing to securities offered by an issuer shall hold such 
securities with a depository: 
 
Provided that nothing in this sub-section shall affect the right of a person to continue holding securities 
issued before the commencement of this Code in physical form unless such person intends to sell or 
transfer such securities, in which case such securities shall first be dematerialised. 
 
(3) Where securities are held with a depository under sub -section (2), the issuer shall inform the 
depository of the details of allotment of the securities, and, on receipt of such information, the 
depository shall enter the name of the allottee as the beneficial owner of those securities in its records. 
 
(4) Nothing contained in sections 88 and 89 of the Companies Act, 2013 shall apply to a depository 
in respect of securities or other regulated instruments held by it on behalf of the beneficial owners. 
 
37.2  Some stakeholders suggested that a proviso be inserted in Clause 55 to provide that nothing 
contained therein shall apply to units of mutual funds. 
 
It was submitted that, keeping in view the critical role of mutual funds in promoting financial inclusion 
in India, particularly in tier -II and tier -III cities, it has not been made mandatory for mutual fund 
unitholders to open demat accounts. At present, mutual fund units may be held through Statements 

171 
 
of Account, and most investors continue to hold their units in that form. Considering the costs 
associated with opening and maintaining demat accounts and the objective of promoting financial 
inclusion, it was suggested that Clause 55 should not apply to units of mutual funds. 
 
 
37.3  On the suggestion regarding the non -mandatory requirement of demat accounts for mutual 
fund unitholders and the need to exempt units of mutual funds from the applicability of Clause 55 in 
view of financial inclusion considerations and the costs associated  with opening and maintaining 
demat accounts, the Ministry agreed with the concerns raised and stated that appropriate drafting 
changes would be made. 
 
37.4  The Committee have examined Clause 55 and note that it seeks to mandate that all 
securities or other regulated instruments held through a depository shall be in dematerialised 
form. The Committee further note that the provision requires every person subsc ribing to 
securities to hold such securities with a depository. 
 
The Committee, however, observe that the mandatory requirement of holding securities in 
dematerialised form may have unintended implications for mutual fund unitholders, who are 
presently permitted to hold units in the form of Statements of Account without  the need to 
open and maintain demat accounts. 
 
The Committee are of the view that mandating demat accounts for mutual fund units may 
increase compliance costs for small investors and could adversely affect financial inclusion, 
particularly in tier -II and tier -III cities, where a significant number of r etail investors access 
mutual fund products through the existing non-demat framework. 
 
37.5  The Committee, therefore, recommend that Clause 55 be suitably amended to 
expressly exempt units of mutual funds from the mandatory requirement of holding securities 
through a depository. Such an exemption would preserve the existing investor -friendly 
framework for mutual fund investments, promote financial inclusion and avoid imposing 
additional compliance costs on retail investors, while continuing to provide investors with the 
option of holding mutual fund units in dematerialised form if they so choose. 
  

172 
 
CHAPTER XXXVIII 
 
XXXVIII Registration of Transfer of Security, etc. 
 
Clause 58 
 
38.1  Clause 58 provides for the registration of transfer of security, etc., as under:— 
 
58  (1) Every depository shall, on receipt of information from a depository participant, register the 
transfer of a security or other regulated instrument in the name of the transferee. 
 
(2) The records of a depository in respect of any security or other regulated instrument shall be 
conclusive proof of the title of the transferee over such security or other regulated instrument, as the 
case may be. 
 
(3) Every depository shall, on receipt of information from a depository participant, register any transfer 
of a security in favour of an asset reconstruction company along with, or consequent upon, the 
transfer or assignment of a financial asset of any bank or financial institution under sub-section (1) of 
section 5 of the Securitisation and Reconstruction of Financial Assets and Enforcement of Security 
Interest Act, 2002. 
 
(4) Every depository shall, on receipt of information from a depository participant, register any issue 
of new shares in favour of any bank or financial institution or asset reconstruction company or any 
other assignee of such bank, financial institution or asset reconstruction company, as the case may 
be, by conversion of part of their debt into shares pursuant to the reconstruction of debt of the 
company agreed between the company and the bank, financial institution or asset reconstruction 
company. 
 
Explanation.—For the purposes of this section, the expressions “asset reconstruction company”, 
“bank” and “financial institution” shall have the same meanings as assigned to them respectively in 
clauses (ba), (c) and (m) of sub -section (1) of section 2 of the Securitisation and Reconstruction of 
Financial Assets and Enforcement of Security Interest Act, 2002. 
 
38.2  Certain experts expressed concern that sub-clause (2) of Clause 58 should be modified, as, 
in its present form, it may not adequately account for necessary safeguards. It was submitted that the 

173 
 
provision does not address circumstances such as fraud, forgery, coercion, manifest error or technical 
glitches. 
 
It was further submitted that the provision may be suitably revised, particularly since Clause 62(6) 
provides that “the depository shall indemnify such beneficial owner for any loss caused to the 
beneficial owner due to the negligence of the depository or its member.” It was pointed out that the 
expression “loss caused due to the negligence” may be inconsistent with treating the depository’s 
records as “conclusive proof” of the transferee’s title. 
 
 
38.3  On the concerns raised regarding the adequacy of safeguards under Clause 58(2), particularly 
that the provision, in its present form, does not adequately account for circumstances such as fraud, 
forgery, coercion, manifest error or technical glitches, and  that treating the depository’s records as 
“conclusive proof” may be inconsistent with the depository’s obligation under Clause 62(6) to 
indemnify beneficial owners for losses caused due to negligence, the Ministry agreed with the 
concerns raised and stated that appropriate drafting changes would be made. 
  
38.4  The Committee have examined Clause 58 and note that it seeks to accord conclusive 
evidentiary value to the records of a depository in respect of the title of the transferee over 
securities or other regulated instruments. 
 
While the provision is intended to promote certainty and efficiency in the transfer of securities 
held through the depository system, the Committee observe that it does not expressly provide 
for exceptional circumstances such as fraud, forgery, coercion, m anifest error or technical 
glitches. 
 
The Committee further observe that the indemnification mechanism provided under Clause 
62(6) for losses arising from the negligence of the depository may require suitable 
harmonisation with the conclusive evidentiary value accorded to depository records under 
Clause 58(2). 
 
The Committee, therefore, recommend that Clause 58(2) be suitably amended to incorporate 
an enabling provision permitting rectification of depository records in cases involving fraud, 
forgery, coercion, manifest error, technical glitches or other exception al circumstances. The 
Committee further recommend that the provision be appropriately harmonised with the 
indemnification framework under Clause 62(6), so as to ensure consistency in the statutory 
framework, adequately safeguard the rights of beneficial ow ners, and preserve the integrity 
and reliability of the depository system. 
  

174 
 
CHAPTER XXXIX 
 
XXXIX Pledge or Hypothecation of Securities or Other Regulated Instruments Held through 
a Depository 
 
Clause 59 
 
39.1  Clause 59 provides for the pledge or hypothecation of securities or other regulated 
instruments held through a depository, as under:— 
 
59 (1) Subject to such regulations and bye -laws as may be made in this behalf, a beneficial owner 
may, with the previous approval of the depository, create a pledge or hypothecation in respect of a 
security or other regulated instrument owned by it through a depository. 
 
(2) Every beneficial owner shall give information of such pledge or hypothecation to the depository, 
and the depository shall thereupon make entries in its records accordingly, and such entry shall be 
conclusive proof of such pledge or hypothecation, as the case may be. 
 
39.2  Certain stakeholders suggested that the following proviso be inserted in sub-clause (2):— 
 
“Provided, however, that the conclusive proof of title shall be subject to such transfers being valid and 
lawful and not having been obtained by an act of fraud, malfeasance or misfeasance.” 
 
It was submitted that, under the Depositories Act, 1996, section 7 relating to transfer and section 12 
relating to pledge or hypothecation do not recognise the records of a depository as conclusive proof 
of ownership. It was further submitted that the proposed Code seeks to accord enhanced evidentiary 
value to the records of the depository but does not adequately account for cases involving fraudulent 
transfers, in respect of which the regulators or market infrastructure institutions may not have direct 
oversight. 
 
Accordingly, it was suggested that an appropriate proviso be inserted to safeguard the interests of 
depositories and depository participants in cases involving unauthorised, fraudulent or dishonest 
transfers. 
 
 

175 
 
39.3  On the issue of the adequacy of safeguards under Clause 59(2), the Ministry agreed with the 
concerns raised and stated that appropriate drafting changes would be made. 
 
39.4  The Committee have examined Clause 59 relating to the pledge or hypothecation of 
securities or other regulated instruments held through a depository and note that it provides 
that the entries made by the depository in its records shall constitute conclusive proof of such 
pledge or hypothecation. 
 
The Committee further note that, unlike the existing provisions of the Depositories Act, 1996, 
the proposed provision accords conclusive evidentiary value to the records of the depository 
without expressly recognising situations where such entries may have  arisen on account of 
fraud, malfeasance, misfeasance or other unauthorised or unlawful transfers. 
 
39.5  The Committee are of the view that, while according conclusive evidentiary value to 
the records of the depository would promote certainty and efficiency in the securities market, 
the provision should also incorporate adequate safeguards to address except ional 
circumstances involving fraudulent or unlawful transactions. 
 
The Committee, therefore, recommend that Clause 59(2) be suitably amended by 
incorporating an appropriate proviso clarifying that the conclusive evidentiary value of entries 
in the records of the depository shall be subject to the pledge or hypothecation h aving been 
validly created and not obtained through fraud, malfeasance, misfeasance or any other 
unlawful means, so as to adequately protect the interests of beneficial owners, depositories 
and depository participants while preserving the integrity of the depository system. 
  

176 
 
CHAPTER XL 
 
XL Public Issue and Listing of Securities 
 
Clause 64 
 
40.1  Clause 64 of the Bill provides for public issue and listing of securities. The provisions of the 
Bill are reproduced below:— 
 
64 (1) Without prejudice to the provisions of this Code or any other law for the time being in force, no 
securities shall be offered to the public or listed on a stock exchange unless the issuer fulfils such 
eligibility criteria and complies with such requirements as may be specified by regulations. 
 
(2) Every issuer intending to offer securities to the public or for listing shall, before issuing the offer 
document, make an application to each stock exchange for permission to list and shall comply with 
such conditions as may be specified by regulations. 
 
(3) Where securities are listed on any stock exchange, such person shall comply with the conditions 
of the listing agreement with that stock exchange and such other conditions as may be specified by 
regulations. 
 
(4) Where the permission under sub -section (2) has not been granted or refused, the issuer shall 
forthwith repay all money, if any, received from the applicant within such period and in such manner 
as may be specified by regulations, and the issuer and every director or trustee thereof, as the case 
may be, who is in default shall be jointly and severally liable to repay that money. 
 
(5) The Board may, by general or special order— 
 
(a) prohibit any company from issuing a prospectus, any offer document or advertisement soliciting 
money from the public for the issue of securities; or 
 
(b) specify the conditions subject to which the prospectus, offer document or advertisement, if not 
prohibited, may be issued. 
 

177 
 
40.2  Some stakeholders suggested that greater clarity be provided regarding the timelines and 
procedures for the issuance of offer documents and the submission of IPO listing applications. 
 
The Ministry, in its reply, stated that the provision is based on section 17A(2) of the Securities 
Contracts (Regulation) Act, 1956, which requires an application to be made to a stock exchange. The 
Ministry further stated that the Board has been empowered  to make regulations specifying the 
eligibility criteria and other requirements relating to public issues and the listing of securities. The 
detailed timelines and procedures may, therefore, be specified through regulations. It was also stated 
that, at present, the detailed procedural requirements are contained in the Securities and Exchange 
Board of India (Issue of Capital and Disclosure Requirements) Regulations, 2018. 
 
40.3  One stakeholder suggested that the words “each stock exchange” in Clause 64(2) be replaced 
with “one or more stock exchanges” to clarify that an issuer may choose to list its securities on one 
or more selected stock exchanges, rather than being required to seek listing on every stock exchange. 
 
It was further submitted that, in sub-clause (5)(a) of Clause 64, the term “company” has been used. 
Since the term “company” has not been defined in the Bill, the definition under the Companies Act, 
2013 would apply, thereby making the provision restrictive and excluding other entities such as body 
corporates, REITs and InvITs, which may also be listed under the proposed Code. It was, therefore, 
suggested that the term “issuer” may be used in place of “company” so that the Board’s powers are 
not unduly restricted. 
 
40.4  During the course of evidence, the Secretary, Department of Economic Affairs, on the issue 
of the use of the expressions “each stock exchange” and “company”, stated as under:— 
 
“Seeking approval from each stock exchange was never the legislative intention. We intend to amend 
it to provide that the application will be made only to those stock exchanges where the issuer intends 
to list. It is not necessary to seek approval from all  stock exchanges. The second suggestion under 
this clause has also been accepted, wherein the term ‘company’ will be replaced by the word 
‘person’.” 
 
40.5  On the suggestion regarding the introduction of a fast -track approval mechanism for IPOs 
meeting specified eligibility thresholds, the Ministry stated that, under Clauses 64 and 65, the Board 
has been vested with broad regulation -making powers to govern v arious aspects relating to public 
issues and listing. Such requirements may be specified at the implementation stage, if considered 
necessary. 
 

178 
 
40.6  Some stakeholders also suggested that the Board be empowered to specify categories of 
issuers that may not be permitted to list or make further issues of securities on account of activities 
carried on in contravention of Government policy. 
 
The Ministry, in its detailed reply, stated as under:— 
 
“Clause 64(1) already provides regulation-making power to the Board to govern eligibility criteria and 
other requirements to be complied with by an issuer in relation to an issue. Further, Clause 64(5) 
empowers the Board to prohibit any company from issuing offer documents or soliciting money from 
the public for the issue of securities, and to specify the conditions subject to which such documents 
may be issued. Clause 64 also provides that every issuer intending to offer securities to the public or 
for listing shall make an application to each stock exchange for permission to list and shall comply 
with such conditions as may be specified by regulations. Further, Clause 65 empowers the Board to 
make regulations governing requirements for issuers in matters r elating to, inter alia, corporate 
governance requirements, disclosures by issuers and any other matters incidental thereto. Clause 39 
empowers a market infrastructure institution (in this case, a stock exchange) to make bye -laws 
governing conditions such a s norms for fair and time -bound disclosure of material information by 
issuers, the terms and conditions for accessing any services provided by the market infrastructure 
institution, etc. In light of these provisions, such requirements may be provided at the implementation 
stage, if required.” 
 
40.7  The Committee have examined Clause 64 of the Bill and note that it provides that 
securities may be offered to the public or listed on a stock exchange only after the issuer 
fulfils the specified eligibility criteria and complies with the applicable requirements. 
 
The Committee further note that the existing provision relating to listing is based on section 
17A(2) of the Securities Contracts (Regulation) Act, 1956, which requires an application to be 
made to a stock exchange. The Committee also note that the Board h as been empowered to 
specify, by regulations, the eligibility criteria and other requirements relating to public issues 
and the listing of securities. 
 
During the course of examination, the Committee were given to understand that an issuer 
intending to offer securities to the public is required to make an application only to those 
stock exchanges where it proposes to list its securities before issuing the offer document. 
However, the Committee observe that the drafting of Clause 64(2) gives the impression that 
an issuer is required to submit an application to every stock exchange. 
 
The Committee, therefore, recommend that suitable drafting changes be made to Clause 64(2) 
so as to clearly reflect the legislative intent. 

179 
 
 
40.8  The Committee further note that the term “company” has been used in Clause 64(5)(a), 
although it has not been defined in the Bill. The Committee observe that, in such a situation, 
the definition under the Companies Act, 2013 would apply, thereby restricting the scope of the 
provision and excluding other entities such as body corporates, REITs and InvITs, which may 
also be listed under this Code. 
 
The Committee are of the considered view that the word “company” occurring in Clause 
64(5)(a) should be replaced with an appropriate expression so as to enable the Board to 
regulate all listed entities. 
 
40.9  The Committee note with concern that the detailed timelines and procedures 
governing the issuance of offer documents, submission of listing applications, the fast-track 
approval mechanism for initial public offers and the framework relating to issuers wh o may 
not be permitted to list or make further issues of securities on account of serious governance 
contraventions have not been incorporated in the Bill. 
 
The Committee further note the Ministry’s submission that these matters may be provided for 
at the implementation stage by exercising the regulation -making powers available under 
Clauses 64 and 65. 
 
The Committee, therefore, recommend that the necessary regulations in this regard be framed 
within a definite time frame so as to address these issues at the earliest. 
  

180 
 
CHAPTER XLI 
 
XLI Listing and Disclosure Requirements 
 
Clause 65 
 
41.1  Clause 65 of the Bill provides for listing and disclosure requirements, as under:— 
 
65 (1) The requirements for issuers in matters relating to issue of capital, minimum offer size, issue 
and transfer of securities, corporate governance requirements, disclosures by issuers, substantial 
acquisition of shares and takeover of companies, publi c shareholding norms, concentration norms, 
prudential norms, risk management and any other matters incidental thereto shall be such as may be 
specified by regulations. 
 
(2) Without prejudice to anything contained in sub -section (1), the Central Government may, by 
notification, in the public interest, exempt any listed public sector company from any or all of the 
requirements specified in sub-section (1). 
 
Explanation.—For the purposes of this sub-section, “public sector company” means a body corporate 
constituted by an Act of Parliament or any State Legislature and includes a Government company as 
defined in clause (45) of section 2 of the Companies Act, 2013. 
 
41.2  Some stakeholders expressed concern that the exemption proposed for public sector 
companies under Clause 65(2) amounts to discriminatory treatment of listed companies and may 
adversely affect the securities market. 
 
The Ministry stated that the scope of the exemption available to public sector companies may be 
suitably restricted and that appropriate drafting changes would be made. 
 
41.3  During the course of evidence, the Committee desired to know why public sector companies 
should not be required to comply with the same standards as any other listed company in matters 
relating to raising capital. In response, the Secretary stated as under:— 
 
“We checked a few other statutes like the Companies Act and the Competition Act. Some exemptions 
are provided there as well.” 

181 
 
 
41.4  During the deliberations, it emerged that the Competition Act does not exempt public sector 
companies from its substantive provisions. While the Government may choose to retain monopolies 
in certain sectors as a matter of public policy, the issue under co nsideration relates to the raising of 
capital from the securities market. 
 
41.5  On being asked about the reasons for retaining Clause 65(2) in the Bill, the Secretary, during 
the course of evidence, stated as under:— 
 
“.. because sometimes Central Public Sector Companies (CPSCs) get listed and, going forward, they 
may need some exemptions on a case-to-case basis.” 
 
With regard to the current practice, the Secretary further stated:— 
 
“Sir, right now, the formulation is that CPSCs which get listed, if they need an exemption, they have 
to approach SEBI. Under these regulations, including the LODR Regulations, they seek an 
exemption, which SEBI grants.” 
 
41.6  The Committee have examined Clause 65 of the Bill and note that it empowers the 
Board to specify, by regulations, the listing and disclosure requirements applicable to issuers. 
The Committee further note that the Clause empowers the Central Government to exempt any 
listed public sector company, by notification in the public interest, from any or all of such 
requirements. 
 
41.7  The Committee observe that Clause 65(2) of the Bill provides for exemption of public 
sector companies from any or all of the listing and disclosure requirements. The Committee 
further observe that the principles governing such exemptions have been left to the Executive. 
 
The Committee do not find merit in comparing the exemptions available to public sector or 
State-owned companies under statutes such as the Companies Act or the Competition Act, 
as the objectives of those enactments are materially different from those of th e proposed 
Securities Markets Code. 
 
41.8  The Committee note from the submissions of the Ministry that there may be 
circumstances in which public sector undertakings may require exemptions in the public 
interest. 

182 
 
 
The Committee are of the view that, instead of providing a broad statutory exemption merely 
on account of Government ownership, a narrow, tailored and time -bound carve-out in the 
public interest may be provided, where warranted, for specified debt -laden or fi nancially 
distressed public sector undertakings. 
 
41.9  The Committee are of the opinion that, as a general principle, similar listing and 
disclosure requirements should apply equally to public sector and private sector listed 
companies. 
 
The Committee observe that Clause 65(2), in its present form, confers a blanket power to 
exempt all public sector companies from the mandatory listing and disclosure requirements. 
The Committee are of the view that, in the interest of the securities market, the scope of such 
exemption should be appropriately narrowed. 
 
The Committee, therefore, recommend that any exemption from the listing and disclosure 
requirements for public sector undertakings should be granted only on a case-to-case basis, 
in the public interest, for reasons to be recorded in writing. The Committee further recommend 
that the Explanation to Clause 65(2) be suitably redrafted accordingly. 
  

183 
 
CHAPTER XLII 
 
XLII Delisting of Securities 
 
Clause 66 
 
42.1  Clause 66 of the Bill provides for the delisting of securities, as under:— 
 
66 (1) An issuer may apply to a stock exchange to get its securities delisted from such stock exchange 
in such manner as may be specified by regulations. 
 
(2) Without prejudice to sub-section (1), a stock exchange may delist the securities of an issuer, after 
recording the reasons therefor, for continued contravention of any listing conditions or on any of the 
grounds as may be specified by regulations: 
 
Provided that the securities of an issuer shall not be delisted unless such issuer has been given an 
opportunity of being heard. 
 
42.2  On the issue of investor safeguards during the delisting process, the Ministry, during the 
course of evidence, submitted that the process of voluntary and compulsory delisting is presently 
governed by the SEBI (Delisting of Equity Shares) Regulations, 202 1 and the SEBI (Listing 
Obligations and Disclosure Requirements) Regulations, 2015, which contain adequate safeguards. 
 
The Ministry further submitted that section 21A of the Securities Contracts (Regulation) Act, 1956 
enables a stock exchange to delist securities, after recording the reasons therefor, on any of the 
grounds prescribed under the Securities Contracts (Regulat ion) Rules, 1957. The provision also 
requires that the concerned company be given a reasonable opportunity of being heard before its 
securities are delisted. Further, under section 21A(2) of the Securities Contracts (Regulation) Act, 
1956, a listed company or an aggrieved investor may prefer an appeal before the Securities Appellate 
Tribunal against the decision of the stock exchange to delist its securities. 
 
42.3  On being further asked about the provisions of the Bill through which investors have been 
protected, the Ministry stated as under:— 
 

184 
 
“Clause 66(1) is a new provision on voluntary delisting which is based on the existing regulations. It 
has been given a statutory anchor and regulation -making power has been provided to specify the 
procedure for voluntary delisting. Clause 66(2) is a modified provision based on section 21A(1) of the 
Securities Contracts (Regulation) Act, 1956 providing for delisting at the option of a stock exchange. 
The stock exchange may delist the securities of an issuer for continued contravention of the listing 
conditions or on any other grounds which may be specified through regulations. The clause provides 
for an opportunity of hearing to the issuer before its securities are delisted. Further, under Clause 
85(1)(g), an aggrieved company can appeal against the order of the stock exchange. Th e existing 
regulations governing voluntary and compulsory delisting are continued under the Securities Markets 
Code framework and the existing safeguards will continue to apply. Moreover, specifying the detailed 
safeguards in the regulations would provide the necessary flexibility to update the framework in a 
timely manner while ensuring investor protection. As submitted by SEBI, such requirements need to 
be customised depending on the nature of the securities and are revised from time to time.” 
 
42.4  On the types of delisting of securities, the Secretary, during the course of evidence, stated as 
under:— 
 
“There are currently two types—voluntary delisting and compulsory delisting. These are governed by 
the SEBI (Delisting of Equity Shares) Regulations and the SEBI (Listing Obligations and Disclosure 
Requirements) Regulations, 2015.” 
 
42.5  With regard to investor safeguards, the Secretary further stated as under:— 
 
“We would like to submit that Clause 66(1) is a new provision and Clause 66(2) is a modified provision 
based on the Securities Contracts (Regulation) Act. Some of the safeguards prescribed under the 
SEBI regulations in the case of voluntary delisting inclu de an immediate public announcement, 
appointment of an independent merchant banker, independent due diligence by the stock exchange, 
an escrow mechanism and the reverse book building process for fair price discovery.” 
 
42.6  The Secretary also informed the Committee that the mechanism for determining the delisting 
price and the operation of the reverse book building process are prescribed in the SEBI regulations. 
 
42.7  When asked whether the power to specify the grounds for delisting has been delegated to the 
Board, the Secretary replied in the affirmative. 
 
42.8  The Secretary further stated that the regulatory framework also contains a number of 
safeguards, including special provisions for small companies, the manner of operating the exit window 
and protection of residual shareholders. 

185 
 
 
42.9  With regard to the provisions relating to compulsory and voluntary delisting, the Secretary 
stated as under:— 
 
“For compulsory delisting, a few grounds have been mentioned in sub -clause (2). For voluntary 
delisting, it is covered in the regulations. The stock exchange may delist after recording reasons for 
contravention of any listing conditions. The other grounds can be specified through regulations. An 
opportunity of being heard is also provided.” 
 
42.10  On the issue of whether a shareholder has the right to file an appeal in the case of voluntary 
delisting, the Secretary stated as under:— 
 
“Sir, I will have to check that. But this particular process will succeed only if the votes of the public 
shareholders are obtained. The safeguard provided in the regulations is shareholder approval, 
subject to the condition that the special resolution suc ceeds only if the votes cast by public 
shareholders in favour are at least twice the votes cast against it. That is the safeguard presently 
available under the regulations. I have not seen any appeal provision. Sir, we will examine the matter.” 
 
42.11  On being further asked about an appeal in the case of voluntary delisting, the Secretary stated 
as under:— 
 
“SEBI has informed us that both voluntary delisting and compulsory delisting can be appealed before 
the Securities Appellate Tribunal. In continuation of that, we are proposing to modify the current 
formulation because it is presently linked only to Clause 66(2), which deals with compulsory delisting. 
We propose to link it to Clause 66 as a whole, which covers both voluntary and compulsory delisting, 
so as to make the position amply clear.” 
 
42.12  The Committee have examined Clause 66 of the Bill and observe that it enables an 
issuer to apply to a stock exchange for the delisting of its securities. The Committee further 
note that the stock exchange may also, on its own, delist the securities of an issuer, subject 
to affording the issuer an opportunity of being heard. 
 
42.13  The Committee note that a number of safeguards are presently available to investors 
during the delisting process under the SEBI (Delisting of Equity Shares) Regulations, 2021 
and the SEBI (Listing Obligations and Disclosure Requirements) Regulations, 2015 . These 
include an immediate public announcement, appointment of an independent merchant banker, 
independent due diligence by the stock exchange, an escrow mechanism, price discovery 

186 
 
through the reverse book building process, special provisions for small companies, an exit 
window and protection of residual shareholders. 
 
The Committee observe that, although these safeguards are substantial, they are presently 
contained only in subordinate legislation and find no place in the principal statute. 
Consequently, an investor reading the Code would remain unaware of the statutory  
protections available. 
 
The Committee, therefore, recommend that the essential principles of investor protection and 
safeguards in relation to delisting be incorporated in the principal statute itself. 
 
42.14  The Committee further observe that, under Clause 66(2) read with Clause 85(2)(b), 
which substantially carries forward section 21A(2) of the Securities Contracts (Regulation) 
Act, 1956, an issuer or an investor aggrieved by a decision of a stock exchange in relation to 
compulsory delisting may prefer an appeal before the Securities Appellate Tribunal. 
 
The Committee do not find a corresponding provision in respect of voluntary delisting under 
Clause 66(1). The Committee note that the principal safeguard presently available in the case 
of voluntary delisting is the requirement of a special resolution, whi ch succeeds only where 
the votes cast by the public shareholders in favour are at least twice the votes cast against it. 
 
The Committee, therefore, recommend that the Ministry examine the matter and provide, under 
Clause 66 read with Clause 85, a right of appeal to the Securities Appellate Tribunal for an 
investor aggrieved by a voluntary delisting under Clause 66(1), and mak e the necessary 
consequential amendments in the Bill. 
  

187 
 
CHAPTER XLIII 
 
XLIII Right of a Clearing Corporation to Have Precedence over Other Rights and Attachments 
 
Clause 70 
 
43.1  Clause 70 of the Bill provides that the right of a clearing corporation shall have precedence 
over other rights and attachments. The provisions of the Clause are reproduced below:— 
 
70. Subject to the provisions of the Insolvency and Bankruptcy Code, 2016, the right of a clearing 
corporation to recover its dues from the clearing members, incurred in discharge of their clearing and 
settlement functions, from the collaterals, deposits and other assets of the clearing members held by 
the clearing corporation for this purpose shall— 
 
(a) in the case of insolvency, winding -up, liquidation or resolution, as the case may be, of such 
clearing member, have precedence over the rights of any other person in such insolvency, winding -
up, liquidation or resolution proceedings; 
 
(b) to the extent of the claims of the clearing corporation, have priority over any other attachment by 
any authority constituted under any law for the time being in force. 
 
43.2  One stakeholder suggested that the phrase “subject to the provisions of the Insolvency and 
Bankruptcy Code, 2016” occurring in Clause 70 be substituted with the phrase “notwithstanding 
anything contained in any other law for the time being in force.”  
 
 
It was submitted that the provision, in its present form, could adversely affect the amounts payable to 
investors whose transactions are cleared through clearing members. It was, therefore, suggested that 
the aforesaid words be deleted, as the existing for mulation may place the interests of secured 
creditors ahead of those of investors who are not shareholders. 
 
43.3  When asked to justify the use of the expression “subject to the provisions of the Insolvency 
and Bankruptcy Code, 2016”, the Secretary, during the course of evidence, stated as under:— 
 

188 
 
“The concern accepted is that Clause 70 seeks to protect the finality of settlement transactions and 
prevent the reversal of completed trades. The entire securities market works on the assumption and 
trust that once a trade is executed, it is completed; netting and settlement have occurred, shares have 
been transferred, payments have been made and deliveries have taken place. Therefore, to protect 
finality, Clause 70 presently states ‘subject to the provisions of the IBC’. However, we found merit in 
accepting the suggestion that it should read ‘notwithstanding anything contained in the IBC’, as it 
would give primacy to Clause 70.” 
 
43.4  During the course of examination, the Secretary, while explaining why the claims of clearing 
corporations and the assets held for clearing and settlement functions should be protected where a 
company undergoes resolution under the Insolvency and Bankruptcy Code, stated as under:— 
 
“We examined certain domestic and international laws in this regard. The Payment and Settlement 
Systems Act provides precedence over the IBC. The Bilateral Netting of Qualified Financial Contracts 
Act, 2020 also provides precedence over the IBC. The princi ple is that netting and settlement are 
important functions, and should one of the companies become insolvent, the trades that have 
commenced should be allowed to be settled. The IBC itself offers protection from liquidation, but it 
does not offer protectio n from resolution, which is the point we are making. Even in the case of 
resolution, perhaps this needs to be given precedence. The IOSCO Principles and the UNCITRAL 
Legislative Guide also provide that insolvency law should recognise financial contracts th at permit 
set-off and netting of outstanding obligations promptly. The laws in the United States, Canada and 
Singapore similarly provide that the netting and settlement process should remain outside the 
insolvency process, whether in liquidation or resolut ion. Liquidation or resolution should not be the 
determining factor. We can provide suitable language.” 
 
43.5  During the course of evidence, when the Committee further enquired whether the Ministry 
supported replacing the words “subject to the provisions of the Insolvency and Bankruptcy Code, 
2016” with “notwithstanding anything contained in…”, the Secretary replied as under:— 
 
“Yes, Sir. That is what we were submitting last time also.” 
 
43.6  The Committee have examined Clause 70 of the Bill and note that it accords precedence 
to the dues recoverable by a clearing corporation in discharge of its clearing and settlement 
functions over other rights and attachments. 
 
The Committee further note that the securities market operates on the fundamental principles 
of certainty and finality of trades, ensuring that, once a trade is executed, netting and 
settlement are completed, securities are transferred and payments are made. 
 

189 
 
43.7  The Committee note with concern that the use of the phrase “subject to the provisions 
of the Insolvency and Bankruptcy Code, 2016” may place the interests of secured creditors 
ahead of those of investors who are not shareholders. 
 
The Committee further note from the submissions of the Ministry that the Payment and 
Settlement Systems Act, 2007 and the Bilateral Netting of Qualified Financial Contracts Act, 
2020 accord precedence to netting and settlement over the Insolvency and Bankruptcy Code, 
2016. 
 
The Committee also note that the IOSCO Principles and the UNCITRAL Legislative Guide on 
Insolvency Law recognise that insolvency laws should give effect to financial contracts 
permitting set-off and netting of outstanding obligations. The Committee further note that the 
laws of the United States, Canada and Singapore similarly keep the netting and settlement 
process outside the insolvency process, whether in liquidation or resolution. 
 
43.8  In view of the foregoing, the Committee are of the considered opinion that preserving 
the certainty and finality of completed transactions is essential for maintaining market 
integrity, investor confidence and the stability of the securities market, which operates on the 
assumption and trust that, once a trade is executed, netting and settlement have been 
completed, securities have been transferred, payments have been made and deliveries have 
taken place. 
 
The Committee, therefore, recommend that the words “subject to the provisions of the 
Insolvency and Bankruptcy Code, 2016” in Clause 70 be substituted with the words 
“notwithstanding anything contained in the Insolvency and Bankruptcy Code, 2016” so as to 
give primacy to the rights of the clearing corporation over other rights and attachments and 
to preserve the finality of settlements. 
 
The Committee, therefore, recommend that the necessary amendment be carried out in Clause 
70 of the Bill to safeguard the interests of investors. 
  

190 
 
CHAPTER XLIV
