Chapter X of the Bill provides for the Investor Charter and Investor Grievance Redressal 
Mechanism, as under: 
 
XLIV Investor Charter 
 
Clause 71 
 
44.1  Clause 71 of the Bill provides for an Investor Charter, as under:— 
 
71 The Board may make an Investor Charter which shall provide for the principles for the protection 
of investors, facilitate their participation in the securities markets, and cover such other matters 
concerning investors, in such manner as may be specified by regulations. 
 
44.2  One stakeholder suggested that Clause 71 be substituted with the following:— 
 
“The Board shall, by regulations, make an Investor Charter which shall provide for the principles for 
the protection of investors, facilitation of investor participation in the securities markets, and such 
other matters concerning investors as may be specified.” 
 
44.3  On being asked about making the Investor Charter mandatory, the Secretary, during the 
course of evidence, stated as under:— 
 
“…the Chapter basically says that the Board may make an Investor Charter. So, there was one issue 
about ‘may’ and ‘shall’. It was agreed that we should probably use ‘shall’.” 
 
44.4  The Committee have examined Clause 71 of the Bill and note that it empowers the 
Board to specify, by regulations, an Investor Charter providing for the principles for the 
protection of investors, facilitation of their participation in the securities marke ts and such 
other matters concerning investors. 
 
44.5  The Committee observe that Clause 71, in its present form, does not mandatorily 
require the Board to make an Investor Charter. The Committee are of the view that making the 

191 
 
Investor Charter mandatory would better align the Code with its objective of strengthening 
investor protection and reinforcing investor confidence. 
 
The Committee, therefore, recommend that the word “may” occurring in Clause 71 be 
substituted with the word “shall”, thereby making it mandatory for the Board to provide for an 
Investor Charter. 
 
44.6  The Committee are also of the view that the Investor Charter framed by the Board 
should set out the rights of investors and prescribe timelines for grievance redressal. The 
Committee further recommend that the Investor Charter should be published and periodically 
reviewed so as to keep pace with evolving market practices and developments. 

192 
 
CHAPTER XLV 
 
XLV Investor Grievance Redressal Mechanism 
 
Clause 72 
 
45.1  Clause 72 of the Bill provides for an investor grievance redressal mechanism, as under:— 
 
72 (1) The Board may provide for an investor grievance redressal mechanism and direct securities 
market service providers or issuers or their agents to provide similar mechanisms to receive, monitor 
and redress investor grievances in such manner and within  such period as may be specified by 
regulations. 
 
(2) Upon receipt of a grievance from an investor under the mechanism provided under sub -section 
(1), the Board, the securities market service provider or the issuer shall, within such period as may 
be specified by regulations, not exceeding seven days, inf orm the investor about the receipt of the 
grievance. 
 
45.2  One stakeholder suggested that complaints relating to the non-redressal of grievances should 
be directly entertainable by the Ombudsperson, instead of requiring the complainant to first approach 
the investor grievance redressal mechanism envisaged under C lause 72(1) of the Bill. It was 
submitted that such an arrangement would expedite the resolution of complaints. 
 
45.3  While stating that the issue has already been addressed in the Bill, the Ministry, in its detailed 
reply, submitted as under:— 
 
“Under the existing framework, investors have the option to approach dispute resolution mechanisms 
administered by SEBI -regulated entities, including pre -conciliation, online conciliation and online 
arbitration, without being subject to any mandatory waiting period. Investors may thereafter file their 
grievances on the SCORES platform. The Securities Markets Code does not preclude the continued 
use of such first-level mechanisms and, in fact, equates them with the initial tier of grievance redressal 
under C lause 72. The preservation of this approach seeks to balance speed of resolution with 
procedural fairness and does not undermine investor confidence. 
 
The Securities Markets Code establishes a two -tier framework for investor grievance redressal. 
Where a grievance is not resolved at the first level within 180 days, the investor may approach the 

193 
 
Ombudsperson under Clause 74, upon which the first-level proceedings shall abate. This is intended 
to ensure that the Ombudsperson mechanism is invoked only after the primary mechanisms have 
been given a fair opportunity to resolve the grievance. 
 
The Securities Markets Code entrusts SEBI with the duty to protect investor interests and regulate 
the securities markets. In this context, SEBI officers acting as Ombudspersons are envisaged to 
discharge their functions independently and impartially, with  SEBI remaining a non -party to the 
dispute. The powers and functions of the Ombudsperson are clearly set out under Clauses 72 to 78, 
and orders passed by the Ombudsperson are appealable to the Securities Appellate Tribunal under 
Clause 85(1)(f), thereby ensuring independent oversight. If any amendments to the regulations are 
required to align with this framework, they may be considered at the implementation stage.” 
 
45.4  One stakeholder submitted that a discretionary grievance redressal framework could lead to 
uneven implementation. It was suggested that a mandatory and uniform grievance redressal 
framework is essential in a retail-led market and would ensure predictable investor-facing standards 
across market participants. 
 
Accordingly, it was proposed that Clause 72(1) be substituted with the following:— 
 
“The Board shall provide an investor grievance redressal mechanism and shall require every 
securities market service provider, issuer or its agent to establish a similar mechanism to receive, 
monitor and redress investor grievances in such manner and within such period as may be specified 
by regulations.” 
 
45.5  Some stakeholders also suggested that it should be mandatory for the Board to provide an 
investor grievance redressal mechanism. When the Committee raised the issue with the Ministry, the 
Secretary, Department of Economic Affairs, stated during the cours e of evidence that the concern 
had been accepted and that the word “may” could be substituted with “shall”. 
 
45.6  Some stakeholders suggested that market infrastructure institutions be excluded from the 
applicability of the proposed provision. Responding to the suggestion, the Ministry stated as under:— 
 
“Market infrastructure institutions provide services to investors in the securities markets, similar to 
certain functions performed by issuers. Accordingly, the investor grievance redressal provisions 
under the Code are also applicable to market infrastructure institutions. At present, SEBI also enables 
investors to redress grievances against market infrastructure institutions through its SCORES 
platform.” 
 

194 
 
45.7  When enquired about the existing timelines for grievance redressal, the Secretary, during the 
course of evidence, stated as under:— 
 
“Currently, there is no statutory grievance redressal mechanism. SEBI follows an administrative 
mechanism and has recently introduced a platform for this purpose. Under the existing platform, the 
market service provider receives the complaint. At present, the timelines provide for 
acknowledgement within seven days. The complaint is then required to be resolved within twenty -
one days. If the complainant is not satisfied, the matter may be taken up again with the same entity, 
which takes another fifteen days. Thus, the entire process presently takes about sixty to ninety days.” 
 
45.8  The Committee have examined Clause 72 of the Bill and note that it empowers the 
Board to provide, by regulations, an investor grievance redressal mechanism and to direct 
securities market service providers, issuers and their agents to establish similar mechanisms 
for receiving, monitoring and redressing investor grievances. 
 
45.9  The Committee further note that market infrastructure institutions have been brought 
within the ambit of Clause 72 on the ground that they form an integral part of the investor 
grievance redressal architecture and are themselves securities market service providers. 
 
The Committee agree with the Ministry and recommend that the drafting of Clause 72(1) be 
suitably clarified so as to place it beyond doubt that the obligation to establish an investor 
grievance redressal mechanism extends to every securities market service provider, including 
market infrastructure institutions, intermediaries and self-regulatory organisations, as well as 
to issuers and their agents. 
 
45.10  The Committee observe that the grievance redressal mechanism envisaged under 
Clause 72 is, in its present formulation, discretionary because of the use of the word “may” in 
sub-clause (1). The Committee are of the considered view that the Board should be  under a 
mandatory obligation to provide an investor grievance redressal mechanism. 
 
The Committee, therefore, recommend that the word “may” occurring in Clause 72(1) be 
substituted with the word “shall”. 
 
45.11  The Committee note the information furnished by the Ministry regarding the 
functioning of the SEBI Complaints Redress System (SCORES), under which a complaint is 
required to be acknowledged within seven days, the concerned entity is required to resolve 
the complaint and submit its action taken report within twenty-one calendar days, and a review 
may thereafter be sought, the entire process taking approximately sixty to ninety days. 

195 
 
 
The Committee are of the firm view that a tiered grievance redressal framework can be justified 
only if the first tier is both time-bound and effective. 
  

196 
 
CHAPTER XLVI 
 
XLVI Board to Designate Ombudsperson to Redress Grievances 
 
Clause 73 
 
46.1  Clause 73 of the Bill provides for the Board to designate an Ombudsperson to redress 
grievances, as under:— 
 
73 (1) The Board may designate one or more of its officers as Ombudspersons to receive and redress 
the grievances of investors under this Chapter. 
 
(2) The Ombudsperson shall sit at such places having jurisdiction over such areas as the Board may 
notify in this behalf. 
 
(3) The Ombudsperson shall redress grievances in such manner and within such timelines as may 
be specified by regulations. 
 
46.2  During the course of examination, the Committee pointed out that the timelines for disposal 
of grievances had not been prescribed under Clause 73(3) of the Bill and had instead been left to be 
specified through regulations. When asked why the timelines ha d not been provided in the Bill, the 
Secretary, during the course of evidence, stated as under:— 
 
“As of now, it is envisaged that this will come through regulations. There is no timeline. What it says 
is that it may be specified through regulations based on the complexity of the case.” 
 
46.3  On being further asked whether an outer time limit could be prescribed in the statute itself, the 
Secretary stated during the course of evidence:— 
 
“We could give an outer limit and maybe within that regulations can be made. We can give an outer 
limit.” 
 
When the Committee further emphasised that the timeline should be provided in the statute itself, the 
Secretary stated:— 

197 
 
 
“We are saying 120 days, four months, after seeing the data.” 
 
46.4  One stakeholder submitted that an Ombudsperson drawn from amongst the officers of SEBI 
to adjudicate complaints against SEBI-regulated intermediaries creates an inherent structural conflict 
of interest, since SEBI both supervises the regulated entities an d controls the adjudicatory 
mechanism. It was suggested that effective investor grievance redressal requires institutional 
independence. Reference was also made to the Reserve Bank of India’s Integrated Ombudsman 
Scheme as a useful model, with independently appointed Ombudspersons, a dedicated budget and 
a publicly available annual report. It was, therefore, suggested that statutory independence of the 
Ombudsperson be expressly provided. 
 
Responding to the issue, the Ministry stated as under:— 
 
“The grievances before an Ombudsperson pertain to disputes between an investor and securities 
market service providers or issuers. SEBI remains an independent party in such disputes. Therefore, 
concerns relating to impartiality may not arise.” 
 
46.5  The Committee have examined Clause 73 of the Bill and note that it empowers the 
Board to designate one or more of its officers as Ombudspersons to receive and redress 
investor grievances. The Committee further note that the Clause empowers the Board to 
determine their territorial jurisdiction and to specify, by regulations, the manner and timelines 
for grievance redressal. 
 
46.6  The Committee further observe that Clause 73(1) provides for the Ombudsperson to 
be designated from amongst the officers of the Board itself. The Committee note the Ministry’s 
submission that the grievance before the Ombudsperson is not against the Board but against 
a securities market service provider or an issuer, and that the Board remains an independent 
third party to such disputes. 
 
The Committee, while accepting this position, are of the view that the credibility of the 
mechanism will depend upon the demonstrable independence of the Ombudsperson from the 
operational and enforcement wings of the Board. 
 
46.7  The Committee observe that no statutory timeline has been prescribed for the 
Ombudsperson to dispose of grievances and that the matter has been left entirely to 
regulations. The Committee are of the view that, in the absence of a statutory outer limit, th e 

198 
 
very object of establishing an Ombudsperson under the Code, namely, speedy and effective 
grievance redressal, may be defeated. 
 
The Committee note the Ministry’s submission that an outer time limit for disposal of 
grievances by the Ombudsperson can be provided. The Committee, therefore, recommend 
that an outer limit of 120 days for the redressal of grievances by the Ombudsperson sh ould 
be expressly provided in the statute itself. 
  

199 
 
CHAPTER XLVII 
 
XLVII Procedure for Filing of Grievance and Complaint 
 
Clause 74 
 
47.1  Clause 74 of the Bill provides for the procedure for filing grievances and complaints, as 
under:— 
 
74 (1) Any investor aggrieved by any deficiency in the services rendered by a securities market 
service provider or by any act or omission of an issuer shall seek resolution of his grievance through 
the investor grievance redressal mechanisms referred to in section 72 within a period of one hundred 
and eighty days from the date of first filing of such grievance. 
 
(2) If the grievance of an investor is not resolved within the period specified above, he may file a 
complaint in writing before the Ombudsperson within a period of thirty days, in such form and manner 
as may be specified by regulations: 
 
Provided that no complaint shall be maintainable where the investor has initiated proceedings before 
any court, tribunal or authority in respect of a matter which is directly or substantially in issue in such 
complaint. 
 
Explanation.—For the purposes of this sub -section, the expression “deficiency” means any fault, 
imperfection, shortcoming or inadequacy in the quality, nature or manner of performance which is 
required to be maintained by or under this Code or any rules or regulations made thereunder, or has 
been undertaken to be performed by a securities market service provider in pursuance of a contract 
or otherwise in relation to any service in the securities market, and includes— 
 
(i) any act of negligence, omission or commission by such person which causes loss or injury to the 
investor; and 
 
(ii) deliberate withholding of relevant information by such person from the investor. 
 
(3) Any proceedings before the investor grievance redressal mechanisms under section 72 shall 
abate upon the filing of a complaint before the Ombudsperson. 

200 
 
 
47.2  Some stakeholders expressed the concern that, under the existing framework, an investor 
may raise a grievance through the SCORES platform or through the Online Dispute Resolution (ODR) 
mechanism by way of conciliation or arbitration. If the investor is aggrieved by an arbitral award, the 
same is challengeable before the competent court under the Arbitration and Conciliation Act, 1996. It 
was submitted that the creation of the Ombudsperson would introduce a parallel avenue for grievance 
redressal and could encourage forum shopping. 
 
47.3  In its detailed reply, the Ministry stated as under:— 
 
“Under the existing framework, investors presently have the option to approach dispute resolution 
mechanisms administered by market infrastructure institutions, including pre -conciliation, online 
conciliation and online arbitration, without being subject t o any mandatory waiting period. The 
Securities Markets Code does not preclude the continued use of such first-level mechanisms and, in 
fact, equates them with the initial tier of grievance redressal under Clause 72. The preservation of 
this approach seeks to balance speed of resolution with procedural fairness and does not undermine 
investor confidence. 
 
The Securities Markets Code establishes a two -tier framework for investor grievance redressal. 
Where a grievance is not resolved at the first level within 180 days, the investor may approach the 
Ombudsperson under Clause 74, upon which the first-level proceedings shall abate. This is intended 
to ensure that the Ombudsperson mechanism is invoked only after the primary mechanisms have 
been given a fair opportunity to resolve the grievance. 
 
The Securities Markets Code entrusts SEBI with the duty to protect investor interests and regulate 
the securities markets. In this context, SEBI officers acting as Ombudspersons are envisaged to 
discharge their functions independently and impartially, with  SEBI remaining a non -party to the 
dispute. The powers and functions of the Ombudsperson are clearly set out under Clauses 72 to 78, 
and orders passed by the Ombudsperson are appealable to the Securities Appellate Tribunal under 
Clause 85(1)(f), thereby ensuring independent oversight.” 
 
47.4  One stakeholder suggested that sub-clause (1) be revised to read as follows:— 
 
“Any investor aggrieved by any deficiency in the services rendered by a securities market service 
provider or by any act or omission of an issuer shall first seek resolution of his grievance through the 
investor grievance redressal mechanisms referred to i n section 72. Such grievance shall be 
processed within a period of one hundred and eighty days from the date of its first filing.” 
 

201 
 
In response, the Ministry stated as under:— 
 
“The Code provides a two -tier framework for investor grievance redressal. Clause 72 recognises 
various existing investor grievance redressal mechanisms established by SEBI, securities market 
service providers, issuers or their agents. Investors are first required to seek resolution through such 
mechanisms. If the grievance is not resolved during that period, Clause 74(2) enables investors to 
escalate the grievance to the Ombudsperson. However, investors may also choose to continue 
pursuing the grievance through the mechanisms under Clause 72 even after the expiry of 180 days. 
Thus, escalation to the Ombudsperson after 180 days is merely an option available to the investor 
and is not mandatory. The current language adequately reflects this intention and no further changes 
may be required.” 
 
47.5  Some stakeholders further submitted that the definition of “deficiency” is subjective, as it 
includes expressions such as “imperfection”, “shortcoming” and “inadequacy” in the quality of 
performance, which could result in unnecessary disputes and vexatious litigation. 
 
47.6  In response, the Ministry stated that the provision extends to every person providing services 
in the securities market in order to strengthen investor protection. The objective is to provide statutory 
recognition to the existing investor grievance redressal framework and promote time-bound resolution 
of grievances. It was further stated that the detailed procedure for filing complaints and the exclusions 
therefrom may be prescribed through regulations, and that the interpretation of the expression 
“deficiency” would evolve through judicial and quasi-judicial precedents. 
 
47.7  During the course of evidence, when the Committee pointed out that the Explanation defining 
the term “deficiency” should be repositioned so as to avoid interpretational ambiguity, the Secretary 
stated as under:— 
 
“…the term ‘deficiency’ is defined in sub -section (2), whereas it is first used in sub -section (1). 
Therefore, we will reposition it.” 
 
47.8  One stakeholder suggested that a statutory timeline be prescribed for the disposal of 
complaints by the Ombudsperson. It was submitted that, although the Bill prescribes a period of 180 
days for first-tier grievance redressal under Clause 74, it does not prescribe any timeline for disposal 
of complaints by the Ombudsperson. 
 
In response, the Ministry stated as under:— 
 

202 
 
“No timeline for redressal of investor grievances by the Ombudsperson has been prescribed because 
the time required would depend upon the facts and circumstances of each case, including the 
availability of documents and witnesses, making it difficult to specify a definitive timeline. In any case, 
Clause 73(3) already empowers the Board to specify, by regulations, the manner and timelines for 
grievance redressal by the Ombudsperson.” 
 
47.9  When the Committee pointed out during the course of evidence that the present language of 
Clause 74(1) creates confusion regarding the responsibility for resolving the grievance, the Secretary 
stated as under:— 
 
“The responsibility has to be on the service provider. The present language suggests that the investor 
himself has to resolve his grievance. That is what is creating the confusion. The language has to be 
changed.” 
 
47.10  In order to prevent multiple proceedings and ensure a single dispute resolution process, one 
stakeholder suggested that an appropriate provision be inserted to clarify that, where arbitral 
proceedings have already concluded, the matter should not be entertained by the Ombudsperson. It 
was further suggested that any parallel or overlapping dispute resolution mechanisms, including 
those under the SEBI Master Circular dated 31 July 2023, should not apply. 
 
In response, the Ministry stated as under:— 
 
“The concern may be accepted and appropriate drafting changes may be made to clarify that, where 
proceedings before an arbitration tribunal appointed under Clause 72 have been concluded within 
180 days, the complainant shall not be entitled to approach the Ombudsperson.” 
 
47.11  When the Committee enquired whether the complainant should necessarily wait for 180 days 
before approaching the Ombudsperson, the Secretary stated during the course of evidence as 
under:— 
 
“We had earlier proposed a blanket time frame of 180 days, and the Committee had suggested 90 
days. We are of the view that even a period of 90 days would work. There is no need to provide an 
additional period of 30 days.” 
 
It was further informed that the timeline for resolution through the investor grievance redressal 
mechanism had been proposed to be reduced from 180 days to 90 days. 
 

203 
 
47.12  On the issue whether a complainant should be required to wait for the prescribed period 
before approaching the Ombudsperson even where the grievance had already been disposed of, but 
not to his satisfaction, the Ministry stated as under:— 
 
“Appropriate drafting changes will be made to clarify that the complainant may approach the 
Ombudsperson even before the expiry of 180 days, after availing of the grievance redressal 
mechanism under Clause 72, if the resolution is not to his satisfaction.” 
 
47.13  The Committee have examined Clause 74 of the Bill and note that it requires an investor 
to first seek redressal of his grievance through the investor grievance redressal mechanisms 
referred to in Clause 72 within a period of one hundred and eighty days fro m the date of first 
filing of the grievance. The Committee further note that, if the grievance remains unresolved 
within the said period, the investor may either continue with the existing grievance redressal 
mechanism or, within thirty days thereafter, file a complaint before the Ombudsperson. 
 
The Committee further note that the Clause defines the expression “deficiency” and provides 
that no complaint shall be maintainable before the Ombudsperson where proceedings have 
already been initiated before any court, tribunal or other authority in respe ct of the same 
matter. 
 
47.14  The Committee observe that the present language of the Clause places the 
responsibility for resolving the grievance upon the investor rather than upon the grievance 
redressal mechanism or the service provider. 
 
The Committee, therefore, recommend that suitable drafting changes be made so as to clearly 
fix accountability for grievance redressal upon the concerned mechanism or service provider, 
rather than upon the aggrieved investor. 
 
47.15  The Committee further observe that, under the present drafting, a complainant is 
required to wait for the expiry of the prescribed period before approaching the 
Ombudsperson, even where the grievance has already been disposed of, but the complainant 
is dissatisfied with the outcome. 
 
The Committee are of the considered view that a complainant should be entitled to approach 
the Ombudsperson before the expiry of the prescribed period where the grievance has already 
been resolved through the grievance redressal mechanism but the resolutio n is not 
satisfactory. 
 

204 
 
The Committee, therefore, desire that the necessary drafting changes be made in the Bill 
accordingly. 
 
47.16  The Committee observe that Clause 74(1) presently provides a period of one hundred 
and eighty days for resolution of grievances through the investor grievance redressal 
mechanism. The Committee are of the view that this period may reasonably be reduced so 
that an investor may approach the Ombudsperson at an earlier stage where the grievance 
remains unresolved or the investor is dissatisfied with the resolution. 
 
The Committee, therefore, recommend that the period of 180 days prescribed under Clause 
74(1) be reduced to 90 days. Necessary drafting changes may accordingly be made in the 
Clause, along with all consequential amendments required in the other affected provisions of 
the Bill. 
 
47.17  The Committee observe that the Explanation defining the expression “deficiency” has 
been placed under sub-clause (2), although the expression is first used in sub-clause (1). The 
Committee are of the view that the present placement of the Explanation may g ive rise to 
interpretational ambiguity. 
 
The Committee, therefore, recommend that appropriate drafting changes be made to remove 
such ambiguity. 
  

205 
 
CHAPTER XLVIII 
 
Appeal to Tribunal 
 
Clause 85 
 
48.1 Clause 85 provides for appeals to the Tribunal, as under:— 
 
85 (1) Any person aggrieved by— 
 
(a) an order passed under section 20; 
 
(b) an order passed under sub-section (1) of section 21; 
 
(c) an order passed under sub-sections (2) and (4) of section 27; 
 
(d) an order passed or a decision made under clause (a) of sub -section (1) of section 29 or sub -
section (1) of section 38; 
 
(e) an order passed under clause (b) of sub-section (3) of section 31; 
 
(f) an order passed under sub-section (1) of section 75; 
 
(g) an order passed or decision made by any market infrastructure institution; or 
 
(h) an order passed by the Insurance Regulatory and Development Authority of India, the Pension 
Fund Regulatory and Development Authority or the International Financial Services Centres 
Authority, 
 
may prefer an appeal to the Tribunal having jurisdiction in the matter. 
 
(2) Without prejudice to the provisions of sub-section (1), an appeal may be preferred to the Tribunal 
in the matter by— 
 

206 
 
(a) an issuer aggrieved by the decision of the stock exchange to refuse to list the securities of such 
company under sub-section (4) of section 64; or 
 
(b) an issuer or an investor aggrieved by the decision of the stock exchange delisting the securities 
under sub-section (2) of section 66. 
 
(3) Every appeal under sub-sections (1) and (2) shall be filed within a period of forty -five days from 
the date on which a copy of the order made by the Board, Insurance Regulatory and Development 
Authority of India, the Pension Fund Regulatory and Develop ment Authority, the International 
Financial Services Centres Authority, Ombudsperson, adjudicating officer, self -regulatory 
organisation or market infrastructure institution, as the case may be, is received by the person 
aggrieved and shall be in such form, manner and be accompanied by such fee as may be prescribed: 
 
Provided that the Tribunal may entertain an appeal after the expiry of the said period of forty-five days 
if it is satisfied that there was sufficient cause for not filing it within that period. 
 
(4) On receipt of an appeal under sub-sections (1) and (2), the Tribunal may, after giving the parties 
to the appeal an opportunity of being heard, pass such orders thereon as it thinks fit, confirming, 
modifying or setting aside the order, decision or dir ections appealed against and provide a copy of 
such order free of cost to the parties. 
 
(5) The Tribunal may, under sub -section (4), grant permission for listing of securities and a stock 
exchange shall act in conformity with the order of the Tribunal. 
 
(6) The appeal filed under sub-sections (1) and (2) shall be dealt with expeditiously and preferably be 
disposed of within a period of six months from the date of receipt of the appeal. 
 
48.2  One stakeholder suggested that the provision for an appeal to the Securities Appellate 
Tribunal against an order of the Ombudsperson be deleted. It was submitted that an appeal against 
such an order may lie before an appellate authority from outside SEBI, without any further appeal to 
the Securities Appellate Tribunal or the Supreme Court. 
 
It was further submitted that permitting appeals against orders of the Ombudsperson to lie before the 
Securities Appellate Tribunal may delay the disposal of other matters pending before the Tribunal. In 
the alternative, a separate appellate mechanism may be provided for appeals against orders of the 
Ombudsperson, with an appellate authority from outside SEBI and without any further appeal to the 
Securities Appellate Tribunal or the Supreme Court. 
 

207 
 
48.3  The Ministry, in its reply, stated as under:— 
 
“Creating a separate mechanism for appeal against orders of the Ombudsperson would entail the 
creation of a new forum, which would require significant expenditure and capacity -building. On the 
other hand, the existing infrastructure and expertise of the Se curities Appellate Tribunal, which 
already deals with legal issues relating to the securities markets, can be utilised for such appeals to 
ensure streamlined resolution of grievances.” 
 
48.4  Some stakeholders expressed concern that the scope of orders of SEBI appealable before 
the Securities Appellate Tribunal had been restricted. It was submitted that the Bill limits the appellate 
jurisdiction of the Tribunal to a specified list of orders an d that orders of SEBI not covered under 
Clause 85 may fall outside its jurisdiction. 
 
It was, therefore, suggested that the provisions of section 15T of the Securities and Exchange Board 
of India Act, 1992 be incorporated in Clause 85 so that any order of SEBI or of an adjudicating officer 
is appealable before the Securities Appellate Tribunal. 
 
48.5  When the issue was taken up with the Ministry, the Secretary, during the course of evidence, 
stated as under:— 
 
“…we are accepting that all orders of the Adjudicating Officer should be appealable. Our intention 
was never to restrict appeals only to final orders. Interlocutory orders were not intended to be 
excluded. If greater drafting clarity is required, we are wi lling to incorporate it. Similarly, clarification 
may be added regarding all orders of the Board.” 
 
48.6  One stakeholder expressed concern that the proviso to Clause 85(3) permits the Securities 
Appellate Tribunal to condone delay without prescribing any maximum period up to which such delay 
may be condoned. It was submitted that the absence of an outer time  limit may prolong uncertainty 
regarding the finality of the decision. 
 
48.7  During the course of evidence, the Committee pointed out that the period for condonation of 
delay is subject to an upper limit under several other enactments. Under the Competition Act, 2002, 
the period is sixty days; under the Insolvency and Bankruptcy C ode, 2016, it is thirty days with a 
further period of fifteen days; under the Central Goods and Services Tax Act, 2017, it is three months 
with a further period of one month; under the Arbitration and Conciliation Act, 1996, it is three months 
with a further period of thirty days; and under the Electricity Act, 2003, it is forty-five days with a further 
period of sixty days. 
 

208 
 
48.8  Responding to the issue of prescribing an upper limit for condonation of delay, the Secretary, 
Department of Economic Affairs, stated as under:— 
 
“We can amend it accordingly by providing that the appeal shall be filed within forty-five days and that 
delay may be condoned for a further period not exceeding forty -five days, similar to the provision 
contained in the statute governing the National Company Law Appellate Tribunal. There is an upper 
time limit up to which delay may be condoned.” 
 
48.9  One stakeholder suggested the insertion of a clarificatory provision to the effect that any order 
or decision of the Pension Fund Regulatory and Development Authority appealable to the Tribunal 
should be subject to further appeal only in the manner and be fore the forum provided under the 
Pension Fund Regulatory and Development Authority Act, 2013, and that no writ or other proceeding 
should lie in respect of such orders except as so provided. 
 
48.10  The Ministry, in its written submission, stated that Clause 85 has been rationalised from 
section 15T(1) of the Securities and Exchange Board of India Act, 1992, sections 21A(2), 22A and 
23L(1) of the Securities Contracts (Regulation) Act, 1956, and section 23A(1) of the Depositories Act, 
1996, which provide for appeals from certain orders of SEBI, adjudicating officers, stock exchanges, 
the Pension Fund Regulatory and Development Authority, the Insurance Regulatory and 
Development Authority of India and the International Financial Services Centres Authority. 
 
The Ministry further stated that the appellate framework under the Securities Markets Code is aligned 
with that under the Pension Fund Regulatory and Development Authority Act, 2013, under which an 
appeal against an order of the Pension Fund Regulatory and  Development Authority lies before the 
Securities Appellate Tribunal, followed by a further appeal to the Supreme Court. It was also stated 
that writ jurisdiction is a constitutional remedy and cannot be barred by statute. 
 
48.11  Some stakeholders suggested that Clauses 85(1)(e) and 85(1)(g) be omitted on the ground 
that SEBI is the regulator of market infrastructure institutions. 
 
48.12  During the course of examination, the Ministry submitted that orders passed by market 
infrastructure institutions are also quasi -judicial orders in view of the powers delegated to them by 
SEBI. An appeal from such orders should, therefore, lie before the Securities Appellate Tribunal. It 
was further stated that the right of appeal against an order or decision of a market infrastructure 
institution is based on the existing provision contained in section 23L(1) of the Securities Contracts 
(Regulation) Act, 1956. 
 

209 
 
48.13  One stakeholder further submitted that Clause 85(1)(g) is inconsistent with the provision 
contained in Clause 31(4) of the draft Code and should be omitted, as SEBI is the regulator of market 
infrastructure institutions. 
 
48.14  The Ministry, in its reply, stated that the appeal from an order or decision of a market 
infrastructure institution is based on the existing provision contained in section 23L(1) of the Securities 
Contracts (Regulation) Act, 1956. Clause 31(4) enables an aggrieved person to inform SEBI, which 
may take appropriate action against such market infrastructure institution in exercise of its regulatory 
powers. Further, the aggrieved person may also prefer an appeal before the Securities Appellate 
Tribunal, thereby providing independent oversight over the decisions of a market infrastructure 
institution. 
 
48.15  On being asked whether a similar provision was required to be inserted in the International 
Financial Services Centres Authority Act, 2019 to clarify that all appeals against orders of the 
International Financial Services Centres Authority shall lie befor e the Securities Appellate Tribunal, 
the Ministry stated during the course of evidence that the concern had been accepted and that 
appropriate drafting changes may be made by adding a Schedule to the Code providing for an 
amendment to that Act. 
 
48.16  On being further asked whether special Benches may be constituted for particular authorities 
or officers, the Ministry stated that Clause 80(2) empowers the Central Government to establish more 
than one Bench of the Securities Appellate Tribunal. Any requirement for establishing separate 
Benches may be considered at the implementation stage, if necessary. 
 
48.17  On the issue of whether the Securities Appellate Tribunal should be vested with the power of 
contempt to ensure the enforceability of its orders, the Secretary, Department of Economic Affairs, 
stated as under:— 
 
“…in the spirit of decriminalisation, we would submit that it should probably not have the power of 
contempt.” 
 
48.18  On being asked whether Technical Members could pass administrative orders in the absence 
of a Judicial Member, the Secretary stated during the course of evidence as under:— 
 
“Sir, the procedure is provided in Clause 86. The Clause states that the Tribunal shall not be bound 
by the procedure laid down by the Code of Civil Procedure, but shall be guided by the principles of 
natural justice and, subject to the provisions of this Code and the rules made thereunder, shall have 
the power to regulate its own procedure. We felt that this could be addressed under that provision 
because we could not find any statute in which this is specifically mentioned.” 

210 
 
 
48.19  Stakeholders raised concerns regarding delays in the functioning of the Securities Appellate 
Tribunal, including delays arising from vacancies in the office of Judicial Member. During the course 
of evidence, the Committee desired to know whether a Technical Member could pass administrative 
orders in the absence of a Judicial Member. The Secretary stated as under:— 
 
“We were not able to find any parallel provision. Rather, we found jurisprudence indicating that the 
presence of a Judicial Member is essential.” 
 
48.20  On being asked about the nature of administrative orders, the Secretary stated during the 
course of evidence as under:— 
 
“These are procedural orders.” 
 
48.21  Further, on the issue of routine orders, the Ministry submitted during the course of evidence 
that routine orders of the Securities Appellate Tribunal passed by Technical Members, which have 
no adjudicatory implications or legal consequences for the parti es, may be dealt with as a matter of 
procedure under Clause 86(4), which enables the Presiding Officer of the Tribunal to make provisions 
for the distribution of business. Further, under Clause 86(1), the Tribunal has the power to regulate 
its own procedure. 
 
The Secretary further stated as under:— 
 
“…as far as routine orders of the Securities Appellate Tribunal passed by Technical Members are 
concerned, which have no adjudicatory implications or legal consequences for the parties, such 
orders may not attract the principles laid down by the Supreme Court in its various judgments. What 
we understood from those judgments, and after discussion, is that nothing precludes Technical 
Members from passing such orders even under the existing framework.” 
 
On being further asked whether the language should be amended to make it clear that, in day-to-day 
administrative matters, the presence of a Judicial Member is not necessary, the Secretary stated as 
under:— 
 
“The language does not need to be changed and should remain as it is. The view was that the existing 
language does not preclude such action.” 
 

211 
 
48.22  During the course of evidence, when the Committee enquired whether interim orders were 
appealable, the Secretary stated as under:— 
 
“Since they are intended to be appealable, we will have to provide appropriate language to clarify the 
position. We will do that.” 
 
48.23  The Committee have examined Clause 85 of the Bill and note that it provides for 
appeals to the Tribunal and specifies the orders and decisions against which such appeals 
may be preferred. The Committee further note that an appeal is required to be filed w ithin a 
period of forty -five days from the date on which a copy of the order is received by the 
aggrieved person. The proviso to Clause 85(3) empowers the Tribunal to entertain an appeal 
after the expiry of the said period where it is satisfied that sufficient cause existed for the 
delay. 
 
48.24  The Committee observe that the scope of orders of SEBI appealable before the 
Securities Appellate Tribunal appears to have been restricted under the Bill, as Clause 85 
limits the appellate jurisdiction of the Tribunal to a specified list of orders. Conseq uently, 
orders of SEBI which are not expressly covered under Clause 85 may fall outside the 
jurisdiction of the Tribunal. 
 
The Committee further observe that the present drafting may give rise to an interpretation that 
certain orders of the Board or the adjudicating officer, including interim orders, are not 
appealable before the Securities Appellate Tribunal. However, under s ection 15T of the 
Securities and Exchange Board of India Act, 1992, orders of the adjudicating officer are 
appealable before the Tribunal. 
 
The Committee, therefore, recommend that suitable amendments be made in the Bill to ensure 
that any person aggrieved by an order of the Board or an adjudicating officer, including an 
interim order, has the right, in the interest of natural justice, to pref er an appeal before the 
Securities Appellate Tribunal. The Committee, further, recommend that separate bench may 
be considered in the Securities Appellate Tribunal to exclusively deal with the matters arising 
out of the orders of ombudsperson.  
 
48.25  The Committee further observe that the proviso to Clause 85(3) deals with condonation 
of delay beyond the prescribed period of forty -five days. However, the statute does not 
prescribe an upper limit up to which such delay may be condoned. 
 
The Committee note that several other statutes governing tribunals, including the Competition 
Act, 2002, the Insolvency and Bankruptcy Code, 2016, the Central Goods and Services Tax 

212 
 
Act, 2017, the Arbitration and Conciliation Act, 1996 and the Electricity Act, 2003, prescribe an 
upper limit for condonation of delay. 
 
The Committee are of the considered view that prescribing an upper limit for condonation of 
delay is necessary to ensure finality and certainty in appellate proceedings. The Committee, 
therefore, recommend that an outer limit of forty -five days for condona tion of delay be 
expressly provided in the statute in the interest of expeditious adjudication and justice. 
 
48.26  During the course of examination, it was brought to the notice of the Committee that a 
corresponding provision is required to be inserted in the International Financial Services 
Centres Authority Act, 2019 to clarify that all appeals against orders of th e International 
Financial Services Centres Authority shall lie before the Securities Appellate Tribunal. 
 
The Committee, therefore, desire that appropriate drafting changes be made in the Bill by 
adding a Schedule to the Code providing for the necessary amendment to the International 
Financial Services Centres Authority Act, 2019. 
 
48.27  The Committee note that delays in the functioning of the Securities Appellate Tribunal 
may sometimes arise because of the absence of a Judicial Member due to delay in 
appointment or other factors. The Committee further observe that a Technical Member of the 
Tribunal is presently not expressly empowered to pass even routine administrative orders in 
the absence of a Judicial Member. 
 
The Committee were given to understand that such administrative orders relate to procedural 
matters. The Committee recognise the necessity of the presence of a Judicial Member for 
adjudication involving substantive questions of law. 
 
The Committee note the Ministry’s submission that routine orders which have no adjudicatory 
implications or legal consequences for the parties may be dealt with as a matter of procedure 
under Clause 86(4), which enables the Presiding Officer of the Tribuna l to provide for the 
distribution of business. The Committee further note that the Securities Appellate Tribunal 
has the power to regulate its own procedure. 
The Committee, therefore, recommend that an express provision be made enabling a 
Technical Member of the Tribunal to pass routine procedural orders so as to reduce delays 
arising from procedural matters. The Committee further, recommend that a separate bench in 
SAT be made to deal exclusively with the grievance arising out of the orders of 
Ombudsperson. 
  

213 
 
CHAPTER XLIX 
 
PROHIBITION OF FRAUDULENT OR UNFAIR PRACTICES AND MARKET ABUSE 
 
Prohibition of Fraudulent or Unfair Practices 
 
Clause 92 
 
49.1  Clause 92 of the Bill provides for the prohibition of fraudulent or unfair practices, as under:— 
 
92 Prohibition of fraudulent or unfair practices.—No person shall, directly or indirectly, commit any of 
the following fraudulent or unfair practices,— 
 
(a) use or employ, in connection with the issue, purchase, sale of or any dealings in securities listed 
or proposed to be listed on a stock exchange, any manipulative or deceptive device or contrivance in 
contravention of the provisions of this Code or the rules or regulations or bye-laws made thereunder; 
 
(b) employ any device, scheme or artifice to defraud in connection with the issue or dealing in 
securities which are listed or proposed to be listed on a stock exchange; 
 
(c) engage in any act, practice, course of business which operates or would operate as fraud or deceit 
upon any person, in connection with the issue or dealing in securities which are listed or proposed to 
be listed on a stock exchange, in contravention of  the provisions of this Code or the rules or 
regulations or bye-laws made thereunder; 
 
(d) acquire control of any company or securities more than the specified percentage of equity share 
capital of a company whose securities are listed or proposed to be listed on a stock exchange in 
contravention of the regulations made under this Code; 
 
(e) make any false or fraudulent entry in any record or any material and wilful omission of any 
statement in the record, to be prepared or submitted under the provisions of this Code or the rules or 
regulations or bye-laws made thereunder; 
 
(f) engage in such other fraudulent or unfair practices in the securities markets as may be specified 
by regulations. 
 

214 
 
 
49.2  Clause 93 of the Bill provides for the prohibition of market abuse, as under:— 
93 Prohibition of market abuse. —No person shall, directly or indirectly, commit any of the following 
acts of market abuse, namely:— 
 
(a) engage in insider trading; 
 
(b) use or employ any device, scheme or artifice to defraud the investors in the securities markets; 
 
(c) deal in securities while in possession of material or non-public information or communicating such 
material or non-public information to any other person, in a manner which is in contravention of the 
provisions of this Code or the rules or regulations or bye-laws made thereunder; 
 
(d) publish or cause to publish or report or cause to report by a person dealing in securities, any false 
or misleading information relating to securities with the intent of artificially inflating, depressing, 
maintaining or causing fluctuation in the price of, supply of or demand for such securities, or creating 
a false or misleading impression as to the price of, supply of or demand for such securities; 
 
(e) use its position to inflate, depress, maintain or cause fluctuation in the price of, supply of or 
demand for securities, or create a false or misleading impression as to the price of, supply of or 
demand for securities; 
 
(f) place any order in securities, while directly or indirectly in possession of information which is not 
publicly available, regarding a substantial impending transaction in securities, its underlying or its 
derivative; or 
 
(g) engage in such other activities as may be specified by regulations which adversely affects the 
integrity of the securities markets. 
 
49.3  Some stakeholders pointed out that the conduct described in Clauses 92 and 93 substantially 
overlaps and that the same act may be classified either as a civil default under Clause 92 or as a 
criminal offence under Clause 93. It was pointed out, in particular, that the employment of a “device, 
scheme or artifice to defraud” appears in virtually identical terms in Clauses 92(b) and 93(b); that acts 
amounting to price manipulation are covered by Clause 92(c) as well as Clauses 93(d) and 93(e); 
and that the open-ended delegation in Clause 92(f) is replicated in Clause 93(g). 
 
Since the consequences are radically different —a monetary penalty in one case and imprisonment 
extending to ten years, together with the collateral consequences under the law relating to money -
laundering, in the other —it was submitted that the overlap crea tes a serious risk of arbitrary or 

215 
 
inconsistent enforcement and double jeopardy. It was, therefore, suggested that the sub-clauses be 
redrafted so as to be mutually exclusive or, alternatively, that a proviso be inserted in Clause 92 
excluding acts amounting to market abuse from its scope. 
 
49.4  On being asked about the overlap between Clauses 92 and 93, the Secretary, Department of 
Economic Affairs, submitted as under:— 
 
“We will have a look at this and come back on this. We have received a number of suggestions on 
this. Many of the suggestions have been regarding Clauses 92 and 93. These are very significant 
provisions in which we have attempted to separate market abuse from fraudulent and unfair practices. 
What comes under Clause 92 will be a civil offence. What comes under Clause 93 will be a criminal 
offence.” 
 
“Market abuse is currently not a term used in any of the Acts. It has, however, appeared repeatedly 
through judgments and orders. Therefore, we are trying to codify it. But given the concerns raised 
and the repeated representations received, we are of the view that this needs to be modified. Clause 
93 needs to be modified to sharpen the focus so that very serious and systemic offences, which have 
a significant and systemic impact, are covered under Clause 93, while Clause 92 should relate to 
fraudulent and unfair practices. So, we will have to work on this further and come back to you.” 
 
49.5  On the issue of the potential overlap between “fraudulent or unfair practices” under Clause 92 
and “market abuse” under Clause 93, the Ministry, while acknowledging the concern in its written 
submission, further stated that the ambit of Clause 92(b) may p resently overlap with Clause 93(b). 
Consequently, Clause 93 was being reviewed to ensure that the scope of market abuse is limited to 
conduct having an adverse impact on the integrity of the market. It was also stated that appropriate 
enabling powers may be conferred upon SEBI under Clauses 92 and 93 to effectively administer and 
enforce these provisions. 
 
The Ministry, in its written reply, furnished the following formulation to address the concerns raised:— 
 
Changes to Clause 92 —To add a prohibition against misappropriation of funds and an enabling 
provision for administration of the Clause 
 
92 (f) engage in or conceal the misappropriation or diversion or unauthorised use of funds raised from 
the securities market or raised or pooled from the investors in contravention of the provisions of this 
Code or any rules or regulations made thereunder; 
(g) engage in such other fraudulent or unfair practices in the securities markets as may be specified 
by regulations. 
(2) The administration and enforcement of this section shall be in such manner as may be specified 
in the regulations. 

216 
 
 
Changes to Clause 93 —To circumscribe the scope of Clause 93(b) in order to avoid overlap with 
Clause 92(b) and to add an enabling provision for administration of the Clause 
 
93. (b) use or employ any device, scheme or artifice to defraud which adversely affects the integrity 
of the securities markets. 
 
(2) The administration and enforcement of this section shall be in such manner as may be specified 
in the regulations.” 
 
49.6  Some stakeholders submitted that the Securities Markets Code has decriminalised every 
offence except the offence under Clause 93. It was pointed out that, while offences under all three 
existing enactments were earlier criminal offences, only one provisio n in the proposed Code would 
continue to attract criminal liability. 
 
Concern was particularly raised regarding Clause 93(g), which permits the Board to specify by 
regulations such other activities as may constitute market abuse. It was submitted that the 
consequences of contravention are severe and may extend to imprisonment for ten years. Therefore, 
the creation of criminal offences should not be left to regulations but should remain the exclusive 
function of Parliament. 
 
A similar concern was raised regarding Clause 92(f), which permits the Board to specify other 
fraudulent or unfair practices. It was suggested that, at the very least, conduct attracting penal 
consequences should be set out in the principal legislation and  should not be left to subordinate 
legislation. 
 
It was further submitted that Clause 93(g) should be deleted and that the list of acts constituting 
market abuse and attracting criminal sanctions under Clause 96 should be exhaustive and expressly 
set out in the Code. SEBI may be authorised to issue guida nce regarding the interpretation of the 
existing provisions but should not be empowered, by regulations, to create new categories of criminal 
conduct. 
 
It was contended that the definition of criminal offences is a core legislative function which cannot be 
delegated to the executive or to a regulator, with reference being made to In re: Delhi Laws Act, 1912 
(AIR 1951 SC 332). It was, therefore, submitted that permitting SEBI to expand the scope of Clause 
93 through regulations may be constitutionally vulnerable on the ground of excessive delegation. 
 
49.7  Some stakeholders further pointed out that, as a matter of sound legislative policy, the 
foundational elements of criminally actionable market abuse should be set out in the principal 
legislation. Otherwise, courts may uphold convictions based on norms which have not undergone 
legislative scrutiny. 

217 
 
  
49.8  On being asked whether the power conferred upon the Board under Clause 93(g) to specify 
additional acts constituting market abuse through regulations, thereby expanding the scope of 
conduct attracting criminal liability under Clause 96, amounts to excessi ve delegation, and whether 
such acts should instead be exhaustively specified in the Code itself, the Ministry, in its written 
submission, stated as under:— 
 
“The Supreme Court of India has consistently held that economic laws stand on a different footing in 
terms of constitutional analysis. The limits of delegation are also well defined. 
 
The legislature, after establishing the legislative policy, may confer discretion on an administrative 
agency regarding the execution of the policy and leave it to the agency to work out the details within 
the framework. 
 
The delegation contemplated under Clauses 92 and 93 is consistent with the existing jurisprudence, 
legislative structure and regulatory practices under the securities laws. Section 12A of the Securities 
and Exchange Board of India Act, 1992 provides for ce rtain broad acts to be categorised as unfair 
trade practices and manipulative conduct, while leaving the detailed identification of such practices 
to subordinate legislation, including the Securities and Exchange Board of India (Prohibition of 
Fraudulent a nd Unfair Trade Practices relating to Securities Market) Regulations, 2003 and the 
Securities and Exchange Board of India (Prohibition of Insider Trading) Regulations, 2015.” 
 
49.9  The Committee note that Clauses 92 and 93 seek to distinguish between fraudulent or 
unfair practices attracting civil consequences and market abuse attracting  both civil and  
criminal consequences. The Committee further note the rationale advanced by the Ministry 
that the objective of introducing a separate framework for market abuse is to confine criminal 
liability to serious and systemic misconduct affecting the integrity of the securities market. 
 
49.10  The Committee, however, observe that the present formulation gives rise to significant 
legal and constitutional concerns. The Committee find that certain acts described under 
Clauses 92 and 93 substantially overlap, thereby creating uncertainty as to whether the same 
conduct would attract civil penalties or criminal prosecution. In particular, the Committee note 
the potential overlap between Clause 92(b) and Clause 93(b), which has also been 
acknowledged by the Ministry. Such overlap is likely to res ult in inconsistent enforcement, 
uncertainty for market participants and avoidable litigation.  
 
The Committee are of the considered view that provisions carrying criminal consequences 
must be drafted with a high degree of precision and certainty. Accordingly, particularly the 
overlapping fields of operation under Clause 92(b) and Clause 93(b), be com prehensively 
reviewed and redrafted so that their respective scopes are clearly demarcated. In this regard, 
Clause 93(b) should be confined to the use or employment of a device, scheme or artifice to 
defraud investors which has a demonstrable adverse impact on the integrity of the securities 

218 
 
market. The Committee further recommend that the other provisions of Clause 93 be 
correspondingly examined to ensure that only grave, wilful and systemic acts of market abuse 
having a demonstrable adverse impact on the integrity of the securities market attract criminal 
liability under Clause 96. 
 
The Committee further recommend that the Principal Clause (headnote) of Clause 93 be 
suitably amended to reflect that the provision is confined to acts of market abuse involving 
serious and systemic misconduct affecting the integrity of the securities market. The 
Committee further recommend that the reference to “bye -laws” in Clause 93(c) be deleted  
because all possible acts of market abuse which require criminal action are covered under 
93(a) to 93(f).  Further, 93(g) confer the power to SEBI to declare an y activity as criminal and 
the power to authorise to criminal action should be in the limit and should not be delegated 
and therefore 93(g) should be deleted. The essential ingredients of any conduct attracting 
criminal liability under Clause 96 should be clearly specified in the parent legislation, with 
subordinate legislation being limited to carrying out and operationalising the provisions of 
the Code. 
 
The Committee further note that, acknowledging the concerns raised, the Ministry has 
proposed that the provisions relating to fraudulent or unfair practices under Clause 92 and 
market abuse under Clause 93 be removed from Clause 103 and that separate penal ty 
provisions be introduced in the Chapter relating to penalties for contraventions of Clauses 92 
and 93, respectively. 
 
The Committee observe that the proposed formulation would provide for a separate and 
specific penalty framework for fraudulent or unfair practices and market abuse, while 
prescribing a similar gradation of penalties based on the extent of unlawful gain or loss 
caused and whether the same is quantifiable. 
 
The Committee are of the view that such separate treatment may contribute to greater clarity 
in the enforcement framework, provided that the substantive scope of the two contraventions 
is clearly demarcated and that the same conduct is not made simultaneou sly liable to a civil 
penalty under Clause 92 and criminal prosecution under Clause 93, except where the Code 
expressly and clearly provides for such consequences. 
 
The Committee, therefore, recommend that the proposed restructuring of the penalty 
provisions be considered in conjunction with a comprehensive redrafting of Clauses 92 and 
93 so as to ensure consistency between the nature of the contravention, the applicable penalty 
and the consequences flowing therefrom. 
 
49.11  The Committee are further of the considered view that Clause 93(g), which empowers 
the Board to specify additional acts of market abuse through regulations, warrants 
reconsideration. Since contravention of Clause 93 attracts criminal prosecution and 

219 
 
imprisonment under Clause 96, the Committee are of the view that the categories of conduct 
constituting criminal market abuse should be expressly and exhaustively specified in the 
primary legislation. 
 
The Committee are of the view that, as a matter of sound legislative policy, the foundational 
elements of criminally actionable market abuse should be set out in the Code itself. The 
Committee note that the Supreme Court, in In re: Delhi Laws Act, 1912 (AIR 1951 SC 332), held 
that essential legislative functions cannot be delegated. 
 
Accordingly, the Committee recommend that “ Clause 93(g) should be deleted because all 
possible acts of market abuse which require criminal action are covered under 93(a) to 93(f). 
Further, 93 (g) confer the power to SEBI to declare any activity as criminal and the power to 
authorise to criminal action should be in the limit and should not be delegated and therefore 
93(g) should be deleted”. And that all acts constituting market abuse and attracting criminal 
liability under Clause 96 be exhaustively specified in the Code itself. The Board may be 
empowered to make regulations only for carrying out the provisions of this Chapter and 
clarifying their implementation, and not for creating new categori es of criminally punishable 
conduct.  
 
The Committee further recommend that the conduct constituting market abuse and attracting 
criminal liability under Clause 96 be exhaustively set out in the parent legislation. The Board 
may, however, be empowered to make regulations for carrying out the pr ovisions of the 
relevant Chapter and for clarifying or operationalising the statutory framework, but such 
power should not extend to creating new categories of criminally punishable conduct. 
 
49.12  The Committee also recommend that any residual regulation -making power under 
Clauses 92 and 93 be guided by clear statutory principles and objective standards so as to 
ensure legal certainty, avoid excessive delegation, preserve the distinction between civil and 
criminal liability, and promote transparent, consistent and fair enforcement of the Code. 
 
The Committee would like to recommend the following formulation for the Principal Clause 93 
(headnote) so as to clearly define and limit the scope of “Acts of market abuse” attracting 
severe criminal liabilities and also action under PMLA  -- 
“93.   No person shall directly or indirectly commit any of the following Acts on market abuse, 
which involve serious systemic misconduct affecting the integrity of the securities market, 
namely:- ” 
Further, the Committee recommend that Schedule II to the Bill be suitably amended to delete 
the reference to Clause 94. The Committee wants to make it very clear that changes proposed 
in Clause 93 regarding market abuse in the Principal Clause (headnote) and Clause 93(a) to 
93(g) shall become effective for the purposes of inclusion of ‘Clause 93 read  with Clause 96’ 
in the Second Schedule of the Bill. 

220 
 
CHAPTER L 
 
OFFENCES AND PENALTIES 
 
Punishment for Failure to Comply with Orders 
 
Clause 94 
 
50.1  Clause 94 of the Bill provides for punishment for failure to comply with orders, as under:— 
 
94 Without prejudice to the imposition of any penalty under this Chapter, where any person— 
 
(a) fails to comply with an interim order made under sub-section (2) of section 27; or 
 
(b) fails to comply with the final order made under section 20, 
 
he shall be punishable with imprisonment for a term which shall not be less than one month but may 
extend to ten years, or with fine which shall not be less than ten lakh rupees but may extend to twenty-
five crore rupees, or with both. 
 
50.2  Some stakeholders pointed out that different levels of penalty amounts have been prescribed 
under the provisions, ranging from one crore rupees to a maximum of one hundred crore rupees. It 
was also submitted that the framework appears inconsistent, particularly in view of the absence of a 
similar formulation in Clause 96. 
 
Responding to the issue, the Ministry stated that Clauses 94 to 96 provide for criminal liability arising 
from the following acts:— 
 
1. non-compliance with orders of an adjudicating officer; 
2. non-compliance with directions of an investigating officer; and 
3. market abuse. 
 

221 
 
50.3  The Ministry further stated that all other contraventions under the existing securities laws have 
been decriminalised and that Clauses 97 to 109 provide for penalties in respect of such defaults and 
contraventions. It was submitted that, while the framewo rk under the Code is consistent, the 
provisions relating to offences and penalties may, for greater clarity, be placed in separate Chapters. 
 
50.4  On the concern that decriminalisation should be confined strictly to minor infringements which 
do not cause prejudice to any person, the Ministry replied as under:— 
 
“Under the Securities Markets Code, offences have been limited to three specific situations, as 
provided in Clauses 94, 95 and 96, and the remaining offences under the existing laws have been 
decriminalised. Thus, civil penalties have been provided for a wide range of contraventions under the 
Code. 
 
While decriminalisation has been undertaken to promote ease of doing business, it was also 
necessary to ensure adequate deterrence against violations of the Code. Accordingly, the penalties 
have been updated and enhanced so as to maintain deterrence even in the absence of imprisonment. 
 
Penalties under the securities laws were last revised in 2014 through the Securities Laws 
(Amendment) Act, 2014. Since the securities market has expanded significantly in terms of size, 
complexity and participation since 2014, it was considered necessary to further revise the penalties, 
keeping in view the increased scale and sophistication of financial offences. 
 
Clauses 97 to 102 and Clause 109 relate to minor and technical contraventions. In respect of such 
contraventions, the maximum penalty that may be imposed is ten crore rupees or three times the 
amount of unlawful gain or loss. 
 
Clauses 103 to 108 relate to contraventions of a more serious nature. In respect of such 
contraventions, the maximum penalty that may be imposed is one hundred crore rupees or three 
times the amount of unlawful gain or loss. 
 
The Securities Markets Code, therefore, calibrates the penalties according to the gravity of the 
contraventions so as to ensure proportionality while maintaining deterrence.” 
 
50.5  During the course of evidence, it was pointed out that the Chapter deals with different 
categories of civil and criminal offences. Clauses 94 to 96 deal with criminal offences, whereas 
Clauses 97 to 109 deal with civil contraventions. On the issue of maki ng a clear distinction between 
civil and criminal offences, the Secretary stated as under:— 

222 
 
 
“We should separate these two for better understanding.” 
  
50.6  The Committee note that Clause 94 of the Bill provides that a person who fails to 
comply with an interim order or a final order made under the relevant provisions of the Code 
shall be punishable with imprisonment for a term which shall not be less than one month but 
may extend to ten years, or with fine which shall not be less than ten lakh rupees but may 
extend to twenty-five crore rupees, or with both. 
 
The Committee further observe that the Chapter contains provisions relating to both civil and 
criminal offences. 
 
50.7  The Committee note that criminal offences punishable with imprisonment or fine are 
dealt with under Clauses 94 to 96, whereas civil penalties are dealt with under Clauses 97 to 
109. The Committee observe that these two categories are fundamentally distinc t in legal 
jurisprudence and are enforced through different fora. Criminal offences are adjudicated by 
the courts, whereas civil contraventions are adjudicated by the adjudicating officer. 
 
The Committee are, therefore, of the considered view that, in the interest of legislative clarity 
and ease of understanding, Chapter XIII, relating to offences and penalties, should be suitably 
restructured by separating the provisions relating to criminal offences from those dealing with 
civil penalties. 
  

223 
 
CHAPTER LI 
Punishment for Market Abuse 
 
Clause 96 
 
51.1  Clause 96 of the Bill provides for punishment for market abuse, as under:— 
 
96  Without prejudice to the imposition of any penalty under this Chapter, where any person commits, 
attempts to commit or abets the commission of market abuse, he shall be punishable with 
imprisonment for a term which may extend to ten years or with fine which shall not be less than ten 
lakh rupees but may extend to twenty-five crore rupees, or with both. 
 
51.2  One stakeholder suggested that a reference to Clause 94 be incorporated in Clause 96. 
Responding to the suggestion, the Ministry stated that Clause 96 provides for punishment for market 
abuse, which includes certain fraudulent acts, whereas Clause 94 provides for punishment for failure 
to comply with orders passed under sections 20 and 27. It was further stated that these are two 
distinct subject matters. The Ministry also submitted that penalties for fraudulent or unfair practices 
under Clause 92 are  provided under Clause 103 and that such contraventions have been 
decriminalised, with a corresponding enhancement in the quantum of penalties to maintain 
deterrence. 
 
51.3  It was further suggested that the use of the conjunction “or” dilutes the severity of the 
punishment, as it leaves open the possibility of imposing only a monetary penalty even in cases 
warranting custodial punishment. 
 
Responding to the suggestion, the Ministry stated as under:— 
 
“Under section 24 of the Securities and Exchange Board of India Act, 1992, any offence is punishable 
with imprisonment for a term which may extend to ten years or with fine which may extend to twenty-
five crore rupees, or with both. Under Clause 96, any pe rson who commits, attempts to commit or 
abets the commission of market abuse shall be punishable with imprisonment for a term which may 
extend to ten years or with fine which shall not be less than ten lakh rupees but may extend to twenty-
five crore rupees , or with both. The discretion continues to vest with the Special Court to impose 
imprisonment or fine or both. Even where the Special Court decides to impose only a fine, such 
punishment would follow a criminal prosecution, as Clause 96 is categorised as a criminal offence 
under the Code, thereby retaining its deterrent effect.” 
 

224 
 
51.4  A suggestion was also received regarding the inconsistency between the term of 
imprisonment specified in Clause 96 and that mentioned in the Notes on Clauses. When asked about 
this inconsistency during the course of evidence, the Secretary stated as under:— 
 
“…there is an inconsistency in the term of imprisonment mentioned in Clause 96 and the Notes on 
Clauses relating to Clause 96. We have examined it and appropriate drafting changes can be made.” 
 
51.5  The Committee have examined Clause 96 of the Bill and note that it provides that any 
person who commits, attempts to commit or abets the commission of market abuse shall be 
liable to imprisonment for a term which may extend to ten years, or with fine whic h shall not 
be less than ten lakh rupees but may extend to twenty-five crore rupees, or with both, without 
prejudice to the imposition of any penalty under Chapter XIII. 
 
51.6  The Committee observe that the present arrangement of the provisions relating to civil 
penalties and criminal offences lacks clarity. The Committee further note that the Ministry has 
accepted that the existing drafting contains inconsistencies. 
 
The Committee, therefore, recommend that the Chapter be suitably restructured so that a clear 
distinction is maintained between offences and the penalties applicable thereto. 
 
51.7  The Committee further observe that there is a discrepancy between the term of 
imprisonment specified in Clause 96 and that stated in the Notes on Clauses relating thereto. 
The Committee are of the view that such inconsistency may give rise to interpretati onal 
ambiguity. 
 
The Committee, therefore, recommend that appropriate drafting changes be made to ensure 
that Clause 96 and the corresponding Notes on Clauses are fully aligned with each other and 
with the legislative intent. 
  

225 
 
CHAPTER LII 
Penalty where No Specific Provision for Penalty is Provided 
 
Clause 109 
 
52.1  Clause 109 of the Bill provides for a residuary penalty where no specific provision for penalty 
is provided, as under:— 
 
109  If any person contravenes any of the provisions of this Code or the rules or regulations made 
thereunder for which no penalty or punishment is provided in this Code, such person shall be liable 
to the following penalty:— 
 
(a) where the default results in unlawful gain or causes loss to an investor or any other person, and 
such gain or loss is quantifiable, a penalty which shall not be less than one lakh rupees but may 
extend to three times the amount of such unlawful gain or loss, whichever is higher; 
 
(b) where the default does not result in unlawful gain or loss, or such unlawful gain or loss is not 
quantifiable, for reasons to be recorded in writing, a penalty which shall not be less than one lakh 
rupees but may extend to one lakh rupees for each day during which such default continues, subject 
to a maximum of ten crore rupees. 
 
52.2  One stakeholder suggested that, instead of prescribing a fixed monetary ceiling, the penalties 
under Clause 109 may be calibrated in proportion to the financial scale of the entity. 
 
Responding to the suggestion, the Ministry stated as under:— 
 
“A residuary penalty has been provided under Clause 109, which may be applied where no specific 
penalty has been prescribed. The penalty under this Clause is divided into two categories. The first 
category is linked to unlawful gains and losses where they are quantifiable. The second category 
provides a monetary ceiling where such gains or losses are not quantifiable. Given that this is a 
residuary provision, the prescribed amount provides a sufficient deterrent effect. The Code provides 
a graver set of penalties under Clauses 103 and 104 for conduct considered to be more egregious. 
Further, Clauses 19 and 111 provide detailed factors which may be taken into account while 
determining penalties.” 
 

226 
 
52.3  On being further asked why an approach similar to that adopted under the Competition Act, 
2002, whereby penalties are linked to the financial scale of the entity, had not been adopted, the 
Ministry submitted as under:— 
 
“The existing position has been retained under the Securities Markets Code. The provision is a 
residuary penalty for contraventions for which no separate penalty or punishment has been provided 
under the Code. The penalty under this Clause is divided into two categories. The first category is 
linked to unlawful gains and losses where they are quantifiable. In such cases, a penalty of up to 
three times the amount of such unlawful gain or loss may be imposed. The second category covers 
cases where unlawful gains and losses are not quantifiable. In such cases, a maximum penalty of ten 
crore rupees may be imposed. Given that this is a residuary provision, the prescribed amount 
provides a sufficient deterrent effect. The Code provides a more stringent set of pena lties under 
Clauses 103 and 104 to address conduct that is considered more egregious. 
 
Moreover, the Competition Act, 2002 and the Securities Markets Code have distinct purposes and 
objectives. Under the Competition Act, the size of the enterprise is a primary consideration, since 
preventing abuse of dominant position is one of the principal  objectives of that Act. Under the 
Securities Markets Code, however, investor protection is the primary consideration, and the impact 
of the contravention on investors is the principal criterion for determining penalties. 
 
Further, under Clause 19, deterrence is one of the factors that the adjudicating officer may take into 
account. Therefore, where necessary, higher penalties may be imposed on larger enterprises to 
ensure adequate deterrence. However, such action would depend upon the facts and circumstances 
of each case. Linking penalties directly to the scale of the entity may result in penalties that are 
disproportionate to the violation, which may not be appropriate in the context of securities laws, where 
the objective is to regulate conduct in the securities market and protect investors.” 
 
52.4  During the course of evidence, the Committee emphasised that the Hon’ble Supreme Court 
has repeatedly held that penalties should be proportionate to the nature of the offence. Responding 
to the issue, the Secretary stated as under:— 
 
“We are saying that the principle of proportionality is embedded in the Securities Markets Code at 
different places.” 
 
52.5  The Ministry further stated that the provision is similar to section 450 of the Companies Act, 
2013. 
 
52.6  The Committee have examined Clause 109 of the Bill and note that it provides for a 
residuary penalty in respect of contraventions for which no separate penalty is prescribed 

227
