under the Code. The Committee further note that Clause 109 distinguishes between defaults 
where the unlawful gain or loss is quantifiable, in which case the penalty may extend to three 
times such gain or loss, and defaults where the unlawful gain or loss i s not quantifiable, in 
which case a maximum penalty of ten crore rupees may be imposed. 
 
The Committee, however, observe that a similarly graded structure is not readily discernible 
in several of the other specific penalty provisions contained in the Bill. While noting the 
Ministry’s submission that Clause 109 is modelled on section 450 of the Companies Act, 2013, 
the Committee also recognise that the Hon’ble Supreme Court has consistently held in its 
various judgments that penalties should be proportionate to the nature and gravity of the 
offence. 
 
The Committee, therefore, desire that the Ministry ensure that the penalty provisions under 
the Code are appropriately graded according to the nature and gravity of the contravention 
and are consistent with the principle of proportionality. 
  

228 
 
CHAPTER LIII 
General Conditions for Board and other Regulatory Authority 
 
Clause 121 
 
53.1  Clause 121 of the Bill provides for the general conditions applicable to the Board and other 
regulatory authorities, as under:— 
 
121  The Board and the other regulatory authority, while exercising their powers and performing 
their duties under this Chapter, shall— 
 
(a) facilitate investor participation in other regulated instruments, ensure investor protection and 
promote the orderly development of the securities markets; 
 
(b) consult and coordinate to ensure and achieve harmonisation of regulatory action in respect of any 
other regulated instrument; and 
 
(c) make information available to each other relating to the issuance, holding, listing, trading and 
settlement of other regulated instruments in a timely manner. 
 
53.2  One stakeholder suggested that clauses 121(b) and 121(c) be amended to provide that the 
Board and the other regulatory authority shall “consult and coordinate to ensure and achieve 
harmonisation of regulatory action in respect of the issuance, holding, listing, trading and settlement 
of any other regulated instruments.” 
 
53.3  On being asked whether Clause 121(b) may be made more specific and focused on 
coordination relating to the issuance, holding, listing, trading and settlement of other regulated 
instruments, the Ministry concurred with the suggestion and agreed to make app ropriate drafting 
changes. 
 
53.4  The Committee have examined Clause 121 of the Bill and note that it lays down the 
general conditions applicable to the Board and the other regulatory authorities while 
exercising their powers and performing their functions, including facilitating investor  
participation, ensuring investor protection and promoting the orderly development of the 
securities markets. 

229 
 
 
The Committee, however, observe that Clause 121(b), in its present formulation, is couched 
in broad terms and does not specifically identify the areas in which regulatory coordination is 
required. The Committee, therefore, recommend that Clause 121(b) be s uitably redrafted so 
as to expressly provide for coordination in matters relating to the issuance, holding, listing, 
trading and settlement of other regulated instruments. 
  

230 
 
CHAPTER LIV 
National Institute of Securities Markets 
 
Clause 129 
 
54.1  Clause 129 of the Bill provides for the National Institute of Securities Markets, as under:— 
 
129  (1) The National Institute of Securities Markets, a public trust established by the Board, shall be 
deemed to have been established under this Code. 
 
(2) The Board shall regulate the National Institute of Securities Markets for capacity building of 
intermediaries. 
 
54.2  Some stakeholders suggested that the term “intermediaries” be substituted with “securities 
market service providers and investors” so as to broaden the scope and applicability of the provision. 
 
54.3  On being asked about the suggestion relating to the substitution of the term “intermediaries” 
with “securities market service providers and investors”, the Ministry, in its written reply, 
acknowledged the validity of the suggestion and stated that appropr iate drafting changes may be 
incorporated. 
 
54.4  During the course of deliberations, the Secretary, Department of Economic Affairs, reaffirmed 
the Ministry’s position as under:— 
 
“It says that ‘for intermediaries’ should be substituted by ‘ securities market service providers and 
investors’. We feel that is a very good suggestion and we should broaden the scope.” 
 
54.5  The Committee have examined Clause 129 of the Bill and note that it provides statutory 
recognition to the National Institute of Securities Markets, which has hitherto functioned 
without an explicit legislative foundation, and entrusts the Board with regulating the Institute 
for the capacity building of intermediaries. The Committee appreciate the objective of 
providing a statutory basis to the National Institute of Securities Markets and recognise the 
important role played by the Institute in promoting education, research, training and capacity 
building in the securities market ecosystem. 
 

231 
 
54.6  The Committee further note the concerns expressed by stakeholders that the existing 
reference to “intermediaries” narrows the scope of the Institute’s mandate, whereas the 
securities market comprises a much wider ecosystem, including securities market ser vice 
providers, investors and other market participants. The Committee further note that the 
Ministry has agreed to substitute the term “intermediaries” with “ securities market service 
providers and investors”. The Committee are of the view that such substitution would more 
appropriately reflect the broader role of the National Institute of Securities Markets in 
strengthening investor awareness, financial literacy and capacity building. 
  

232 
 
CHAPTER LV 
Power of Board to Delegate 
 
Clause 132 
 
55.1  Clause 132 of the Bill provides for the power of the Board to delegate, as under:— 
 
132  The Board may, by general or special order in writing, delegate to any Member, officer of the 
Board or any other person, subject to such conditions, if any, as may be specified in the order, such 
of its powers and functions under this Code, except the powers exercisable under sections 128, 146 
and 149, as it may deem necessary. 
 
55.2  A suggestion was received that the power of delegation should not extend to “any other 
person”, on the ground that core regulatory functions should remain non -delegable in order to 
preserve institutional accountability and regulatory integrity. 
 
On being asked about the rationale for permitting delegation of powers to “any other person” under 
Clause 132, the Ministry, in its written reply, stated as under:— 
 
“Clause 132 is based on section 19 of the Securities and Exchange Board of India Act, 1992 and the 
same position has been retained under the Securities Markets Code. Clauses 146 and 149 provide 
for the regulation-making and subsidiary instruction-making powers of the Board, which encompass 
the entire scope of the quasi-legislative functions of SEBI. These powers are expressly excluded from 
delegation under the Code. Wherever the Securities Markets Code provides a specific regulation -
making power, for example, the designation of the adjudicating officer under Clause 17, such power 
is to be exercised collectively by the Board. 
 
Delegation of powers by the Board under Clause 132 is consistent with the overall scheme of the 
Code. In practice, SEBI issues orders under section 19 of the Securities and Exchange Board of India 
Act, 1992. Such orders are generally in the nature of distr ibution of administrative responsibilities 
among various officers of SEBI, unless a specific regulation-making power is provided. These orders 
are also published on the SEBI website. 
 
Various functions of the Board under the Code may be delegated for reasons of administrative 
efficiency or to utilise the expertise of other persons. For instance, under Clause 15(3), the 
Investigating Officer is empowered to requisition the services of an y police officer or officer of the 
Central Government to assist in an investigation. Similarly, under Clause 136, the Recovery Officer, 

233 
 
who is an officer of the Board, may seek the assistance of the local district administration, including 
the local police, while exercising recovery powers.” 
 
55.3  With regard to comparable legislation, the Ministry stated as under:— 
 
“Section 54A of the Reserve Bank of India Act, 1934, section 230 of the Insolvency and Bankruptcy 
Code, 2016, section 49 of the Pension Fund Regulatory and Development Authority Act, 2013 and 
section 23 of the Insurance Regulatory and Development Authority  Act, 1999 permit the respective 
authorities to delegate their functions to other members or officers.” 
 
55.4  On being asked about the prevailing practice, the Secretary, Department of Economic Affairs, 
stated during the course of evidence that the Ministry would verify whether the power to delegate 
functions to “any other person” had ever been exercised and, if so, the manner in which such power 
had been exercised. 
 
55.5  On being asked whether any contemporary legislation permits a regulator to delegate its 
powers to “any other person”, the Secretary, Ministry of Finance, referred to section 49 of the Pension 
Fund Regulatory and Development Authority Act, 2013, which contains a similar provision, and stated 
that the Ministry would examine whether, and in what manner, such powers had been exercised 
under both the Pension Fund Regulatory and Development Authority Act, 2013 and the Securities 
and Exchange Board of India framework. 
 
55.6  On being asked whether regulatory functions, such as investigation, inquiry or adjudication, 
could be delegated to private persons outside the control of the Government, the Secretary, 
Department of Economic Affairs, stated that the Ministry would examine the issue. 
 
55.7  The Committee have examined Clause 132 of the Bill and note that it empowers the 
Board to delegate its powers and functions to any Member, officer of the Board or “any other 
person”, subject to the conditions specified therein, except in respect of the po wers 
exercisable under sections 128, 146 and 149. 
 
The Committee are of the view that the expression “any other person” is unduly broad and 
may permit the delegation of regulatory functions to persons outside the control of the 
Government or the Board. The Committee consider that such powers require a high degree of 
institutional accountability and ought not to be delegated to persons who are not part of the 
regulatory framework. 
 
The Committee, therefore, recommend that the expression “any other person” be deleted from 
Clause 132 and that the necessary drafting changes be carried out accordingly. 

234 
 
CHAPTER LVI 
Protection of Action Taken in Good Faith 
 
Clause 139 
 
56.1  Clause 139 of the Bill provides for protection of action taken in good faith, as under:— 
 
139 No suit, prosecution or other legal proceeding shall lie against the Central Government or the 
Board or any officer of the Central Government or any Member referred to in sub-section (1) of section 
4, officer or other employee of the Board for anything wh ich is done or intended to be done in good 
faith under this Code or the rules or regulations or bye-laws made thereunder. 
 
56.2  Some stakeholders suggested that the protection available for actions taken in good faith 
should also be extended to the members and officers of the Market Infrastructure Institutions (MIIs) 
and the Securities Appellate Tribunal (SAT) while discharging their functions under the Code. It was 
submitted that the Securities Appellate Tribunal performs adjudicatory functions under the Code and, 
therefore, protection against legal proceedings for bona fide acts should also be extended to its 
Presiding Officer, Members, officers and employees, in line with comparable statutory frameworks. 
 
It was further submitted that the role and responsibilities of Market Infrastructure Institutions, 
particularly stock exchanges and clearing corporations, have increased under the Securities Markets 
Code, 2025. Since Market Infrastructure Institutions act as first-level regulators and undertake various 
regulatory functions in good faith, and are presently afforded protection under the Securities Contracts 
(Regulation) Act, 1956, it was suggested that a harmonised framework be provided under the 
proposed Cod e by extending similar protection to the officers and members of the Market 
Infrastructure Institutions and the Securities Appellate Tribunal. 
 
56.3  During the course of deliberations on the issue of extending protection for actions taken in 
good faith to the Market Infrastructure Institutions and the Securities Appellate Tribunal, the 
Secretary, Department of Economic Affairs, stated as under:— 
 
“To extend the good faith protection to MII and SAT, which we have accepted.” 
 
56.4  The Committee have examined Clause 139 of the Bill and note that it provides 
protection from legal proceedings to the Central Government and its officers, the Board, its 
Members, officers and employees, for actions taken in good faith under the provisions of the 
Code. 

235 
 
 
The Committee further note that the Securities Appellate Tribunal performs adjudicatory 
functions under the Code, while the Market Infrastructure Institutions, particularly stock 
exchanges and clearing corporations, perform important regulatory functions a s first-level 
regulators. The Committee also note that the roles and responsibilities of the Market 
Infrastructure Institutions have increased significantly under the proposed Securities Markets 
Code as compared to the Securities Contracts (Regulation) Act, 1956. 
 
The Committee are of the considered view that the Presiding Officer, Members, officers and 
employees of the Securities Appellate Tribunal, while discharging their adjudicatory 
functions, and the officers and members of the Market Infrastructure Institution s, while 
discharging their statutory functions, exercise important responsibilities and are required to 
act and take decisions in good faith in the discharge of their respective duties. 
 
The Committee are of the view that extending similar protection to such functionaries would 
enable them to discharge their statutory responsibilities fairly, independently and without 
apprehension of unnecessary legal proceedings, while remaining accountab le under the 
framework of the Code. The Committee further note that the Securities Contracts (Regulation) 
Act, 1956 already provides protection for actions taken in good faith by the Market 
Infrastructure Institutions. 
 
The Committee, therefore, recommend that the protection available under Clause 139 be 
extended to the Presiding Officer, Members, officers and employees of the Securities 
Appellate Tribunal, and to the officers and members of the Market Infrastructure Institutions, 
with appropriate safeguards, and that the necessary drafting changes be made. 
  

236 
 
CHAPTER LVII 
Exemption from Tax on Income 
 
Clause 140 
 
57.1  Clause 140 of the Bill provides for exemption from tax on income, as under:— 
 
140  Notwithstanding anything contained in the Income -tax Act, 2025 or any other law for the time 
being in force relating to tax on income, profits or gains, the Board shall not be liable to pay income-
tax or any other tax in respect of its income, profits or gains derived. 
 
Explanation.—For the removal of doubts, it is hereby clarified that, until the provisions of the Income-
tax Act, 2025 come into force, the corresponding provisions of the Income-tax Act, 1961 shall apply. 
 
57.2  On being asked whether the Ministry had consulted the Central Board of Direct Taxes 
regarding the exemption proposed to be granted to the Board from income -tax in respect of its 
income, profits or gains, the Secretary stated during the course of evidence as under:— 
 
“The matter would be taken up with the CBDT.” 
 
57.3  Thereupon, the Secretary, Department of Economic Affairs, reiterated that the matter would 
be taken up with the Central Board of Direct Taxes. 
 
57.4  The Committee have examined Clause 140 of the Bill and note that it seeks to exempt 
the Board from payment of income -tax or any other tax in respect of its income, profits or 
gains. The Committee further note that, since exemptions relating to income-tax are governed 
by the Income-tax Act, 2025, the corresponding provisions of that Act should appropriately 
reflect the exemption proposed under the Code to ensure its effective implementation. 
 
The Committee, therefore, recommend that the Ministry pursue the matter with the Central 
Board of Direct Taxes to ensure that the exemption proposed under Clause 140 is 
appropriately reflected in the relevant provisions of the Income-tax Act, 2025. 
  

237 
 
CHAPTER LVIII 
Code not to Apply in Certain Cases 
 
Clause 143 
 
58.1  Clause 143 of the Bill provides for cases where the Code shall not apply, as under:— 
 
143  (1) The provisions of this Code shall not apply to the Central Government, the Reserve Bank, 
any local authority or any corporation established by a special law, or any person who has effected 
any transaction with or through the agency of any such authority referred to in this sub-section: 
 
Provided that the provisions of this Code shall apply to such transactions or securities that are listed 
or proposed to be listed, and to the issuer of such securities. 
 
(2) Without prejudice to the provisions contained in sub -section (1), if the Central Government is of 
the opinion that, in the interest of trade and commerce or the economic development of the country, 
it is necessary or expedient so to do, it may, by noti fication, exempt any class of contracts from the 
application of the provisions of this Code, subject to such conditions, limitations or restrictions, if any, 
as may be specified. 
 
58.2  Some stakeholders suggested that the clause be amended by inserting the words 
“State/Union Territory Government” after “Central Government” so as to make it explicit that the 
provisions of the Code would not apply to the State Governments and Union Territory Governments 
as well. 
 
58.3  On being asked about the suggestion relating to the addition of “State/Union Territory 
Government” after “Central Government”, the Ministry, in its written reply, agreed with the suggestion 
and stated that the provision is based on section 28 of the Secur ities Contracts (Regulation) Act, 
1956, which refers to “Government” and not specifically to the “Central Government”. Accordingly, 
the Ministry stated that the provision may be aligned with the corresponding provision of the Securities 
Contracts (Regulation) Act, 1956. 
 
58.4  During the course of deliberations, the Secretary, Department of Economic Affairs, stated as 
under:— 
 

238 
 
“DEA is of the view that only the term ‘Government’ may be used, as the same is defined in the 
General Clauses Act, 1897 to mean the following: 
 
According to the General Clauses Act, 1897: 
 
Section 3 – Definitions 
 
(23) ‘Government’ or ‘the Government’ shall include both the Central Government and any State 
Government; 
 
(60) ‘State Government’,— 
 
(c) as respects anything done or to be done after the commencement of the Constitution (Seventh 
Amendment) Act, 1956, shall mean, in a State, the Governor, and in a Union territory, the Central 
Government. 
 
Hence, Clause 143 may be amended by replacing ‘Central Government’ with ‘Government’.” 
 
58.5  Thereupon, the Committee observed that, since the proposed legislation is a Union 
enactment, the words “State/Union Territory Government” should be inserted after “Central 
Government”. 
  
58.6  The Committee have examined Clause 143 of the Bill and note the submission of the 
Ministry that, under the General Clauses Act, 1897, the term “Government” includes both the 
Central Government and the State Governments and that, in the case of Union Territories, the 
relevant provisions of that Act also apply. However, the Committee are of the view that, since 
the Securities Markets Code, 2025 is a proposed Union legislation, it would be more 
appropriate to expressly specify “State/Union Territory Government” after “Central 
Government” so as to avoid any ambiguity regarding the applicability of the Code. 
 
The Committee, therefore, recommend that Clause 143 be suitably amended to insert 
“State/Union Territory Government” after “Central Government”. 
  

239 
 
CHAPTER LIX 
Process of Issuing Regulations 
 
Clause 147 
 
59.1  Clause 147 of the Bill provides for the process of issuing regulations, as under:— 
 
147 (1) The Board shall ensure transparency while making regulations by— 
 
(a) publishing draft regulations, along with such other details, on its website and inviting public 
comments for a specified period prior to issuing the regulations; 
 
(b) publishing a general statement of its response to the public comments, not later than the date of 
notification of the regulations; and 
 
(c) periodically reviewing such regulations. 
 
(2) Notwithstanding anything contained in sub-section (1), where the Board is of the opinion that the 
regulations are required to be made urgently in the public interest, it may, for reasons to be recorded 
in writing, dispense with the requirement of publication of the draft regulations. 
 
59.2  Some stakeholders suggested that suitable exemptions from public consultation should be 
expressly provided in the Securities Markets Code in respect of internal matters. It was submitted that 
the Securities and Exchange Board of India has framed the Securities and Exchange Board of India 
(Procedure for Making, Amending and Reviewing of Regulations) Regulations, 2025, prescribing the 
process for regulation -making, public consultation and stakeholder engagement in the interest of 
transparency, notwithst anding the absence of any statutory requirement mandating public 
consultation prior to the making of regulations. 
 
It was further submitted that regulation 9 of the said Regulations exempts public consultation in 
respect of regulations relating to the internal organisational matters of the Board; procedural 
requirements or issues which, in the opinion of the Chairperson, do not involve any substantive policy 
changes; and amendments to the said Regulations. 
 

240 
 
59.3  In response to the suggestion regarding suitable exemptions from public consultation for 
internal matters, the Secretary, Department of Economic Affairs, stated during the course of 
deliberations as under:— 
 
“SEBI had requested exemption also for internal matters and we had been instructed to examine 
formulations adopted in other Acts, which we had presented earlier. We found that the Competition 
Act contains suitable language, which we have proposed in response to the stakeholder consultation. 
Accordingly, a provision may be added to provide that, where the subject matter of the regulations 
relates solely to the internal functioning of the Board, it may, for reasons to be recorded in writing, 
dispense with the requirement of public consultation.” 
 
59.4  The Committee have examined Clause 147 of the Bill and note that it seeks to ensure 
transparency in the process of making regulations by requiring the publication of draft 
regulations, inviting public comments thereon, publishing the Board’s response to s uch 
comments and undertaking periodic review of the regulations. 
 
The Committee, however, note with concern that the Bill does not provide any exemption from 
public consultation in respect of matters relating solely to the internal functioning of the 
Board. The Committee are of the view that regulations relating to the i nternal organisational 
matters of the Board, procedural requirements or issues that do not involve any substantive 
policy changes, and amendments to the regulations governing the regulation-making process 
may not require public consultation. 
 
The Committee, therefore, recommend that an exemption from public consultation be 
provided only where the regulations relate solely to the internal functioning of the Board, 
subject to the reasons for such exemption being recorded in writing. 

241 
 
Chapter- LX 
The Prevention of Money-laundering Act, 2002 
Clause 155 
60.1 The Prevention of Money-laundering Act, 2002 shall be amended in the manner provided in 
the Second Schedule. 
 
60.2 During the deliberations, concerns were raised regarding the proposal under Clause 
155 to include certain offences under the proposed Securities Markets Code as scheduled 
offences under the Prevention of Money -Laundering Act, 2002 (PMLA). It was pointed th at 
such inclusion could result in disproportionate enforcement consequences, particularly where 
the underlying contraventions involve technical, procedural or regulatory non -compliance 
rather than serious fraudulent or criminal misconduct, thereby imp osing significant collateral 
consequences on regulated entities.  
In this regard, on the question as to what criteria were adopted by the Ministry for identifying 
the offences proposed to be included in the Schedule to the PMLA and whether there was 
any legal, regulatory or impact assessment undertaken before finalising this proposal. And  
safeguards envisaged to ensure that the stringent provisions of the PMLA are invoked only in 
cases involving serious market abuse or other grave misconduct. The Ministry in its written 
reply stated as under: 
 
Current Position 
 
Currently,  SA -12A (Prohibition of manipulative and deceptive devices, insider trading and 
substantial acquisition of securities or control) and SA -24 (Offences for contravention of the 
provisions of the Act) are incorporated as Scheduled Offences under PM LA. Under SA-24, 
any contravention of the SEBI Act and the rules and regulations thereunder was a 
contravention.  
 
Position under SMC 
 
Under SMC, the offences have been decriminalised and limited to only 3 categories: 
1) Non compliance of orders of an AO [clause 94]. 
2) Non-compliance of directions of an IO [clause 95].   
3) Market Abuse [clause 96]. 
 
Clause 155 read with Schedule II of SMC thus provides a consequential amendment to the 
PMLA. Through this amendment, SA -12A and 24 in the Schedule are replaced with the 
following: 
1) Clause 93 (Prohibition of Market Abuse) read with Clause 96 (Punishment for Market 
Abuse). 
2) Clause 94 (Punishment for Non Compliance of orders of the Adjudicating Officer). 
 

242 
 
The linkage to PMLA has only been extended to Clause 94 and 96, instead of the omnibus 
criminal provision under SA -24 in the existing position to balance the objective of 
decriminalisation with ensuring deterrence and to promote market certainty.  
 
International Position 
Recommendation 3 of International Standards on Combating Money Laundering and the Financing 
of Terrorism & Proliferation (FATF Recommendations) requires that: “Countries should apply the 
crime of money laundering to all serious offences, with a view to inc luding the widest range of 
predicate offences”.   The list of designated offences under FATF Recommendations includes “insider 
trading and market manipulation”. Market manipulation is a subset of the umbrella term “market 
abuse” and includes acts like inflating prices, indices, and influencing dealings in securities.   
Thus, according to the FATF recommendations as well, countries are required to categorise 
contraventions such as insider trading and market manipulation as contraventions under money 
laundering. 
In the United States, money laundering offences under 18 U.S.C. § 1956 apply to the proceeds of a 
"specified unlawful activity", which expressly includes a range of federal securities offences such as 
securities fraud, insider trading and market manipulation. The United Kingdom follows an all -crimes 
approach under the Proceeds of Crime Act 2002, under which the proceeds of any criminal offence, 
including offences under the financial services and market abuse regime, constitute "criminal 
property" and may give rise to money laundering offences. Singapore adopts a scheduled predicate 
offence model under the Corruption, Drug Trafficking and Other Serious Crimes (Confiscation of 
Benefits) Act 1992 (CDSA), where money laundering offences apply to the proceeds of specified 
"serious offences"; the First Schedule to the CDSA expressly includes numerous offences under the 
Securities and Futures Act 2001, including insider trading, false trading, market manipulation and 
other market misconduct offences. Thus the position under SMC is consistent with other countries. 
 
60.3 Certain stakeholders have also expressed apprehension that disclosures or admissions made 
during settlement proceedings under Clause 26 of the SMC may subsequently be relied upon in 
proceedings under the PMLA, thereby exposing the person concerned to paral lel criminal 
consequences. They have, therefore, suggested that appropriate statutory safeguards may be 
considered to prevent any unintended prejudice. In response to the concern raised, the Ministry in its 
written reply stated that: 
Clause 26 of the SMC is an expansion of the settlement procedure under the existing framework 
which allows individuals facing civil or administrative enforcement actions to resolve their cases by 
disclosing all relevant information and reaching a settlemen t with SEBI. The settlement does not 
require an admission of guilt as provided in the SEBI (Settlement Proceedings) Regulations, 2018. 
The same position is continued under the SMC. Given the predicate offence under the PMLA gets 
established only when the offence in the primary act is proved, no proceedings under the PMLA can 
be initiated when a contravention is settled which is in relation to civil and administrative proceedings 
only.  
However, it is noted that settlement of civil and administrative proceedings does not bar criminal 
prosecution if the same act or contravention can result in both civil and administrative actions (by 
SEBI), as well as criminal proceedings (in court) (such as the prohibition of market abuse under 
Clause 93 of the SMC). These two sets of enforcement measures, civil or criminal, can operate 

243 
 
independently or simultaneously, depending on SEBI’s assessment of the severity of the violation . 
Thus, settlement does not technically remove criminal liability.  
This position is also supported by judicial precedents. The Bombay High Court, while deciding 
whether exoneration of adjudication proceeding will result in ipso facto  quashing of criminal 
prosecution, has held that:  
“20. …. exoneration in departmental or regulatory proceedings will bind criminal 
prosecution only in very limited situations . Three conditions must be satisfied. First, the 
adjudicating authority must have examined all the facts and evidence in detail and given a 
clear finding. An order passed only on technicalities like limitation or jurisdiction cannot bar 
prosecution. Second , there must be a clear conclusion that the allegations were wholly 
baseless or not proved at all. Third, the order must contain a clean declaration of innocence, 
holding the person not guilty of the misconduct. A mere absence of penalty or grant of 
benefit of doubt does not amount to exoneration on merits.” 
A similar issue was also discussed by the Bombay High Court in Manoj Gokulchand Seksaria case, 
which held that in view of the consent terms passed by SEBI, it would not be in the interest of justice 
to continue with the criminal proceedings, as it would be tantamount to an abuse of the process of 
the law. However, the Supreme Court set aside this case and remanded the matter to the Division 
Bench of the High Court without commenting on the merits of this point. 
 
Thus, if the same facts or issues are also involved in criminal proceedings, the situation is not fully 
resolved for the person settling the matter. For criminal proceedings, the individuals have separate 
recourse to seek compounding of an offence under Clause 117 and obtain immunity from 
prosecution for any offence under Clause 135. 
 
60.4 With regard to the concern that the offence of “Market Abuse” is broadly framed and leaves 
significant aspects of the prohibited conduct to be specified through subordinate legislation, it was 
suggested that the definition and essential ingredients of the offence be articulated with greater 
precision in the parent legislation, so that only clearly defined and serious instances of market 
misconduct attract the additional consequences under the PMLA. It was further pointed out that, in 
Clause 93(b), the use of the expression “material or non-public information”, instead of “material and 
non-public information”, may unintentionally widen the scope of the offence. In response to these 
concerns, the Ministry, in its written reply, stated as under: 
Delegation to Subordinate Legislation 
 
Section 12A of the SEBI Act, 1992 which contains a list of various prohibitions relating to usage of 
manipulative and deceptive devices, insider trading and substantial acquisition of securities or 
control. Contravention of Section 12A carries criminal liability. It is to be noted that earlier SEBI 
expanded the scope of these practices by issuing regulations such as SEBI (Prohibition of Fraudulent 
and Unfair Trade Practices) Regulations, 2003 and SEBI (Prevention of Insider Trading) Regulations, 
2015. These regulations were issued under the general regulation-making powers of the SEBI as no 
statutory anchor was provided under section 12A to expand the list of prohibitions. 
 
Under SA-24, the contraventions of these regulations would also attract criminal liability. This 
framework, under which SEBI identifies specific prohibited conduct through subordinate legislation, 

244 
 
has operated effectively for several years and has been retained under the SMC. 
 
60.5 The Committee note that Clause 155 read with Schedule II of the Bill seeks to make 
certain offences under the Securities Markets Code, 2025, predicate offences under the 
Prevention of Money-Laundering Act, 2002. In this regard, the Ministry has stated that , while 
the existing framework under the SEBI Act, 1992 includes an omnibus provision covering 
contraventions of the Act and the rules and regulations made thereunder, the proposed 
Securities Markets Code seeks to decriminalise such contraventions and  retain criminal 
liability only for three categories of offences, namely, non -compliance with orders of the 
Adjudicating Officer under Clause 94, non -compliance with directions of the Investigating 
Officer under Clause 95 and market abuse under Clause 96. The Ministry has further stated 
that the linkage with the PMLA has been limited to market abuse under Clause 93 read with 
Clause 96 and non-compliance with orders of the Adjudicating Officer under Clause 94, with 
a view to balancing decriminalisation with the need to maintain deterrence and promote 
market certainty. 
60.6 The Committee observe that Clauses 93 and 96, which relate to market abuse and its 
punishment, respectively, have been included in Schedule II to the PMLA. The Ministry has 
furnished a detailed justification for this inclusion, inter alia, by referring to the FATF 
Recommendations and the international practice of treating serious securities market 
offences, including insider trading and market manipulation, as predicate offences for money 
laundering. The Ministry has also emphasised that the provisions relating to market abuse are 
intended to address serious conduct affecting the integrity of the securities markets and has 
stated that the scope of Clause 93(b) is proposed to be narrowed to prevent overlap with 
Clause 92 and to ensure greater precision in defining the offence. 
However, the Committee note that Clause 94, which provides for punishment for non -
compliance with orders of the Adjudicating Officer, has also been included in Schedule II to 
the PMLA. While the Ministry has justified the inclusion of market abuse offences  under 
Clauses 93 and 96 on the ground that such offences constitute serious market misconduct 
and are consistent with international standards and practices. Non-compliance with an order 
of the Adjudicating Officer is, in essence, a failure to comply with a regulatory or adjudicatory 
direction and does not, by itself, constitute conduct involving market manipulation, insider 
trading or other substantive market abuse. Further, the Securities Markets Code itself 
provides a specific enforcement mechanism for d ealing with non-compliance with orders of 
the Adjudicating Officer under Clause 94. The Committee are, therefore, of the view that 
subjecting such non-compliance, in addition, to the consequences of its classification as a 
scheduled offence under the PMLA would be disproportionate. The Committee are of the view 
that the mere fact that an offence carries criminal liability under the Securities Markets Code 
may not, by itself, be sufficient to justify its inclusion as a scheduled offence under the PMLA, 
particularly given the stringent and wide -ranging consequences that may follow from such 
classification. 
60.7 The Committee, therefore, recommend that the reference to Clause 94 be omitted from 
Schedule II to the PMLA, in view of the distinct nature of the offence of non -compliance with 
an order of the Adjudicating Officer.  

245 
 
60.8 The Committee emphasise that the proposed amendments to Clause 93, including the 
amendment of the principal clause (headnote) , the amendment to Clause 93(b), the deletion 
of the reference to “bye -laws” in Clause 93(c), and the deletion of Clause 93(g), assume 
particular significance in view of the proposed inclusion of Clause 93 read with Clause 96 in 
Schedule II to the PMLA under Clause 155. The Committee, therefore , recommend that the 
amendments to Clause 93 recommended in the preceding paragraphs of this Report be made 
applicable in the Schedule II as well naturally, so as to ensure that the scope and ingredients 
of the market abuse offences attracting the consequences of a scheduled offence under the 
PMLA are clearly, precisely and appropriately defined in the legislation. 
 

246 
 
CHAPTER LXI 
Excessive Delegation 
 
61.1  Several stakeholders expressed concerns that the Securities Markets Code, 2025 delegates 
a large number of substantive matters to rules and regulations, particularly in relation to market 
structure, innovation, investor classification, registration and supervision  of intermediaries, market 
misconduct, governance standards and regulatory procedures. Concerns were also expressed that 
the definition of “market participant” under Clause 2 may be interpreted to extend to investors in a 
manner that could result in regulatory overreach. Further concerns were raised regarding Clause 31, 
which enables the delegation of registration and supervisory functions to Market Infrastructure 
Institutions (MIIs) and Self -Regulatory Organisations (SROs), on the ground that core regulato ry 
functions, such as registration, grant, refusal, suspension and cancellation of registration, ought 
ordinarily to remain with the Board. It was also contended that such delegation may result in 
interpretational issues, compliance burdens and inconsisten cy with the existing regulatory 
architecture. 
 
61.2  Further concerns were raised regarding Clause 92(f), which enables the Board to specify 
additional fraudulent and unfair trade practices through regulations; Clause 93(g), which empowers 
the Board to specify additional acts constituting market abuse, read  with Clause 96 prescribing 
criminal penalties; Clause 143, which confers broad powers on the Central Government to exempt 
any class of persons, entities or transactions from the operation of the Code; Clause 17(4), read with 
Clause 146(2)(j), which e nables the Board to specify the procedure governing adjudication 
proceedings through regulations; and other provisions, including Clauses 11, 13(5), 33, 37 and 42, 
where substantial regulatory powers are proposed to be exercised through delegated legislation. It 
was contended that such provisions may give rise to concerns of excessive delegation, particularly 
where they relate to criminal liability, adjudicatory safeguards or other essential legislative functions. 
 
61.3  In response to these concerns, the Ministry submitted that the regulation-making power of the 
Board has been retained and expanded under the Securities Markets Code to ensure effective 
regulation of the securities market. Since the Board is a specialised regulator possessing subject-
matter expertise, the operational details required for implementation of the Code are to be specified 
through subordinate legislation. The Ministry clarified that the definition of “market participant” has 
been introduced for drafting consistency and is intended to serve as an umbrella expression covering 
issuers, investors and intermediaries participating in the securities market. 
 
With regard to Clause 31, the Ministry submitted that the delegation of certain registration and 
supervisory functions to MIIs and SROs is only an enabling provision intended to leverage their 
specialised expertise and operational reach, while the Board co ntinues to retain overall regulatory 
supervision, specify eligibility conditions, impose conditions on delegated entities and review their 
actions. The Ministry further stated that, once the legislature has laid down the legislative policy, it 
may confer d iscretion upon an administrative authority regarding the implementation of that policy 
and that the jurisprudence relating to delegated legislation and sub-delegation is well settled. 

247 
 
 
61.4  With regard to Clauses 92 and 93, the Ministry submitted that the proposed framework is 
consistent with the existing securities law architecture, under which section 12A of the Securities and 
Exchange Board of India Act, 1992 prescribes broad statutory pr ohibitions, while the detailed 
identification of fraudulent, unfair and insider trading practices is provided through subordinate 
legislation. The Ministry further stated that regulations framed under the Code would be subject to 
public consultation and parliamentary scrutiny. 
 
61.5  The Committee have examined the provisions of the Securities Markets Code, 2025 and 
note that it seeks to establish a comprehensive and future-ready regulatory framework for the 
securities market by conferring substantial regulation -making powers upon the  Board and 
enabling the delegation of certain functions to Market Infrastructure Institutions and Self -
Regulatory Organisations. The Committee recognise that the securities market is dynamic, 
technology-driven and constantly evolving and that delegated legislation is indispensable for 
prescribing technical standards, operational requirements and other matters requiring 
flexibility and periodic modification. 
 
The Committee further note the rationale advanced by the Ministry that a specialised regulator 
such as the Board is best placed to specify detailed implementation measures within the 
broad legislative framework laid down under the Code. The Committee also recognise that, 
when exercised within clearly defined statutory boundaries, delegated legislation facilitates 
the effective implementation  of the law by enabling timely regulatory responses to market 
developments, technological innovations and evolving bus iness practices without requiring 
frequent legislative amendments. Such flexibility promotes regulatory efficiency, operational 
certainty and ease of doing business. 
 
61.6  The Committee are of the view that delegated legislation should be exercised within 
clearly defined statutory boundaries so that the regulatory framework remains transparent, 
predictable and does not give rise to a perception of excessive delegation or unguided 
discretion. The Committee consider that regulations framed through a process of meaningful 
consultation and adequate scrutiny inspire confidence among investors and regulated 
entities, provide greater regulatory certainty, facilitate ease of doing business and 
significantly reduce the likelihood of avoidable litigation. 
 
61.7  At the same time, the Committee are of the considered view that an appropriate balance 
must be maintained between regulatory flexibility and legislative certainty. While delegated 
legislation is well suited for specifying procedural, technical and administrative matters, 
provisions involving substantive rights and obligations, criminal liability, adjudicatory 
safeguards, eligibility conditions, enforcement standards and the essential features of the 
regulatory framework should ordinarily be incorporated in the Code itself. 
 

248 
 
The Committee observe that several provisions of the Code confer broad discretionary powers 
upon the Board in areas such as registration and supervision of intermediaries, governance 
standards for market institutions, determination of market misconduct, gr ant of exemptions 
from the operation of the Code and adjudicatory procedures. In the absence of adequate 
statutory guidance, such broad delegations may give rise to legal uncertainty, inconsistent 
interpretation and avoidable litigation, while diminishing regulatory certainty for market 
participants. 
 
61.8  The Committee further observe that Clause 31 enables the Board to delegate 
registration and supervisory functions to Market Infrastructure Institutions and Self -
Regulatory Organisations. While the Committee recognise the operational efficiencies and 
institutional expertise that such entities may bring to the regulatory framework, they are of the 
view that functions relating to the grant, refusal, suspension and cancellation of registration 
involve significant legal and commercial consequences and, therefore, require an appropriate 
statutory framework. 
 
Accordingly, the Committee recommend that the Code should clearly specify the nature and 
extent of the functions that may be delegated, the principles governing such delegation, the 
accountability framework applicable to delegated entities, the supervisory  and oversight 
powers of the Board and an effective mechanism for review of decisions taken by Market 
Infrastructure Institutions or Self -Regulatory Organisations. The Committee further 
recommend that such delegation should remain enabling in nature and sh ould not dilute the 
Board’s primary responsibility and accountability as the statutory regulator. 
 
61.9  The Committee are particularly concerned that certain provisions of the Code, 
including Clauses 92(f), 93(g), 17(4) read with Clause 146(2)(j) and Clause 143, confer 
delegated powers in areas having significant legal consequences. The Committee are of the  
considered view that matters relating to the creation or expansion of criminal liability, 
prescription of essential procedural safeguards governing adjudication, conferral of broad 
exemption powers and other issues affecting substantive rights and li abilities should be 
governed by clear statutory principles. While delegated legislation may appropriately 
supplement the statutory framework by specifying implementation details and operational 
requirements, it should not determine matters involving essential legislative policy or 
substantive legal consequences. 
 
61.10  In view of the foregoing, the Committee recommend that the Government undertake a 
comprehensive review of all provisions involving extensive delegation of legislative powers, 
particularly Clauses 11, 13(5), 17(4) read with Clause 146(2)(j), 31, 33, 37, 4 2, 92(f), 93(g) read 
with Clause 96 and 143, with a view to ensuring that the Code itself lays down adequate 
legislative policy, objective standards, eligibility criteria, procedural safeguards and guiding 
principles. The Committee are of the view tha t delegated legislation should supplement the 
statutory framework by specifying matters of implementation and administration and should 

249 
 
not become the principal source for determining substantive rights, obligations or regulatory 
policy. 
 
61.11  The Committee further recommend that every enabling provision conferring regulation-
making or delegated powers should clearly define the scope, purpose and limits of such 
delegation. Regulations framed under the Code should remain consistent with the objectives 
and scheme of the Code and, wherever practicable, be preceded by meaningful public 
consultation and adequate regulatory scrutiny. Where such regulations materially affect 
rights, obligations or regulatory requirements, they should be accompanied by an appropriate 
explanatory statement setting out their objectives and rationale. Such an approach would 
strengthen transparency, accountability and regulatory certainty, reinforce inve stor 
confidence, facilitate ease of doing business and minimise the scope for ambiguity and 
avoidable litigation, while preserving the flexibility necessary for the effective regulation of a 
dynamic and evolving securities market.

Bill which is generally three months and may be extended with the permission of the Hon’ble 
Speaker. He also pointed out that the present Bill had been referred to the Committee with a 
request from the Minister-in-charge to present the report by the first day of the next session, 
that is, the Budget Session of 2026. However, the Committee observed that the time allocated 
for examination and reporting is less than half of the usual three-month period which may be 
insufficient given the need for a comprehensive review in view of the Bill’s potential impact.

working days.

PART I 
LIST OF WITNESSES 
 
Insolvency and Bankruptcy Board of India (IBBI) 
 
1. Shri Ravi Mital, Chairperson 
2. Shri Bhushan Kumar Sinha, Whole-time Member 
3. Shri Jithesh John, Executive Director 
4. Ms. Medha Shekar, Assistant General Manager 
 
At the outset, the Hon'ble Chairperson welcomed the Members and the witnesses to the 
sitting of the Committee convened to deliberate on the Securities Markets Code, 2025. He 
observed that the IBBI continues to play a critical role in implementing the insol vency and 
bankruptcy framework, thereby enhancing credit discipline and investor confidence. The 
Chairperson noted that IBBI’s insights and recommendations have consistently contributed to 
strengthening the country’s regulatory architecture and will be useful in ensuring that the Code 
meets its objectives of enhancing investor confidence, preserving market integrity, and 
promoting sustainable growth. The Chairperson also apprised the witnesses of Direction 55(1) 
issued by the Speaker. 
2. Thereafter, the Committee deliberated with the IBBI representatives on various 
provisions of the Securities Markets Code, 2025, including the mechanism for clearing 
corporations, the Insolvency and Bankruptcy Code waterfall mechanism, strengthening of the 
Securities Appellate Tribunal (SAT), clauses 68, 86, 139, 136(3) and 142 of the Code, section 
238 of the Insolvency and Bankruptcy Code, and matters concerning financial service 
providers. 
3. The Committee also sought clarifications on various other issues relating to the subject 
and the witnesses responded to some of those queries. The Chairperson then directed them 
to furnish written replies to the remaining points, which could not be readil y replied by them 
during the discussion, to the Secretariat within ten days.  
 
The witnesses then withdrew.

The Hon'ble Chairperson welcomed the witnesses to the sitting of the Committee 
convened to deliberate on the Securities Markets Code, 2025. He observed that both industry 
bodies viz., FICCI and ASSOCHAM, have consistently represented industry interests, 
facilitated dialogue between policymakers and market participants, and contributed 
meaningfully to economic reforms. The Chairperson further emphasized that collaboration 
among regulators, industry bodies and market participants will be essential for the Cod e to 
achieve its objectives. The Chairperson also apprised the witnesses of Direction 55(1) issued 
by the Speaker. 
2. During the deliberations, the major issues discussed included, inter alia, preserving 
the independence of the Securities and Exchange Board of India (SEBI); retention of 
registration and supervision with the capital markets regulator; registration of inve stors and 
sponsors; settlement principles under the Securities Markets Code, 2025; pooled investment 
vehicles; Ombudsperson; listing of companies without an offering of securities; treatment of 
unlisted companies; criminalization of misconduct; inter -regulatory jurisdictional disputes; 
definition of commodities; investor -facing operational matters such as nomination, 
transmission of securities, and succession in joint -account; investor protection and ease of 

280 
 
doing business; grievance redressal mechanism; clause 93; investment schemes and the 
status of crypto-assets; absence of a framework for algorithmic trading; systemic disclosure 
exemptions for listed public sector undertakings; structural separation of inv estigation and 
adjudication; market abuse; introduction of a sunset framework or mandatory 
dematerialization of physical securities; extension of the cooling -off period; and regulatory 
sandboxes. 
3. The Committee also sought clarifications on various other issues relating to the subject 
and the witnesses responded to some of those queries. The Chairperson then directed them 
to furnish written replies to the remaining points, which could not be readil y replied by them 
during the discussion, to the Secretariat within ten days. 
 
The witnesses then withdrew.

feasible within the existing policy framework.

Committee. Thereafter, the Committee took up the draft Report on the Bill 'Securities 
Market Code, 2025' pertaining to the Ministry of Finance for consideration and 
adoption.

to the Parliament in the ongoing Monsoon Session.

AS INTRODUCED IN LOK SABHA 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
Bill No. 200 of 2025 
 
THE SECURITIES MARKETS CODE, 2025 
A 
BILL 
to consolidate and amend the laws relating to the securities markets and for matters 
connected therewith or incidental thereto. 
BE it enacted by Parliament in the Seventy-sixth Year of the Republic of India 
as follows:— 
CHAPTER I 
PRELIMINARY 
5 1. (1) This Code may be called the Securities Markets Code, 2025. 
(2) It shall come into force on such date as the Central Government may, by 
notification in the Official Gazette, appoint: 
Provided that different dates may be appointed for different provisions of this 
Code and any reference in any such provision to the commencement of this Code 
10  shall be construed as a reference to the coming into force of that provision. 
 
 
 
 
Short title and 
commencement. 

 
 
307 
 
 
Definitions. 2. (1) In this Code, unless the context otherwise requires,— 
(a) “adjudicating officer” means a person designated as such under 
sub-section (1) of section 17; 
(b) “Bench” means a Bench of the Tribunal; 
(c) “beneficial owner” means a person whose name is recorded as such 
with a depository in respect of any securities or other regulated instrument and 
the expression “beneficial ownership” shall be construed accordingly; 
(d) “Board” means the Securities and Exchange Board of India referred 
to in sub-section (1) of section 3; 
(e) “bye-laws” means the bye -laws made by a market infrastructure 
institution under section 39; 
(f) “Chairperson” means the Chairperson of the Board appointed under 
section 4; 
(g) “clearing corporation” means a person undertaking the activity of 
clearing and settlement of trades in securities, but does not include a system 
provider defined in the Payment and Settlement Systems Act, 2007. 
Explanation.––For the purposes of this clause,–– 
(i) “clearing” means the process of transmitting, reconciling and to 
the extent applicable, confirming the payment obligations or transfers of 
securities prior to settlement; and 
(ii) “settlement” means the completion of delivery and payment of 
funds or securities or underlying of a derivative, to the relevant parties 
in a contract in order to settle their obligations or to provide a title to the 
securities or underlying or both; 
(h) “clearing member” means a person having clearing and settlement 
rights as specified in the bye-laws of a clearing corporation; 
(i) “commodity” means any kind of goods, including any activity, 
service, right, interest or event relating to or associated with such goods, as 
may be notified by the Central Government in consultation with the Board; 
(j) “contract” means a contract for or relating to the purchase or sale of 
securities; 
(k) “depository” means a person engaged in the activity of providing 
depository services, but shall not include a person providing depository 
services in relation to a Government security, a Registry or a ledger account 
maintained by the Reserve Bank or its agent in accordance with the provisions 
of the Government Securities Act, 2006; 
(l) “depository services” means any service relating to,— 
(i) recording a transaction in securities or other regulated 
instrument, including the allotment or transfer or transmission of 
ownership of such securities or other regulated instrument in 
dematerialised form in the record or register of a depository; 
(ii) distribution of any monetary or securities -based benefits 
associated with any securities or other regulated instrument; 
(iii) facilitating electronic voting and exercising any interest and 
rights associated with any securities or other regulated instrument; or 
(iv) such other services as may be specified by regulations;

40 
(m) “derivative” includes,— 
(i) a security derived from an underlying; 
(ii) a contract for the actual or intended delivery of the underlying 
on a future date and which is not a spot delivery contract; 
(iii) a contract that derives its value from prices or indices of prices 
or difference in prices of such underlying which may be settled with 
physical delivery of the underlying or by paying the difference in cash 
or equivalent; 
(iv) a contract which provides for a right, with or without any 
obligation for the actual delivery of the underlying on a future date; 
(v) a contract or a swap agreement for exchange of cash flows or 
underlying in future; 
(vi) a contract which provides for exchange of one or more 
payments in future based on the value of the underlying; 
(vii) a derivative structured upon the same or a different kind of 
derivative; 
(viii) such other instruments as the Central Government may 
notify; and 
(ix) a combination of  any contract or agreement specified  in 
sub-clauses (i) to (viii), 
but shall not include derivative as defined in clause ( a) of section 45U of the 
Reserve Bank of India Act, 1934 and such other instruments as the Central 
Government may notify in this regard; 
(n) “Fund” means the Fund referred to in sub-section (1) of section 124; 
(o) “goods” means any kind of movable property, other than actionable 
claims, money and securities; 
(p) “Government security” shall have the same meaning as assigned to 
it in clause (f) of section 2 of the Government Securities Act, 2006; 
(q) “intermediary” means,–– 
(i) an asset management company; 
(ii) a banker to an issue; 
(iii) a credit rating agency; 
(iv) a custodian; 
(v) a debenture trustee; 
(vi) a depository participant; 
(vii) an index provider in relation to indices traded in the securities 
markets; 
(viii) an investment adviser; 
(ix) an investment manager; 
(x) a Know Your Customer Registration Agency; 
(xi) a merchant banker; 
(xii) a portfolio manager; 
(xiii) a pooled investment vehicle; 

 
 
309 
 
 
(xiv) a registrar to an issue; 
(xv) a research analyst; 
(xvi) a share transfer agent; 
(xvii) a stock broker; 
(xviii) a trading member; 
(xix) a trustee of trust deed; 
(xx) an underwriter; 
(xxi) a vault manager; and 
(xxii) such other persons associated with the securities markets, as 
notified by the Board under sub-section (3) of section 34, 
but shall not include a person carrying out the activities of the nature 
mentioned in section 34 relating to instruments referred to in Chapter IIID of 
the Reserve Bank of India Act, 1934; 
(r) “investment scheme” means the scheme referred to in section 32; 
(s) “Investor Protection and Education Fund” means the fund referred to 
in section 125; 
(t) “issuer” means any person who makes or proposes to make or has 
made an issue of securities which are listed or to be listed; 
(u) “listing” means the admission of securities for trading on a stock 
exchange and the expression “listed” shall be construed accordingly; 
(v) “market infrastructure institution” means a stock exchange, a 
clearing corporation or a depository and includes such other class or classes 
of market infrastructure institutions, as may be notified by the Central 
Government, except those authorised or required to be authorised by other 
regulatory bodies; 
(w) “market participant” means a person or its agent participating in the 
securities markets as an issuer or an investor. 
Explanation.—For the purposes of this clause, an intermediary shall be 
deemed to be a market participant, if it participates in the securities markets 
as an investor or issuer; 
(x) “member”, in relation to— 
(i) a stock exchange or a clearing corporation, means a trading 
member or a clearing member; 
(ii) a depository, means a depository participant; and 
(iii) any other market infrastructure institution notified under 
clause (v), means a member of such market infrastructure institution; 
(y) “notification” means a notification published in the Official Gazette 
and the expressions “notify” or “notified” shall be construed accordingly; 
(z) “other regulated instrument” means an instrument that is primarily 
regulated by other regulatory authority; 
(za) “other regulatory authority” means an authority or body constituted 
or any department of the Central Government or the State Government 
authorised, under any other law for the time being in force to regulate goods, 
services, activities or transactions;

(zb) “person” includes— 
(i) an individual;

(iii) a company;

incorporated or not;

preceding sub-clauses; 
(zc) “pooled investment vehicle” means a fund referred to in 
sub-section (1) of section 33; 
(zd) “prescribed” means prescribed by rules made under this Code; 
(ze) “record” includes the records maintained in the form of books 
or in such other form as may be specified by regulations and 
electronic record as defined in clause ( t) of section 2 of the Information 
Technology Act, 2000; 
(zf) “Recovery Officer” means any officer of the Board authorised by 
general or special order in writing to exercise the powers of a Recovery Officer 
under this Code; 
(zg) “regulations” means the regulations made by the Board; 
(zh) “Schedule” means a Schedule appended to this Code; 
(zi) “securities” include— 
(i) shares, scrips, stocks, bonds, secured or unsecured debentures, 
warrants, or debenture stock or other marketable securities of a like 
nature in or of any incorporated company or a pooled investment vehicle 
or other body corporate; 
(ii) hybrid instruments; 
(iii) convertible instruments; 
(iv) derivatives; 
(v) units or any other instruments issued in respect of any 
investment scheme by a pooled investment vehicle to the investors in 
such schemes; 
(vi) security receipts managed and regulated in accordance with 
the provisions of the Securitisation and Reconstruction of Financial 
Assets and Enforcement of Security Interest Act, 2002; 
(vii) a certificate or an instrument, by whatever name called, issued 
to an investor by any issuer as a special purpose distinct entity which 
possesses any debt or receivable including mortgage debt, assigned to 
such entity, and acknowledging the beneficial interest of such investor 
in such debt or receivable including mortgage debt, as the case may be; 
(viii) global depository receipts and Indian depository receipts; 

 
 
311 
 
 
(ix) notes or papers issued for the purpose of raising of 
capital, which are listed or proposed to be listed; 
(x) Electronic Gold Receipts; 
(xi) Government security managed and regulated under the 
provisions of the Reserve Bank of India Act, 1934 and the 
Government Securities Act, 2006; 
(xii) zero coupon zero principal instruments; 
(xiii) bullion spot delivery contract and bullion depository 
receipt with underlying bullion in respect of issuance and trading 
in an International Financial Services Centre under the provisions 
of the International Financial Services Centres Authority 
Act, 2019; 
(xiv) onshore rupee bonds issued by multilateral institutions 
such as the Asian Development Bank and the International Finance 
Corporation; 
(xv) other regulated instrument, to the extent it is permitted 
for listing under section 119; 
(xvi) such other instruments as may be notified by the Central 
Government; and 
(xvii) rights or interests in securities, 
but shall not include any unit linked insurance policy or scrips or any such 
instrument or unit, by whatever name called, which provides a combined benefit 
risk on the life of the persons and investment by such persons and issued by an 
insurer defined in clause (9) of section 2 of the Insurance Act, 1938; 
(zj) “securities markets” means the markets related to securities; 
(zk) “securities markets service provider” means an intermediary, a 
market infrastructure institution or a self-regulatory organisation; 
(zl) “self-regulatory organisation” means a person, formed and promoted 
by a class of intermediaries or investors; 
(zm) “Special Court” means the Special Court established or designated 
by the Central Government under section 113; 
(zn) “stock exchange” means a person engaged in assisting, regulating 
or controlling the business of buying, selling or dealing in securities and 
undertaking other related functions, but shall not include agencies permitted 
to assist or facilitate buying, selling or dealing in instruments or securities 
under Chapter IIID of the Reserve Bank of India Act, 1934; 
(zo) “subsidiary instructions” mean the instructions made under  
section 149 and include any circular, master circular, guideline and such other 
instrument; 
(zp) “trading member” means a person having trading rights in any stock 
exchange and includes a stock broker; 
(zq) “Tribunal” means the Securities Appellate Tribunal referred to in 
section 79; 
(zr) “underlying” in respect of a derivative, means a security or a 
commodity or an actionable claim or money or currency or indices on any 
instrument or prices or yields or volatility or liquidity or credit score or ratings 
or events or activity or progress or a situation or financial measures or other 
instruments from which such derivative derives its value;

(2) The words and expressions used herein and not defined, but defined in the 
Reserve Bank of India Act, 1934 and the Companies Act, 2013 shall have the same 
meanings assigned to them in those Acts. 
10 CHAPTER II 
ESTABLISHMENT, MANAGEMENT AND ADMINISTRATION OF BOARD 
3. ( 1) The Securities and Exchange Board of India established and 
incorporated under section 3 of the Securities and Exchange Board of India 
Act, 1992 shall be deemed to have been established and incorporated under this 
15  Code, having its head office in Mumbai. 
(2) The Board may establish its offices at other places in India. 
(3) On and from the date of commencement of this Code, all properties and 
assets, movable or immovable, of or belonging to the Board and all rights and 
liabilities of the Board, shall continue to vest in the Board. 
20  4. (1) The Board shall consist of the following Members to be appointed by 
the Central Government, namely:— 
(a) Chairperson of the Board; 
(b) two Members from amongst the officers of the Ministry of the 
Central Government dealing with Finance and administration of the 
25 Companies Act, 2013, ex officio; 
(c) one Member from amongst the officers of the Reserve Bank, 
ex officio; and 
(d) eleven other Members of whom at least five shall be whole-time 
Members. 
30  (2) Without prejudice to anything contained in section 6, on the date of the 
commencement of this Code, the Chairman and Members appointed under 
section 4 of the Securities and Exchange Board of India Act, 1992, shall be deemed 
to be the Chairperson and Members appointed under clauses (a) and (d) of 
sub-section (1) for the remainder period. 
35  (3) The Chairperson and Members referred to in clauses (a) and (d) of 
sub-section (1) shall be appointed by the Central Government, in such manner as 
may be prescribed and the members referred to in clauses (b) and (c) of that 
sub-section shall be nominated by the Central Government and the Reserve Bank, 
respectively. 
40  (4) The Chairperson and Members referred to in clauses (a) and (d) of 
sub-section (1) shall be persons of ability, integrity and standing in dealing with 
problems relating to securities markets or have special knowledge or experience of 
law, finance, economics, technology, commodity markets, accountancy, 
administration or in any other discipline which in the opinion of the Central 
45  Government is useful to the Board: 
Provided that while appointing Members referred to in clause (d) of 
sub-section (1), the Central Government shall endeavour to appoint at least three 
persons with expertise in the securities markets. 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
Securities and 
Exchange Board 
of India. 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
Composition of 
Board. 

 
 
313 
 
 
Terms and 
conditions of 
service of 
Chairperson and 
Members of 
Board. 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
Removal of 
Members from 
office. 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
Management of 
Board. 
5. (1) The Chairperson and every whole-time Member of the Board shall hold 
office for a term not exceeding five years: 
Provided that no person shall hold office as the Chairperson or a whole-time 
Member on attaining the age of sixty-five years. 
(2) Every other Member referred to in clause (d) of sub-section (1) of 5 
section 4 shall hold office for a term not exceeding three years: 
Provided that no person shall hold office as other Member on attaining the age 
of seventy years. 
(3) The other terms and conditions of service of the Chairperson and the 
Members referred to in clauses (a) and (d) of sub-section (1) of section 4 shall be 10 
such as may be prescribed. 
(4) The term of office of ex officio Members shall continue as long as they 
hold the office by virtue of which they are such Members. 
(5) Notwithstanding anything contained in sub-section (1), or sub-section (2), 
the Central Government shall have the right to terminate the services of the 15 
Chairperson or a Member appointed under clauses (a) and (d) of sub-section (1) of 
section 4, at any time before the expiry of the period specified under sub-section (1) 
or sub-section (2), by giving notice of not less than three months in writing or three 
months’ salary and allowances in lieu thereof, and the Chairperson or such Member, 
as the case may be, shall have the right to relinquish his office, at any time before 20 
the expiry of the period specified under sub-section (1) or sub-section (2), by giving 
notice of not less than three months in writing to the Central Government. 
(6) The Chairperson and the whole-time Members shall not accept any 
employment under the Central Government or any State Government or any 
appointment with a securities markets service provider or a market participant or 25 
any other person associated with securities markets for a period of one year from 
the date on which they cease to hold office, except with the prior approval of the 
Central Government. 
6. The Central Government shall remove a Member from the office, if such 
Member— 30 
(a) is, or at any time has been, adjudged as an insolvent; 
(b) is of unsound mind and stands so declared by a competent court; 
(c) has been convicted and sentenced to imprisonment for an offence 
which in the opinion of the Central Government, involves moral turpitude; 
(d) has, in the opinion of the Central Government, acquired any financial 35 
or other interests that is likely to prejudice his functions; or 
(e) has, in the opinion of the Central Government, so abused his position 
as to render his continuation in the office detrimental to public interest: 
Provided that no Member appointed under clauses (a) and (d) of sub-section (1) 
of section 4 shall be removed under clauses (d) and (e) of this section unless such 40 
Member has been given an opportunity of being heard in the matter. 
7. (1) The general superintendence, directions and management of the affairs 
and business of the Board shall vest in a Board of Members referred to in section 4 
to exercise all powers and do all acts and things which may be exercised or done by 
the Board. 45 
(2) Save as otherwise provided by regulations, the Chairperson shall also have 
powers of general superintendence and direction of the affairs of the Board and may 
also exercise all powers and do all acts and things which may be exercised or done 
by the Board, except the powers under sections 128 and 146. 
50 

 
 
314 
 
 
 
8. (1) The Members of the Board shall meet at such times and places, and shall 
observe such rules of procedure in regard to the transaction of business at its 
meetings, including quorum at such meetings and maintaining arrangements for 
recording, safe-keeping and publications of the decisions, as may be specified by 
5  regulations: 
Provided that the Members of the Board may by circulation, take decisions in 
such manner as may be specified by regulations. 
(2) The Chairperson, or if he is unable to attend a meeting of the Board for 
any reason, any other Member chosen by the Members present from amongst 
10  themselves at the meeting, shall preside over the meeting. 
(3) All questions which come up before any meeting of the Board shall be 
decided by a majority of the Members present and voting, and in the event of 
equality of votes, the Chairperson, or in his absence, the person presiding shall have 
a second or casting vote. 
15  (4) Any Member, who has such direct or indirect interest, including the interest 
of any family member, as may be specified by regulations, in any matter coming up 
for consideration at a meeting of the Board shall, as soon as possible after the said 
interest has come to his knowledge, disclose the nature of his interest at such 
meeting and such disclosure shall be recorded in the proceedings of the meeting of 
20  the Board, and such Member shall not take any part in any deliberation or decision 
of the Board with respect to that matter. 
(5) The provisions of sub-sections (3) and (4) shall mutatis mutandis apply to 
all decisions taken by way of circulation. 
9. (1) The Board may appoint such other officers and employees as it considers 
25  necessary for the efficient discharge of its functions under this Code. 
(2) The qualifications, method of recruitment and terms and other conditions 
of service of officers and employees of the Board appointed under sub -section (1) 
shall be such as may be specified by regulations. 
10. No act or proceeding of the Board shall be invalid merely by reason of,— 
30 (a) any vacancy in, or any defect in the constitution of the Board; 
(b) any defect in the appointment of a person acting as a Member of the 
Board; or 
(c) any irregularity in the procedure of the Board not affecting the merits 
of the case. 
35  11. ( 1) Subject to the provisions of this Code, the Board shall protect the 
interest of investors in securities and promote the development of, and regulate the 
securities markets, by such measures as it may deem fit. 
(2) Without prejudice to the generality of the foregoing provisions, the Board 
shall exercise the powers and perform the following functions, namely:–– 
40 (a) register and regulate the market infrastructure institution; 
(b) register and regulate the intermediaries; 
(c) register and regulate the self-regulatory organisations; 
(d) regulate the business in securities markets; 
(e) regulate the issue, listing, delisting, transfer of securities, dealings in 
45 securities and other related matters by market participant, subject to the 
provisions of Chapter XV;
